<meta>This page marks the beginning of the actual fables and dialogues in the book, following the table of contents seen on the previous page. It introduces the first chapter, which sets a cosmic stage for the moral lessons to follow, using the Sun and Moon to discuss the vice of pride.</meta>

a iij

# On the Sun and the Moon. Chapter 1.

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration depicting the Sun and the Moon. On the left, the Sun is personified with a human face and surrounded by alternating straight and wavy rays. On the right, the Moon is also personified with a face and rays, but it is shown being pierced vertically through the center by a sword. Three four-pointed stars are positioned between the Sun and Moon, and two more are to the right of the Moon.</image-desc>

<image-desc>A large decorative drop-cap initial 'S' at the beginning of the text, featuring ornate floral and foliate scrollwork patterns.</image-desc>

<margin>Definition of the Sun?</margin>
**S**un is, according to the philosopher <note>The "philosopher" in medieval texts almost always refers to Aristotle, whose views on the cosmos were central to education.</note>, the eye of the world, the joy of God, the beauty of heaven, the measure of the lands, the power and origin of all things that are born, the lord of the planets, and the leader and protector of all stars.
<margin>Of the Moon</margin>
The Moon, indeed, is the ornament of the night, the mother of reason, the servant of moisture <note>original: "humoris ministra." This refers to the historical belief that the moon influenced the "humors" or fluids of the body and the tides.</note>, the measure of the earth, the mistress of the sea, the transformer of the air, and the rival of the sun. And because she is the rival of the sun, she began to disparage and defame him. The Sun, sensing this, spoke to the Moon, saying: "Why do you disparage and blaspheme me? I have always illuminated and protected you through my light, yet you have always hated and attacked me." To which the Moon replied: "Depart from me, for I do not love you. Because of your great splendor, I am valued as nothing in the world. If you were not raised high over the world, I would be the one in power." To her, the Sun said: "Ungrateful one! Let your own magnificence suffice for you. If I shine in the day, you indeed shine through the night. Let us therefore obey our Creator and do not be proud; instead, allow me to shine and to protect the Lord’s good gifts." 

But the Moon, even more enraged, withdrew in fury and called the stars to her. She gathered a very great army and began to do battle with the Sun. She sent arrows toward the Sun and strove to strike him with spears. The Sun, however, since he was positioned higher up, descended and divided the Moon with a sword; he cast down the stars, saying: "I will always do this to you when you are round." For this reason, the moon never remains round <note>A mythological explanation for the phases of the moon—waxing and waning.</note> and the stars have their fall <note>Referring to "falling stars" or meteors.</note>. The Moon remained in confusion, saying: "It was better to share a small portion than to lose the whole." 

In this way, many who are proud and haughty wish to rule alone <note>original: "soli dominari," a pun on "sol" (sun) and "soli" (alone).</note>, and they desire to have neither a superior nor an equal.

<margin>What is pride?</margin>
<margin>is man?</margin>
Wherefore the gloss <note>A "gloss" is a scholarly or religious commentary written in the margins of a text to explain its deeper meaning.</note> says: **Pride** is an inflamed arrogance which, despising inferiors, strives to rule over superiors and equals. For indeed, to wish to be above everyone is blameworthy; but to support another who is lesser than oneself is glorious, as Chrysostom says <note>Saint John Chrysostom, an early Church Father known for his eloquent preaching.</note>. For of such people, the poet says:

<summary>This opening chapter presents a fable about the Sun and Moon to illustrate the destructive nature of pride and the importance of accepting one's place in the natural order. It explains the phases of the moon and falling stars as divine punishments for the Moon's rebellion against the Sun.</summary>
<keywords>Sun, Moon, Pride, Astronomy, Moral Fable, John Chrysostom, Aristotle, Medieval Cosmology</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on pride following the rivalry between the Sun and the Moon. It transitions from general moral warnings about the dangers of high status into a specific fable about a cloud's hubris toward the planet Saturn, supported by the biblical example of King Nebuchadnezzar's fall.</meta>

<insert>Here. Those who climb higher experience greater falls.</insert>

They are lifted high so that they might fall with a heavier crash. And note that the greater the ascent, the greater and more dangerous the fall. For he who falls from a level and low place rises again quickly; however, he who falls from a height cannot rise. For the branches of a tree—as Chrysostom <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407), an influential early Christian preacher known for his eloquence.</note> says—which are at the top are quickly broken by a great wind, but those at the root are preserved. Whence also Quintus Curtius <note>A Roman historian (1st century AD) who wrote a famous history of Alexander the Great.</note> says that someone once told Alexander that although a great tree may grow for a long time, it is uprooted in a single hour. And although the lion is so proud, he is nevertheless made into food for small birds. A certain philosopher, coming to the tomb of Alexander, said: "Yesterday, the whole world was not enough for this man; today, he is content with a tomb five feet long."

## ¶ On Saturn and the Cloud. Chapter Two.

<margin>On the distance between the planets [or planetary order]</margin>
There are seven planets according to the sayings of the philosophers: namely Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, the Sun, Venus, Mercury, and the Moon. But there is a great distance between planet and planet, as Master Moses <note>Moses Maimonides (1138–1204), the preeminent Jewish philosopher, whose "Guide for the Perplexed" influenced medieval Christian thought.</note>, the greatest philosopher, relates, as is found in the legends. In such a way that

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration within a square frame. On the left side are several stylized, stacked cloud shapes. In the upper right of the frame is a large, bright eight-pointed star.</image-desc>

each circle of every planet has a thickness [equivalent to] a journey of five hundred years; that is, as much space as someone could travel in five hundred years on a flat road. Provided, however, that each day's journey is forty miles, and each mile is two thousand paces. Now on a certain occasion, a certain large and thick cloud began to lift itself up, saying: "Great is my excellence, for because of my magnitude the planets are unable to appear in the world while I place myself in the air. If, therefore, I am far and distant from them yet still cloud them so, how much more will I be able to darken and trample them—and much more the earth—if I ascend higher." And saying this, she began to stretch upward. And when she had ascended as far as Saturn and wished to climb above him, Saturn said: "Who are you who desires to climb where no one [else] ascends?" To him the cloud [replied]: "I shall ascend above you and cast you down." Hearing this, Saturn was immediately disturbed, ran to arms, and blocked her path. Moreover, he cast the cloud down and reduced her to nothing, saying: "It is fitting for him to fall back who wishes to stand above all." For this happened to Nebuchadnezzar <note>The Babylonian king who, according to the Bible, was humbled by God for his pride.</note>, who busied himself with climbing above all kings and princes of the earth so that he might be king of kings and lord of earthly lords. Of whom it is said in Daniel, chapter four: "When the heart of Nebuchadnezzar was lifted up and his appearance was hardened into pride, he was deposed from his throne." Whence it was said to him: "God shall cast you out from among men, and your dwelling shall be with beasts and wild animals; you shall eat hay like an ox, and seven times <note>original: "septem tempora." This refers to the seven years of madness described in the Book of Daniel.</note> shall pass over you." As it is said in the *Scholastic Histories* <note>The *Historia Scholastica* by Petrus Comestor, the standard medieval textbook on sacred history.</note>: He was not changed according to the passing of time, but according to the alienation of his mind, and his sense and use of language were taken away from him. And it seemed to him that he was an ox or a bull in his front parts, and a lion in his rear parts. Daniel also prayed for him. And so seven times...

<summary>This page discusses the moral theme of hubris through the metaphor of high falls and a fable where Saturn destroys an ambitious cloud, concluding with the biblical account of Nebuchadnezzar’s madness.</summary>
<keywords>pride, Saturn, Alexander the Great, Nebuchadnezzar, planetary distances, Moses Maimonides, Chrysostom, hubris, medieval cosmology</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page concludes the story of King Nebuchadnezzar’s penance following his prideful fall and begins a new astronomical fable about the North Star. It continues the book’s theme of the necessity of stable leadership and the dangers of ambition.</meta>

...ra <note>original: "ra" - the end of a word continued from the previous page, likely "tempora" (times) or "figura" (figure).</note>, that is, seven years were changed into seven months at his prayers <gloss>five</gloss> in which, however, the years were changed into seven months at his prayers. During those seven months, he suffered through a state of infancy for forty days. For another forty days, he returned to his heart <note>A biblical idiom meaning he regained his reason and conscience.</note>. He wept and prayed so much that from his great weeping his eyes became like raw flesh. Many people went to him and saw him. When the seven months were completed, he was restored. Yet he did not want to stop his penance immediately, but performed penance for up to seven years, eating neither bread nor meat and drinking no wine.

### ¶ On the North Star and other stars: Chapter Three.

<image-desc>Two side-by-side woodcut panels. The left panel is an ornate decorative initial 'T' featuring scrolling vines and floral motifs around a central flower. The right panel is a square frame containing eleven stars: one large eight-pointed star in the upper right, and ten smaller stars of six or seven points arranged in three horizontal rows (three in the top row, four in the middle, and three in the bottom).</image-desc>

<margin>On the position of the North Star</margin>
The North Star <note>original: "Transmontana stella" - literally the star "across the mountains," the standard medieval term for the Pole Star.</note>, so called, remains in the navel of the sky. For it does not set nor circle the sky like the others, but sitting in the middle, it rules and revolves all the stars. For it is the boundary or path of the sea, toward which the compass needle <note>original: "calamita" - an early term for a magnetic needle or lodestone used by sailors.</note> looks always and at every hour. All the other stars gathered to this star, saying with one voice: "Should you always sit and rest while we circle the sky daily and serve you unceasingly? Give way to a greater one and move back a little." To them it said: "You do not know what you ask. For I tire myself out greatly by ruling you, and if I did not keep you in your guards <note>meaning their fixed orbits or boundaries.</note>, you would stray much in your movements <note>original: "montibus" (mountains), likely a scribal error for "motibus" (movements).</note>. Therefore, I advise you to have peace." The stars, however, were not appeased by these words; rather, they replied with one voice, saying: "We beseech you to allow us to choose another ruler." The North Star, being disturbed, withdrew and renounced its lordship.
<margin>##</margin>
The stars then gloried and exulted greatly, and they held an election for a ruler. But because they were not found in unity, they began to argue and fight with one another; for which reason, like sheep without a shepherd, they wandered, not remaining in their established paths. Finally, acknowledging their error, they repented and returned to their ruler, and they honorably confirmed it, saying: "He who has a good ruler should never choose a worse one." Thus we also ought to act. For when we have a ruler who rules us rightly, we ought to honor and love him, not change him, but support him with all our strength because of the labors he sustains in ruling us. A rank of government is called a great honor, but it is a heavy burden. Whence Bernard <note>St. Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a highly influential medieval theologian and mystic.</note> says: "What is honor but a burden? What is power but torment?" <margin>Bernard</margin>

<summary>This page concludes the penance of Nebuchadnezzar and presents a fable about the North Star (Polaris) being challenged by the other stars, illustrating the chaos that ensues when a stable leader is removed.</summary>
<keywords>Nebuchadnezzar, penance, North Star, Polaris, astronomy, leadership, St. Bernard of Clairvaux, compass, navigation</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the celestial bodies and the hierarchies of the universe, shifting from the North Star to the nature of earthly power and the relationship between the stars, the sun, and the Creator.</meta>

<margin><note>original: "valeri⁹"</note> Valerius</margin>
What is our high rank <note>original: "sublimitas"</note> if not a storm that brings shipwreck? Who can hold honor without pain? Who can be in high office without tribulation? Who can have dignity without vanity? As Valerius relates in his seventh book regarding that king of subtle wit and counsel: when the crown <note>original: "dyadema"</note> was handed to him, he held it for a long time before placing it on his head, and considering it, he said: "O noble and happy scepter! If only you were not full of such anxieties and dangers." <margin>Augustine</margin> Because of this, Augustine says: Nothing is more laborious, nothing more difficult, and nothing more dangerous than to lead. This is also why Valerius tells in his seventh book of Cornelius Scipio, to whom Spain happened to be offered as a gift. He replied that he did not wish to go there, adding the reason: that he did not know how to act rightly there. For he did not consider himself a man sufficiently brilliant for such great honor and danger.

->¶ On the Evening Star and the Morning Star. Chapter Four.<-

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration depicting two identical eight-pointed stars side-by-side, bordered by a double-lined frame.</image-desc>

The Evening Star <term>Hesperus<gloss>the planet Venus appearing in the evening</gloss></term> is a star of the night. And the Morning Star <term>Lucifer<gloss>the planet Venus appearing in the morning</gloss></term> is the star of the early day which appears in the morning. Whence the verse: "The Evening Star shines by night, and the Morning Star by day." These two stars gathered all the others to themselves, and with all of them, they proceeded to the Creator of the stars, saying: "Lord Saturn, you have radiated upon us and placed us fittingly, but in this we have been supplanted: for our splendor and beauty do not always sparkle. Therefore, we piously pray that you should obscure the sun and deprive it of light, so that we may shine throughout the day as well." To them, the Creator replied: "This is not permitted. For the sun is the decorator of the day, the divider of the hours, and the origin of all things being born; without it, nothing could sprout. Therefore, I will not hear your prayers in this matter." Finally, the stars petitioned the Creator, saying: "O Maker of all creatures, at least hear us in this: stir up and destroy the clouds <note>original: "nebulas"</note> in the air which hinder our light. For because of the clouds, we are often unable to be contemplated or seen." To this, the Creator said: "Be silent, and do not present unjust things. It is even written in Cato: 'Ask for what is just, or what appears honorable.' For the clouds irrigate the world with their downpour. If it did not rain, the earth would be entirely unable to germinate or sprout." And saying this, he drove the stars away and said: "Let those who do not seek things that are orderly or pleasing be cast out." In this, it is clear that he who wishes to be heard ought to ask for those things that are just and honorable to do, and which are according to reason and the will of God. For this reason, Augustine says: "When you ask for those things which God praises and permits, ask with confidence." For that petition is granted by God. Whence it is that Isidore says: <margin>Seneca</margin> Many who pray are not heard because God confers better things upon them than those they requested. For Seneca relates in his second book *On Benefits* that Alexander the Great gave a city to a certain man asking for a penny. And when the man said that such a great gift was not appropriate for him, Alexander replied: "I am not looking at what is fitting for you to receive, but what is fitting for me to give." For God acts in this way: he often does not grant the things requested so that he may give better and more ample things. And as Isidore says: God often does not hear many according to their will, so that he may hear them according to their salvation.

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing reflections on leadership and the nature of prayer, drawing on classical and patristic authorities. The previous page discussed the North Star as a symbol of stable rule.</meta>

<margin>Valerius</margin>
<margin>Augustine</margin>
What is high office <note>original: "sublimitas"</note> if not a storm that brings shipwreck? Who can be in honor without pain? Who in high rank without tribulation? Who in dignity without vanity? As Valerius relates in his seventh book regarding that king of subtle wit and counsel, who, when the crown <note>original: "dyadema"</note> was handed to him, held it for a long time before placing it on his head, and considering it, said: "O noble scepter, how happy you would be if you were not so full of anxieties and perils." Because of this, Augustine says: Nothing is more laborious, nothing more difficult, nothing more dangerous than to lead. Therefore Valerius tells in his seventh book about Cornelius Scipio, to whose house the governance of Spain had fallen by lot; he responded that he did not wish to go there, adding as a reason that he would not know how to act rightly. For he did not consider himself a man sufficiently capable of such great honor and danger.

## ¶ On Hesperus and Lucifer. Chapter four.

<image-desc>To the right of the first six lines of text is a square-framed woodcut depicting two eight-pointed stars side-by-side.</image-desc>

Hesperus <note>The evening star, typically the planet Venus appearing after sunset.</note> is the evening star. And Lucifer <note>The morning star, Venus appearing before sunrise.</note> is the morning star which appears at dawn. Whence the verse: "Hesperus shines by night, and Lucifer by day." These two stars gathered all the others to themselves, and with all these they went before the Creator of the stars, saying: 
> Lord Saturn <note>In medieval cosmology, Saturn was the highest of the seven wandering "planets" and often depicted as a governing or creative force in these fables.</note>, you have irradiated us and placed us fittingly. But in this we are supplanted, because our splendor and beauty do not always shine forth. Therefore, we piously pray that you should obscure the sun and deprive it of light, so that we may shine throughout the day as well. 

The Creator replied to them: "This is not permitted. For the sun is the adorner of the day, the divider of the hours, and the origin of all living things, and without it nothing could sprout. Therefore, I will not hear your prayers in this matter." Finally, the stars petitioned the Creator, saying: 
> O Maker of all creatures, at least hear us in this. Stir up and destroy the clouds in the air which hinder our light. For because of the clouds, we are many times unable to be contemplated and seen. 

To this, however, the Creator said: "Be silent and do not present unjust things. It is even written in Cato <note>Referring to the *Distichs of Cato*, a collection of moral proverbs used as a primary school text in the Middle Ages.</note>: 'Ask for what is just, or what seems honest.' For the clouds irrigate the world with their flooding. If it did not rain, the earth would be quite unable to germinate and sprout." And saying this, he drove back the stars and said: "Let those who do not ask for things that are orderly or pleasing be driven away." From this it is clear that whoever wishes to be heard ought to ask for those things which are just and honest to do, and which are according to reason and the will of God. For this reason Augustine says: "When you ask for those things which God praises and permits, ask with confidence. For that petition is granted by God." This is why Isidore <note>Saint Isidore of Seville, a 7th-century scholar.</note> says: "Many who pray are not heard because God bestows better things on them than what they requested." For Seneca relates
<margin>Seneca</margin>
in the second book of *On Benefits* that Alexander <note>Alexander the Great.</note> gave a city to a certain man who asked for a penny <note>original: "denariu"</note>. And when the man said such a gift was not suitable for him, Alexander replied: "I am not asking what is fitting for you to receive, but what is fitting for me to give." For God acts in this way, because often he does not grant the things requested so that he may give better and more ample things. And as Isidore says: "For often God does not hear many according to their will, so that he may hear them according to their salvation."

## ¶ On the rainbow and the crab. Chapter five.

<vocab>Hesperus, Lucifer, Valerius Maximus, Augustine, Cornelius Scipio, Saturn, Seneca, Isidore of Seville, high office, stars, On Hesperus and Lucifer, On the rainbow and the crab</vocab>

<summary>This page uses a fable about the evening and morning stars to teach that prayers must be for just and necessary things, while also reflecting on the burdens of leadership through classical anecdotes.</summary>
<keywords>leadership, prayer, Hesperus, Lucifer, Alexander the Great, divine providence, astronomy, moral philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous discussion on the celestial bodies (the Evening and Morning Stars), this page shifts focus to the Rainbow and the constellation Cancer, using a fable to illustrate the dangers of discord and the strength found in unity.</meta>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration within a rectangular border at the top of the page. On the left side is a large, seven-pointed star with radiating internal lines. On the right side is a rainbow, represented as a semi-circular arch consisting of three concentric bands.</image-desc>

<image-desc>An ornate square woodcut drop cap 'A' featuring intricate floral and vine motifs.</image-desc>The Rainbow <note>original: "Arcus domini," literally the Bow of the Lord</note> of the Lord first appeared in the clouds after the Great Flood. it possesses two colors: watery and fiery. The watery color is a reminder of the first flood, and the fiery color represents the coming Judgment <note>a reference to the medieval belief that the world would end in fire</note>. It is said in the *Scholastic Histories* <note>original: "historijs scolasticis," referring to a popular 12th-century biblical paraphrase by Peter Comestor</note> that this bow did not appear for forty years before the construction of the Ark had even begun.

<margin>Cancer <gloss>the Crab</gloss></margin> Now, Cancer is a certain sign of the heavens, about which a verse exists: "Cancer is a sign; Cancer is everywhere malignant." <margin>Correctly</margin> Cancer, however, approached the Rainbow with an angry spirit and said: "Your audacity is great, for you seize the whole heaven and busy yourself with hindering my path and the course of the other stars. You shall be removed quickly, or you will be beaten by us most vehemently." 

To him, the Rainbow replied: "You are not well, brother; for I do not seek to hinder your path. For this reason, I show myself during the day, while you always run through the night. If you desire to do battle with me, you have meditated poorly on this, because a great company of stars stays with me, and the clouds and thunders are ready to fight against you. But I counsel that we go to a just judge, so that he may be able to end our complaint by a formal sentence."

And when they stood together before the judge and reported all these things, the judge said: "Wicked Cancer, what you seek is against the law. If you only travel in the night and the Rainbow only in the day, how can it be believed that he could hinder your path? Therefore, I deliver this sentence against you: that you shall never appear in the day and that you are condemned to pay the legal costs." Cancer, stripped of his case, blushed with shame, saying: "He who seeks out disputes brings confusion upon himself." 

For in this way, many desire to argue against the law and to litigate with others without cause; wherefore, having been stripped of their wealth, these evil men are subdued. <margin>Ecclesiastes <note>The quote that follows is actually from the Book of Proverbs 16:28, though the margin attributes it to Ecclesiastes</note></margin> Whence it is said: "A perverse man stirs up strife, and a wordy man separates princes." An evil man always seeks out quarrels. Whence Isidore <note>St. Isidore of Seville, a 7th-century encyclopedist</note> says: "Just as concord is accustomed to build up, so contention and discord destroy." <margin>Bede</margin> Bede <note>The Venerable Bede, an 8th-century English monk and scholar</note> says: "Through discord, the greatest things fall apart."

A certain father of a family had three sons. As he was dying, he called them to him, saying: "Bring me some small sticks." When they had been brought, he said to them: "Bend them and break them." They were not able to do so because the sticks had been bound together. The father said to them: "Pull out one, and then break the others." They were able to bend them, but not at all to break <note>The text ends mid-sentence: "interrūpe..." likely meaning "interrumpere" (to break apart)</note>

<meta>This page transitions from the general nature of the stars to specific celestial phenomena—the rainbow and the zodiac sign of Cancer—using a fable to illustrate the dangers of legalistic quarreling and the strength found in unity.</meta>

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut at the top of the page. On the left side is a large six-pointed star. On the right side is a double-arched rainbow. Below this is a large ornamental historiated drop cap "A" decorated with elaborate foliate patterns, scrolls, and flowers.</image-desc>

**T**he Rainbow of the Lord <note>original: "Arcus domini"</note> first appeared in the clouds after the Great Flood, having two colors: watery and fiery. It possesses a watery color as a reminder of the first flood, and a fiery color as a sign of the coming Judgment <note>The "watery" color represents the past destruction of the world by the Deluge, while the "fiery" color foretells the eventual destruction by fire at the end of time.</note>. It is said in the *Scholastic Histories* <note>referring to the *Historia Scholastica* by Peter Comestor, a popular 12th-century biblical commentary</note> that the rainbow did not appear for forty years before the building of the Ark had even begun. Now, Cancer <note>the zodiac sign of the Crab</note> is a certain sign of the heavens, concerning which this verse exists: "Cancer is a sign; Cancer is everywhere malignant." 

Cancer approached the Rainbow and, proceeding with an angry spirit, said: "Your audacity is great, for you take up the whole sky and busily hinder my path and the course of the other stars. Remove yourself quickly, or you shall be beaten by us most violently." To which the Rainbow replied, <margin>Rightly</margin> "Brother, you are not well, for I do not seek to hinder your way. This is why I show myself during the day, while you always run through the night. If you desire to battle with me, you have considered this poorly; for a great company of stars stays with me, and the clouds and thunders are prepared to fight against you. But I advise that we go to a just judge, so that he may be able to end our complaint by a formal sentence."

When they had come together before the judge and reported all these things, the judge said: "Wicked Cancer, what you seek is against the law. If you only travel in the night and the Rainbow in the day, how can it be believed that he could hinder your way? Therefore, I deliver the sentence against you: that you may never appear in the day, and that you are condemned to pay the legal expenses." Cancer, stripped of his pride, blushed and said: "He who seeks a dispute brings confusion upon himself."

Thus it is that many people desire to argue against the law and litigate with others without cause; because of this, the wicked are stripped and subdued. In Ecclesiasticus <note>The author likely means Proverbs 16:28</note>: "A perverse man stirs up strife, and a wordy man separates princes." The wicked man always seeks out quarrels. Whence Isidore <note>Isidore of Seville, a 7th-century scholar</note> says: "Just as concord is accustomed to build up, so contention and discord destroy." 

<margin>Bede</margin> Bede <note>The Venerable Bede, an 8th-century English monk</note> says: "By discord, even the greatest things fall apart." 

A certain father of a family had three sons. When he was dying, he called them to him and said: "Bring me some small rods." When they had been brought, he said to them: "Bend them and break them." They were unable to do so because the rods were bound together. The father then said to them: "Pull out one, and then you may break the others." They were then able to bend them, but by no means could they break... <note>The text ends mid-sentence; it is a version of Aesop’s fable "The Bundle of Sticks," illustrating that there is strength in unity.</note>

Again the father said: "Try now to break just one [branch] alone." This was immediately snapped and broken. Therefore he said to his sons: "So it will be with you. If you remain together in harmony, no one will be able to terrify or break you. But if you are separated by discord, anyone will be able to tear you apart and trample you <note>original: "suppeditare," meaning to trip up or trample underfoot</note>."

## ¶ Concerning the Heavens and the Earth. Chapter 6.

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial letter 'T' set within a square frame. The letter and its background are ornately decorated with dense floral and foliage patterns, including leafy scrolls and blossoms.</image-desc>The Earth, according to the philosopher <note>likely referring to Aristotle or a similar classical authority</note>, is the physician of the world and the guardian of its fruits; she is the lid of the underworld, the nurse of living things, the truth-teller of all, and the provider of earthly life. Once, however, the heavens sent down upon this earth rains, storms, lightning, and thunder, so much so that it burdened her greatly. She, being angry, called out to the Air, saying: "Brother, do not intervene between me and the heavens, because I shall cast him down entirely. For he has brought such injury upon me that I desire to avenge myself in every way." But the Air replied: "Sister, do not do this; rather, give place to your anger <note>original: "da locum ire," a phrase meaning to let the passion subside</note>. For even if the heavens have embittered you now, at another time you will rejoice with him." But the Earth, overcome by her rage,

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a natural landscape. Above is a curved, layered celestial sphere representing the heavens. Below is a rocky, jagged coastline or terrain meeting a body of water with stylized waves.</image-desc>did not wish to cease in the least, but ran to arms and began to do battle with the heavens. Watching this, the Air brought forth a darkness and a whirlwind, so that the Earth could not distinguish the heavens. This darkness remained between the heavens and the earth until the Earth set aside her anger and fury. Afterward, the Air sent out winds and drove away the darkness, saying: "Everyone ought to extinguish a fire and not inflame it. This is what anyone should do when they see their friends are angry. For anger hinders the mind so that it cannot perceive the truth; indeed, a person is 'outside of their own body' when they are angry. Therefore, they must be calmed and restrained by their friends until they are removed from their fury and have put an end to their anger." As 

<margin>Seneca</margin>
Seneca says: "To put an end to anger is the beginning of wisdom." For he who slays his wrath overcomes a great enemy. This is just as Valerius <note>Valerius Maximus, a Roman author of famous anecdotes</note> reports: when Archytas of Tarentum <note>original: "architar ētinus," a Greek philosopher and friend of Plato</note> was angry with his servant, he said: "O unhappy man, I would take my revenge upon you now if I were not angry." From this it is clear that anger must be cut away from the soul.

<margin>Proverbs 27</margin>
As it is said in Proverbs 27: "Anger has no mercy." Therefore, a judge should never pronounce any sentence while in anger. Thus we read in the histories of the Romans that when the Emperor Theodosius <note>Theodosius the Great, famously rebuked by St. Ambrose for a massacre committed in a fit of rage</note> was too quick to carry out orders, a certain wise man advised him that whenever he felt himself becoming angry, before he pronounced any sentence, he should slowly recite the twenty-three letters of the alphabet <note>The Latin alphabet of the time typically lacked J, U, and W</note>, so that having cooled down, he could more maturely see and know what ought to be decided.

## ¶ Concerning the Air and the Wind. Chapter 7.

It transitions into a dialogue between the Air and the Wind, personifying natural forces to deliver a moral lesson on the necessity of correction and discipline.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<sig>b ij</sig>

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration depicting a large, curved bellows-like instrument or horn on the left with several decorative bands. It is blowing a forceful gust of wind to the right, represented by a thick stream of wavy horizontal lines. Below the illustration is a large ornamental historiated initial "A" set within a square frame, filled with dense foliate and floral patterns including flowers and vines.</image-desc>

<margin>Definition of Air</margin>
<margin>Definition of Wind</margin>
**Air**, according to the philosopher <note>original: "sedm philosophum"; this refers to Aristotle, who was often called simply "The Philosopher" in medieval texts</note>, is the breath of all living things, without which all things that exist beneath it are immediately suffocated and die. The **Wind**, however, is the dryness of the earth, the mobility of the waters, and the disturbance of the air. And because it is a disturbance of the air, the Air itself summoned the Wind before the judge and creator of all things, saying: ʘ "O creator of things, look upon me and have mercy on me. You have placed me in a sufficiently ample position, for which I give you thanks, because you have established me as the life of all living things; but in this I am deceived <note>original: "deceptus sum," here meaning frustrated or disadvantaged</note>, because the wind always chills me and renders me immoderate. Therefore, tell him that if he presumes to blow upon me further, I will suffocate him."

To which the creator replied: "You speak poorly, Air. For even if the wind chills and lashes you, it nevertheless renders you purified and tempered. For if the wind did not blow within you, you would be sickly, tiresome, putrid, and corrupt, and hateful to everyone. Therefore, you ought to love him, because he preserves you in perfection and uprightness." The Air, then calmed by the wind, said: "We ought to endure correction and bear it patiently." So let everyone listen to and love those who correct them, because they demonstrate the way of justice. For a sick man who hates his physician does not allow himself to be cured, and thus he will not be set free. And he who hates correction will not be loved. Truly, a man hates the physician when he hates his corrector and cannot tolerate him. Hence Chrysostom <note>St. John Chrysostom, an early Church Father known for his eloquent preaching</note> says: "He incurs hatred who reproves the criminal." And Seneca says: "He who reproves the impious seeks a stain upon himself," for such people are not wise, but rather have been made into fools. Just as Ecclesiastes <note>referring to the biblical book or its attributed author</note> says: "He who loves to be corrected is wise; but the fool is angered if he is corrected." Therefore, correct the wise and they will love you. We read in the life of the blessed Ambrose <note>Saint Ambrose, the 4th-century Bishop of Milan</note> that when the Emperor Theodosius had punished certain citizens without judgment or deliberation, Ambrose, the Archbishop of Milan, expelled him from the church, even though he was a Catholic emperor. And when the Emperor saw this, he said: "For even David committed adultery and homicide." And to this—

**Ambrose** <note>Saint Ambrose (c. 340–397 AD), the Bishop of Milan who famously stood up to imperial authority</note> says: "If you followed him when he was wandering in error, follow him also when he corrects himself." The **Emperor** <note>This refers to Theodosius I, who performed public penance after the Massacre of Thessalonica</note>, hearing this, was struck with remorse and performed penance, saying: "I have found only one man of truth, the bishop Ambrose." And he decreed that no man should be judged to death until forty days had passed <note>original: "ante quadraginta dies"; a reference to the law intended to prevent hasty executions by allowing time for imperial mercy or review</note>.

## On the Shore and the Sea. Chapter 8.

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration showing a body of water with stylized, swirling, spiral-patterned waves situated between two rocky, jagged shores. Small tufts of grass are visible on the left bank.</image-desc>

<image-desc>A circular woodcut decorative initial 'M', intricately designed with symmetrical floral and foliate patterns, serving as the first letter of the following section.</image-desc>

The **Sea**, according to the philosopher <note>original: "scdm phlm" / "secundum philosophum"; referring to Aristotle</note>, is the embrace of the world, the source of moisture, and the host of rivers; for as the book of Ecclesiastes says: "All rivers enter into the sea, and the sea does not overflow; to the place from where the rivers come, they return to flow again" <note>Ecclesiastes 1:7</note>. This sea is great and spacious, says the philosopher. Wherefore, in its magnificence, it spoke to the shore as if it were a living being, saying: "I wonder greatly at your hardness. You have always contradicted me and existed as my opposite. Because of you, I cannot invade the land. I ask that you be moved from your place so that I may be able to trample the land underfoot; otherwise, I shall not cease to contend with you."

To which the **Shore** replied: "You speak poorly, brother. The Creator of all things placed me here, and I, because of obedience to Him, endure great labor in restraining you. You many times boldly climb over me and weigh heavily upon me, yet I bear you patiently for the sake of God. Therefore, you may chatter as much as you like against Him <note>original: "verbosari," meaning to use many words or argue</note>, for I will not change."

The **Sea**, hearing this, responded furiously: "If you can endure, then endure! For I will never leave you in peace, but will beat you with my force <note>original: "visibus," likely a scribe's error for "viribus" (strength/force) or "fluctibus" (waves)</note>."

The **Shore** truly placed its neck patiently under the yoke, saying: "It is fitting for the good to be purified and for the wicked to be chastened." For indeed, any **prelate** <term>prelate: a high-ranking member of the clergy, such as a bishop or abbot</term> and **ruler** <note>original: "rector"</note> ought to fight and act manfully in this way so that the wicked cannot prevail. Nevertheless, **Gregory** <note>Pope Gregory the Great (c. 540–604 AD)</note> says: "Just as the sea always rebels against the shore by which it is held and restrained, so in religious life some always rebel against the prelates who cannot restrain them; but good shepherds ought not to fear the threats of the wicked, but should remain vigilant like a shepherd who is accustomed to guard the sheep against wild beasts. Thus also the ruler of God over his flock..."

<summary>This page concludes a lesson on repentance using Saint Ambrose as an example before beginning a new fable where the Shore patiently resists the aggressive Sea, symbolizing a leader's duty to restrain the unruly.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Ambrose, Emperor Theodosius, penance, Sea and Shore, Aristotle, Ecclesiastes, Gregory the Great, leadership, discipline, patience</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between the Sea and the Shore, transitioning into the importance of vigilant leadership, before beginning Chapter 9 on the interaction between Fire and Water.</meta>

...it ought also to be permitted. But as **Isidore** <note>Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636 AD), a scholar and archbishop whose "Etymologies" was a standard reference in the Middle Ages</note> says: "Wicked shepherds have no care for the sheep." But as it is read in the Gospel regarding hirelings: "They see the wolf coming and they flee" <note>John 10:12</note>. For they flee at the moment they keep silent before the powerful and fear to oppose the wicked. Therefore **Jerome** <note>Saint Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), the translator of the Vulgate Bible</note> comforts us, saying: "Let us take care to please God, and let us not fear the threats of men in the least."

**Peter Comestor** <note>original: "petrus manducator"; a 12th-century French theologian known as "Peter the Eater" because he "devoured" books</note> relates that while **Philip**, king of the Macedonians, was resisting and besieging **Athens**, he said to them: "Give me ten orators whom I shall choose from among you, and I will depart as your ally." **Demosthenes**, a most skilled orator, replied: "The wolves said to the shepherds: 'The whole cause of the discord between us and you are the dogs; but give us the dogs and we will be friends.'" When the shepherds had done this, the wolves fell upon the flock and devoured them as they pleased.

From this it is clear that there is the greatest danger when the **dogs**—that is, the preachers and rulers—abandon the flock or neglect to bark. For just as gentleness in a man is praiseworthy and anger is blameworthy, so it is the opposite in a dog. For a dog is not meant to be gentle, because ferocity is praised in it. Thus also a discrete ferocity <note>original: "ferocitas discreta"; a controlled or discerning toughness used for protection</note> is praised in a ruler.

### On Fire and Water. Chapter Nine.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration contained within a rectangular frame. On the left side, a large fire is depicted with thick logs stacked at the base and swirling, curved tongues of flame rising upwards. On the right side, a bird with spread wings flies above a series of overlapping semi-circular patterns consisting of concentric spirals, representing water or clouds.</image-desc>

**Fire** is light, pure, subtle, mobile, bright, and hot. And because it is so precious in its own nature, it began to exalt itself, saying: "In brief, I prevail over all things on the earth and consume everything; but if I were to prevail in the waters, I would exist as superior to all things." Therefore it called out to the **Water**, saying: "Dearest sister, you are an element of God; you are even as I. Wherefore if I were to be glorified in you and joined with you, I am great, but I shall appear even more magnificent and excellent. Therefore I beseech you, permit me to dwell with you and to be glorified in you."

**Water**, indeed, began to feign a similar cleverness, saying: "With desire I have desired to eat this Passover with you" <note>original: "Desiderio desideraui tecum hoc pascha manducare"; a direct quote of Jesus in Luke 22:15, here used by the Water to trick the Fire</note>. "Approach me safely, and I shall improve you by my power." Fire, hearing this, began to rejoice and...

<sig>b iij</sig>

<summary>This page concludes a lesson on the necessity of "barking" leaders who protect their people like sheepdogs, using an anecdote from Demosthenes. It then begins a new fable where Fire, through pride, seeks to join with Water, who deceptively welcomes the union.</summary>
<keywords>Isidore of Seville, Peter Comestor, Demosthenes, Philip of Macedon, leadership, preachers, shepherds, fire, water, elements</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page concludes the story of Fire and Water (Chapter 9) from the previous page, where they attempted a reconciliation, and begins Chapter 10, which revisits their elemental conflict as a moral lesson on incompatible natures.</meta>

...entered into the water in a friendly way. But Water, while she held the fire within her, proved false to him <note>original: "falsificauit se"; acting contrary to her initial agreement of friendship</note>, saying: "This is the enemy and the opposite of my kind. He has often consumed me and reduced me to nothing; now I can avenge myself and extinguish him." But following the word of the Apostle <note>Likely referring to 1 Thessalonians 5:15 or Romans 12:17: "See that none render evil for evil unto any man"</note>: "I do not wish to return evil for evil lest the world be deprived of such a great good, yet I wish to humble him a little," and saying these things, she took hold of him slightly and began to urinate <note>original: "mingere"; a crude medieval metaphor for water overcoming fire</note> upon the fire. Because of this, Fire began to beg Water not to extinguish him. Water, taking pity on him, did not extinguish him entirely, but led him down to the earth, saying: "To God we give a sweet sound by returning good for evil." But many today, on the contrary, return evil for good when they seek to take vengeance, being unwilling to let offenses go.

For this reason **Jerome** <note>Saint Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), the scholar who translated the Bible into Latin</note> says: just as God has forgiven our sins in Christ, so let us also forgive those who sin against us. This breaks and revokes the injury already committed. Just as it is read in the *Histories of Alexander* <note>The "Alexander Romance," a collection of legendary tales about Alexander the Great</note> that when a certain man had gravely offended him, Alexander did not want to forgive him. **Aristotle** <note>The famous Greek philosopher who served as Alexander’s tutor</note>, however, knowing this, went to him and said: "I wish, my Lord, that today you would be more victorious than you have ever been before." When Alexander replied, "I wish it well," Aristotle said: "You, King, have overcome all the kingdoms of the world; but today you are overcome if you allow yourself to be defeated [by your anger]. If you also conquer yourself, you will be truly victorious. For he who conquers himself is strong against all things," says the philosopher. At these words, Alexander forgave the man and was appeased. Because of this, it is said in the sixteenth chapter of Proverbs <note>The Latin text cites chapter 6 ("vi"), but the verse is Proverbs 16:32</note>: "Better is a patient man than a strong man, and he who rules his soul is better than a conqueror of cities."

## On Water and Fire. Chapter Ten.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration contained within a rectangular frame. On the left side is a large fire with logs at the base and tall, stylized flames. On the right side are three rounded, hilly forms with spiral line patterns, likely representing water or earth. A bird is depicted in flight in the sky above the right-hand section.</image-desc>

Water called out to Fire, saying: "Why, brother, are we always opposites and enemies? It is good to keep the Lord's commandment: 'Love your neighbor as yourself' <note>Matthew 22:39</note>, because the fullness of the law is love."
To which Fire replied: "What you say pleases me, because love for one's neighbor works no evil; therefore let us go and make a dwelling together."
And when they lived together, they never found peace, because Fire often heated the water and reduced it to nothing. Water, however, often extinguished the fire. Therefore, strife and contentions between them did not cease. For this reason, they were divided from one another, saying: "No one ought to join himself with his opposite, nor..."

It features a notable anecdote where Aristotle convinces Alexander the Great that self-mastery is the greatest victory.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the moral instruction following the anecdote of Alexander the Great and Aristotle from the previous page. It concludes a lesson on the dangers of bad company and begins Chapter 11, a dialogue between the River and the Sea regarding gratitude and nature.</meta>

to stand. Thus, a good person should neither associate with nor live with one who is evil and perverse, who is his opposite. For today, justice loses its reputation, its reason, and its good works because of perverse company. Hence the **philosopher** <note>likely Aristotle, frequently cited as "the philosopher" in medieval scholastic texts</note> says: therefore, choose the good and humble with whom you may live, and you shall be good. The **Psalmist** <note>King David, the traditional author of the Psalms</note> says: *With the holy, you will be holy; and with the perverse, you will be perverted* <note>original: "Cum sancto sanctus eris: et cum perverso perverteris"; from Psalm 18:25-26</note>. The **Apostle** <note>Saint Paul</note>, considering it a danger to lead a life with the wicked, wrote to the **Thessalonians** <note>Likely referring to 2 Thessalonians 3:6</note>: *We command you, brothers, in the name of our Lord Jesus Christ, that you withdraw yourselves from every brother who walks disorderly.* Because **Isidore** <note>Saint Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636 AD), a scholar whose encyclopedias were foundational to medieval knowledge</note> says: It is a danger to lead a life with the wicked, and it is ruinous for those of pure will to associate with them. Wherefore the sky, washing the earth with the water it sends down, replied to a **carrier of charcoal** <note>original: "portitori carbonum"; a coal-man. This refers to a fable about a laundryman and a charcoal-burner who cannot live together because one cleans what the other soils</note> when he had lived with him: "Brother, we cannot dwell together; whatever I am able to wash clean throughout the day, you are able to stain and blacken in a single hour." For in this way, everything that a wise or good man acquires over a long time, a fool or an evil man destroys in one hour.

### Of the River and the Sea. Chapter 11.

---

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration within a rectangular border, depicting stylized, curling waves of water with spiral patterns.</image-desc>

A **River** <note>Latin: "Fluvius"</note> is, according to the **philosopher** <note>Aristotle, specifically referencing his theories on the water cycle</note>, an unfailing course, a refreshment of the sun, and an irrigation of the earth. It proceeded to the **Sea**, saying: "You are rightly called the Sea <note>Latin: "mare"</note>, which is to say 'bitter' <note>original: "amarum"; a medieval folk etymology linking the Latin words for sea and bitterness</note>; for you always live in bitterness. Is it not a great bitterness and ingratitude on your part when you continually drink of my sweetness <note>the fresh water of the river</note>, yet you always bestow bitterness back upon me?" The **Sea** replied: "Gold and silver are not mine, but what I have, I give to you. From the inner parts of my body I grant to you abundantly, as to a faithful friend. Therefore, you ought to endure my bitterness peacefully, for if you well..."

of prosperity: from which your greatest and inexpressible sweetness is derived. Hearing this, the water was entirely pacified, saying: "He who wishes to taste sweet things must endure bitter things." In this way, it is necessary for anyone to bear the bitterness of tribulations if they desire to taste the sweetness of heavenly things. Because **Gregory** <note>Pope Gregory I, or Gregory the Great (c. 540–604 AD), a doctor of the Church whose writings on morality were widely read in the Middle Ages</note> says: *Tribulation is the gate of the kingdom of heaven.* This is the gate of the Lord; the righteous shall enter through it <note>original: "hec porta domini iusti intrabunt per ea"; a reference to Psalm 118:20</note>. As the **Apostle** <note>Saint Paul</note> says: *Through many tribulations we must enter into the kingdom of God* <note>original: "Per multas tribulationes oportet nos intrare in regnum dei"; from Acts 14:22</note>. 

But some are like the monkey who, climbing a tree and finding the bitterness of the nut's shell, threw away the fruit and therefore did not taste the sweetness of the nut. In the same way, many foolish people cast aside tribulations when they feel their bitterness, and therefore they will not taste the sweetness of the reward of eternal life—a sweetness which will be given to those who endure tribulations patiently. 

Just as a certain blinded **abbot** <note>a superior of a monastery</note> used to say: "I give thanks to my God, who has delivered me from my enemies—who were accustomed to doing so much evil to me through those two robbers and traitors, namely the eyes, through which he <note>the devil or the flesh</note> committed many sins." Another religious man, when he had lost one eye, began to rejoice. And when others were grieving for him, he asked them for which eye they were mourning. They answered, "For the one that is lost." To which he replied: "He who has two enemies ought not to grieve for the one that perishes, but for the one that remains." 

Wherefore **Jerome** <note>Saint Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), the primary translator of the Latin Vulgate Bible</note> said to a certain blind man: "Do not grieve if you do not have what flies and serpents have." We have even seen certain philosophers pluck out their own eyes so that they might better focus their thoughts upon the earth <note>or "confine their thoughts within themselves," a reference to the Greek philosopher Democritus, who was said to have blinded himself to avoid distractions</note>.

## ¶ Of the Valley and the Mountain. Chapter 12.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a rugged mountain range with several jagged peaks and layered rock formations. Three birds are flying in the sky above the mountains. Below the image, the next section of text begins with a large, square decorative initial 'V' featuring a white letter on a dark background filled with ornate floral and leaf patterns.</image-desc>

A certain Valley lay at the bottom, having above it a high mountain. Overcome by the yoke of subjection, the valley began to speak impatiently against the mountain, saying: "O wicked and profane thing, why do you not cease to weigh me down for so long? Stop, you villain, from such a heavy burden! Change your place, for you have trampled upon me enough; otherwise, I shall take my revenge upon you!" 

To which the Mountain replied: "That which you say cannot be. For the Creator raised me up from the beginning, but placed you in the lowest depths. Therefore, bear it patiently until the last day, on which He shall bring low the mountains and the hills" <note>original: "montes et colles humiliat"; a common biblical theme, notably from Isaiah 40:4 or Luke 3:5, regarding the Day of Judgment</note>.

<summary>This page uses metaphors and religious anecdotes to argue that suffering and "bitterness" are necessary precursors to spiritual "sweetness," concluding with a fable about a Valley complaining of the Mountain's weight.</summary>
<keywords>patience, tribulation, Saint Gregory, Saint Jerome, monkey and the nut, blindness as virtue, humility, subjection, Day of Judgment</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing dialogue from the previous page, concluding the fable of the Mountain and the Valley and transitioning into a new chapter on the nature of gems and the value of hard work.</meta>

...will be [defeated].

¶ But the Valley, because of these words, became more emboldened and began to battle the Mountain with trees and herbs, wounding it with arrows and javelins. ¶ The Mountain, seeing this, was driven to a fury, saying: "I judge you by your own deed, you wicked servant, because you have dared to take up a fight against your lord." And saying this, he cast down a massive part of himself, blocked the mouth of the valley, and enclosed it entirely.

¶ The Valley, humbled by the blows, said: "Servants ought to obey their masters and not oppose them." In the same way, many impatient people act against their superiors. For this reason they are to be held under a yoke. As **Ecclesiastes** <note>The author here cites the Book of Sirach (Ecclesiasticus) 33:25–28, though the medieval text refers to the author as "the Preacher" (Ecclesiastes).</note> says: *Fodder, a wand, and a burden for a servant; set your servant to work.* <note>original: "Cibaria ⁊ virga ⁊ onus seruo. in opere constitue seruū"</note> Thus it is fitting to apply torture and shackles to a wicked servant; keep him at work so he is not idle, let him work under discipline and seek rest. For if you loosen your hand, he will then seek his freedom.

A certain master had a defiant and stubborn servant who, when he was idle, resisted his master as much as possible with words. But the master beat him soundly, withdrew his delicacies, and subjected him to hard labor. Exhausted by the labor, he corrected himself and put a bridle on his tongue. Whence **Ecclesiastes** <note>This quote is actually from the Book of Proverbs 29:21.</note> says: *He who nourishes his servant delicately from childhood shall afterwards find him stubborn.* <note>original: "Qui delicate a puericia nutrit seruum suuz: postea sentiet illum contumacem"</note>

## ¶ Here begins the section on stones and metals, and on
## precious stones. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Chapter 13.

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut initial letter 'G', featuring intricate foliate designs and scrolls within a square frame. The letter serves as the initial for the word 'Gemme'.</image-desc>**Gems** <term>Gemme: precious stones, used here as an allegory for spiritual virtues</term>—all the precious ones—gathered together, saying: "We are more precious to men than all other things, but if we are made available at a man's whim, we shall be despised. For this reason, let us hide in remote places so that we cannot be found without great labor and expense." This is why they are held so dearly by their owners and are not acquired without a high price. Because of this it is said: "He who desires these precious things must exert himself."

So also the servant of Christ, if he desires to lay hold of the precious virtues, must sweat so that he does not remain idle, for idleness is the enemy of the soul. Whence in the **Lives of the Fathers** <note>The *Vitae Patrum*, a famous medieval collection of stories about early Christian desert hermits</note>: *One demon tempts a monk who is working, but a thousand tempt one who is idle.* Therefore **Jerome** <note>St. Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), the scholar who translated the Bible into Latin</note> says: *Always be doing something good, so that the devil may always find you occupied.* <note>original: "Semp[er] aliquid boni facito. vt dyabolus semper inueniat te occupatū"</note> An occupied man is like a well-defended castle, which the enemy cannot harm. Whence **Augustine** <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD)</note> says: *He who is free for good exercise is not easily captured by the tempter.* For the prudent ought to encourage their younger sons to labor, lest they fall into poverty through laziness. Whence a certain...

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the previous discussion on the necessity of discipline and labor for servants to a series of fables and philosophical anecdotes illustrating the value of hard work, the nature of true nobility through virtue, and the mastery over one's passions.</meta>

A wise countryman <note>original: "rusticus"; here meaning a peasant or farmer.</note> planted a vineyard. When he was dying, he told his sons that he had hidden a treasure in the vineyard. However, he did not specify the place where it was located. After the father died, they began to dig the vineyard to find the treasure. From this it happened that the vineyard was made fertile and gave a very generous harvest. Seeing this, the sons realized and saw that the father had said there was a treasure hidden in the vineyard for this reason: so that they might cultivate the vineyard more diligently and work with all idleness removed. A certain philosopher taught his son, saying: "See, son, that the ant is not wiser than you, which gathers in summer with such great and careful labor that which it lives on in winter. Let the rooster not be more vigilant or stronger than you, who observes the morning hours so diligently, and by beating himself <note>A reference to the rooster flapping its wings against its sides before crowing.</note> rouses others to the night songs [of prayer]. And he is so strong that he justifies <note>Meaning he manages or satisfies.</note> many wives and makes them subject to himself—yet you say that you cannot even make one subject to you."

### ¶ Of the emerald and the ring. Chapter 14.

<image-desc>A large rectangular woodcut initial "S" containing intricate floral scrollwork and stylized foliage patterns.</image-desc>
The emerald <note>original: "Smaragdus"</note> is a gem, as Briton <note>William the Briton, a 13th-century writer often cited for definitions.</note> says, to which nothing greener is compared. For it makes green herbs and leaves appear by its sight alone; it fills the eyes and does not satiate. So says Papias <note>An 11th-century lexicographer.</note>. Now, a certain golden ring carried a precious emerald; because of it, everyone looked at it greedily. On a certain occasion, however, the ring spoke ungratefully, saying: "You have stayed in my station for a long time now, and you have never given me payment. Therefore, pay the rent for my dwelling and leave, or I shall cast you out stripped of everything." To which the emerald replied: "If I have stayed with you, through me you have always had honor and were carried on the finger of the king. And if you wish to expel me, sell me now, and from the price of my value pay for your station, because a dwelling will not be lacking for me." But when the ring expelled him from its house and remained without him, it was touched by all [as something common] and at the end, having been melted down, it said: "He who sits with the precious becomes abject if the precious one departs." In this same way, the servant of Christ is to be honored while he retains precious virtues within himself; if, however, he casts them away, he becomes something to be cast out. But as Seneca says: *A good man, if he is good, proves himself by his actions.* Just as the philosopher Diogenes was found proven. When Alexander <note>Alexander the Great.</note> said to him that he was the lord of the world, [Diogenes replied]: "By no means are you a lord, but a servant of my servants. For Pride <note>original: "superbia"</note> is your mistress and my handmaid, for she leads you. I, however, have trampled her underfoot. Carnal lust <note>original: "carnalis concupiscentia"</note>, gluttony, and anger are your mistresses and my handmaidens, because they lead you and dominate you; I, however, have trampled them and conquered them." And therefore

"you are the servant of my servants" <note>original: "tu es seruorum meorum seruus"</note>. And when Alexander’s servants wanted to rush at him, Alexander said: "See that you do not touch him, for he is truly good and speaks the truth." Wherefore Gregory <note>Likely Pope Gregory the Great, a frequent source for moral guidance.</note> says: A man of great dignity, if he lives in the manner he ought—namely, without vices—is a sign of great usefulness.

### ¶ Of the sapphire and the goldsmith. Chapter 15.

<image-desc>Decorative floral initial 'U' featuring elaborate scrollwork and foliage motifs inside a square frame.</image-desc> As Isidore <note>Isidore of Seville, whose 7th-century encyclopedia "Etymologies" was a standard reference for medieval science.</note> says in book sixteen: The sapphire is like the clear sky, which, when struck by the rays of the sun, emits a burning brightness. A certain goldsmith <note>original: "aurifex"</note> intended to place this precious sapphire into the emperor’s crown <note>original: "dyademate"</note>. Beholding this, the sapphire said: "Good master, do not shut me in, for I am free, young, and worldly; therefore I do not wish to be separated from the world, but I desire to enjoy myself and wander through the world." To which the goldsmith replied: "Your enclosure will be your holiness, for I shall place you in your proper spot, where you may live henceforth without fear. For I consider your preciousness and value, and I fear that if you wander through the world, you will be lost." This is what 

<image-desc>Woodcut of a man in medieval attire kneeling on a tiled floor and presenting an ornate crown or diadem with both hands.</image-desc> John says in his case: "The whole world is seated in wickedness" <note>A reference to the biblical passage 1 John 5:19.</note>; for so many are the dangers of this world that neither the heart can think of them, nor the tongue express them. Wherefore Gregory says: We ought to fear seven things, because we are always in danger. Because of these things, my dearest son, I do not want to lose you, nor to leave you in [careless] hands throughout the house, but to keep you in safe custody. And saying this, he set the sapphire in the crown, where it triumphed forever, saying: "It is better to stand in a safe place than to wander." Thus it is safe for a religious man to remain in his cloister and his church. This is what is said in the *Lives of the Fathers* <note>original: "vitis patrum"; a famous collection of stories about early Christian monks.</note>: "Go into your cell <note>The private room of a monk.</note> and it shall teach you all things, for there is peace in the cell, but only war outside of it." Abbot Evagrius said to someone who told him, "I cannot fast, nor work, nor serve the sick": "Go, eat, drink, and sleep; only do not leave your cell." For perseverance in the cell leads a monk to his order, and thus he gradually returned to the works of perfection.

<meta>This page contains a series of moral parables focusing on the value of diligent labor and the preservation of inner virtue. It precedes the discussion of the sapphire and the goldsmith mentioned in the subsequent section.</meta>

A wise peasant planted a vineyard. When he was dying, he told his sons that he had hidden a treasure in the vineyard. However, he did not specify the exact place where it was. After their father died, the sons began to dig up the vineyard in order to find the treasure. Because of this digging, it happened that the vineyard was made fertile and yielded a most abundant harvest of fruit. Seeing this, the sons understood and saw that their father had said the treasure was hidden in the vineyard for this reason: so that they would cultivate the vineyard more studiously and labor with all idleness removed. 

A certain philosopher taught his son, saying: "See, my son, that the ant is not wiser than you, who gathers in the summer with such great and careful labor that which she may live on in the winter. Let not the rooster be more vigilant or stronger than you, who observes the morning hours so diligently and rouses others to song by beating himself <note>A reference to the medieval belief that roosters flap their wings to wake themselves up or stimulate their spirits before crowing.</note> during the night. He is so strong that he rules over many wives and makes them subject to him; yet you say you cannot subject even one to yourself."

## ¶ Of the emerald and the ring. Chapter 14.

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'S' containing intricate foliate scrollwork (rinceau style).</image-desc>

The emerald <note>original: "Smaragdus"</note> is a gem, says Brito <note>William Brito, a 13th-century grammarian and theologian.</note>, to which nothing greener can be compared. For it makes green herbs and leafy branches appear so by its sight alone. It fills the eyes and does not satiate them. So says Papias <note>An 11th-century lexicographer who wrote a major dictionary.</note>. Now, a certain golden ring held a precious emerald; because of the stone, everyone looked at the ring greedily. On a certain occasion, however, the ring spoke ungratefully, saying: "You have stayed in my station for a long time now, and you have never given me any payment. Therefore, pay the rent for my dwelling and leave, or I shall cast you out stripped of everything." To which the emerald replied: "If I have stayed with you, it is through me that you have always been held in honor and carried upon the finger of the king. And if you wish to expel me, sell me now, and from the price of my value, pay for your station; for a dwelling will not be lacking for me." 

But the ring, when it had expelled the emerald from its house and remained without it, was touched by everyone <note>Likely meaning it was handled as common scrap metal rather than a prized jewel.</note> and finally, as it was being melted down, it said: "He who sits with the precious becomes dejected if the precious one departs." In this way, a servant of Christ is to be venerated while he retains precious virtues within himself; but if he casts them away, he himself is to be cast out. 

But as Seneca <note>The Roman Stoic philosopher, frequently cited in medieval moral texts.</note> says: A good man, if he is truly good, proves it by his actions. Just as the philosopher Diogenes was found proven. When Alexander told him that he was the ruler of the world, Diogenes replied: "By no means are you a master; rather, you are the servant of my servants."

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration showing a faceted, oval-shaped gemstone on the left and a ring set with a similar large gemstone on the right, surrounded by a stylized leafy or flame-like setting.</image-desc>

"For Pride is your mistress, but she is my handmaid, because she leads you. But I have trampled her underfoot. Carnal concupiscence, gluttony, and anger are your mistresses and my handmaids, because they lead you and dominate you; but I have trampled them underfoot and conquered them. And therefore..."

<summary>This page uses parables of a hidden treasure in a vineyard, the diligence of animals, and a dispute between a ring and an emerald to illustrate that true value comes from labor and internal virtue rather than external status. It concludes with Diogenes the Cynic asserting his moral superiority over Alexander the Great by claiming to have mastered the vices that enslave the king.</summary>

"...you are the servant of my servants." And when Alexander's servants wished to rush upon him, Alexander said: "See that you do not touch him, for he is good and speaks the truth." Hence Gregory <note>original: "Gregorius"; likely Pope Gregory the Great</note> says: "A man of great dignity, if he lives in the manner he ought—that is, without vices—is a sign of great usefulness."

### ¶ Of the Sapphire and the goldsmith. Chapter 15.

<image-desc>Large decorative drop cap 'E' containing detailed floral and foliate scrollwork within a rectangular border.</image-desc>And Isidore <note>Isidore of Seville, a 7th-century scholar often cited for his encyclopedia of natural history.</note> says in Book 16: The sapphire is like the clear sky, and when struck by the rays of the sun, it emits a burning brightness. Now, a certain goldsmith <note>original: "aurifex"</note> intended to set this precious sapphire in the Emperor's diadem. Noticing this, the sapphire said: "Good master, do not enclose me, because I am free, young, and worldly <note>original: "secularis"; referring to living in the world rather than in a monastery</note>; therefore I do not wish to be separated from the world, but I desire to enjoy myself and wander through the spaces of the world." To which the goldsmith replied: "Your enclosure will be your holiness, for I shall set you in your proper place, where you may live hereafter without fear. For I consider your preciousness and your value, and I fear that if you wander through the world, you will be lost." This is what John says in his letter: "The whole world is seated in wickedness" <note>1 John 5:19</note>. For there are so many perils in this world that neither can the heart think of them, nor the tongue express them. Hence Gregory says: "We ought always to fear because we are always in danger." For this reason, my dearest son, I do not wish to lose you nor to leave you to be passed from hand to hand through the house, but to keep you in safe custody. And saying this, he inserted the sapphire into the diadem, where it boasted perpetually, saying: "It is better to stand in a safe place than to wander." For in this way it is safe for a religious man to remain in his cloister and his church. Therefore it is said in the *Lives of the Fathers* <note>original: "vitis patrũ"; a famous collection of stories about the early Christian desert monks</note>: "Go into your cell, and it will teach you everything. For there is peace in the cell, but outside there are only wars." The Abbot Evagrius <note>A 4th-century Christian monk and ascetic teacher</note> said to a certain man who had told him, "I cannot fast, nor work, nor serve the sick": "Go, eat, drink, and sleep; only do not leave your cell." For perseverance in the cell leads the monk to the proper order, and so gradually he was returned to the work of perfection.
