--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing themes from the previous chapter, which compared the spiritual life to the properties of gemstones. Having discussed the sapphire's desire for worldly freedom, the text now warns of the dangers of leaving religious life and introduces a new fable about a carbuncle and a mirror.</meta>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of an ornate cross flory with a central diamond shape and four smaller diamonds at the terminals. The cross stands on a stepped pedestal. Within the rectangular frame, to the left of the cross, are two small geometric shapes: a square and a circle.</image-desc>

"Why should one always remain in the church, never depart, nor feel anything of the world? I wish to return to the secular world and be refreshed for a little while with worldly people, so that I may be able to rejoice in the world and also reign with Christ." <note>This character attempts to justify his return to the world by claiming he can enjoy earthly life and still achieve salvation.</note> And when he had returned to the world and departed from the most holy church, he was captured by illicit things and the desires of the world, and he exposed himself to all manner of shames. At the end, however, he was found by barbarians and, being unknown to them, he was crushed <note>original: "conlisus"; implying being struck down or dashed to pieces</note> and scattered, so that he never appeared again. Since he was broken, it is only just that he who departs from a sacred place should be scattered. Therefore, let those in religious orders <note>original: "regulares"; those living under a monastic rule</note> beware of returning to the world, lest they suffer a similar fate. For worldly desires are the shipwrecks of the religious. And **Augustine** says: "Just as the love of God is the source of all virtues, so the love of the world is the source of all vices." <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational theologian of Western Christianity.</note> Hence, he who wishes to possess God renounces the world, so that God may be his blessed possession. **Bernard** says: "The perfect servant of Christ has nothing but Christ; or if he has anything besides Christ, he is not perfect." <note>St. Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a Cistercian abbot and influential mystic.</note> Hence Bernard also says: "Those who are enriched with spiritual goods ought not to be entangled in earthly business." It is read in the *Lives of the Fathers* <note>original: "vitis patrum"; a famous collection of the lives and sayings of the early Christian hermits who lived in the Egyptian desert.</note> that a certain man, seeking a place of solitude, prayed to God to show him a suitable location. He was led by an eagle, which he followed as if it were an angel, to a place where there was a palm tree and a spring. There he remained for many years, persistent in prayer, eating from the fruits of the palm and clothing himself in its leaves. However, the Devil, taking the form of a courier <note>original: "cursor"; a messenger or runner</note>, looked upon him with wonder, saying that he had searched for him for a long time. He claimed the man’s deceased father had left an inheritance for which many were prepared to fight, and he urged the man to follow him so he could distribute the property and then return. But as they approached the city, the Devil fled. The man found his parents were actually alive; happy in their company, he remained there. Giving himself over to food and drink and the aforementioned lusts, he ended his life in many shames.

## Of the Carbuncle and the Mirror. Chapter 18.

<image-desc>A large historiated woodcut initial "C" decorated with floral and foliate motifs, featuring a large lily-like flower in the center surrounded by curling vines.</image-desc>The **carbuncle** <note>original: "Carbunculus"; a term used in the Middle Ages for any red gemstone, like a ruby or garnet, believed to glow in the dark like a coal.</note> is a precious stone so called because it is fiery. To it approached a certain glass mirror, saying: "Brother, among precious gems you are splendid and bright; I also shine clearly, so that in me all things are clearly seen. Therefore, if we are joined together, we shall be a hundred times more excellent and more highly valued." To which the carbuncle replied: "I will not consent to what you say, for I consider that you were created from weak material—namely, from glass. I, however, am of the material of precious gems. Therefore, it is not fitting. For as **Isidore** <note>Isidore of Seville, often cited for his medieval encyclopedia, the Etymologies.</note> says: 'The son is usually similar to the mother.' Therefore, because you are not like me, depart."

<summary>This page warns against leaving the religious life for the world, using the tragic example of a monk and a hermit, and begins a new fable where a carbuncle rejects a glass mirror to maintain its purity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the moralizing discourse found in the previous section, shifting from the consequences of leaving religious life to the necessity of resisting the devil's temptations. It introduces a new chapter on the Agate and its interaction with the Cerastes serpent.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>

And he added: "Never should a noble descend to the level of the vile, nor contend with him." So indeed the Christian—who is of a nobler race if he is from Christ, for a 'Christian' is named after Christ—ought not to descend or listen to the devil, because he is lower than all others. Instead, he ought to avoid him and not consent to any fellowship with him. Whence it says in the *Book of Clement* <note>original: "libro clementis"; likely referring to the *Clementine Recognitions*, an early Christian romance attributed to Pope Clement I.</note>: "He who spontaneously submits himself to the wills of the devil shall not have peace against God and men." And **Augustine** <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD).</note> says: "The devil has no power, unless someone should choose to provide the assent of his own will." For which reason **Jerome** <note>St. Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), known for translating the Bible into Latin.</note> says: "Indeed, the power of the devil is not feared, nor ought it to be feared. Because

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration. The left side depicts a circular ring or setting containing a round gemstone with four small diamond-shaped facets. The right side shows a smooth, egg-shaped or oval stone. Both objects are presented on a simple ground indicated by vertical hatching.</image-desc>

fear of him and his boasting are always within your will. For the flesh can do nothing except what the soul wills." It is read in the *Lives of the Fathers* <note>original: "vitis patrum"; a collection of hagiographic writings on the early Christian hermits and monks of the Egyptian desert.</note> that a certain hermit was led by an angel to a certain place where there was a congregation of holy monks, and he saw the surrounding areas full and the vicinity of the place [covered] with a great multitude of demons flying about like flies. However, coming to a certain city where a large market was being held, he saw only one single demon standing quite idly over the gates. When he asked why this was so, the angel of the Lord replied that in the city, everyone did what they wanted according to the devil's will, and therefore one demon was sufficient there. In the abbey, however, everyone was rebellious and contradictory [to the devil]. And therefore many demons were attacking them.

## On the Agate and the Cerastes serpent. Chapter XVIII.

<image-desc>A large decorative initial 'A' set in a square frame. The letter is intertwined with elaborate floral and vine motifs, including leaves and stylized blossoms.</image-desc>

The **Agate** <note>original: "Agathes"</note>, says **Papias** <note>Papias the Lexicographer (11th century), author of an early dictionary.</note>, is a gemstone that has black and white and various circles. **Britto** and **Isidore** <note>Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636 AD).</note> in the sixth book of the *Etymologies* say that it is a gemstone first found in Sicily near a river of the same name. Afterwards, indeed, it was found in many lands. And **Huguccio** <note>Huguccio of Pisa (d. 1210), a scholar of canon law and grammar.</note> says that it makes a person <term>gracious</term> <note>meaning pleasant or favored by others</note>. The **Cerastes** <note>A horned viper common in the Middle East and North Africa.</note> is a serpent so-called, as **Isidore** says in the twelfth book of the *Etymologies*, because horns like a crown are located on its head. Since this serpent was hateful to everyone and avoided and cast out by all, it approached the **Agate**, saying: "O Agate, most precious gemstone, I ask you to descend to me: and place yourself between my horns, since I will carry you excellently. Because you have the power to make your bearer graceful; moreover, I promise you that if you [support] me...

<summary>This page concludes a teaching on resisting the devil's influence through the strength of the will and begins Chapter 18, which describes the properties of the Agate and introduces a fable involving a Cerastes serpent.</summary>
<keywords>Christian nobility, devil's temptation, Lives of the Fathers, Agate, Cerastes serpent, Isidore of Seville, Papias, Huguccio, moral allegory</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues a moral dialogue where a personified Agate stone refuses to cooperate with a predatory figure (implied to be the serpent Cerastes from the previous page). It transitions into Chapter 18, which begins a new allegorical debate between Lead and Gold regarding their inherent value and their endurance when tested by fire.</meta>

"...you would make me favored. I will despoil the lower nobility and the innocent, and I will grant half the plunder to you." 
To him, the **Agate** <note>The agate stone was often personified in medieval "bestiaries of stones" as a symbol of virtue or a protector against poison and evil.</note> replied: "That which you say does not please me. Because the **Apostle** says: 'Not only those who do such things, but also those who consent to them, are worthy of eternal death.' <note>original: "Non solum facientes sed etiam consentientes digni sunt perpetua morte"; a reference to Romans 1:32.</note> And as it is commonly said: 'The one who holds the victim is worth as much as the one who skins him.' Therefore, depart from me, because your company is not fitting for me." 

And saying this, he went forward and said: "We ought to oppose evil, not consent to it. For this is what **David** did, as it is said in the **Psalm**: 'I have hated those who commit transgressions' <note>original: "Facientes prevaricationes odiui"; Psalm 101:3 (Vulgate 100:3).</note>, whether it be my father, or mother, or sister, or brother. The same would apply if it were a friend, or a bishop, or a priest, or anyone established in some position of dignity. Whence in the *Policraticus* <note>original: "policrato libro"; a famous 12th-century book on ethics and government by John of Salisbury.</note>, it is told as an example that the blessed **Jerome** <note>St. Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), the translator of the Latin Vulgate Bible.</note> excluded three clerics from his table because he saw them behaving in an unruly manner, saying: 'It is shameful and unfitting for an honest and serious man to find someone unruly in his company.'"

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a crowned serpent or basilisk with wings and a bird-like head, standing in a stylized landscape next to a rectangular object.</image-desc>

### Chapter 18: Of Lead and Gold

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial letter 'A' featuring intricate floral scrollwork and a bird-like figure within its form.</image-desc>

Animated **Lead** approached **Gold**, saying: "Why do you behave so proudly? Am I not made of the same metallic matter as you? For what reason do you look down on me and not wish for me to be as highly valued in the world as you are? Come to me and test yourself with me in the fire, and you will see the power that dwells within me." 

To him, **Gold** replied: "I know, brother, that you are a metal just as I am. The Creator created you just as He created me, and so I remain. For I do you no injury. Take what is yours and go; do not strive with me." 

To this, **Lead** replied: "Do not liken yourself to me nor defend yourself, for it is necessary for us to be tested in all things. According to the word of the **Apostle**: 'Test all things; hold fast to what is good.' <note>original: "Omnia autem probate: quod bonum est tenere"; 1 Thessalonians 5:21.</note> Therefore Lead said boldly to Gold: "Step into the fire and fight with me; then your virtue and your victory will be revealed." And when they went into the fire together...

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting three oblong, slightly curved objects, possibly metal ingots or pieces of lead and gold, enclosed in a rectangular border.</image-desc>

<summary>This page concludes a moral lesson on refusing to associate with the wicked and introduces a personified debate between Lead and Gold. The metals challenge each other to a trial by fire to prove their true nature and worth.</summary>
<keywords>Agate, Lead, Gold, St. Jerome, John of Salisbury, Policraticus, trial by fire, allegory, moral integrity, metalwork</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page concludes the moral allegory between Lead and Gold from the previous chapter. It transitions into Chapter 20, which presents a dialogue between Gold and Silver, utilizing Aesop’s fables to warn against unequal social or political alliances.</meta>

...the lead was melted and vanished, while the gold remained purified. Vain and proud words are without virtue. For in this way, some who are proud, vain, and conceited think they possess virtues which they do not actually have; therefore, if they are tested, they are reduced to nothing, just like lead in a fire. Therefore, if you wish to be precious in the eyes of the Lord, strive to appear more lowly in your own eyes. On this matter, **Isidore** <note>Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636 AD), a scholar and archbishop whose "Etymologies" was a primary reference for the Middle Ages.</note> says: "Be small in your own eyes, so that you may be great in the eyes of the Lord." <note>original: "Esto parvulus in oculis tuis: vt sis magnus in oculis domini."</note> For you will be all the more precious before God the more you are humble in your own sight. Because he who is lowly to himself is great before God. And as the **Gloss** <note>The "Glossa Ordinaria," a standard collection of biblical commentaries used by medieval scholars.</note> says: "The more glorious you are in your own eyes, the more base you will be before God and his angels."

There was a certain lady in Rome of such great humility and reverence <term>reuerentia - a deep religious awe or respect</term> that she judged herself unworthy to approach the altar; even when the **Body of Christ** <note>The consecrated bread or Host used in the Eucharist.</note> was lifted up, she judged herself unworthy to look upon it. Thus, when communion was once being distributed among the people in Rome, she did not approach to receive it out of her extreme humility and reverence. Because of this, a divine miracle occurred: a dove took the host from the altar and, in the sight of all the people, gave her communion.

## On Gold and Silver. Chapter 20.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration contained within a square black border. It depicts a cluster of approximately seven overlapping rectangular and wedge-shaped blocks, likely representing refined bars or ingots of gold and silver.</image-desc>

**Gold** went to **Silver** and said: "Rejoice, brother, since we hold the leadership among metals. Therefore, if we are joined together, we shall be more sublime and stronger." To this, **Silver** gave an answer, saying: "That which you say, brother, you say out of charity. However, I consider that you have a reddish color and I have a white one; I also consider that you are of great price and value. Therefore, I think that just as we are divided and contrary in color and price, so we shall be in our will. Thus, it is better not to begin than to have to withdraw from what was started." And he added: "We should never equate ourselves with the great, nor stand with them. Because **Ecclesiastes** says: 'He takes a burden upon himself who joins himself to one more honorable than he.' <note>original: "Pondus super se tollit qui honestiori se connecti"; referring to Sirach (Ecclesiasticus) 13:2.</note> Whence the same says: 'Do not be a companion to one richer than yourself.' For this reason, **the Philosopher** <note>A standard medieval title for Aristotle.</note> says: 'Where the poor man begins to make an enemy of the rich, he perishes.'

Likewise, **Aesop** <note>The legendary Greek storyteller to whom many fables were attributed.</note> relates that a goat, a sheep, and a cow made a partnership with a **Lion** so that they might go hunting together. Together they all caught a stag. In the division of the prize, the Lion said: 'I shall be the heir of the first part, for the first honor belongs to me; the primary strength defends the second part for me; the greater labor gives me the third; and as for the fourth part, unless it be mine, my hindering wrath will break our friendship.' And saying this, he began to strike the ground with his tail and gnash his teeth. Terrified, they left the entire stag to the Lion. From this it is clear that a person must take care not to establish a partnership with those more powerful than himself, because he would always have the worse part. And therefore, **Aesop** says: 'The present page warns that the fragile should not be allied with the strong. For the powerful man does not know how to be faithful to the fragile.'"

<summary>This page concludes a lesson on the virtue of humility, illustrated by a miracle in Rome, and begins an allegorical warning against unequal social alliances using the voices of Gold and Silver and Aesop's fable of the Lion's Share.</summary>
<keywords>humility, Lead and Gold, miracle of the dove, Roman lady, Gold and Silver, social hierarchy, Aesop's fables, the Lion's Share, unequal partnerships, Aristotle, Isidore of Seville</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Dialogue of Creatures," a popular medieval collection of moral fables. Following the previous chapter's discussion on the humility of lead and the purity of gold, this page introduces Chapter 21, a debate between Silver and Iron regarding their respective roles in human sin and industry, and concludes with a warning about the dangers of speaking out of turn.</meta>

## ¶ Concerning Silver and Iron. Chapter 21.

<image-desc>A large, ornate woodcut initial letter 'A' decorated with swirling vine and leaf patterns. Two human figures are integrated into the design: one appears to be climbing the vines on the left, and another is positioned within the counter of the letter on the right.</image-desc>

Silver once spoke thoughtlessly and indiscreetly to Iron, saying: "O abominable and cursed lineage of yours! For through you, infinite evils are manufactured throughout the world. For from you are made points, spears, javelins, breastplates, helmets, and swords for executing and punishing men. Through you, battles and divisions arise in the world. If you had not been created, the world would rest in peace." But Iron, hearing this well, reasonably excused himself, saying: "Alas, brother, what is it that you have spoken? I do not do you injury, but if I am a wrongdoer as you say, consider the truth and perceive clearly <note>original: 'perspecies', likely a corruption of 'perspice' (behold/perceive)</note> that without me, no work could be done. Through me, also, crafts are manufactured

<column-break/>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration within a rectangular border depicting three oblong, slightly curved objects with rounded ends, resembling metal ingots or bars, arranged vertically and parallel to one another.</image-desc>

and the earth germinates <note>A reference to iron plowshares used in agriculture.</note> while men work through me. Men desire me and do with me whatever they wish. I, however, contradict nothing, but obey them as the Creator ordained. If, however, they do evil with me, it is not my fault, for I do what I must. But you see the speck in my eyes, yet you do not consider the beam that is in your own eyes. Since through you, all evils happen. For through you, men commit adulteries, thefts, homicides, and the like. For through you, justice and truth perish. Usury <term>usure - the practice of lending money at unreasonably high interest rates</term> and robbery are manufactured. You seduce the souls of men and cast them into perdition. Therefore, it would be good for you to be silent and not speak so immoderately. But he who does not know how to speak does not know how to be silent."

Therefore, a fool does not know how to speak because he cannot be silent. Wherefore a certain philosopher, when asked why he remained silent while others were speaking, replied: "No one knows how to be silent, but I wish to know." At this, Silver was confounded and blushed, saying: "We should always look at the word before we speak." For **Augustine** <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), one of the most influential Latin Church Fathers.</note> says: "Let the word come to the mind <note>original: 'lunam' (moon), which is an OCR error for 'mentem' (mind). The standard quote is: 'Sermo ante veniat ad mentem quam ad linguam.'</note> before it comes to the tongue." A philosopher, when he remained silent among many, was asked why he did this. He replied: "Because I have sometimes regretted having spoken; I have never regretted having been silent." Wherefore **Cato** <note>Dionysius Cato, the supposed author of the 'Distichs of Cato,' a collection of moral advice used to teach Latin in the Middle Ages.</note> says: "For it harms no one to have been silent, but it harms to have spoken." <note>original: 'Nam nulli tacisse nocet: sed nocet esse locutum.'</note>

A certain father of a family had three roosters in his courtyard, and in that house, dishonorable things were being done. Wherefore one of the roosters crowed, saying: "You are doing dishonorable and shameful things which burden and displease our lord." Hearing this, however, the dishonorable man said: "This rooster must not live," and he had him killed. On another day, another raised his voice, saying: "Our brother was slaughtered for speaking the truth." Immediately the wrongdoer killed him too. The third, however, was wise, and therefore he began to crow and say: "He who sees and hears and remains silent, lives in peace."

## ¶ Concerning Tin and Copper against Gold. Chapter 22.

<summary>This page features a debate between Silver and Iron, where Silver blames Iron for warfare, while Iron points out that Silver is the root of greed and corruption. It concludes with various authorities and a fable of three roosters praising the virtue of silence over dangerous speech.</summary>
<keywords>Silver, Iron, morality, Saint Augustine, Cato, silence, greed, warfare, fables, roosters</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page concludes Chapter 21, which began with a debate between Silver and Iron. It transitions from the physical properties of metals to a moral warning against forgery and defamation, citing both legal parables and monastic legends, before introducing Chapter 22.</meta>

<image-desc>A large, ornate woodcut initial 'S' within a square frame, featuring intricate foliate patterns, vines, and scrollwork.</image-desc>

But tin together <note>original: 'stagnum', referring here to tin or an alloy like pewter</note> with copper imitated gold. From these, they fabricated a beautiful and excellent vessel of copper and sold it in the marketplace, claiming it was gold. A certain buyer paid for it and carried it home, rejoicing. However, when he wished to test the perfection of the gold, he found it was copper; indignant at this, he summoned the gold before a judge, saying that he had been deceived by it. Gold also truly excused itself, saying that it had not sold the vessel, nor had it manufactured it, nor indeed had its kind ever been involved in the process. The judge then summoned the sellers before him and raised them upon the rack <note>original: 'eculeo', a Roman-style torture frame used to extract confessions</note> and tortured them 

<column-break/>

until they confessed the truth: that they had done this out of envy to defame gold, so that it would not be so highly valued in the world. The judge then punished them and praised the gold, saying: "He who is good and pure should always sleep securely." For in this way, many desire to defame those who are better than themselves because of envy, by testifying falsely, as there are many such witnesses. Truly, such a person speaking falsehoods will not go unpunished. According to that verse in Proverbs 19: "A false witness shall not be unpunished, and he who speaks lies shall not escape the judgment of God." And likewise, the false man shall perish. And it is read in the *Conferences of the Fathers* <note>A famous collection of monastic wisdom by John Cassian</note>: When Abbot Panicius <note>likely a variant of Paphnutius, a common name in desert father literature</note>, while still a graceful youth, was sitting in his cell, a certain 

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration depicting three elongated, slightly curved objects resembling metal ingots or bars, arranged diagonally within the frame.</image-desc>

brother, moved by envy, hid his own book in Panicius’s bed. Then, going to the place where the brothers had gathered after Mass was celebrated, he asserted that his book had been stolen from him. Three men were sent to search through the cells, and they found the book in Panicius's bed. When he was accused before the brothers, though he was conscious of no guilt, he chose to accept and undergo the penance for theft. But as they were doing this, the envious brother began crying out, possessed by a demon; he revealed the trick he had played by hiding the book, and confessed that he had hidden it for the defamation of the holy man. He begged to be led to Panicius so that he might be liberated from the demon through the abbot's prayers. When he had been led there, he was freed from the false testimony by the abbot's prayers. Concerning false testimony, Saint Bernard <note>original: 'B.', standard medieval abbreviation for Bernard of Clairvaux</note> says: "He who offers false testimony is liable to three persons: first, to the Lord whose presence he despises; second, to the judge whom he deceives by lying; and third, to the innocent person whom he harms with false testimony." Therefore, the witness is bound by law to the restoration of all things, because through his false testimony, his neighbor lost them.

<meta>This page contains the entirety of Chapter 21, featuring a moral debate between Silver and Iron. It serves as a precursor to the following chapter, which deals with the deception of base metals (Tin and Copper) appearing as Gold.</meta>

# ¶ Concerning Silver and Iron. Chapter 21.

<image-desc>A large, ornate woodcut initial 'A' historiated with floral vine-work and a bird-like creature or dragon entwined within the letterforms.</image-desc>

**A**t one time, Silver spoke rashly and indiscreetly to Iron, saying: "O unspeakable and cursed progeny, for through you infinite evils are fashioned throughout the world. For from you are made sword points, spears, javelins, breastplates, helmets, and swords for the execution and punishment of men; through you, battles and divisions fall upon the age. If you had not been created, the world would rest in peace." Iron, however, listening well, reasonably excused itself, saying: "Alas, brother, what is this that you have spoken? I do you no injury, but if I do as you say, consider the truth of the evildoer and you will perceive that without me no work could be performed. Through me also the arts are fashioned, and the earth brings forth fruit while men work by my means. Men desire me and do whatever they wish with me; I do not contradict them but obey them as the Creator ordained. If, however, they do evil with me, it is not my fault, for I do what I must. You, however, see the speck <note>original: 'festucam'; a reference to the 'mote' or 'splinter' in the eye from the Parable of the Mote and the Beam in Matthew 7:3</note> in my eyes, but the beam <note>original: 'trabem'</note> that is in your own eyes you do not consider, since through you all evil events happen. For through you, men commit adultery, theft, murder, and similar things; through you indeed justice and truth perish, and usuries and robberies are fashioned. You seduce the souls of men and send them into perdition. Therefore, it would have been better for you to be silent and not to speak so immoderately. But he who knows not how to speak knows not how to be silent. A fool, therefore, knows not how to speak because he cannot be silent."

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration on the right side depicting three oblong, diagonally oriented metal ingots (likely representing silver), each marked with horizontal banding.</image-desc>

Wherefore a certain philosopher, when asked why he remained silent while others were speaking, replied: "No one knows how to be silent, but I wish to learn." At this, the confused Silver blushed, saying: "Let us always look at the word before we speak." For as Augustine <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational theologian of the Western Church.</note> says: "Let speech come to the polishing <note>original: 'lunam', likely a transcription error for 'limam', meaning a file or polishing tool</note> before it comes to the tongue." A philosopher, while remaining silent among many, was asked why he did this. He replied: "Because I have sometimes repented for having spoken, but never for having been silent." Wherefore Cato <note>Dionysius Cato, the purported author of the 'Distichs of Cato', a popular medieval textbook on morality.</note> says: "For it harms no one to have been silent, but it harms to have spoken" <note>original: 'Nam nulli tacuisse nocet. sed nocet esse locutum'</note>. A certain head of a household had three roosters in his courtyard, and in that house, shameful things were being done. Wherefore one of the roosters crowed, saying: "You perform shameful and foul acts which burden and displease our lord." Hearing this, the dishonest man said: "This rooster must not live," and he had him killed. On another day, another raised his voice, saying: "For speaking the truth, our brother has been slaughtered." Immediately the evildoer killed him as well. The third, however, was wise, and therefore he began to sing and say: "He who sees and hears and remains silent, lives in peace."

<meta>This page transitions from the previous discussion on the deception of metals (Gold, Tin, and Copper) into a new moral allegory involving mechanical objects—a lock and a key—to illustrate the necessity of cooperation and the social contract. It concludes with historical anecdotes regarding loyalty and shared labor.</meta>

<image-desc>A large, ornate woodcut initial letter "C" containing intricate floral and foliate vine patterns.</image-desc>

**A** certain excellent key used to close a lock <note>original: "ferraturam"; literally "ironwork" or "lock-plate"</note> peacefully, so that the master rejoiced in this. On a certain occasion, the ungrateful lock began to murmur, saying toward the key: "O wicked thing, why do you always pursue me, entering every day into my guts and my inner parts, turning and returning? Cease from this grievance; otherwise, I shall cast you out and bend you." To which the key replied: "You speak evil, sister, since for my sake you are guarded and protected. If you wish to cast me out, you will be discarded and broken." However, the lock was not at all appeased by these words; rather, it blocked its entire opening and refused the key, so that the master was unable to open the door at all.

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration depicting a lock plate with a keyhole; a key with a complex decorative bit is shown inserted into the lock from the right side.</image-desc>

The master, seeing this and becoming indignant, tore out that lock and broke it because he was in no way able to open it. Afterward, the key mocked it, saying: "With the one you stand by, you shall be sustained and brought into harmony. Beware also of being an adversary and brawling with the one with whom you have lived familiarly." For Seneca says: <note>original: "Turpe nihil est que cum eo gerere bellum cum quo familiariter vixeris."</note> "Nothing is so shameful as to wage war with the one with whom you have lived on intimate terms." Nevertheless, those who desire to dwell together peacefully ought to bear one another's burdens, according to that word of the Apostle: <note>referring to Galatians 6:2</note> "Bear ye one another's burdens." Tully <note>the common medieval name for Marcus Tullius Cicero</note> says: "There is nothing one cannot endure who loves perfectly." As is read in the *Scholastic History* <note>A famous 12th-century biblical paraphrase by Peter Comestor</note>, Antipater the Idumaean, the father of Herod the Great, was greatly wounded and pierced in a certain battle in the service of the Emperor, which he willingly endured for the love of him. When he was later accused by rivals and rejected, he said to Caesar: "O Caesar, I do not wish to excuse myself to you with words, but let these wounds which I received for love of you speak for me if I love you." Then Caesar received him in glory. 

Likewise, it is read concerning Julius Caesar in the first book of *On the Trifles of the Philosophers* <note>Likely referring to the 'Policraticus' by John of Salisbury</note> that when a certain veteran was endangered one day in a lawsuit and stood before the judges, he asked Caesar to be present in public to help him. Caesar gave him an advocate <note>a lawyer</note>. To which the man said: "O Caesar, I fought for you in the Asian war; I did not seek a proxy, but I myself fought for you." And he uncovered the scars of the wounds which he had received there. Caesar blushed and came to his defense, for he feared to seem so proud and ungrateful. Hence it is said in the same place: "He who does not work, shall not eat." And again the same Caesar said: "He who does not labor to be dear to his soldiers does not know how to love them," just as it is said in praise of Caesar himself that he never said to his soldiers, "Go," but rather "Come," saying that labor shared with a leader is lighter for the soldiers.

---

### ¶ Concerning Lacabo and Latten. Chapter XXIII.

<vocab>Clavis (Key), Ferratura (Lock), Seneca, Tulius (Cicero), Antiphater ydromeus (Antipater the Idumaean), Julius Caesar, Histora Scholastica, De nugis philosophorum (The Trifles of Philosophers), Gesta Romanorum (Deeds of the Romans)</vocab>

<meta>This page continues the theme of social relations and mutual dependence established by the lock and the key on the previous page, transitioning into a dialogue between a chain and a cauldron, followed by a series of moral fables involving animals and plants.</meta>

<margin>
<unclear>Greek: Regarding imaginations</unclear>
<unclear>Greek: Cauldron</unclear>
<unclear>More excellent</unclear>
<unclear>Narrative</unclear>
</margin>

<image-desc>Circular woodcut medallion featuring a symmetrical ornamental design of vines, leaves, and berries, centered around a stylized floral element.</image-desc>

On a certain occasion, a chain spoke of a cauldron, <note>original: "cacabo"; a metal cooking pot or cauldron</note> saying: "You are exceedingly ungrateful because I carry you to the fire. Every day you cook delicate dishes and never offer me anything to eat. Your greed is great; you wish to consume everything and leave me famished." To this, the cauldron replied: "You have only known how to serve in a bad way, and for this reason, you are not worthy of being rewarded, but rather of being punished. Because you hold me suspended over the fire, you cause me to be consumed and worn down. Therefore, if it were possible for me, I would gladly destroy you. But if you wish to serve me according to my will, provide me with things that are useful and necessary, not harmful or contrary." And it

<image-desc>Woodcut illustration depicting a metal cauldron hanging from a notched pothook and chain.</image-desc>

added: "Services rendered are only welcome when they are useful. Therefore, when you desire to serve others, you should serve according to their pleasure so that they may give you thanks; otherwise, you lose the benefit of your labor." And this is what Seneca says: <note>original: "Qui beneficium dare nescit: iniuste petit."</note> "He who knows not how to give a benefit, asks for one unjustly"—that is, he who does not know how to provide a benefit that is actually useful, asks to be rewarded unfairly. Even the smallest and lowliest animals teach us about repayment. Just as it is read that a mouse climbed upon a sleeping lion, and the lion, catching him, wanted to eat him.

> To which the mouse said: "Have patience with me and have mercy on me, and I shall repay the favor to you when I am able."

The lion then began to laugh, thinking what sort of repayment that mouse could possibly give. And while he was mocking him, it happened that the lion was captured in snares set for him. When the mouse learned of this, he gnawed through the ropes by which the lion was held. But an evil nature is not changed in masters by benefits; indeed, they become worse and remain in their own nature. Hence it is said that a certain youth, taking pity on a serpent numb with cold, placed it in his bosom. But when the serpent was warmed, it bit him and killed him. For this reason, Seneca says: <note>original: "Serpens in hyeme non tute palpatur..."</note> "A serpent in winter is not safely handled, for its venom is not lacking, even though the creature itself is numb."

### ¶ Of Rosemary and the Barren Field. Chapter 25.

<image-desc>Ornate decorative drop cap 'V' featuring floral motifs and intertwined stems.</image-desc>

It is read in *On the Virtues of Herbs* that Rosemary, among its other virtues, possesses this one: if you plant it in your vineyard or in your field and guard it cleanly and honorably, the vineyards will rejoice and the harvests will bring forth an increase. For this reason, a certain field of various herbs, which remained unfruitful and barren, in order that it might be able to return fruit, approached the rosemary humbly and devoutly and said:

> "O excellent shepherd and good guardian, descend to me and protect me, because you [are] cleanly and..."

<summary>This page uses allegories of household objects (the chain and pot) and classic fables (the Lion and the Mouse, the Serpent in the Bosom) to discuss the nature of gratitude and service. It concludes by introducing the botanical folklore of rosemary as a protector of harvests.</summary>

<keywords>allegory, gratitude, Seneca, Aesop's fables, Lion and the Mouse, The Frozen Adder, Rosemary, agriculture, moral philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing dialogue from the previous section, moving from the relationship between the chain and the cauldron to a fable about Rosemary and the field. It concludes a discourse on the necessity of literacy and wisdom in leadership before beginning a new chapter on the medicinal and symbolic properties of the herb Rue.</meta>

...I will settle you honorably and I will serve you; only sit and rest in me, so that through you I may be able to bring forth the fruit of good work. 

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting two rocky mounds. On the left mound, a tall, stylized plant with tiered, radiating leaves grows upward. In the center, between the mounds, is a tiered stone structure resembling a pedestal or fountain base. On the right mound, a smaller cluster of leafy vegetation is shown.</image-desc>

Rosemary, <note>original: "Rosmarinus"</note> moved by pity and overcome by the prayers of the field, stayed with it and placed itself in the middle of the land. Under its rule and care, the field was strengthened and became green, and it brought forth fruit sixty-fold and a hundred-fold in patience, saying: "For the sake of one good person, many are protected and consoled." 

This is what the people ought to do when they have no ruler: they ought to choose one upright and wise person who may govern and protect the people wisely. For truly, a wise king is the stability of the people. As it says in the **Book of Wisdom**, chapter six: *If you delight in thrones and scepters, O kings of the people, love wisdom.* <note>original: "Si delectamini in sedibus o reges populi diligite sapiētiam."</note> And again: *Love the light of wisdom, you who rule over the peoples.* 

And in **Ecclesiastes**, chapter ten: *A wise judge will judge his people, and the government of a sensible man will be stable.* <note>In medieval texts, "Ecclesiastes" often refers to the Book of Sirach (Ecclesiasticus) in this context.</note> Likewise: *An unwise king will destroy his people, and cities shall be inhabited through the intelligence of the prudent.* 

For this reason, Solomon asked God for a listening heart <note>original: "cor docile"</note> so that he might be able to judge God's people and discern between good and evil. Hence Vegetius says in his work **On Military Matters**: *There is no one who ought to know more or better things than a prince, whose teaching ought to benefit all his subjects.* One should not choose young men as leaders, because it is not certain that they are prudent, as is said in the third book of the **Topics**. <note>A reference to Aristotle's work on logic and dialectic.</note>

Plato said: *The world will be blessed when either the wise begin to rule or kings begin to be wise.* This is also noted by Valerius and by Boethius in the first book of **The Consolation of Philosophy**. Hence it was called a "Golden Age" when the kingdom was in the hands of the wise. Seneca says this, and it is also mentioned in the fourth book of the **Policraticus**: <note>A 12th-century treatise on government by John of Salisbury.</note> *I do not remember any Roman emperors or leaders being illiterate while their republic flourished.* 

And I do not know what happened since the virtue of letters withered in princes; it is no wonder, since a government cannot stand without wisdom. For Divine Wisdom says in **Proverbs**, chapter eight: *By me kings reign.* <note>original: "Per me reges regnant."</note> 

Therefore, a King of the Romans once urged a King of the Franks to ensure his sons were instructed in the liberal arts, <note>The "liberal arts" were the core academic curriculum of the time, including grammar, logic, and rhetoric.</note> saying: *An illiterate king is like a crowned ass.* <note>original: "Rex illiteratus est quasi asinus coronatus." This famous medieval proverb emphasized that power without education was brutish.</note> 

Socrates reports in his last book that among a certain people—namely on the island of Camplane—nobility does not prevail in the election of a king. Instead, the votes of everyone matter, for the people choose one adorned with good morals, heavy with years and long-standing mercy, and who has no children. If such a king is ever caught in any sin, he is to be put to death.

When crushed with salt, garlic, and nuts and applied as a plaster, it works against all poisonous bites. Because of this power which it has against poison, all the poisonous animals gathered together before it, saying: "Remove yourself from our midst," and they begged...

## On Hyssop and Mercury. Chapter 27.

Hyssop, as Brito <note>Likely William Brito, a 13th-century Franciscan grammarian and theologian.</note> says, is an herb suited for purging the lungs. 
Another author says: Hyssop with oxymel <term>oxymel: a medicinal syrup made of honey and vinegar, used since antiquity to clear the throat and lungs</term> 
evacuates viscous phlegm. Hence the verse: *Hyssop is the herb that purges phlegm from the 
body.* Because of this virtue, Mercury—who according to the 
pagans is declared to be a god, but who was a greedy and avaricious man, a sorcerer, 
a shapeshifter, and an interpreter of speeches, and in whom many other vices existed...

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration framed by a double-line border. It depicts a bearded man in a turban and long robes reclining on a patch of grass and propped up on his right elbow. In this same way, while he was healthy, according to the judgments of God, he became diseased in his lungs and afflicted with rheum <note>original: "pulmonosus r reueritcus." The latter likely refers to 'rheumaticus,' an imbalance of phlegm.</note>. Therefore, being unable to perpetrate his usual evils, he went to Hyssop <note>Ysopus: Hyssop, a plant often used for purification in biblical texts.</note> and said: "The power is within you to heal the sick. Because in words and words <note>The text reads 'verbis r verbis,' but this is likely a scribe's error for 'herbis et verbis' (herbs and words), part of a common medieval proverb about the three sources of healing: herbs, words (charms/prayers), and stones.</note> and stones there are virtues. Therefore, show your power in me and cure the lung and the phlegm that is within me, and I promise to God and to you that I will give you a portion of my robberies and thefts." To him, Hyssop replied: "It is manifest that in health you committed infinite evils, and truly, if you are healed, you will do even worse things. But the power of God which is in me—as you assert—does not know how to grant patronage to the criminal. Therefore, depart from me, for you shall never be cured by me." Thus he drove him out with shame, saying: "The wicked, if they are healthy, are always worse." On this point, note that God sometimes strikes the impious with the suffering of infirmities so that they are unable to sin; nor are they purged by such great suffering, but rather it is a great sign of eternal striking. Whence Isidore <note>Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636 AD), a scholar and bishop whose encyclopedic works were foundational to medieval knowledge.</note> says: 
> All divine striking is either a purging of the present life or the beginning of the punishment to follow. <note>original: "Omnis diuina percussio. aut est purgatio vite presentis aut initium pene sequentis."</note>

For in some, He begins the lashes in this life so that they may endure in eternal striking. For it is usually said by some: "God does not judge twice for the same thing." <note>A reference to the legal and theological principle 'Non bis in idem.'</note> Yet they do not attend to that which is written: that the Lord liberated His people from the land of Egypt, but secondly, because they did not believe, He destroyed them. For although a single fault is not struck twice, yet it is understood as a single striking which, having begun here, is perfected there; just as in those who are not at all corrected, the striking of the preceding lashes is the beginning of the following torments. Hence it is what is said by the Psalmist: *They are covered with their own confusion as if with a double cloak.* <note>original: "Operiuntur sicut diploide confusione sua." From Psalm 109:29.</note> For a "diplois" is a double garment <term>diplois: a double-folded mantle</term> which they uniquely wear who are damned both by temporal and eternal punishment. Hence Aesop <note>Esopus: The legendary Greek fabulist.</note> says that there was a certain kite <note>miluus: a bird of prey known for its scavenging and rapacity.</note> so rapacious that it snatched everywhere even against the holy churches, so that he was hated by all his kind because he had done many evils. At last, having become sick unto death, feeling compunction and humbling himself, he sent for his mother, saying:
> "Sweetest mother, I am most miserable. I greatly fear death because I have offended many. Go, therefore, to the temple of the gods, offering a sacrifice to them for my escape from this infirmity, because I am prepared to be converted to a better life."

To whom the mother replied:
> "In your life you have disturbed the gods and the sacred divinities of the gods. The justice of the gods will render to each according to his works. In health you did many evils; if you escape, God knows that you will be worse."

And this which [was] said...

<summary>This page uses a dialogue with Hyssop and a fable about a kite to argue that sickness is often a divine restraint on the wicked, warning that superficial deathbed repentance is viewed with skepticism by both God and nature.</summary>

## Concerning Southernwood and the Hare. Chapter 28.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration enclosed in a rectangular frame depicting a hare on the left standing on its hind legs. The hare extends its right paw, holding a small leafy sprig, toward a large, dense, bushy plant (Southernwood) growing from a mound of earth or rocks. In the background on the right, there are smaller tufts of grass.</image-desc>

Horace <note>original: "oratius"; the Roman poet Horace mentions southernwood in his Epistles as a medicine requiring professional skill.</note> calls Southernwood <note>original: "Abrotanum"; a woody perennial herb (Artemisia abrotanum) known for its bitter, lemony scent and medicinal properties.</note> "wood," and as is extracted by its own properties further below, it draws out rust <note>original: "arugiã," likely referring to copper rust or verdigris, though in this context it refers to its ability to draw out foreign objects or impurities from the body.</note>. Whence a certain hare <note>original: "lepus"</note> came to it with a thorn fixed in its foot, saying: "O healer of souls and bodies, heal me." Saying this, he showed the plant his right foot. Southernwood, moved by pity, placed itself over the wound, drew the thorn out from there, and cured him. 

The hare, truly not unmindful of the benefit, daily carried a flask of water upon his shoulders and bore it to the root of the Southernwood, offering the water so that the Southernwood might remain green and fresh, saying: "We are servants to our benefactors." For the wicked and the ungrateful do not act in this way; rather, how quickly they consign received benefits to oblivion. Whence Solomon <note>The biblical King Solomon, often cited in medieval texts as the source of all wisdom.</note>, being asked what among men grew old most quickly, responded: "A benefit." And for that reason, Cato <note>original: "catho"; referring to the "Distichs of Cato," a popular medieval schoolbook of moral proverbs.</note> says: "Be mindful of a recorded benefit." Likewise: "When a poor friend gives you a small gift, accept it calmly and remember to praise it fully." 

Therefore, you ought to return a benefit to a friend with interest <note>original: "cum usuris"; used here in a moral sense rather than a financial one, meaning to give back more than was received.</note> if you are able, or at least hold the benefit bestowed by friends frequently in your memory and praise the benefactor for it. For Seneca <note>The Roman Stoic philosopher, famous for his writings on ethics and benefits.</note> says: "Memory is a sufficiently great interest for a benefit." He also says: "He is ungrateful who returns a benefit without interest." We read in church history that a certain lioness, having a cave near the cell of the blessed Macarius <note>Saint Macarius of Egypt (c. 300–391), a desert hermit known for his gentleness toward animals.</note>, found her cubs were born blind...

<summary>This page recounts a fable of a hare that shows gratitude to a Southernwood plant for healing its foot, contrasting this with human ingratitude. It cites classical and biblical authorities like Horace, Solomon, Cato, and Seneca to emphasize the duty of remembering and returning favors.</summary>

<keywords>Southernwood, Hare, gratitude, medicinal plants, Horace, Solomon, Cato, Seneca, Saint Macarius, moral fables, healing</keywords>

...which she carried before his feet. And the holy man, understanding, prayed kindly for the cubs, and through prayer, their sight was restored <note>original: "illuminati sunt," literally "were illuminated," a common medieval term for curing blindness.</note>. She did not consider this with ingratitude, because she often carried the skins of all the beasts she caught to the door of his cell, as if for payment. Likewise, a certain beast named Gemma <note>original: "gemma"; in traditional hagiography, this story usually involves a hyena, though here it is given a name.</note> approached the cell of the blessed Macarius <note>Saint Macarius of Alexandria, a 4th-century desert hermit known for his rapport with wild animals.</note> with her son who was born blind, gesturing for healing. He, understanding, prayed for the son, and immediately he saw, and he departed with thanksgiving. And after a little while, she returned with all her offspring laden with sheepskins, offering them to the man of God as a gift. And with her head bowed, she departed as if giving thanks.

## ¶ Concerning Plantain and the Monkey. Chapter 31

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration within a rectangular border. On the left, a monkey-like animal sits and faces a central figure. The central figure is a man wearing a long, pleated robe and a hood (resembling a scholar or monk), who gestures toward a large plant on the right. The plant, representing plantain (Plantago), grows in a wide, low vessel and features broad, ribbed leaves and several tall flower spikes.</image-desc>

Plantain <note>original: "Plantago"; a common hardy plant used in medieval medicine to treat everything from fevers to dog bites.</note> is a herb most useful for healing quartan fevers <note>original: "quartanis"; a form of malaria where the fever returns every four days.</note>. Wherefore there was a certain monkey <note>original: "simia"</note> who had a son suffering from a quartan fever. But she could find no way to restore her son's health, even though she spent much on physicians and medicines. Therefore, weary of the expense, she came to Macer <note>original: "Maceren"; referring to Macer Floridus, the traditional name given to the author of a famous 11th-century poem on the medicinal properties of herbs.</note> saying: "I have circled the heaven and the earth and walked through it, yet I found no rest for my son. Now I have found you. Now that I have found you, a great knower of the powers of herbs, I humbly beseech you: give your counsel and help so that I may be able to free my child from this quartan disease." Macer, however, so that she might find health for her cause, said: "Drink the juice of four roots of plantain, and surely the quartan fever will soon be cured." The monkey, hearing this, quickly prepared the medicine and thus freed her son, saying: "Let us seek a learned physician if we greatly desire health." So also should we, if we desire the health of our souls: let us take care to find a learned priest and confessor who knows how to bind <unclear>a</unclear>nd [loose]...

<vocab>Plantain, monkey, quartan fever, Macer Floridus, Macarius, medicine, herb</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes a story about Saint Macarius healing animals and begins a moralized fable where a monkey seeks medical advice from the herbalist Macer Floridus to cure her son's fever using plantain.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Macarius, animal gratitude, Plantain (Plantago), Macer Floridus, quartan fever, herbal medicine, spiritual confession</keywords>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page concludes a discourse on the necessity of confession and spiritual healing before beginning Chapter 30. Note that while the previous page provided in the prompt discussed Chapter 31, this text returns to Chapter 30, suggesting a non-sequential order or a collection of fables where the pirate's tale serves as a moral bridge.</meta>

...to bind and to loose <note>original: "ligare atq$z$ soluere"; a reference to the "Power of the Keys" granted to the Church to forgive or withhold forgiveness of sins.</note>. As Augustine <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), one of the most influential Latin Church Fathers.</note> says: Seek a priest who knows how to bind and loose you. For you ought to do this so that you may find the salvation of your soul. Because Isidore <note>Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636 AD), a scholar known for his encyclopedic "Etymologies."</note> says: Every sinner receives the healing of their wounds through mercy. But medicine must be applied according to the disease, and remedies must be applied according to the wound.

Just as it is read that certain pirates, caught in a storm, vowed that if they escaped they would confess their sins. And having escaped, they confessed to a certain hermit. When their captain was confessing, and because the hermit—on account of the captain's outrages—enjoined him to go to the Pope <note>Only the Pope could grant absolution for certain "reserved" or especially heinous sins in medieval canon law.</note>, the pirate killed the hermit. And he did the same to a second hermit. But the third to whom he confessed treated him gently; and since he could not be softened nor uprooted from his malice, like the best physician of souls, he enjoined him that at least when he died, someone should place him in a tomb. He, thinking about what he would become and how quickly he would become like the dead, agreed. And doing this often and thinking about death, he set his affairs in order and did the strictest penance in the wilderness. Hence the commentary <note>original: "glosa"; a marginal or interlinear explanation of a text.</note> says: Nothing serves so much to tame the desires of the flesh as to ponder what your death will be like.

# ¶ Concerning Vervain, the Wolf Doctor, and the Fox. Chapter 30.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration consisting of two parts. The upper, horizontal panel depicts a wolf wearing a cloak and a fox holding a leafy sprig (verbena) in its mouth; they are approaching a man lying in a canopied bed. The lower left panel is a square containing a large, elaborate decorative initial letter 'A' surrounded by intricate floral and vine-like ornamentation.</image-desc>

Macer <note>original: "Acer," a common scribal variant for Macer Floridus, the traditional author of a popular medieval poem on the healing properties of herbs.</note> says that if ever you visit a sick person, you should carry vervain <note>original: "verbena"; an herb used in folk medicine and ancient rituals, here used as a tool for prognosis.</note> and ask him: "How is it with you?" And if he answers, "Well," he will recover; and if he says, "Ill," there is no hope of deliverance. 

Hence there once was a certain wolf, a most famous doctor, who was treating a certain sick man, giving him hope of a quick recovery. The fox, however, knowing the power of vervain, in order to deceive the wolf, went to visit the aforementioned sick man, carrying a small sprig of vervain with her. And when she came to him, she asked him how he was doing. And the sick man said: "It is ill with me." The fox, therefore, being made certain of the sick man's death...

<summary>This page concludes a lesson on the importance of confession using the story of a murderous pirate who finds redemption through reflecting on death. It then begins a fable about a fox using vervain to accurately predict a patient's death, contrasting her knowledge with a wolf doctor's false hope.</summary>
<keywords>confession, Augustine, Isidore, pirates, repentance, Vervain, Wolf, Fox, medieval medicine, prognosis</keywords>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Dialogus Creaturarum" (Dialogue of Creatures), a collection of moralizing fables. While the previous page discussed the penance of a pirate, this text transitions into Chapter 30, featuring a debate between an Asp and a Wolf regarding medical prognosis, followed by traditional folk tales about the dangers of a stubborn tongue. It concludes with the beginning of Chapter 31 regarding the Mandrake plant.</meta>

...he <note>Likely the Asp, as identified in the following sentences.</note> went to the Wolf physician, saying, "What do you say, physician, regarding that sickly man?" To whom the Wolf replied, "He will soon be recovered. For he is, as it were, in a state of convalescence according to <note>original: "scdm" (secundum)</note> the movement of his pulse and urine."

But the Asp, smiling slightly, said, "You are mistaken, physician, being ignorant of the art of medicine. For he cannot escape death in any way, because the sentence of death has been given to him." 

But the Wolf contradicted him. Arguing together, they placed a wager in the presence of many. Meanwhile, after eight days, that sick man departed from this world. And the Wolf, confused and despoiled <note>Having lost his wager.</note>, remained saying, "To place a wager is not a sign of sense, but of a man's downfall. Therefore, take care and do not obligate yourself, nor say things for certain which you do not know, lest you be deceived." 

Just as Socrates says to a certain inquirer asking how one could speak best: "In this way: you shall say nothing except what you have known well." And as the philosopher <note>Likely referring to Aristotle or Seneca, common authorities in medieval texts.</note> says: "If you fear to say that of which you might later repent, it is always better to say nothing than to speak thus." 

But many desire to defend those things which they say, whether good or bad, and to struggle by arguing with all their might. Whence, when a certain woman was accustomed to argue with her husband, and when they were passing through a meadow, the man said that it had been mowed <note>original: "falcatum"; cut with a scythe.</note>, and she said that it had been shorn <note>original: "tonsus"; clipped close.</note>. Taking this opportunity, the man proceeded from words to blows and cut off her tongue. However, when she could no longer speak, she showed with her fingers—moving them like scissors—that the meadow had been shorn. 

Likewise, another certain woman arguing with her husband called him "louse-ridden" <note>term: "pediculosum"; a common medieval insult implying one is infested with lice.</note>. He, moved by force so that he might confound her, threw her into a riverbed <note>original: "aluu" (alveum)</note>, treading upon and suffocating her. Since she could not speak, with her hands raised, she represented the crushing of lice between her thumbnails. 

Wherefore Ecclesiastes <note>Likely a reference to the Book of Sirach (Ecclesiasticus) 28:18: "Many have fallen by the edge of the sword: but not so many as have fallen by the tongue."</note> says: "Many among men have died in many ways, but the greater part have perished by the tongue."
