### ¶ Concerning the Mandrake and the Goddess Venus, the Adulteress. Chapter 31.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicts a woman in late medieval or early Renaissance dress, featuring a long gown and a high, draped headpiece. She stands in a landscape with her hands gesturing toward a mandrake plant. The plant is shown with large, broad leaves and three flowers. To the right of the woman, the roots of the mandrake are partially visible and appear to have a human-like form, a common artistic convention for the mandrake in this period. A bird is shown in flight in the upper right background.</image-desc>

Augustine <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo.</note> says the Mandrake is a type of fruit <note>original: "pomi"; literally 'apple', but used generally for fruit.</note>. Regarding this type of fruit, I know some believe that when taken as food, it brings fertility to sterile women. Because of this miraculous virtue which it possesses, Venus—the goddess of adultery, who had dealings with Mars, Anchises, and Adonis—went to the mandrakes and suppliantly begged...

<meta>catchword: di (likely leading to "dixerit" or "dicens" - "said")</meta>

<summary>This page contains fables warning against arrogance and stubbornness in speech, specifically through the tales of the Wolf physician's failed wager and wives who refuse to yield in arguments. It also introduces the lore of the mandrake plant and its association with fertility and the goddess Venus.</summary>
<keywords>Wolf physician, Asp, Socrates, stubbornness, tongue, mandrake, Venus, fertility, Dialogus Creaturarum, moral fable</keywords>

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter 31 of the "Dialogue of Creatures," which concerns the Mandrake. The previous page ended with an encounter between a sterile woman and a Mandrake plant. The Mandrake, often associated with fertility and magic in medieval lore, is here portrayed as a moral authority rebuking the woman for her perceived lack of virtue.</meta>

...judging. "Look upon me as the best of fruit, and do not despise my prayers. Grant that I may eat a little of you, so that I may be able to conceive children by my lovers. For I am sterile, and without you, I can do nothing at all. Only hear me, and ask whatever you wish."

To whom the Mandrake <note>original: "mādragora"</note> replied: "O most shameless of all women! The air and the earth are polluted by the stench of your lust <term>luxuria: in medieval theology, this refers specifically to the sin of lechery or sexual excess</term>. How much worse would it be if you gave birth to lustful children? I would rather see you slaughtered than multiplied. Get away from me as quickly as you can, for I am already sick to my stomach from the stench of your impurity." Thus, he drove her away in shame, saying:

"Let harlots be driven away; do not converse with them." For Ecclesiastes <note>A book of the Bible; the quote here reflects common medieval moralizing attributed to the text</note> says: "The conversation of women, if they are impure, burns like a fire." For a chaste man ought to act in this way if he wishes to preserve his integrity. We should not even lend our ears to the words of such women, but rather repel and restrain them. 

As Jerome <note>St. Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), a prominent Church Father known for his teachings on asceticism</note> relates concerning a certain martyr: after he had overcome many tortures, he was placed in a garden on a bed of flowers, where there was a beautiful harlot who fondled his flesh. He, biting off his own tongue, spat it with blood into her face, out of a zeal for purity and chastity.

A certain Queen of France, when she had seen a certain master poet named Prudentius <note>original: "prudentem"</note>, who had very beautiful hands, called him to her. Taking him by the hands, she said, "Oh, how worthy are these fingers <note>The Latin "dicti" is likely a scribal error for "digiti" (fingers), as the poet uses the latter in his reply</note> to touch and handle the borders of a queen!" To this, the poet pulled back his hands and said, "Let not these things you say be done, my lady. For if my fingers were to touch your borders <note>original: "littora," literally 'shores,' here used figuratively for the queen's garments or person</note>, how could I eat with them ever again? For my fingers would be so uncleansed by your touch that I would henceforth loathe to bring them to my mouth."

### On the Rosebush and the Partridge, Chapter 32

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration depicting a bird, likely a partridge (perdix), standing in the middle of a thorny rose bush (rosarium). The bird faces left and has patterned feathers on its breast. The rose bush has several circular blooms and rosehips. The scene is set on a small, shaded mound of earth.</image-desc>

A most beautiful rosebush <note>original: "rosarium"</note> was blooming in a certain garden, full of fragrant roses. It happened that a partridge <note>original: "perdix"</note> passed that way, and looking upon this rosebush, it trod upon its roses. Then the bird turned toward it, saying: "O beauty, flower of flowers, grant me some of your roses, for I greatly desire to refresh myself a little in these scents." To which the rosebush replied: "Come to me, most beloved sister, and take from the more beautiful ones at will..."

<vocab>mandragora (mandrake), luxuria (lust/excess), ecclesiastes, Hieronimus (Jerome), castitas (chastity), regina francie (queen of France), poeta (poet), rosarium (rosebush), perdix (partridge)</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes a moral fable about a Mandrake rebuking a woman for her lust and begins a new chapter featuring a dialogue between a Rosebush and a Partridge. The text emphasizes medieval virtues of chastity and the avoidance of temptation through anecdotes involving St. Jerome and a French queen.</summary>
<keywords>chastity, Mandrake, St. Jerome, Queen of France, Prudentius, Partridge, Rosebush, moral fables, Dialogue of Creatures</keywords>

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page indicates a jump in the manuscript from Chapter 31 (The Mandrake) to Chapter 34. The text begins mid-sentence, concluding a moral allegory likely from the end of Chapter 33, before transitioning into the new chapter regarding the Buckthorn and the Buck.</meta>

...[missing text]. And when the partridge <note>original: "perdix"</note> flew over the rose garden, the thorns pricked its shins and feet so that it gladly returned without any roses, saying: "Roses are beautiful and precious, but thorns are also bitter." 

This rose garden represents the world. The roses among the thorns are worldly riches, which the Savior mentions, as Luke testifies, "the word is..." <note>A reference to the Parable of the Sower in Luke 8:14, where "thorns" represent the cares and riches of life that choke the word of God</note>. And regarding this, Blessed Bernard <note>St. Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a highly influential Cistercian abbot</note> says: "For who would ever believe me if I attempted to call riches thorns, especially since thorns sting while riches delight? And yet they are indeed thorns, because they tear the mind with the pricks of their anxious thoughts and, when they lead one toward sin, they draw blood as if from an inflicted wound." And as Bernard says: "Worldly riches promise their master to be a servant, yet they make him a slave. They promise security and instead inflict fear." 

Hence Isidore <note>Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636), whose "Etymologies" served as a standard encyclopedia for the Middle Ages</note> says in the sixth book of his *Etymologies*: "Those who wish to become rich fall into temptations and the snare of the devil. Desires, which are very harmful and useless, drown a person in destruction." 

Thus John <note>Likely referring to a medieval collection of stories or John of Salisbury</note> tells of Crates the Theban philosopher, who, having gathered a great weight of gold, threw it into the sea, saying: "Begone, evil desires, into the deep! I shall drown you lest I myself be drowned by you." A similar example is given by Bernard concerning a certain philosopher who carried a great weight of gold with him. But while deliberating on his path and considering that he could not possess riches and virtue at the same time, he threw the gold away from himself and said: "O riches, begone and stay far from us!"

# Concerning the Buckthorn and the Buck.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a horned animal, likely a buck or goat (the "Damna"), standing in a grassy field. It is leaning forward to eat the leaves of a gnarled, thorny bush (the "Ramnus") situated to the right.</image-desc>

Papias <note>Papias the Grammarian (11th century), a pioneer in lexicography</note> says that the Buckthorn <note>original: "Ramnus"</note> is a white thorn or a thorny wood. The Buckthorn is a very dense species of thorn because, while it is among the grasses, it appears soft and beautiful, yet thorns are present within it. 

Later, a Buck <note>original: "damna"; likely a variant of "dama," the Latin word for a fallow deer or buck</note>—that is, a goat—went to the Buckthorn while it was soft and beautiful in the grass, and fed upon it sweetly and with great flavor. After some days, however, the Buck remembered the flavor of the Buckthorn and returned to it, wishing to eat as before. But by then, many thorns had hardened and remained upon it. When the Buck bit into it, the thorns became fixed in its throat and palate, and they tormented its entire being.

<summary>This page uses the metaphor of thorns to warn against the dangers of worldly wealth, citing various philosophers and Church Fathers, before beginning a new chapter on the Buckthorn plant and the Buck.</summary>
<keywords>thorns, worldly riches, St. Bernard of Clairvaux, Crates of Thebes, Isidore of Seville, Papias, Buckthorn, greed, asceticism</keywords>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the vanity of worldly pursuits from the previous page, concluding a section on the importance of a good end to one's life. It then begins Chapter 34, "Of the Myrtle and the Woman."</meta>

<sig>e iij</sig>

...before, tormented by distress and pain, she cursed her son, saying: "Unhappy one, you began well, but now you have ended badly." Many act thus, who begin well in the beginning, but finish badly in the end, because of which they bring a curse upon themselves. But as Jerome <note>original: "io."; referring to St. Jerome's Epistle 54: "In Christians, we do not look for the beginning, but the end."</note> says: "In a Christian, the beginning is not sought, but the end." Paul began badly, but he finished well. For this reason, the beginnings are praised, but the end of the preaching is condemned <note>This suggests that a good start is worthless if the final message or life's conclusion is poor.</note>. Wherefore Isidore <note>St. Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636), author of the Etymologies</note> says: "The end in the life of a man is always to be sought, because God does not look at what kind of people we were before, but what kind we turned out to be near the end of life." And Cyprian <note>St. Cyprian of Carthage (c. 210–258), an early Christian writer</note> says: "By his end, each person will either be justified or condemned."

A certain soldier, wishing to enter the religious life <note>original: "religionem"; in the Middle Ages, this specifically referred to entering a monastic order</note> and considering the dangers he caused with his tongue, sent one of his vassals to the abbot to tell him his intention: and that he was mute, prepared to obey in all things. Thus, he was received and believed to be mute. And when he had made much progress in the spiritual life, the abbot took him along to a certain soldier struggling in his final moments. Seeing the dying man being miserably dragged away by demons at his passing, the "mute" monk wept much. And as they were leaving, another soldier met them, promising the abbot that once his affairs were settled, he would enter the religious life as quickly as possible. But as he went ahead of them, he fell from a certain bridge into the water and was drowned. The soul of this man was seen by the mute soldier being carried to heaven by angels in the form of a monk <note>This reflects the medieval belief that the sincere intention to join an order was spiritually equivalent to being a member if death occurred prematurely.</note>; because of this, the soldier laughed joyfully. The abbot then commanded him, by the virtue of obedience, to speak and say why he had laughed. He said: "You have done badly against my purpose by making me speak," and he told him what he had seen. When the abbot prostrated himself before him in respect, the man raised him up and asked to be enclosed <note>To become an anchorite or hermit, living in total isolation.</note> so that he might keep his original vow of silence.

## Of the Myrtle and the Woman. Chapter 34.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a woman on the left wearing a long, flowing gown and a head covering, standing in a simple landscape with tufts of grass. She gestures with raised hands toward a large, leafy plant (myrtle) on the right, which grows from a rocky, tiered mound. The scene is framed by a thin black border.</image-desc>

Isidore says in his seventeenth book: the myrtle <note>original: "Mirtus"</note> is a certain tree said to be "bitter" <note>original: "amari"; there is a wordplay here between "amare" (bitter) and "mare" (sea)</note> because it is more of a coastal tree; hence it is called "myrtle" by physicians. The books of medicine write that this tree is suitable for women in many of their needs. 

Wherefore there was once a certain sick woman who consumed all her goods on doctors and medicines, yet was unable to find a remedy. At last, she went to the sea and found a myrtle on the shore. Praying to it, she said: "O noble tree, have mercy on me, and grant me, unhappy as I am, a little branch from yourself so that it may heal my wounds." To whom the myrtle replied: "If I give you some of mine, what..."

<summary>The page concludes a moral lesson on finishing life well through the story of a soldier who feigned muteness to avoid sin, then begins a chapter on the medicinal and symbolic properties of the myrtle tree.</summary>
<keywords>salvation, Saint Jerome, Saint Isidore, Saint Cyprian, monasticism, silence, miracles, Myrtle, medicinal plants, women's health</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues from a section of moral tales. Although the previous page ended with a story about a soldier, this page begins mid-dialogue in the conclusion of Chapter 34, "The Myrtle and the Woman," before moving into a discussion on prayer and the next fable.</meta>

"...[if] I shall have a price from you." To which the woman replied, "Gold and silver I have none, for I have spent all my goods on my illness. But I promise to God and to you that I will always keep the memory of you in my prayers, and of the goods which God shall give me, I will bestow a portion." To her, the Myrtle <note>In these medieval fables, plants and animals often act as moral characters who speak with humans.</note> replied: "The best price you will give me is if you will see to it that prayers and orations are poured out to the Lord for me. Therefore, climb upon me and take whatever you wish, so long as you keep your promise." And it added: "We are bound to give to those who wish to pray for us." In this way, we also ought to do good to the servants of Christ, so that they may pour out orations and prayers to the Lord for us, because holy prayers obtain what they desire. As the Gloss <term>Gloss: a standard medieval commentary on the Bible, usually written in the margins of a manuscript</term> says: "Prayer, like a certain fruit <note>original: "fructum"; likely a scribe's error for "scutum" (shield), which was a more common medieval metaphor for prayer protecting one from God's wrath</note>, protects from the wrath of God." Wherefore Origen <note>Origen of Alexandria (c. 184–253), an influential early Christian scholar and theologian</note> says: "One is worth more by praying than many sinners are by fighting." Just as we read in Exodus 17: At that time when Moses was in the desert with the children of Israel, a certain king <note>Amalek, leader of the Amalekites</note> fought against him. And when he saw himself being overcome by his enemy, he ran to his prayer, and with eyes raised to heaven, he prayed. And thus his people were winning; and when he lowered his hands, his enemy was winning. However, the hands of Moses were heavy, and he could not hold them high for long. Wherefore it is said that two men went—one to Moses' right arm, the other to his left—holding up his hands until his enemy was turned to flight. Wherefore, prayer triumphs even in physical war. We read in the histories from across the sea <note>referring to chronicles of the Crusades</note> that when Godfrey of Bouillon <note>a leader of the First Crusade and first ruler of the Kingdom of Jerusalem</note> and his lords were at the siege of Antioch and were besieging Kerbogha <note>original: "corobozam"; the governor of Mosul who led a massive army against the Crusaders in 1098</note>, and when a multitude of Turks and Saracens had surrounded them, they were so afflicted by hunger and thirst that they had nothing to eat. Their horses, indeed, were eating the bark of trees. These men, however, when they had prayed and gone out against the Saracens as if about to die, God sent down upon them and upon their horses a heavenly dew, by whose sweetness they and their horses were so refreshed and strengthened for three days that they overcame their Saracen enemies and put them to flight, and they plundered their goods.

## Of the cedar of Lebanon.
### Chapter 35.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration positioned to the right of the opening paragraph of Chapter 35. It depicts a stylized cedar tree with a thick trunk and a dense, rounded canopy of large, circular leaf clusters or fruit. The tree grows from a base of stratified, rocky terrain. The image is enclosed within a simple rectangular line border.</image-desc>

A certain cedar planted in Lebanon appeared towering and lofty above all others, so that many ascended into the mountains to see it. Being made joyful by this vision, they praised the cedar in many ways. For this reason, the cedar began to exalt itself, saying to itself: "I am greatly praised by everyone. For this reason, I am worthy of praise. But I think that if the shoots and little trees that are around me flourish and grow and multiply, I shall appear very small. Therefore, it is safer, before they rise up, to humble and mutilate them, lest they take away my glory and honor." And saying this, it struck down and uprooted all the shoots and little trees that were budding around it, because of which it appeared bare. Not many days later, a wind <note>original: "chorus"; specifically the northwest wind, known for its strength</note> from Lebanon blew, and the cedar, being overly elevated and uprooted, was bent, saying: "Those who are superior are nothing if they do not possess those who are lesser." But many neglect to believe this; rather, they desire only to appear great.

<summary>This page discusses the spiritual and practical power of prayer through biblical and historical examples, then transitions to a fable about a cedar tree that falls because it destroyed the smaller plants that would have sheltered it.</summary>
<keywords>prayer, Moses, Exodus, Godfrey of Bouillon, Siege of Antioch, Crusades, pride, humility, Cedar of Lebanon, Origen</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the power of prayer. The opening line concludes a moral lesson—likely the one involving Moses and the Amalekites—before transitioning into a new fable about an Olive tree and a Reed, followed by a chapter on two trees growing from a single point.</meta>

...and to uproot the lesser ones. Just as it is said through the parable that an olive tree and a reed were arguing together. And the olive said to the reed: "You are a wretched, useless thing. I, however, provide oil for the service of the Lord <note>original: "servituti dominum"; olive oil was essential for lighting church lamps and for holy anointing</note>." 

To which the reed replied: "You will soon see how useful I am." 

And suddenly, shaken by a great wind, the olive tree collapsed and was broken. The reed, however, when the wind came upon it, bent in every direction and suffered no harm. Then the reed said to the olive: "The weak with humility is worth more than the strong with pride." 

For a child, when he is born, gives us an example of humility, because after the manner of beasts he is born to live upon his legs and as if with four feet <note>A reference to infants crawling on all fours before they learn to walk upright</note>; "he is compared to the beasts and is made like unto them <note>A paraphrase of Psalm 49:12</note>." He is also born weeping and not laughing. Wherefore the Book of Wisdom <term>Wisdom: The "Book of Wisdom" or the "Wisdom of Solomon," a book of the Bible found in the Apocrypha or Deuterocanon</term> says in chapter seven: "The first voice I uttered was weeping, as all do." 

Augustine says in *The City of God*: "The child who is born begins with weeping, not knowing what evils he is about to suffer." It is said that only Zoroaster <note>The legendary founder of Zoroastrianism; in medieval lore, he was often associated with magic and was famously said to be the only human born laughing</note> laughed when he was born; and yet that laughter profited him little. For he was the inventor of the magic arts, and while he was the King of the Bactrians <note>original: "butanorum"; likely a corruption of "Bactrianorum," referring to the ancient region in Central Asia</note>, he was slain by Ninus, the King of the Assyrians. 

John <note>Referencing the Gospel of John, chapter 11</note> also says that the Lord wept when he raised Lazarus, because he was compelled to call his dearest friend back to this hostile life for the sake of saving others. Wherefore, it is also a custom among many, as the song <note>original: "salmus"; possibly referring to a specific "psalm" or a corrupted reference to an author like Solinus</note> says, that when a child is born, he is lamented by his parents and kinsmen, but when someone dies, he is carried to the grave with dancing.

### Of two trees. Chapter 36.

**A**rbores <note>The large decorative 'A' in the woodcut begins the word "Arbores" (Trees)</note>—two trees were sprouting together from one peak. One was most beautiful in its placement, leaves, and fruit; the other, however, was deformed in all respects. Many people gathered at these trees, and seeing such a great difference between them, they said: "It is only right to cut down that most foul tree, which so greatly spoils the beauty of the other." 

And when they wished to cut it down, the twisted tree spoke to them, saying: "O unbelievable men! It is written in the law: 'Judge with righteous judgment <note>A quote from John 7:24</note>.' For when the Lord went along..."

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on humility and the nature of human birth. It shifts here to the theme of divine and human judgment, using the biblical story of Sodom as a model for legal prudence before introducing a new fable about a dolphin and an eel.</meta>

...to Sodom so that he might judge the impious, he said to Abraham: "The outcry of Sodom has increased <note>original: "Clamor sodomorum multiplicatus est"</note>. I will go down and see whether they have finished the work according to the cry that has reached me." It is as if he were saying: do not believe the evil deeds of men until you have proven them. For this reason, a judge cannot punish a sin known only to himself <note>A fundamental principle of medieval law: a judge must rule based on evidence presented in court, not on his own private knowledge</note>. Wherefore it is said in Job, chapter eight <note>The text cites Job 8, but the story that follows is actually the famous account of the woman caught in adultery from the Gospel of John, chapter 8</note>: "Woman, where are they who accused you? Has no one condemned you?" She said to him: "No one, Lord." And Jesus said: "Neither will I condemn you. Go, and from now on, sin no more." Therefore, do not condemn me before the proof of my fruits. For the Savior says: "By their fruits you shall know them."

Now, those who heard this stood still and tested the flavor of those fruits. And when they tasted of the more beautiful ones and found almost no flavor in them, they cursed them. Afterward, they ate of the uglier ones. When they found the flavor to be equally delicious and the scent to be divine, they praised the fruit, saying: "It is wisdom to test before passing sentence." For as Isidore <note>Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636), a scholar and bishop whose works were foundational to medieval education</note> says: "Condemn no one before judgment; prove before you sentence." And B. <note>likely referring to Boethius or Bernard of Clairvaux</note> says: "He who damns the just man kills one who is not yet dying. And he who strives to release the guilty from punishment works to bring to life one who will not live." A judge should never deliver any sentence in anger or without examination. For a judge ought to deliver weighted sentences. Because, as it is said in Proverbs 27: "Anger has no mercy."

Wherefore Valerius <note>Valerius Maximus, a 1st-century Roman author of famous anecdotes used frequently by medieval writers to illustrate moral points</note> relates that when a certain philosopher king was drunk, he delivered an unfair sentence against a certain widow. Looking at him, she said she was appealing from the "drunk philosopher" to the "sober philosopher." When he had slept off the wine, he revoked the sentence. Similarly, a story is told of a sinful woman who was judged by the cruelty of Ptolemy, the most wise king of Egypt. She appealed from his cruelty to the kindness of the said king. Later, considering this, he revoked the sentence, saying that his kindness and the gentleness of his piety ought, by right, to take precedence over cruelty.

## ¶ Of the dolphin and the eel. Chapter 37.

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration depicting a sea scene. The water is filled with stylized wavy lines and decorative swirls. On the right, a large fish representing a dolphin, covered in scales with a pointed snout and fins, faces left. On the left is a long, slender eel with its tail coiled in a loop.</image-desc>

When a dolphin found an eel in the ocean waves, he seized her. He then strove to pursue her. He caught her many times, but because of the slipperiness <note>original: "liquiditatem," referring here to the eel's slick or fluid texture</note> of the eel, he was not able to hold her, at which he was greatly saddened. The eel, however, wishing to mock him and escape from him, spoke to him craftily, saying: "O wonderful dolphin..."

<summary>This page discusses the necessity of impartial judgment and evidence, citing biblical and classical examples of rulers who reconsidered harsh sentences. It begins a new fable about a dolphin struggling to hold onto a slippery eel.</summary>
<keywords>judgment, justice, Sodom and Gomorrah, Jesus, Isidore of Seville, Valerius Maximus, Ptolemy, dolphin, eel, mercy</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the fable of the eel and the dolphin introduced on the previous page. The eel is currently speaking to the dolphin, attempting to lure it into a trap by appealing to its exhaustion and hunger.</meta>

<sig>f</sig>

"I am very sorry for you because you are exhausted and sad from swimming after me, but you labor in vain; for you will never catch me in the depths of the waters. But come with me to the swamp and you shall have me as you wish on dry land." However, the foolish dolphin, because of its greed and gluttony, began to swim after the eel, wishing to destroy it entirely. The eel truly went to the swamps, and as it had said, it climbed up onto the dry ground, saying: "Come to me, for I am held fast by the roots of the plants, and you shall satisfy yourself with me." Then the dolphin gave a great leap to seize the eel, but the eel hid beneath the earth, and the dolphin remained stranded on the dry land. At this moment, a certain fisherman arrived and took the dolphin at his pleasure, saying: "He who goes with an enemy, it is no wonder if he then falls." Therefore, shun your humble enemy and do not believe him, but beware of him lest he deceive you. For Seneca <note>Seneca the Younger (c. 4 BC–65 AD), a Roman Stoic philosopher and advisor to Nero</note> says: "It is the mark of a learned man to fear an enemy, however humble." <note>original: "Inimicum quamvis humilem docti est metuere"</note> For it is told in the mortal teachings of the philosophers that Xerxes <note>Xerxes I (r. 486–465 BC), the King of the Persian Empire who led a massive invasion of Greece</note>, King of the Medes, brought war against the Greeks. He gathered an innumerable army, because of which one of his followers said to him: "The Greeks will not wait for your great army; rather, at the first rumor of your arrival, they will show their backs." Another said: "Greece could not conquer even a small army." Another said: "It is to be feared that the King might find the cities deserted and empty, and therefore he would not be able to show the strength of his army." And another said: "The seas are too narrow for your fleets, the fields for your soldiers, the plains for your infantry, and even the sky for the arrows of the Medes." While the King was being incited in this manner by an excessive estimation of himself and was scorning his enemies, the philosopher Damacus <note>Likely a reference to Demaratus, an exiled Spartan king who acted as an advisor to Xerxes and famously warned him about the bravery of the Greeks</note> said: "This multitude which pleases you is to be feared by you. For it is true that nothing is ever greatest to a king, nor can that which cannot be ruled by a king endure for long. Nothing is so great that it cannot perish." And what Damacus said came to pass. For that so great army—because of its overconfidence and a lack of foresight and leadership—was defeated by a few men who were wisely organized and cautious.

### On the Siren and the Slippery One. Chapter 37.

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration showing a siren (mermaid) swimming in water with spiral wave patterns. The siren has a human female torso and a fish tail. To the left, a man is in a small wooden boat, leaning out over the water towards her.</image-desc>

The Siren is a monster of the sea <note>term: "monstrum maris" — In medieval bestiaries, sirens were often used as allegories for worldly temptations or the dangers of lust</note>. From the navel upward, she is a beautiful maiden, but below she is entirely a fish. This Siren sings so sweetly that because of the sweetness of her song, sailors abandon their rudders and fall asleep. Because of this, many are cast into danger. Now, a certain imprudent and lustful man, while sailing through the sea, caught sight of a very beautiful Siren, who immediately...

<summary>This page concludes a fable about a dolphin lured to its death by a "humble" eel and provides a historical warning against overconfidence via King Xerxes, before introducing the lore of the Siren.</summary>
<keywords>fables, Seneca, Xerxes the Great, hubris, Demaratus, sirens, mermaid, temptation, medieval bestiary</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing discourse on the dangers of gluttony and desire. The previous page concluded with the story of a dolphin lured to its death by a deceptive eel; this page shifts to the deception of the Siren and the selfishness of the "sea-calf."</meta>

desiring: he cried out to <term>lust</term><note>original: "luxuria." In medieval Latin, this often refers specifically to sexual desire or "extravagance."</note>. The Siren, however, called out and sang more sweetly. She also prepared herself to deceive this slippery man and said: "How I fear for myself! But if you wish to unite with me, descend into the waves, and you shall enjoy my body as you please." He was so <term>disfigured by lust</term><note>original: "infenustatus." Literally "robbed of Venus," meaning his reason and beauty were destroyed by his passion.</note> that he seemed to feel nothing. Therefore, he threw himself into the sea waves so that he might lie with her. But she, seeing this, left him to perish in the waves and swam through the sea as was her custom, saying: "A man who trusts a woman harms himself greatly." Therefore, let the immodest beware, lest they perish in the same way because of the beauty of women. For Solomon <note>Solomon was traditionally considered the author of the Book of Proverbs, which contains many warnings against "strange women."</note> says: "Because of the beauty of women, many have perished." <note>original: "propter speciem mulierum multi perierunt"</note> Regarding this, he himself advises, saying: "Do not gaze upon a maiden, lest you be ensnared by her beauty." Considering this danger, Job <note>The biblical figure known for his patience and righteousness.</note> said: "I have made a covenant with my eyes, so that I should not think of a maiden." <note>original: "Pepigi fedus cum oculis meis ut ne cogitarem de virgine"</note> Because of this, Bernard <note>Saint Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a Cistercian monk and influential mystic.</note> says: "A woman's face is a poisoned arrow; it wounds the soul and injects venom." Hence, when Paris had taken Helen as his wife, certain philosophers went to see her, and seeing her beauty, they covered their eyes, saying: "Let us flee, let us flee!" For it naturally harms the soul more to look at beautiful women than ugly ones. As Aulus Gellius <note>A 2nd-century Roman author and grammarian.</note> reports, the philosopher Democritus <note>Democritus (c. 460–370 BC), the Greek "laughing philosopher," famous for his atomic theory.</note> plucked out his own eyes. The reason for this was threefold, according to others: first, because his sight hindered his internal meditations; second, because he saw the wicked flourish with too much impatience; and third, because he could not look at women without <term>lust</term><note>original: "concupiscentia."</note>.

### ¶ Concerning the gluttonous sea-calf. Chapter 39.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting two scenes of a "Ventus marinus" (sea-calf). The creature is portrayed with the head and forequarters of a wolf or dog, a scaly torso, and a broad, upright fish tail. On the left, it stands on a grassy patch of land. On the right, it is in wavy water, swallowing three fish at once.</image-desc>

The <term>sea-calf</term><note>original: "Ventus marinus." While it literally means "sea wind," the description and woodcut suggest a seal or a legendary sea-beast like a sea-dog.</note> is a beast of the sea that seeks food for itself in both the waters and on land. It dives into the waves like a fish and travels on the shore like a wild beast. One of these, however, when he found watery food on the land, would eat it there so that it would not be snatched from him by sea beasts. But when he took land food, he would eat it in the waters so that it would not be snatched from him by land beasts. Acting in this way always, he granted nothing to anyone who asked; because of this, he was hateful to all, both in the sea and on land. The time of affliction—that is, old age—arrived, and he himself was made old and powerless, so that he was able neither to swim much through the sea nor to walk upon the ground. Therefore, being destitute and starving, he begged for alms; but because he never [gave] of his own...

<summary>This page uses the myth of the Siren and the asceticism of philosophers to warn against the dangers of lust, then introduces the "sea-calf" as an allegory for greed and the isolation caused by selfishness.</summary>
<keywords>Siren, lust, Solomon, Job, Democritus, sea-calf, greed, old age, Paris and Helen, morality</keywords>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing discourse on greed and charity. The previous page ended with a reference to Saint Bernard of Clairvaux warning against the temptations of beauty and the flesh.</meta>

...[no one] had given to him: no one offered him anything of their own. Therefore, he said: "Why should one love the man who does not wish to give of his own?" <note>original: "Quare debet amare qui de suo non vult dare."</note> For thus many, because of **greed** and **gluttony**, are unable to bestow anything of their own to those who ask, fearing that they themselves will lack necessities. But as <term>Gregory</term><note>Saint Gregory the Great (c. 540–604), a Pope and Doctor of the Church whose writings were foundational for medieval morality.</note> says: "Earthly goods are multiplied by the very fact that they are distributed to the poor." <note>original: "Terrena bona per hoc quod pauperibus distribuuntur multiplicatur."</note> And the same author says: "He who grants alms receives more than he lets go." And the gloss states: "He who gives to the poor shall not be in want. He who despises the one who prays [for help] shall suffer penury."

When a certain man was preaching at <term>Saint Victor in Marseille</term><note>A famous Abbey in southern France. The OCR "arsilia" is a transcription of "massilia" (Marseille).</note>, the monks asked why they were poorer and more burdened with debts than they used to be, even though they lived more sparingly and received greater rents. He replied to them that they used to have a true steward in their house who provided all their necessities; but he had left with a certain companion of his, having been justly expelled from the abbey, and would not return henceforth unless he were recalled. The one who had been expelled was "It-shall-be-given-to-you." And the other was "Give." <note>This is a clever medieval wordplay on the Latin phrase "Date et dabitur vobis" (Give and it shall be given to you). The preacher suggests that by stopping their charitable giving, the monks lost the divine "steward" that ensured their own abundance.</note> But if they kept the usual hospitality of their predecessors, abundance would return. As God says: "Give, and it shall be given to you." (Matthew 7). <note>The text cites Matthew 7, though this specific phrasing is more commonly associated with Luke 6:38.</note>

# ### Chapter 40: Of the five young dentex fish, the old buckthorn, and the fisherman

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicts a fisherman on the right side, walking toward the left. He wears a short tunic, a cap, and a woven basket at his hip. He is dipping a long-handled net into water filled with many fish swimming to the left. The water is indicated by horizontal wavy lines.</image-desc>

Five young <term>dentex</term><note>original: "dentales." A Mediterranean fish known for its prominent teeth. In this fable, they represent headstrong youth.</note> were playing in the sea waves. But a certain fisherman, passing by and seeing them, stretched out his nets and prepared to catch them. The dentex, seeing this, said: "It is good to swim together with all our strength and tear those nets apart so that they may never deceive fish again. For we are strong and can do this valiantly."

A certain <term>buckthorn</term><note>original: "ramnus." While typically a thorny land shrub, in this "Dialogus Creaturarum" (Dialogue of Creatures), it is personified as a wise, rooted elder of the sea, possibly referring to a sea-fan or coral-like growth.</note>, old and wise, was resting in the deep. Hearing this, he rose and went to them, saying: "Little sons, it is folly that you have thought. I advise you, if you desire safety, to avoid the nets; otherwise, you will suffer, caught and bound in the nets and snares."

But the dentex, who were young and too confident in themselves, despised the counsel of the elder. They swam together with all their might and struck against the nets, desiring to tear them. But the nets, yielding to their blow, felt nothing at all and caught the fish. The fish afterwards wailed together and lamented, saying: "It is better to believe one who is older and wiser."

Therefore, it is counsel for everyone that he should attend to the **wisdom and advice of elders**, and not to that of fools and the young.

<summary>This page concludes a lesson on how charity ("Give") leads to prosperity ("It shall be given") before presenting a fable about young fish who ignore an elder's advice and get caught in a net.</summary>
<keywords>charity, greed, Gregory the Great, Marseille, St. Victor, dentex fish, buckthorn, wisdom of elders, fables, Dialogus Creaturarum</keywords>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing discourse on wisdom versus folly, following the previous discussion on greed and charity. It transitions from general advice on seeking counsel from elders to a specific animal fable illustrating the dangers of deceitful appearances.</meta>

For fools love foolish things. They draw their own plans toward folly. For the young do not have mature judgment; they love youthful things and cling to them. As **Job** says: "Wisdom is among the ancient, and in length of days is understanding." <note>original: "In antiquis est sapientia: et in longo tempore prudentia." From Job 12:12.</note> Hence the book of **Ecclesiasticus**, chapter 8, commands: "Let not the discourse of the ancients escape you, for they have learned from their fathers." <note>original: "Non te prætereat natura ratio seniorum." From Sirach 8:9.</note> For as **Tullius** <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BC), the Roman statesman and orator.</note> says in his book *On Old Age*: "Great things are not accomplished by physical strength, speed, or ferocity of body, but by counsel, authority, and knowledge." Those who give counsel do more than others. They are like the pilots in a ship. Thus the philosopher <note>Likely a reference to Aristotle, often called "The Philosopher" in medieval texts.</note> says: "Counsel in wars is worth more than leaders in arms." And for this reason **Alexander** <note>Alexander the Great.</note> obtained victory. As **Trogus Pompeius** <note>A Roman historian of the 1st century BC.</note> says in his ninth book: when Alexander was choosing an army for a dangerous war, he did not choose robust young men, but veterans who had served with his father and uncle, so that he might consider them not so much soldiers as masters of warfare. Indeed, he led no ranks except those over sixty years old. The opposite was true of **Darius’s** army; therefore the former was the victor, and the latter was defeated.

## Concerning the Pike and the Basilisk. Chapter 41.

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration depicting a meeting between creatures. On the right, a large fish (a pike or "lucius") swims in stylized wavy water, facing left with sharp teeth visible. Above it, a long, slender serpent curves across the top of the frame. On the left, a basilisk, depicted as a bird-like creature with a rooster's head and a curled, scaly tail, stands on land facing the fish. Stylized circular swirls represent clouds or water patterns.</image-desc>

The "sea-wolf" is the <term>pike</term><note>Latin: "lucius". The text identifies the pike (a freshwater fish) as a "sea-wolf," common in medieval bestiaries where animals were often categorized by behavior rather than modern biology.</note>, and it has a bone in its right jaw in the shape of a cross. If you search diligently, you will find it. Take it and wrap it in a linen cloth and carry it with you, and you shall not be taken captive; and if you are captured, you shall not be held. This is said to have been often proven. But the **Basilisk**—which is a venomous serpent that kills animals with its sight and breath—went to the seashore and, dressed in the habit of monks, religiously called the sea-wolf to itself, saying: "O brother, since you are marked with the sign of the cross, you are a perfect Christian. Therefore, come to me, for I desire to be taught the Christian faith by you and to be baptized, so that I may escape eternal judgment and deserve to enjoy everlasting joy."

<summary>This page argues that wisdom belongs to the elderly and that military success depends more on counsel than physical strength, using Alexander the Great as an example. It then introduces a fable where a deadly basilisk, disguised as a monk, attempts to lure a pike (the "sea-wolf") by appealing to the cross-shaped bone in its jaw.</summary>
<keywords>wisdom, old age, Alexander the Great, Cicero, pike, basilisk, deception, military counsel</keywords>

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing discourse on discernment and the dangers of deceit, following the previous discussion on the wisdom of elders. It transitions into specific examples of hypocrisy and the hazards of prideful ambition through the stories of Saint Hilary and a foolish sturgeon.</meta>

The Pike <note>original: "Lucius." In medieval bestiaries, the Lucius is the pike, often used as a symbol of sharp-eyed discernment.</note>, however, looking at him and recognizing him, said: "O hypocrite, 'the cowl does not make the monk.' <note>original: "cucula non facit monachum." A famous medieval proverb meaning that outward appearances or clothing do not reveal a person's true character.</note> Your words are wicked and deceitful. You do not wish to be baptized by me, but rather you desire to deceive and trick me. Therefore, I will not listen to you." Having said this, he submerged himself in the waves and swam away, leaving the other in confusion, saying: "The boastful hypocrite is false and crafty." For our Savior commanded us to be wary of such people, saying: "Beware of those who come to you in sheep's clothing, but inwardly are ravenous wolves." <note>From Matthew 7:15.</note> Concerning them, **Isidore** <note>St. Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636 AD), a scholar whose "Etymologies" was a primary source for medieval knowledge.</note> says: "Hypocrites are indeed evil in secret, yet they show themselves to be good in public." To whom it is well said by the Divine voice: "Woe to you hypocrites, for you have been made like whitewashed tombs, which indeed appear beautiful to men on the outside, but inside are full of the bones of the dead. So you also appear righteous to men on the outside, but inside you are full of greed and iniquity." <note>From Matthew 23:27-28.</note> We read that when the blessed **Hilary** <note>Saint Hilary of Poitiers (c. 310–367 AD), a prominent bishop and theologian.</note> went to dispute against heretics, the devil, in the likeness of a servant, carried his cloak and other things for him. Appearing in many acts of service and as if feeling compassion, he asked Hilary to dilute his wine less with water on account of the labor of the journey. When he had done this, the devil persuaded him to drink it pure. Next, he persuaded him to eat meat, and thus his penance was changed. When he had come to a certain village, the devil said there was a certain religious woman there who desired to speak with him. Once they were in conversation together, the holy man was enticed by her and sought to fall into sin. But God revealing this diabolical work to the blessed **Martin** <note>Saint Martin of Tours (c. 316–397 AD).</note>, Martin suddenly arrived and put the demon to flight, exposing him. And so God delivered Hilary through the blessed Martin.

### Chapter 42: Concerning the sturgeon who went into the sea and was devoured.

---

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration within a rectangular border showing two large fish in a stylized, wavy sea. The fish on the left has its mouth wide open, displaying sharp teeth, and is in the process of swallowing the tail of the fish on the right.</image-desc>

A certain great and famous sturgeon lived in the River Po <note>The Padus or Po River in northern Italy.</note>, whom all the fish of the Po feared because of his excellence and strength. He, becoming arrogant, said within himself: "Why should I associate with such lowly fish and receive praise and honors from them? It is better that I plunge into the great and spacious sea, where there are creeping things without number and various sea beasts, because by them, on account of my magnificence, I shall be exalted among the great." And saying this, he left the river and swam into the sea. However, while he was swimming in the sea and looking upon the sea beasts, he repented. For he desired to return, but knew not what to do because of the ferocity of the beasts. Meanwhile, a certain most ferocious octopus <term>polpus: traditionally a "polyp" or octopus, often depicted in medieval lore as a dangerous sea monster</term> catching sight of him...

<summary>This page uses the stories of a discerning pike and Saint Hilary to warn against religious hypocrisy and the devil's disguises, followed by a fable about a proud sturgeon who regrets leaving his river for the dangerous sea.</summary>
<keywords>hypocrisy, Saint Hilary, Saint Martin, pike, sturgeon, River Po, pride, temptation, devil, fables</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a series of moral fables used to illustrate spiritual truths. It concludes a warning against gluttony and hypocrisy, using the examples of the Pike and the Tench, moves into a well-known Aesopic fable about a Lion and a Fox to emphasize the importance of observation, and begins a new chapter on the virtue of "The Queen" (a type of fish) and her rejection of an unsuitable suitor.</meta>

...we succumb to the appetite of the throat. The **Tench** <note>original: "Tincba," likely a misspelling of *Tinca*, a freshwater fish often mentioned in medieval medicine and lore.</note> also said: "It is not good to let go of such a timely morsel because of an empty fear. I will try it first and feast upon its sweetness. You, however, wait for the outcome of the matter." But while she was swallowing the bait, she felt the snare of the hook and desired to turn back, but the fisherman pulled her toward him. The **Pike** <note>original: "Lucius."</note>, however, fleeing, said: "Let us correct our own ways because of this evil, companions, lest we also perish." So also we ought to correct ourselves by the misfortune of others. As **Cato** <note>Dionysius Cato, the reputed author of the *Distichs of Cato*, a collection of moral advice used widely in medieval schools.</note> says: "Let the misfortune of your neighbor be your warning." <note>original: "Malum vicini tui te castiget."</note> And **Seneca** <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca, the Roman Stoic philosopher whose writings were highly influential in medieval Christian morality.</note>: "It is good to see what should be avoided in the misfortune of another." Likewise: "From the defeat of another, the wise man mends his own ways." For this reason, he is wise who knows well how to arrange his affairs and takes warning for himself from the evils that befall others. 

As **Aesop** <note>The legendary Greek storyteller whose fables were standard teaching tools in the Middle Ages.</note> relates, a certain diligent **Lion** had become weakened and was lying in his cave. The animals came to visit him, and while they were approaching him, he would seize and eat them. At last, the **Fox** came for a visit, standing outside at the gate of the cave; for she was afraid to approach the lion. To whom the lion said: "Come here, my sister, so that I may have pleasant conversations with you." To whom the fox replied: "I see many footprints of those entering, but I can see no footprints of those returning."

->¶ Concerning the Queen and the Water-Snake. Chapter 45.<-

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting two mythical aquatic creatures in a sea of stylized, swirling waves. On the left is a large, spotted fish with multiple fins or legs along its underbelly. On the right is a dragon-like creature with a canine-shaped head, feathered wings, bird-like talons, and a long, curling scaly tail.</image-desc>

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'R' featuring elaborate floral scrolls and intricate foliate patterns filling the frame of the letter.</image-desc>

The **Queen** <note>original: "Regina." In this context, it refers to a specific type of fish, perhaps a barbel or sturgeon, personified here as a royal figure.</note> is a scaly fish that is caught in rivers. And her name is derived from "to rule" <note>original: "rego regis." The text uses a common medieval etymological style to link the fish's name to the Latin verb for ruling.</note> because she rules herself well. To this queen came the **Water-Snake** <note>original: "ydris." Usually a *hydrus*, a water serpent or mythical creature often depicted as an enemy of the crocodile in bestiaries.</note> and said: "O most beautiful queen, you are my chosen favorite above all others; therefore I wish to join myself with you and be united in marriage. And so I come to you in friendship." To whom the queen replied: "This indeed cannot happen because it is not fitting. For it is written in Ecclesiasticus: 'Every animal loves its own kind. So also every man loves his neighbor. All flesh will be joined to its own kind, and every man will associate with his own.' <note>A reference to Ecclesiasticus (Sirach) 13:15-16: "Every beast loveth his like, and every man loveth his neighbor. All flesh consorteth according to kind, and a man will cleave to his like."</note> Therefore, because you are not of my kind, you shall never join with me—"

<summary>This page uses the fables of the hooked fish and the lion's cave to teach the importance of learning from the mistakes of others. It begins a new chapter where a "Queen" fish rejects the advances of a water-snake by citing the biblical principle that creatures should only mate with their own kind.</summary>

<keywords>fables, Pike, Tench, Aesop, Lion and Fox, Seneca, Cato, Regina fish, Water-snake, Ecclesiasticus, moral education, animal allegory</keywords>

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page concludes a moral anecdote regarding a water-snake (possibly continuing the story of the suitor from the previous page) and its spiritual interpretation, then transitions into a new chapter featuring a debate among various fish regarding their status and quality, specifically highlighting those found in Lake Garda.</meta>

...you will be caught. The **water-snake** <note>original: "ydrus," a term used in medieval bestiaries for a water serpent or the mythical hydra.</note>, however, having been mocked and deceived, returned saying: "I am confounded and rejected, because of which I shall never be happy." In this way, every Christian ought to respond to the devil, who is that ancient serpent <note>A reference to Revelation 12:9.</note> and more cunning than all the living things that are under heaven. Say to him: "Depart from me, for you are not of my kind—that is, of the kind of the elect." For as **James** says: "Resist the devil and he will flee from you." <note>James 4:7.</note> And in the **Apocalypse**: "Therefore be strong in battle and fight with the ancient serpent." <note>A common medieval liturgical or para-scriptural exhortation based on themes from the Book of Revelation.</note> We ought, therefore, to fight against him, for he is weak against those who resist. Concerning this, **Isidore** <note>Saint Isidore of Seville, whose 7th-century works were foundational to medieval knowledge.</note> says: "In the eyes of the carnal, the devil is terrible; but in the eyes of the elect, his terror is worthless." Just as it is read in the ***Lives of the Fathers*** <note>original: "vitis patruum," a famous collection of stories about the early Christian hermits and monks in the Egyptian desert.</note> that a certain brother, having returned to the world, exposed himself to all shameful acts and luxuries; at last, being penitent, he tormented himself in a certain tomb. Demons, tempting him, promised him riches and harlots. In the end, they beat him nearly to death. And since he remained motionless and in weeping, the demons cried out: "You have conquered, monk, you have conquered!" and they fled. He, however, set the malice and iniquity of their thoughts before him and chose to want to die rather than obey the demons; and then he was changed, as if into an angel of God, as an example for many.

<margin>Lombardy: grayling</margin>
### Concerning the carp, the grayling, the turtle, and the dolphin. Chapter 46.

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration showing several fish swimming in water represented by stylized swirling lines. On the left, a large fish with sharp teeth has caught a smaller fish in its mouth. To the right, another fish swims forward, and another smaller fish is seen below the predator. The water is filled with concentric circular patterns.</image-desc>

<margin>namely, spapavano <note>Likely a local dialect name for a specific fish.</note></margin>
<margin>Lake Garda</margin>
The river fish, on a certain feast day after a banquet, were strolling together in great tranquility and peace. But the **Carp** <note>original: "carpio."</note> began to disturb the feast by drawing himself up and saying: "Above all others, I am worthy to be praised, because my flesh is more aromatic and delicate than can be said. I am not nourished in ditches and marshes or in ponds; rather, I was raised in the great **Lake Garda** <note>original: "lacu magno garde," the largest lake in Italy, famous then and now for its fish.</note>. For this reason, I ought to be the prince among you." The **Grayling** <note>original: "Temallus."</note>, however, hearing this, jumped up in indignation and said: "It is not as you say, for I shine more than you in scent and flavor. Who can be compared to me? He who finds me has found a treasure. If you, in Lake Garda..."

<margin>Saint Juvenal</margin> <note>original: "S Jnevano." Likely a reference to Saint Juvenal of Narni, a 4th-century bishop, or perhaps a scribal error for another authority.</note>
...you only delay, while I dwell in spacious rivers. **So** there were disputes and contentions between them. Therefore the feast was turned into turmoil because some supported the side of one and some the side of the other, so much so that they wanted to tear each other apart. In the meantime, a certain learned and ancient turtle spoke, saying: "Brothers, it is not good to battle because of vain praisers. For I do not praise myself, though I am worthy of praise, since it is written: **'Let another man praise you, and not your own mouth'** <note>Proverbs 27:2. A common medieval exhortation against boasting.</note> because all praise in one's own mouth becomes foul. Therefore, it is better that these two go together to the sea-judge—that is, the dolphin—who is a just and upright judge fearing the Lord, for he will conclude this matter correctly." This speech calmed everyone, and those two went together to the dolphin, reporting all these things and praising themselves as much as they could. **To whom the dolphin said:** "My little children, I have never seen you because you hide in the rivers, while I dive in the sea waves. Therefore, I can by no means settle this unless I first taste you." And saying this, he gave a leap and swallowed them, saying: **"No one ought to praise themselves above all others, nor to exalt themselves."** <note>original: "nec saluare." In this context, it implies saving one's own reputation through vanity.</note> In this way, some people always praise themselves and commend their own work, desiring because of vanity and pride to raise themselves above all. But the just and humble despise themselves. And as **Saint Gregory** <note>original: "S." In medieval theological texts, "S." often abbreviates Sanctus Gregorius (Saint Gregory the Great), known for his writings on humility.</note> says: "Then our works grow through merit when they decrease within ourselves through humility." Wherefore **Job** in chapter 31 says: **"If I have kissed my hand with my mouth, which is a very great iniquity."** <note>Job 31:27–28. In medieval interpretation, kissing one's own hand symbolized self-congratulation—taking credit for God's work.</note> He kisses his own hand who praises what he does. For this reason, Truth <note>A common title for Christ in medieval texts.</note> instructs us, as **Luke** says in chapter 17: **"When you have done all those things which are commanded of you, say: 'We are unprofitable servants; we have done that which was our duty to do.'"** For it is told in a fable <note>original: "Fabulatur enim." This introduces a new illustrative story, likely from a bestiary or Aesopic tradition.</note> that the birds found a nest made of roses and aromatic flowers. The eagle, the king, said that the nest should be given to the most noble bird. He called the birds of the heaven together and asked all who heard which was the most beautiful bird. The cuckoo responded: **"The Cuckoo!"** And again the eagle asked which was the strongest bird, and the cuckoo responded: **"The Cuckoo!"** And the eagle, being indignant, said: "Unhappy cuckoo! You always praise yourself and never commend another; and yet you are neither the most beautiful, nor the swiftest, nor the strongest, nor do you sing well, but you always cry the same thing. And therefore I give a sentence of condemnation against you: that you shall never have this nest, nor any other." <note>This explains the natural behavior of the cuckoo, which lays its eggs in the nests of other birds rather than building its own.</note> Thus many people always praise themselves, and always sing **"Cuckoo!"**

...you only delay, while I dwell in the spacious rivers." Thus there were disputes and contentions among them. For this reason, the feast was turned into turmoil because some supported one side and some the other, so much so that they wished to tear one another apart. Meanwhile, a certain learned and ancient **turtledove** <note>original: "turtur"; while usually a land bird, the turtledove frequently appears in medieval fables as a symbol of wisdom and chastity regardless of the setting.</note> spoke, saying: "Brothers, it is not good to fight because of vain boasters. For I do not praise myself, even though I am worthy of praise, since it is written: *Let another’s mouth commend you, and not your own* <note>Proverbs 27:2</note>, because all praise in one’s own mouth becomes foul. Therefore, it is better that those who praise themselves go to the sea-judge—that is, the **dolphin**—who is a just and upright judge, fearing the Lord, for he will settle this matter correctly." And saying this, he gave a leap and swallowed them, saying: "No one ought to praise themselves above all others, nor save themselves." Hence, **Job** says in chapter 31: *If I have kissed my hand with my mouth, which is the greatest iniquity.* <note>Job 31:27. In medieval exegesis, "kissing one's hand" was a metaphor for taking credit for one's own good deeds rather than giving glory to God.</note> One kisses his own hand who praises that which he performs. For this reason, the Truth instructs us, as **Luke** says in chapter 17: *When you have done all the things commanded of you, say: we are useless servants; we have done that which we ought to have done.* It is also told in a fable that the birds found a nest made of roses and aromatic flowers, and the **Eagle**, their king, said that the nest should be given to the most noble bird. He had the birds of heaven called together and asked all who were listening which bird was the most beautiful. The **Cuckoo** responded: "The Cuckoo!" And again, the Eagle asked which bird was the strongest, and the Cuckoo responded: "The Cuckoo!" The Eagle, being indignant, said: "Unhappy Cuckoo! You always praise yourself and never commend another, and yet you are not the most beautiful, nor the fastest, nor the strongest, nor do you sing well—but you always cry the same thing. And therefore, I give a sentence of damnation against you: that you shall never have this nest, nor any other." Thus, many people always praise themselves and always sing "Cuckoo."

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing fables following the dolphin's judgment. The previous page established that pride leads to destruction, a theme that carries directly into the story of the frog and the crab.</meta>

<image-desc>A large, ornate woodcut initial 'R' decorated with dense foliate scrollwork and floral motifs.</image-desc>A frog, when she saw a crab swimming on the bank of a river, said: "Who is this so foul and deformed who has dared to disturb my water? Since I am so strong and powerful in the waters and on the lands, I shall go to him and drive him away." And saying this, she gave a leap and attacked the crab, saying: "Why have you not blushed, you wretch, to enter into my place of rest? Since you are so despicable and black, are you not ashamed to muddy the clear and bright water?" The crab, however, as is his nature, began to pull back, saying: "Do not say such things, sister, because I desire to have peace and love with you; therefore, do not set yourself against me." 

The frog, seeing him move backward, believed he did this out of fear of her. Therefore, she began to harass him even more with words and actions, saying: "Do not pull yourself back, you foulest creature; because you cannot escape today, I shall give your flesh to the fishes." And saying this, she gave a leap, wishing to kill him. But the crab, seeing that he could not escape, roused his terror and bit the frog with his claws and tore her apart, saying: "He must fight bravely who cannot avoid the fight." 

And so, everyone ought to flee from war and dispute to the best of their ability; but if one cannot avoid it at all, before they allow themselves to be killed or die, they should fight with all their strength against their attackers. For the law says: **It is permitted to repel force with force.** <note>original: "Vim vi repellere licet." A fundamental principle of Roman Law (Digest 43.16.1.27) regarding self-defense.</note> For whatever anyone does for the protection of their own body, they are seen to have acted rightly. It is better to meet the threat before the time than to seek vengeance after the end. From such things, the Psalmist sought to be freed and rescued, saying: **"Deliver me, O Lord, from those who rise up against me."** <note>original: "Ab insurgentibus in me libera me domine." Referring to Psalm 18:48.</note> and afterward he prayed that such people be destroyed, saying: **"Scatter the nations that desire wars."** <note>original: "Dissipa gentes que bella volunt." Referring to Psalm 68:30.</note> 

For we ought to fight not only for ourselves, but for the defense of our parents and for the preservation of the republic. Just as Valerius <note>Valerius Maximus, a 1st-century Roman historian famous for his collection of historical anecdotes.</note> posits that when King Darius invaded the borders of the Scythians <note>original: "siccarum." Likely a transcription error for "Scytharum." Darius the Great's campaign against the Scythians is a famous historical example of overextension.</note>, the Scythians sent word that they might endure the depopulation of their fields and vineyards with equanimity; but if he touched the tombs of their parents, then he would feel the power and strength of the Scythians, because they were prepared for their defense. 

Regarding the preservation of the republic, we ought to be so zealous that it is fitting to expose ourselves to death. Wherefore Valerius relates that when Codrus, the king of the Athenians, was being hard-pressed by enemies, he received an oracle <note>original: "mensum." Likely a corruption of "responsum" or "vaticinium."</note> from Apollo that his army would obtain victory if he allowed himself to be killed by his enemies. When this response became known even to the enemies, they commanded that no one should touch Codrus. Then he, having changed his clothes, went out to the enemies and struck a certain soldier provocatively <note>original: "false percussit." He feigned an attack or struck in disguise to provoke a lethal response.</note>; the soldier, being struck, rushed upon him and killed him instantly. When the body of Codrus was recognized, the enemies, mindful of the oracle, immediately fled, leaving everything behind.

## ¶ Concerning the fisherman and the little fish. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Chapter 44.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'P' featuring internal foliate scrollwork.</image-desc>A certain fisherman, while he was fishing, caught a certain little fish; and when he wished to slit its throat, the fish cried out, saying: "O fisherman, have mercy on me! You will have little profit from me if you kill me. But if you allow me to go, I promise to God and to you that I will make you gain a great deal, because I will return to you daily with a large company of others..."

<summary>This page uses the fable of the Frog and the Crab to discuss the ethics of self-defense and national duty, citing Roman law and Greek history. It then begins a new fable about a fisherman and a small fish pleading for its life.</summary>
<keywords>self-defense, self-sacrifice, King Codrus, Scythians, Valerius Maximus, Roman law, Athenian history, fables, pride, war</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the sequence of animal fables used for moral instruction. The previous page concluded a dispute between river fish judged by a dolphin, ending with a warning against self-exaltation. This page presents a new fable about a frog and a crab, followed by historical examples of just war and self-defense, and begins the fable of the fisherman and the little fish.</meta>

<image-desc>A large ornamental woodcut initial 'R' depicting floral and foliate patterns entwined within the letterform.</image-desc>

**A frog**, when she saw a **crab** swimming on the bank of a river, said: "Who is this so ugly and deformed who has dared to disturb my water, since I am strong and powerful in the waters and on the lands? I shall go to him and drive him away." And saying this, she gave a leap and attacked the crab, saying: "Why were you not ashamed, you wretch, to enter into my resting place? Since you are despised and black, are you not embarrassed to disturb water so clear and bright?" The crab, however, as is his nature, began to pull back, saying: "Do not say such things, sister, because I desire to have peace and love with you; therefore, do not set upon me." The frog, seeing him retreat, believed he did this because of fear of her. Whereupon she began to harass him even more with words and actions, saying: "Do not pull back, you most foul creature, because you cannot escape today; I shall give your flesh to the fishes." And saying this, she gave a leap, wishing to kill him. 

The crab, however, seeing that he could not escape, roused himself <note>The Latin 'se terrificavit' suggests making himself formidable or terrifying in response to the threat.</note> and bit and tore the frog with his claws <note>original: "griffis"</note>, saying: "**He must fight bravely who cannot avoid the struggle.**" 

Thus, everyone ought to flee from war and dispute as much as they are able; but if one cannot avoid it at all, before permitting oneself to be slaughtered or to die, one should fight with all one's strength against the attackers. For the law says: **"It is permitted to repel force with force."** <note>original: "Cum vi repellere licet." A fundamental principle of Roman and Medieval Canon Law regarding self-defense.</note> For whatever anyone does for the protection of their own body, they are seen to have done rightly. It is better to meet the danger before it happens than to seek revenge after the end. From such things the Psalmist asked to be freed and rescued, saying: **"Deliver me, O Lord, from those who rise up against me."** <note>Psalm 59:1.</note> And later he prayed for such people to be scattered, saying: **"Scatter the nations that desire wars."** <note>Psalm 68:30.</note> 

For we ought to fight not only for ourselves, but for the defense of our parents and for the sake of saving the Republic. As Valerius <note>Valerius Maximus, a 1st-century Roman author who compiled "Memorable Deeds and Sayings."</note> relates: when King Darius invaded the borders of the Scythians <note>original: "siccarum," a common scribal variant for Scytharum.</note>, the Scythians sent word that they would endure the depopulation of their fields and vineyards with equanimity; but if he touched the sepulchers of their parents, then he would feel the power and strength of the Scythians, because they were prepared for their defense. 

Furthermore, we ought to be so zealous for the saving of the Republic that it is fitting to expose ourselves to death. Regarding this, Valerius relates that when Codrus, King of the Athenians, was being pressed by his enemies, he received a response from Apollo <note>The Oracle of Delphi.</note> that his army would obtain victory if he permitted himself to be killed by his enemies. When this response became known even to the enemies, they commanded that no one should touch Codrus. Then he, having changed his clothes <note>Disguised as a commoner or peasant.</note>, went out toward the enemies and struck a certain soldier under false pretenses; and having been struck in return, the soldier rushed upon him and killed him instantly. When the body of Codrus was recognized, the enemies, mindful of the oracle, immediately fled, leaving everything behind.

**A certain fisherman**, while he was fishing, caught a certain **little fish**; and when he wished to slit its throat, it cried out, saying: "O fisherman, I am of poor size; you will have little profit from me if you kill me. But if you permit me to go away, I promise to God and to you that I shall make you gain very much indeed, because I will return to you every day with a great company of others..."

<meta>This page continues a sequence of moral fables. The previous page concluded with a story about a frog and a crab, illustrating the principle of self-defense. This page transitions into a new fable about a treacherous fish, focusing on the theme of ingratitude.</meta>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicts two men wading in water. The man on the left is using a large basket-like net to catch fish; several fish are already inside the net, and he reaches for another in the water. ...of fish. And I will lead many into your nets." To whom the fisherman said: "How will I recognize you among so many fish?" But he [the fish] replied: "Cut off a little bit of my tail so that you may know me among the others." The fisherman believed him; he cut off his tail and let him go. But the ungrateful little fish, whenever the man went fishing, would come and drive the fish away from him, saying: "Brothers, beware of that deceiver who deceived me and cut off my tail. For he will do the same to you if you are not careful. And if you do not believe me, at least believe my works." Saying this, he showed his cut-off tail. The fish indeed shrank away from him and fled from him more quickly. The fisherman, however, whenever he went fishing, caught nothing, because of which he lived in poverty. It happened also that a long time later, after this plan, the fisherman caught him along with others. Recognizing him, he killed him cruelly, saying: "He who remains ungrateful, it is just if he be cut down." For thus many ungrateful people always return evil for good. Of whom the Book of Proverbs speaks:

> He who returns evil for good, evil shall not depart from his house. <note>original: "Qui reddit mala pro bonis non recedet malicia de domo eius." Proverbs 17:13.</note>

This is indeed great ingratitude and unworthy of merits. Of which *Saint Bernard* <note>Saint Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a highly influential Cistercian abbot and mystic.</note> says:

> Ingratitude is an enemy of the soul, the destruction of merits, the scattering of virtues, and the loss of benefits. Ingratitude is an enemy of the soul, a parching wind drying up the fountain of piety, the dew of mercy, and the streams of grace.

Against the ungrateful and those who do not acknowledge benefits, the example of that peasant <term>villanellus</term> <note>Literally "a little villager" or peasant.</note> can be told, who went daily to the woods with his little donkey. He found a certain dragon crushed by a tree, whom he himself freed. The dragon, however, afterward wanted to eat the donkey, saying that all the greatest services are wasted. But the peasant took counsel from a fox, who led the dragon back to its original place and freed the peasant and the donkey. Likewise, the example of "Litigious Girard" <note>Likely a reference to a folk character or a variation of a trickster tale.</note> who was like a fool and had nothing but a single cat. The cat, seeing everyone going to the Emperor and bearing gifts, said to himself: "I too shall go to the Emperor so that he may enrich my master." He found first a <unclear>regalem</unclear> <note>Literally "a royal thing"; in this context, likely a noble game animal like a stag.</note> and then a bear, upon which he sat, and he gave them to the Emperor on behalf of his master. To whom the Emperor sent many gifts and greatly enriched them. Afterward, the cat feigned death and tested his master; he found him not caring for him, for which the cat reproached him greatly. Whence *Saint Bernard* says:

> He is not worthy of things to be given who does not give thanks for things already given.

And *Saint Augustine* <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), one of the most important Church Fathers.</note> says:

> What God had given freely, the ungrateful man lost. <note>original: "Quod deus dederat gratis. tulit ingratus."</note>

<summary>This page uses three fables—the treacherous fish, the dragon and the peasant, and the cat of Girard—to illustrate the spiritual and social dangers of ingratitude. It concludes with theological warnings from Saint Bernard and Saint Augustine on how ingratitude destroys divine grace.</summary>
