# Of the Eagle and the Lion. Chapter 49.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration contained within a rectangular black border. The scene depicts a gathering of various animals in a sparse landscape. On the left, a fox sits next to a stag, with a lion standing behind them, facing to the right. A small bird is perched in a leafless tree in the upper left background. On the right, a large eagle faces the lion, surrounded by three other smaller birds. In the center, between the lion and the eagle, a single six-petaled flower grows from the ground.</image-desc>

The **Eagle**, with other birds in flight and their tents <note>The text uses "tentoria" (tents), a metaphor for the birds' encampment or nesting grounds in a military sense</note> pitched in the open fields, was battling with the **Lion** and the wild beasts. Daily they came together to the contest, and many rushed forward from both sides to join the struggle. Meanwhile, the **Fox** secretly called to the **Swallow**, saying: "Now is the time for us to redeem ourselves. For we are able to see vengeance taken upon our princes who rule over us; let us act shrewdly so that they battle one another and both perish."

The swallow agreed to do this and flew to the eagle, praising her greatly, saying: "You are the queen of birds and their empress. If you are willing to heed my advice, you shall also be the king and prince of the wild beasts." The eagle consented and promised to follow the counsel. But the swallow, returning to the fox, arranged with her and others that the eagle should fight one-on-one with the lion, and whoever emerged as the victor would be the prince and leader of both birds and beasts.

However, while they were struggling in the heat of the contest, the eagle said: "O leonine nobility, if you look closely, we have been seduced by false advisors. They desire above all to be freed from our rule, and for that reason, they have tricked us. Surely it is better that each of us rules over our own kind than that we destroy ourselves through these deceivers."

The lion believed this, and they were reconciled, saying together: "They are wicked seducers and false counselors." Therefore, let princes beware of such people, lest they be moved too quickly by polished words. For many seducers are found. For **Seneca** <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca, a Roman Stoic philosopher often quoted in medieval moral texts</note> says: "Be moved by the matter itself and not by polished words." <note>original: "Ad rem mouearis z non ad verba composita."</note> For everyone ought to guard themselves and take precautions lest they be deceived by false advisors. Because of this, **Ecclesiastes** <note>likely referring to the Book of Sirach, also known as Ecclesiasticus, which was often confused with Ecclesiastes</note> commanded: "Save your soul from bad counsel." <note>original: "Consilio malo serua animam."</note>

For a prince ought to have an understanding of the dangers surrounding him, especially the plots of those flatterers. For flatterers are like **Sirens** <note>mythological creatures whose beautiful songs lured sailors to their doom</note> seducing with charming voices, as seen in **Isaiah 13**: "And sirens [shall be] in the temples of pleasure" <note>original: "Et syrene in delubris voluptatis"</note>—from whom one must especially beware and understand their deceptions. Whence **Aesop**, the philosopher and nephew of **Plato** <note>A common medieval legend; historically, Aesop was a freed slave from Samos, not a relative of the philosopher Plato</note>, is read regarding flattery...

<summary>This fable tells of a Fox and a Swallow who attempt to overthrow their rulers, the Lion and the Eagle, by inciting them to a duel. The rulers realize the deception just in time, leading to a moral discourse on the dangers of flatterers and false advisors.</summary>
<keywords>Eagle, Lion, Fox, Swallow, flattery, false counsel, Seneca, Aesop, leadership, deception</keywords>

# Of the Eagle who Summoned the Birds. Chapter 50.

The **Eagle** summoned an assembly <term>capitulum</term> <note>original: "capitulum alitum"; a formal meeting or "chapter" of the birds</note> of the birds so that, while they remained together, they might correct their errors. Behold, hunters appeared stretching out their nets and snares to capture the birds. Observing this and recognizing the danger, the **Eagle** began to cry out through her heralds that all birds ought to follow the eagle's banner and fly together with her if they desired to escape. Those who were obedient and flew with her escaped, but there were certain gluttonous and disobedient ones who looked upon the bait and, lusting after it, flew into the snares. Whence, ensnared and trapped, they fell miserably, saying: "Those who do not wish to obey must perish wickedly." 

Therefore, one must beware the beginning of disobedience, because disobedience brings death, a curse, and punishment in the present life. Whence it was said to **Adam** in Genesis 3: "Because you have eaten of the tree of which I commanded you not to eat, cursed is the ground in your work; thorns and thistles shall it bring forth to you." It was also said to **Saul** in the first book of Kings: "Because you have rejected the words of the Lord, the Lord will reject you, that you may not be king." On account of which **Bernard** <note>Likely Saint Bernard of Clairvaux, a highly influential medieval theologian</note> says: "The defeat of disobedience is great," because it cost the angel heaven, Adam paradise, and Saul his kingdom. **Solomon** lost divine love, as **Valerius** <note>Valerius Maximus, a 1st-century Roman author of historical anecdotes</note> says in his second book, providing an example of how the fathers punished sons who did not observe military discipline. **Aurelius** <note>Likely referring to Aulus Postumius Albus, who, according to Roman legend, executed his son for leaving his post to fight the enemy, despite winning the skirmish</note> [punished] his son because he did not keep his command, forcing him to serve among the footmen. It is read in the *Chronicles of the Romans* that when **Julius Caesar** passed the five-year period promised to him for subduing enemies, although he had triumphed gloriously, the honor due to him was nevertheless denied, nor was he permitted to enter the city, because beyond the limit if

# Of the Herodius and the Kite. Chapter 51.

The **Herodius** <term>Herodius</term> <note>In medieval bestiaries, this bird is often identified as a gyrfalcon or a species of heron considered the "king of birds" for its ability to fly higher than the eagle.</note> is a most rapacious bird. It is the greatest of all flying things and even defeats the eagle. The commentary <term>glosa</term> <note>A marginal or interlinear explanation of a biblical text.</note> says this regarding the Psalm of the Herodius <term>etc.</term> <note>Likely referring to Psalm 104:17, "As for the stork, the fir trees are her house," where some translations use "herodius" for "stork."</note> This Herodius was soaring through the air, but a kite <term>milvus</term> began to hiss after him and mock him, saying: "Wait for me, you wicked one, for I shall pluck you entirely bald! Do you wish to lord it over all the birds? I shall take vengeance upon you for them all!" The **Herodius**, however, continued to fly clearly and did not care for these silly words. But the kite began to repeat his words, because of which the Herodius, becoming inflamed with anger, ascended above him and disemboweled him entirely with his talons, saying: "He who wishes to harass the strong perishes and seeks his own death." Therefore, we can see that it is no small danger to speak against the powerful or to harass them. As it is said in **Ecclesiasticus 8**: "Do not strive with a powerful man, lest perhaps you fall into his hands." Whence **Aesop** <term>Esopus</term> relates that a certain wolf was drinking in a river, and a certain lamb was drinking with him further downstream. And the wolf raised his voice toward him, saying: <margin>disturber</margin> "You are troubling the water of my drink." To which the lamb replied: "Lord, I do you no injury, nor do I trouble it for you." But the wolf said: "You threaten me with damages! Do you not know what I did to your father not yet six months ago?" <margin>not yet</margin> To which the lamb said: "I have not even lived for such a long time." At this the wolf cried out: "Do you speak as a liar?" <term>falsifer</term> <note>original: "falsifer .i. villane"; meaning a bearer of falsehoods or a low-born rogue.</note> and he devoured him. So do the powerful of this world and the prelates <note>High-ranking church officials.</note> do to their subordinates, because they devour and destroy them without any cause. Therefore it is said: "To the Master, an occasion is never lacking." <note>A proverb meaning those in power can always find an excuse to exert their will.</note>

## Of the Crane that wanted to fly to the sun. Chapter 52.

<summary>This page uses the hierarchy of birds and an Aesopian fable of the wolf and lamb to warn against challenging those in power, noting that the powerful often invent excuses to exploit the weak.</summary>
<keywords>Herodius, Kite, Aesop, Wolf and Lamb, power dynamics, Ecclesiasticus, justice, prelates</keywords>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the story of the Herodius and the Kite (Chapter 51) to the fable of the Crane and the Eagle. It concludes Chapter 51 and contains the full text of Chapter 52's fable regarding pride, followed by a historical anecdote about the Roman Emperor Valerian. It ends with the heading for Chapter 53 (noted as 52 in the OCR, but following the sequence).</meta>

->[Illustration: A sun with a human face shines over a landscape where a crane stands on the ground, looking downward.]<-

**T**he **Crane** <term>Grus</term>, standing upright and seeing the **eagle** fly all the way to the **sun** and gaze upon it clearly, said to herself: "I am beautiful and large; like the eagle, I wish to fly to the sun and gaze upon it with unblinking eyes just like the eagle, and then I shall be as greatly honored as she is." And when she raised herself toward the stars, she began to grow weary beyond her strength, yet she did not reach the sun. Nor out of her arrogance did she wish to descend; instead, she began to strive upward beyond her power. Because of this, being greatly burdened and unable to raise herself to the sun, she fell beyond recovery, saying: "He who strives upward beyond his power, afterwards falls down against his will."

<margin>
against his will <note>original: "contra il suo volum"</note>
<image-desc>A small hand-drawn manicule (pointing hand) in the left margin pointing towards the start of the second paragraph.</image-desc>
</margin>

In this way, many proud and puffed-up people desire to fly above everyone else. And because pride always has a fall, they shall be humbled against their will. For he who exalts himself shall be humbled. Wherefore **Bernard** <term>St. Bernard of Clairvaux</term> says: "He who exalts himself as much as he can, shall be cast down as much as God can." And **Augustine** <term>St. Augustine of Hippo</term>: "If you extol yourself, God will cast you down. If you cast yourself down, God will lift you up." For this is the judgment of God, to which nothing can be added or taken away: "He who humbles himself shall be exalted," etc. Truth itself says this, as **Pope Leo** says: "See, brothers, a great miracle. God is high; if you raise yourself, He flees from you; if you humble yourself, He descends to you."

**Orosius** <term>Paulus Orosius</term> <note>original: "oratius"; likely a scribal error for the 5th-century historian Orosius.</note> relates in his fifth book that the Emperor **Valerian** <note>original: "Valerius"; refers to the Roman Emperor Valerian, r. 253–260 AD.</note>, the eighth after **Nero**, because of his pride and faithlessness, stirred up a persecution against the worshippers of **Christ** <note>original: "xpi", a common abbreviation for the Greek-derived "Christi".</note> throughout the whole world, desiring to erase and trample upon the name of Christ. Wherefore he caused all those who invoked the name of Christ to be killed. However, when he fought with **Sapor** <term>Shapur I</term>, King of the Persians, he was defeated and captured along with his entire army. God delivered him into the hand of Sapor because of his pride, and he was made his servant all the days that he lived. For whenever Sapor would mount his horse, he would first trample upon the prostrate emperor with his feet, using him as a footstool, and afterwards he would mount.

### ¶ Of the starling that caught the hare. Chapter 52.

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing fables from the previous page, moving from the crane's failed flight to stories about a "Sterla" bird and a wolf. It emphasizes the dangers of vanity and the public display of one's virtues or possessions.</meta>

<margin>?</margin>
**T**he **Sterla** <term>Sterla</term> <note>Likely a local or archaic name for a stork or a similar large wading bird, described here as having a large beak.</note> is a bird similar to a crane. For it has a large and dangerous beak. This bird caught a **hare** with its beak. But although it was starving, it did not want to eat its prey immediately, saying: "I wish to show such an excellent prize to others, so that I may be magnified by those who see it." And when it had led the prey to the assembly of birds, the stronger birds, coveting this prize, snatched it away, and did not leave it any part. The Sterla truly remaining hungry in its sorrow, said: "He who wishes to reveal his business desires to lose it." Thus, some who are vain and boastful wish to show everything they possess to others, so that they might be more highly praised for it. But as **Gregory** <term>St. Gregory the Great</term> says: "He who carries treasure openly on the road invites a fight." <note>original: "Preliari desiderat qui thesaurū publice portat in via."</note> There are also many who desire all the good they do to be known by others. To them, **Augustine** <term>St. Augustine of Hippo</term> speaks, saying:
<margin><image-desc>A manicule (pointing hand) in the right margin, indicating the adjacent line of text.</image-desc></margin>
"Keep your deeds hidden as much as you can; and if you cannot do so entirely, let the intention of hiding them be in your heart." And **Gregory** says: "Let a good work be done in public in such a way that your intention remains hidden." Likewise, there is a beast which, when it kills, he who boasts of the victory he has achieved dies. <note>This likely refers to the spiritual "death" caused by the sin of pride.</note> **Aesop** <term>Esopus</term> tells that a certain **wolf** caught a very tender kid from among the goats. The kid said to him: "Rejoice and be glad with great joy that you have such a kid in your power. But before you eat me, I pray you to sing, and while you sing, I shall dance." And then the wolf began to sing and the kid to dance. Hearing them, the **dogs** made an attack upon the wolf, whom they pursued, forcing him to leave the kid behind; and so the kid was saved and returned with the dogs. By this example, it is noted that everyone ought to use their goods in silence, lest some evil happen to them by using them in another way.

<meta>This page continues a series of moralizing animal fables (exempla). The previous page discussed the vanity of the Sterla bird and the importance of keeping one's virtues hidden; this page transitions to the Ostrich and the folly of seeking physical perfection over spiritual health.</meta>

### The Ostrich

**T**he **Ostrich** <term>Strutio</term> <note>From the Latin *struthio*, referring to the flightless bird known for its feathers.</note> is a large and powerful bird, feathered and winged. However, it cannot lift itself toward the stars because of the weakness of its wings. Once, there was an ostrich that was quite beautiful and handsome; it had very strong and lovely wings. Yet, in its wings, it carried two twisted feathers which caused it great sorrow. For this reason, it went to a **surgeon** <term>cyrurgicus</term> <note>A medieval term for a healer or medical practitioner who performs manual operations.</note> saying: "You know that I am distinguished and handsome, but I want you to cut off these twisted feathers for me, as they disfigure me somewhat." 

The surgeon cut off those twisted feathers and anointed the ostrich's wings with such an ointment that all the other feathers of the wings fell out. Because of this, the bird was forever powerless to fly. Indeed, the ostrich, enraged by this, wept until death, saying: "As the mighty God <note>original: "strenuus deus"</note> fashioned us, let us never change." 

Thus, there are some curious and vain people who, while they are formed well enough by their **Creator** <term>conditor</term>, do not give thanks to him. Rather, if they have any blemish on their body, they strive by all means to heal it, yet they take no care to heal the blemishes of the soul. But as **Augustine** <term>St. Augustine of Hippo</term> says: "The invisible Groom <note>A common medieval metaphor for Christ as the spouse of the soul.</note> does not require outward beauty." And as **Ecclesiastes** says: "Glory is deceptive and beauty is vain." <note>original: "Fallax gloria & vana est pulchritudo." This is a quote from Proverbs 31:30, though the text attributes it to Ecclesiastes.</note> Regarding such people, Augustine says: "Behold, all things are beautiful along with the impious, yet they themselves are ugly."

<margin><image-desc>A hand-drawn manicule (pointing hand) in the left margin points toward the boxed section of text below.</image-desc></margin>

Since there was no corner that was not covered with purple cloth and other precious things, a certain philosopher was present who, when he wished to spit, spat in the face of the king. And when the servants, because of this, wanted to lead him away to be hanged, the king did not permit it; instead, he asked the philosopher why he had done this. To which he replied: "I saw every other place full of gold and silver and precious gems and purple cloth, and therefore I spat into the king's beard, which was thickened and dirty from the grease of the food. I did not see a less clean place."</insert> 

Hearing this, the king was struck with remorse and humbled. Those...

<meta>This page continues the theme of vanity and the folly of attempting to alter one’s natural state, transitioning from the previous page's story of the Ostrich to the famous fable of the Crow in borrowed feathers and a tale of a social-climbing rooster.</meta>

...Indeed, those who deck and decorate themselves with others' belongings are soon stripped bare. Just as **Aesop** <term>Aesop</term> <note>The legendary Greek storyteller to whom many fables involving animals are attributed.</note> relates, a certain **crow** <term>cornix</term> that was ugly and black set out for a wedding; but before entering the wedding, it took a single feather from every bird and adorned itself. Thus, it was beautiful—not by nature, but by artifice. And when it entered the house of the wedding, the other birds gathered there marveled at its beauty. However, the birds whose feathers had been stolen came forward and each took back their own feathers. Thus the crow remained black and ugly as before. It happened in **Florence** <note>The use of a real city like Florence was a common technique in medieval sermons to make the moral lesson feel more immediate and "true."</note> during a general procession, that a certain **monkey** <term>fymia</term> <note>Likely a pet monkey, which were status symbols for the wealthy in the Middle Ages.</note> jumped onto the neck of a certain lady and, in front of all the people, snatched away the braids of false hair she was wearing; and so, like the crow once the borrowed feathers were removed, she remained shameful and bald-headed. And this happened by the judgment of God.

->¶ Of the falcon and the rooster and their master. Chapter 55.<-

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration divided into three sections or depicting three stages. On the left, a man in a tunic and hose stands, holding a small object (perhaps a lure or food) towards a bird flying in the air. In the center, a large bird of prey, likely a falcon, is perched on a stylized tree growing from rocky ground. On the right, a man in similar attire holds a rooster by its body, while another bird (the falcon) flies toward his other hand.</image-desc>

A certain **knight** <term>miles</term> had a **falcon** <term>falco</term> in which he took great delight; he carried it upon a **glove** <term>cyrotheca</term> <note>A heavy leather gauntlet used in falconry to protect the hunter's hand from the bird's talons.</note> and fed it sumptuously. On a certain occasion, the knight released the falcon into the air, wishing to call it to something great. He whistled after it so it would return, but the falcon did not want to come down. Meanwhile, a **rooster** <term>gallus</term>, seeing this, began to exalt himself, saying: "Wretch that I am, what am I doing? I am always shamefully pecking at the ground. Am I not as beautiful and large as the falcon? Surely I shall fly onto the glove and feast upon the food of my master." And when he had flown onto the glove, the knight, anxious about his falcon, was pleased; he seized the rooster and quickly slit its throat. Showing the rib of the rooster to the falcon, he called it back to his hand; and the falcon, seeing such choice meat and desiring it, joyfully swooped down to him, saying: 

<margin>Pointing hand (manicule) in right margin.</margin>
It is not **wisdom** <term>sensus</term> to lift oneself above one's **station** <term>status</term>, but wisdom is to rest in one's station. For thus a man ought to act, and he ought to remain humble.

--- Page 68 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing discussion on why one should remain in one's natural social and physical state, transitioning from the previous page's focus on false appearances to the dangers of attempting roles for which one is not naturally suited.</meta>

...in his own state, not to turn his eyes toward that which is not becoming. Because of this, **Ecclesiasticus** <term>Ecclesiasticus</term> <note>Also known as the Book of Sirach, a book of ethical teachings from the Old Testament Apocrypha.</note> says:
<margin><image-desc>manicule</image-desc> <note>A hand-drawn finger pointing to emphasize the text.</note></margin> "Set a limit to your own wisdom. Seek not the things that are too high for you, and search not into things that are stronger than you." <note>original: "Prudentie tue pone moduz. Altiora te ne quesieris. et fortiora te ne scrutatus fueris."</note> And **Seneca** <term>Seneca</term> <note>The Roman Stoic philosopher, often quoted in medieval texts for his moral wisdom.</note> says: "Seek that which you are able to find; learn that which you are able to know."
**Aesop** <term>Aesop</term> relates that a certain nobleman had a small **puppy** <term>cagnoletum</term> and a **donkey** <term>asinum</term>. He used to send the donkey to the mill for heavy labor, but with the puppy he would play, and he fed it from his own bowl. Seeing himself worn out by toil while the dog received favors, the donkey began to think to himself: "That little dog knows nothing except how to jump on his master with his feet and cry out with his tongue, and yet he is so loved. I want to act in this way too."
And so, raising his front legs, he threw them over his master's shoulders and thundered with his voice <note>The donkey’s braying.</note>. The master, being terrified, began to cry out; the servants came with clubs and sticks and beat the donkey almost to death. And the donkey, reflecting, said: "It would have been better for me to remain quiet than to act like this, because I have gained nothing except a beating." From this, it can be
<margin><image-desc>manicule</image-desc></margin>gathered that a person ought not to do anything except what they are naturally able to do. No one attempts with success that which nature denies. The unwise man causes displeasure from the very thing by which he strives to please.

->¶ Of the hawk, the crane, and the charadrius. Chapter 56.<-

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting three birds in a landscape. On the left, a hawk (astur) is perched near a small tree. A **hawk** <term>astur</term> was pursuing a **crane** <term>grus</term> in the heavens and at last struck it down; however, the crane wounded the hawk with its beak even unto death. The hawk, being thus wounded, sent messengers and gifts to the **charadrius** <term>cadrion</term> <note>A legendary white bird from medieval bestiaries believed to have the power to heal or predict death.</note> saying: "O physician of bodies and souls, descend and heal my wounds, for I shall fully satisfy your payment." The charadrius, indeed, as **Papias** <term>Papias</term> <note>An 11th-century Italian lexicographer who compiled a widely used medieval dictionary.</note> says, is a bird that is entirely white, whose innards cure cloudy eyes; if a man is destined to live, it approaches him, but if he is to die, it flees from him. Thus, recognizing that the hawk was about to die, the charadrius would not approach, saying: "I do not wish to visit those who are about to perish, but rather to stay with the joyful." Many...

<meta>This page continues the moralizing discourse found in medieval exempla—short stories used to illustrate ethical points. Following the previous page’s advice on knowing one's place, the text shifts to the theme of "True Friendship," contrasting those who remain loyal during hardship with "fair-weather friends," before beginning a new fable about predators and prey.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<sig>b iij</sig>

...for such friends are found who visit their friends in times of prosperity and joy, but in times of calamity and misery, they do not wish to see them. For this reason **Seneca** <term>Seneca</term> <note>The Roman Stoic philosopher, highly regarded in the Middle Ages for his moral treatises.</note> says: 
<margin><image-desc>A manicule (pointing hand) highlights the following sentence.</image-desc></margin>
*It is difficult to test friends in prosperous circumstances; in adversity, it is easy.* <note>original: "Difficile est in re prospera amicos probare. in adversa facile."</note> Because **Ecclesiastes** <term>Ecclesiastes</term> <note>Refers here to the Book of Sirach (Ecclesiasticus) 6:7-10.</note> says: *There is a friend who is a friend in name only.* And as **Isidore** <term>Isidore of Seville</term> <note>A 7th-century scholar and bishop whose works were foundational to medieval knowledge.</note> says: *Rare are the friends who remain dear even until the end.* A certain man asked his son if he had faithful friends. The son responded that he had three. But the father said, "Son, a friend is proven in times of distress; go and test them." The son went and took a **young pig** <term>porcastrum</term>, beheaded it, and cut off its feet, hands, and head. He put them in a sack, saying, "Behold the man I have killed." But he was received by none of those friends. For this reason, the father told him an example of two companions, one of whom granted his own betrothed to the other as a wife. Later, that man went to visit his parents and lost everything at sea; having returned to his companion, he did not dare to reveal himself. Instead, in despair, he said he had committed a murder (which he had not done) so that he might die. But he was recognized by his companion by a sign, and the companion then claimed that *he* had killed the man, so that he might liberate his friend. Finally, the actual murderer, seeing this, revealed himself so that these innocent men would not die. **Valerius** <term>Valerius Maximus</term> <note>A Roman author known for his "Memorable Deeds and Sayings."</note> relates in his fourth book, concerning true friendship, the story of two friends, namely **Damon and Pythias** <note>The text uses the medieval variants "Amone and Firozi."</note>. When the tyrant **Dionysius** <term>Dionysius of Syracuse</term> wanted to kill one of them, that man obtained time from him to return home to arrange his affairs; the other, however, acted as a **guarantor** <term>fideiussionem</term> for his return, doubting nothing of his friend. When the appointed day arrived and the man had not returned, everyone condemned the one who had stayed as a reckless fool. But he proclaimed that he did not fear, because of the constancy of his friend. At the established hour, the other arrived. The tyrant, marveling at their courage and friendship, remitted the punishment and asked them to receive him as a third member of their fellowship. If a man acts thus for another man, what ought he to do for God, who allowed himself to be killed for man? Wherefore **Ecclesiastes** <note>Sirach 29:15-16.</note> says: *Forget not the kindness of your guarantor, for he has given his life for you.* <note>original: "Gratiam fideiussionis tui non obliviscaris. dedit enim pro te aiam suam."</note>

## ¶ Concerning the Merlin, the Hawk, and the Quail. Chapter 57.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting two large birds of prey (a merlin and a hawk) perched upon a central branch. To the left, a nest containing four small birds (quails) rests on the ground. The landscape features stylized trees and hills in the background.</image-desc>

A **Merlin** <term>Smerillus</term> and a **Hawk** <term>accipiter</term> <note>The Merlin is a small falcon; the Hawk here represents a larger bird of prey, likely a goshawk.</note> became companions; they took prey together while hunting and divided it between them. On a certain occasion, they seized a **quail** <term>qualeam</term> outside of its nest and said to it: "Choose for yourself: either you wish for us to eat you alone, or you wish to lead us to your nest so that we may feast upon you and your children." The text concludes a story involving quails and the ethical principle of choosing the lesser of two evils, using the Roman hero Marcus Regulus as a primary example. It then introduces Chapter 57, featuring the "Arstancus," a bird that represents those who fear the rigors of a religious life.</meta>

...the quails <note>original: "quales"</note> say, "distress is on every side of me, and I do not know what to choose. But it is better for me to fall into your hands than to perish with my children." And as they were being slaughtered, they spoke, saying: "It is better to endure an evil than to suffer a worse one." So too must we follow the example of the quails: when we cannot escape danger, the lesser evil must always be chosen. According to the saying of the wise: *Of two evils, the lesser evil is to be chosen.* <note>original: "De duobus malis minus malum eligendum est." A classic principle of medieval moral philosophy.</note> For we ought to choose to die for the sake of the republic rather than to live for our own personal benefit.

Concerning this, **Augustine** <term>Augustine of Hippo</term> <note>A foundational Christian theologian (354–430 AD).</note> relates in the first book of *The City of God* that when **Marcus Regulus** <term>Marcus Atilius Regulus</term> <note>A Roman general and consul famous for his heroic adherence to his word.</note> was held captive by the Carthaginians, and many Carthaginian youths were held in Rome, the said Regulus was sent to Rome to negotiate an exchange, swearing that he would return if the exchange could not be made. When he arrived in Rome, he argued against the exchange, saying that he was an old man and had little time left to live, whereas those Carthaginian youths could still wage many wars against the Romans. And though he was begged to stay, he would in no way agree. Returning to Carthage, he died a cruel death. It would have been useful for him to remain, but because of his oath, it would not have been honorable; and for the sake of the Romans' benefit, it would not have been useful.

### ¶ Concerning the Arstancus who refused to follow a rule. Chapter 57.

<image-desc>A large ornamental drop cap 'A' at the beginning of the text block. The frame of the letter is filled with intricate, swirling leafy vines and floral motifs in a woodcut style.</image-desc>

The **Arstancus** <note>A legendary bird often appearing in medieval "Dialogues of Creatures," similar to a falcon.</note> is a bird similar to a falcon, powerful and virtuous. In its youth, it refused to be governed by a "Rule" <term>regula</term> <note>The set of regulations governing a monastic order, such as the Rule of St. Benedict.</note> while its virtues were still shining. But out of fear of the Rule’s severity, it delayed, saying: "I believe I will not be able to fast, to rise for early morning prayers <note>original: "matutinum," the first prayer service of the day, usually held in the middle of the night.</note>, to maintain chastity, and to deny my own will." And because it did not begin what was good out of fear of penance, it did not proceed well; rather, it ended badly, saying: "He who does not do good out of fear, perishes with sorrow." 

For in this way, many desire to fly toward the grace of God, but they fear losing the comforts <note>original: "elementis," referring here to the basic pleasures or materials of the world.</note> of the world. Indeed, the love of **Christ** calls them forward, but the greed of the world calls them back. Such people are accustomed to saying, "I would gladly serve, I would enter a religious order, but I fear that I cannot endure the severity of the Rule." But they do not pay attention to what the **Apostle** <term>St. Paul</term> says: *I can do all things through him who strengthens me* <note>original: "Omnia possum in eo qui me conformat." A reference to Philippians 4:13.</note>—that is, Christ. As **Batto** <note>Likely a reference to a character or author cited in the "Dialogus Creaturarum."</note> says: *To those who hope in Him, there is treasure in poverty, comfort in solitude, glory in rejection, wealth in contempt, and a canopy of protection from the rain and the heat.* For a certain storyteller says that a falcon captured a kite <term>miluum</term> and cast it down beneath its feet; and while it trampled the kite with one foot, it held the other foot raised high, saying: "You are unhappy, though you are as big and strong as I; why do you not..."

<image-desc>Woodcut illustration of a hawk or falcon standing on a patch of ground. The bird is depicted in profile facing left, showing detailed plumage rendered with hatching and strong talons.</image-desc>

<summary>This page uses the story of Marcus Regulus to illustrate the duty of self-sacrifice and the sanctity of oaths, followed by a fable about the Arstancus bird, which serves as an allegory for those who fear the rigors of monastic life.</summary>

...you defend yourself, yet I trample you and wish to disembowel you." To which the other replied: "You speak truly, because I am great and strong beyond you. Indeed, I have a beak and stronger feet, but my heart fails me."
<insert>For thus many are able to do many good deeds, but the heart and the will to act fail them.</insert>

A certain man fell into a river, not knowing how to swim, whereupon he began to cry out: "Saint George <note>Saint George was a popular medieval patron saint, often invoked for protection.</note>, help me!" Saint George, however, appeared and said to him: "Indeed, help yourself! Move your hands and feet and you will be freed. For he who helps himself is helped by God." For thus must he do who has fallen into sin: he ought not only to ask for pardon from God with his mouth, but he must work as best he can so that he may rise again from his sins.

It is related, moreover, in a letter to **Jerome** <term>Saint Jerome</term> <note>A prominent Church Father (c. 347–420 AD), known for translating the Bible into Latin.</note> that when a certain man had fallen into a well, and another coming upon him asked how he had fallen, the man said: "I beg you, do not ask how I fell in here; instead, inquire how you might be able to free me."

### Concerning the Hoopoe and the Parrot. Chapter 59.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a hoopoe, a bird with a distinct fan-like crest on its head, standing on the ground amidst stylized plants. To the left of the bird is a square birdcage or aviary with vertical bars and a striped, peaked roof.</image-desc>

The hoopoe <note>original: "Uppupa"</note> is a certain bird, as **Physiologus** <note>original: "Biso." Likely a corruption of "Physio," referring to the *Physiologus*, a popular medieval book on animal lore.</note> and **Isidore** <term>Isidore of Seville</term> <note>An influential 7th-century scholar; the reference is to book 12 of his *Etymologies*.</note> say. The Greeks call it the hoopoe <note>In Greek, the bird is called "epops." Isidore's text notes the Greeks name it for its appearance or habits.</note> because it considers human dung. He who anoints himself with its blood before going to sleep will see those conspiring against him in his dreams. Because this bird is beautiful and pleasantly crested and varied with feathers, it began to exalt itself. Observing a parrot <note>original: "papagum"</note> staying with the king in a gilded cage <term>cabea</term> <note>original: "cabea," a medieval spelling of "cavea," meaning cage or enclosure.</note>, who was also splendidly fed from the king's food, the hoopoe said: "I am as pleasant as the parrot, yet I seek my food with great labor. This parrot, however, remains honorably without sweat and is filled at his pleasure. I certainly want to go to the king and sing in the cage like the parrot, and feast and rejoice with the lord."

However, when it flew to the king and the king placed it in the cage, the hoopoe began to be greatly embittered, seeing itself captured and under the liberty of another. Whereupon, out of sadness and grief, it lived only a short time and prayed, saying: "Nothing can be compared to or valued against liberty." For thus many speak of the **religious** <term>religious</term> <note>In this context, "religious" refers to monks, friars, or those living under a monastic rule.</note>, saying: "These brothers have it well; they are very well fed, they sing, and they live honorably without great labor." Whereupon, when they wish to weep...

<summary>This page contains moral anecdotes emphasizing that divine help requires human effort, followed by a fable about a hoopoe that envies a parrot's pampered life in a cage only to realize that freedom is more valuable than luxury.</summary>
<keywords>Saint George, Saint Jerome, Isidore of Seville, hoopoe, parrot, liberty, work vs. prayer, monastic life</keywords>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page concludes the moralizing examples regarding liberty and free will that followed the previous chapter's discussion of the hoopoe and the parrot. It then transitions into Chapter 60, a fable about the different natures of a hen and a dove.</meta>

Those who find themselves under a harsh rule and in the power of another regret it, as they lack their own liberty. Hence the philosopher says: "Liberty is not well sold for all the gold in the world." <note>original: "Non bene pro toto libertas venditur auro." This is a famous medieval maxim often attributed to Aesop.</note> **Valerius Maximus** <term>Valerius Maximus</term> <note>A Roman author of the 1st century AD whose collection of "Memorable Deeds and Sayings" was a standard textbook for medieval moralists.</note> relates in his sixth book that **Leonidas** <note>The OCR reads "Licerotides," likely a scribal corruption of Leonidas.</note>, a noble of the Spartans, fought against **Xerxes**, King of the Persians, with three hundred citizens for the liberty of their fatherland. He encouraged his men with a cheerful spirit, saying: "Dine thus, my comrades, as if you are to sup in the underworld." <note>original: "Sic prandete comilitones mei tanquam apud inferos cenaturi." This famous quote suggests they should eat heartily because they would be dead by dinner time.</note> All obeyed him fearlessly. And since there was no hope of escape, he so inspired them that they would endure everything patiently before they would serve the Persians and lose their own liberty.

Likewise, **Horace** <note>The text attributes this to "Oratius" (Horace), but the story of the death of Mithridates/Demetrius is more common in historical works like those of Appian or Justin.</note> relates in his sixth book that **Demetrius**, King of Pontus and Armenia, when his own son besieged him and would not stop his assault, descended in sorrow to the lower part of his house and gave poison to all his wives, daughters, and concubines. Afterward, out of despair, he took the poison himself; but when his life did not end immediately, he offered himself to be slaughtered by one of the enemies entering through the wall, rather than be subjected to another and lose his own free will.

Likewise, **Orosius** <term>Orosius</term> <note>Paulus Orosius (c. 375–418 AD) was a Christian historian and student of St. Augustine.</note> relates that when the people of **Numantia** <note>The OCR says "mantuani" (people of Mantua), but the historical event described—a mass suicide during a Roman siege—refers to the city of Numantia in Spain.</note> were besieged by **Scipio Africanus** and the Romans and saw they could not resist being captured, they closed the gates of their city so the Romans would not enjoy their wealth. They burned themselves, all their possessions, and their city in a fire before they would surrender their liberty to the enemy.

It is read in the **Scholastic History** <term>Historia Scholastica</term> <note>A popular 12th-century biblical history by Peter Comestor that combined scripture with secular history and legend.</note> that in the time of **Abraham**, a certain man from Babylon named **Belus** invaded part of Syria and died immediately. But his wife **Semiramis**, out of a lust for ruling, married her own son named **Ninus** so that she might continue to reign. Ninus later captured all of Syria and built a great city, a three-day journey in size, which was named **Nineveh** after himself. He also had a son by his own mother named **Babylon**, who further expanded the city of Babylon.

### ¶ Concerning the Hen and the Dove arguing as they go to the Eagle. Chapter 60.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration showing an eagle on the left side, perched and facing right. In the center and to the right are a hen and a dove with several smaller chicks on the ground between them. A stylized tree and some small flowers are depicted on the right side against a simple horizon line.</image-desc>

A hen and a dove made a nest together and lived there. But they quarreled frequently because one of them sang too much, while the other moaned too much. While the dove watched her children being slaughtered <note>This likely refers to the common practice of culling young pigeons (squabs) for food.</note>, she became very dejected and moaned. The hen, however, when she produced an egg, rejoiced greatly and sang. Therefore, the dove was troubled, saying: "You...

<summary>This page uses historical examples of the Spartans, Demetrius, and the Numantines to emphasize the supreme value of liberty over life. It then introduces a fable about the conflicting temperaments of a hen and a dove.</summary>
<keywords>liberty, Leonidas, Xerxes, Numantia, Scipio Africanus, Semiramis, Nineveh, Nineveh, fables, hen and dove</keywords>

--- Page 73 ---
<meta>This page continues a dialogue between two birds (a hen and a dove) regarding their different reactions to the loss of their young, transitioning into a discussion on the physiological effects of joy and sorrow, and concluding with the start of a new fable about a fox.</meta>

"...Hen, you do not wish to be with my sorrow when you look upon my children being slaughtered." The Hen, however, argued against this. "You rather do not wish to sing with me; from my vitals I produce an egg and thus free it from such a danger to my offspring." <note>original: "de visceribus educo ouum a taliqꝫ periculo liberoꝝ." The hen argues that her constant egg-laying is a different kind of labor or escape from the grief the dove feels for her lost chicks.</note> For this reason they argued together. Therefore, they went with one mind to the eagle, saying: "Judge us, O queen, if it is fitting for us to live and dwell together." The eagle then delivered her sentence publicly, saying: "Let the joyful stand with the cheerful, and the sad with those in tribulation. For it is not fitting for the cheerful and the merry to walk with those who are placed in sadness. Nor should the bitter and the sad joke with the joyful. But as the Apostle says: 'Rejoice with those who rejoice and weep with those who weep.'" <note>original: "Gaudere cū gaudentibus & flete cum flentibus." A reference to Romans 12:15.</note>

But note that excessive joy sometimes kills suddenly, because then the heart is dilated too much and the heat passes to the exterior, and the heart fails, and so death intervenes. Regarding this, **Valerius** <term>Valerius Maximus</term> <note>A 1st-century Roman author often cited in the Middle Ages for moral examples.</note> relates that when the death of an absent son was announced to a certain woman, while she was mourning, the son suddenly arrived; seeing him, she rushed into his embrace and soon fell down dead. <note>original: "examinata corruit" — literally "fell out of breath" or expired.</note>

Sadness, however, does not kill quickly, because in sadness the heat is first drawn back to the interior, and such agitation of heat prevents nourishment in the limbs and works a consumption in them, and therefore through the process of time, wasting sickness <note>original: "ethica" — likely referencing 'hectic fever' or consumption (tuberculosis-like wasting).</note> follows. Proverbs 17 says: "A sorrowful spirit drieth the bones." <note>original: "spūs tristis exsiccat ossa."</note>

**Tully** <term>Cicero</term> <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman statesman and orator.</note> relates that when certain Roman consuls returned as victors, a triple honor was bestowed upon them. First, all the people met them with joy. Second, all the captives they had taken followed their chariot with hands joined. Third, the victor himself, dressed in the **tunic of Jupiter** <note>original: "tunica iouis" — the ceremonial dress of a Roman general during a triumph, associated with the king of the gods.</note>, sat in a chariot which four white horses led all the way to the **Capitol** <term>Capitolium</term>. 

Lest he should lift himself up too much because of this, they attributed this honor to him in three ways to keep him humble: first, that a man of servile condition was placed with him in the chariot, so that hope might be given to anyone of reaching such honor if their virtue deserved it. Second, that the slave would strike him with blows to the neck, saying: "Know thyself." <note>original: "Cognosce teipsū."</note> Third, on that day, it was permitted for anyone to hurl all the insults they wished against him. 

If, therefore, the pagans so tempered their vain joys, much more strongly ought we Christians to restrain them. Because, as **Gregory** <note>Pope Gregory the Great.</note> says: "Present joys will be followed by perpetual laments." And **Augustine** <note>St. Augustine of Hippo.</note> says: "Joy is vanity; with great expectation it is hoped for so that it might come, and when it has come, it cannot be held."

### ¶ Of the fox, the cock, and the capon. Chapter 61.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'G' containing a scene where a fox stands on its hind legs, looking up at a cock perched on a branch. The entire composition is enclosed in a circular frame resting on an ornate base with a vase and foliage.</image-desc>

A cock and a **capon** <term>capo</term> <note>A castrated rooster, usually raised for meat and noted for being more docile than a cock.</note> lived together in one courtyard, but the cock ruled over the hens. The capon, however, pecked about humbly among them. It also happened that a fox captured the cock and ate him, but he did not touch the crest or crown of his head; rather, he kept it unharmed and brought it to the capon, saying: "O brother capon, your companion has departed from this world, but because of the great affection I wish to have toward you, I have brought you the most beautiful crown of his head. Come down to me and I will crown you, and afterward you shall be the prince of the hens, just as he was." The capon, hearing this and desiring to rule over the hens...

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a rural scene with two foxes. On the left, a fox is shown pouncing on a rooster. To the right, another fox is walking away with a bird in its mouth. ...the hens went out from the henhouse <term>pullinario</term> <note>original: "pullinario"; a place where young fowl or chickens are kept.</note> and approached the fox. The fox, truly rejoicing, seized the one he had incited and slaughtered <term>iugulauit</term> him, saying: "One must not believe everyone, but one must beware of the deceitful." For the Savior commanded us to beware of such people, saying: "Beware of those who come to you in sheep's clothing, but inwardly are ravenous wolves." <note>A reference to the Gospel of Matthew 7:15.</note> Just as Aesop <term>Esopus</term> relates concerning the little crow <term>cornicula</term> who was holding a piece of meat in her mouth and was standing upon a tree. Seeing her, the wolf <term>lupus</term> <note>Interestingly, the text identifies the protagonist as a wolf, though in most versions of this Aesopic fable, it is a fox who deceives the crow.</note> said: "O, if only I could deceive her, I would have the meat she holds." For which reason he said to her: "Your beauty exceeds the beauty of all birds, and your strength exceeds the strength of all other birds; but I grieve because you have no voice, nor can you sing." Soon the crow <note>original: "cornix"</note> began to sing, and the meat fell from her mouth. The wolf, snatching it up, ate it and went away. For in this way it often happens to a person when he hears himself being praised. Whence a certain wise man said: "Let every man guard himself from his brother, and let him have no trust in any brother of his." And do not believe others more concerning yourself than you believe yourself.

## ¶ Of the pheasant and the peacock quarreling, and of the election before the eagle. Chapter 62.

---

<image-desc>A large decorative drop cap 'V' featuring intricate floral and foliate motifs, including winding stems, leaves, and various flowers contained within the rectangular frame of the letter.</image-desc>

The birds <note>original: "Vlucres," a variant of "Volucres."</note> in their division held an election <term>electione</term> and chose the pheasant <term>fasianum</term> and the peacock <term>pauonem</term>. But because of this election, they argued together and wasted their goods. For that reason, the birds ran to the eagle <term>aquila</term>, saying: "We have held an election, but you, as judge, give your confirmation so that the chosen ones may be approved." The eagle then summoned the chosen candidates, wishing to examine the election. But the pheasant magnified himself greatly, saying: "O just judge, as you can see, I am exceedingly delicate, beautiful, and variegated. My meat, seasoned with spices <term>aromatizata</term> <note>original: "arrotizata"; likely a misspelling of "aromatizata," meaning fragrant or spiced, suggesting the bird's value as a delicacy.</note>, tastes and smells better than anything else; for which reason the leadership is fitting for me." The peacock, however, defended himself on the grounds of his virtues, saying: "No, Lord..."

--- Page 75 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative from the previous leaf, where a pheasant and a peacock were brought before the eagle to determine who was more worthy to rule. This page concludes their judgment and transitions into a series of moral reflections on the dangers of high office and the responsibilities of parents, citing Saint Gregory the Great and the Roman Emperor Augustus.</meta>

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration framed by a simple black border. It depicts three birds in a landscape with a low horizon line and textured ground. On the left stands a large eagle in profile, facing right. On the right is a peacock with a long, decorated tail trailing behind, facing left. Between them is a smaller bird, likely a pheasant, also facing left.</image-desc>

“It is just as the pheasant asserts, because I am more beautiful, large, and crested. My tail renders me elevated honor.” And saying this, the peacock raised his tail upward and boasted of himself. The eagle, truly understanding all these things, said to the peacock: “You, peacock, have disgraced yourself <note>original: "vituperasti"</note> when you raised your tail upward, because you showed us your ugly feet. For this reason, you are not worthy to rule <term>principari</term>.” Finally, he said to the pheasant: “But you are tearful and weak, and you do not know how to sing. Thus, indeed, because of the defect of your eyes, I deprive you of the title in the records.” Thus they both remained, saying: “He is not worthy to rule who seeks to be contentious.” For this, as we see, happens very often among those elected; because through the disputes of the election, men's vices are scrutinized, they are stripped of their reputation, and they are defamed. Therefore, it is not good to raise a dispute for the sake of the primacy of honor.

Because Saint Gregory <note>Saint Gregory the Great (c. 540–604 AD), a Pope and influential writer known for his works on pastoral care and morality.</note> says: “The desire for high rank is born from the boasting of the heart. And whoever desires primacy on earth shall find confusion in heaven.” For this ambition must be avoided with all one's strength because of the danger of presiding over others. Because Gregory says: “The higher the place the shepherd occupies, the greater the danger he is in.” For which reason ancient rulers <term>princeps</term> did not allow their sons to be appointed to office unless they were truly able to lead. 

As Helinand the historian <note>Helinand of Froidmont (c. 1160–1229), a medieval poet and chronicler whose works were popular sources for moral anecdotes.</note> relates concerning Belius <note>This likely refers to a specific historical anecdote regarding a Roman emperor, possibly Nerva or Trajan, though the name here appears as "Belyo."</note>. When he had been made Emperor from the rank of senator, and the Senate entreated him to name his son as Augustus Caesar alongside him, he said: “Ought it not to be enough that I myself have reigned unwillingly, since I did not deserve it? For leadership is not owed to blood, but to merits; and he who is born a king reigns uselessly.” 

Without doubt, he ignores parental affection who extinguishes his little ones by placing an unsupportable burden upon them. For this is to suffocate children, not to promote them according to their merits. For they must first be nourished and exercised in virtues. And when they have progressed in these, they may be proven to go before those whom they ought to lead in honor. They fulfilled this commandment in deed. Whence Ecclesiasticus 7 <note>A book of the Bible also known as the Wisdom of Sirach.</note> says: “Do not seek to become a judge unless you have the strength to break iniquity.” 

Whence in the sixth book of Hippocrates <note>The text attributes this to "ypolicrates," though the anecdote is famously found in Suetonius’s biography of Augustus.</note> it is said that although Octavian <note>Octavianus, better known as Augustus Caesar (r. 27 BC – 14 AD), the first Roman Emperor.</note> with his sons could have sufficed to earn great glory, he did not wish it if they could not sufficiently protect their own and others' interests through virtue. Therefore, he began to exercise them in military rank, in running, in jumping, in the practice of swimming, and in throwing stones by hand or with a sling. Truly, he instructed his daughters in wool-working <term>lanificium</term> <note>The traditional Roman virtue of spinning and weaving wool, symbolizing domestic industry and modesty.</note> so that if fortune, beyond hope, should cast them into extreme poverty, they might be able to sustain life through their craft. For they had not only the art but the habit of sewing and weaving, and of shaping and putting together garments. Thus the Church commanded.

<summary>The eagle rejects both the peacock and pheasant as rulers due to their vanity and weakness, leading to a moral discourse on the dangers of leadership and the importance of raising children with practical skills and virtue rather than hereditary privilege.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dispute mentioned at the end of the previous page. After the fox's deception of the poultry and the crow, the narrative shifts to a courtly trial among birds to determine who is fit to lead, transitioning from a lesson on flattery to a lesson on the burdens and requirements of leadership.</meta>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration contained within a rectangular frame depicts an eagle on the left, standing in profile and facing right. On the right, a peacock stands facing left, its long tail feathers trailing behind it. Beside the peacock is a smaller bird, possibly a peahen or pheasant. The ground is indicated by simple horizontal line hatching.</image-desc>

"It is exactly as the pheasant asserts, for I am more beautiful, great, and crested. My tail <note>original: "cauda"</note> grants me sublime honor." And saying this, he raised his tail upward and began to glory in himself. The Eagle, truly understanding all these things, said to the peacock: "You, peacock, have brought shame upon yourself when you raised your tail, because you have shown us your ugly feet <note>In medieval bestiaries, the peacock's ugly feet were often cited as a symbol of the "hidden" sins or lowly origins of the proud.</note>. For this reason, you are not worthy to rule." Finally, he said to the pheasant: "You, however, are tearful and weak, and you do not know how to sing. Therefore, because of the defect of your eyes, I deprive you in my writings." Thus, both remained as they were, saying: "He who seeks to be debated is not worthy to lead."

For this, as we see, happens very often in elections; because of the disputes over an election, men's vices are scrutinized, they are stripped bare, and they are defamed. Whence it is not good to engage in a dispute for the sake of the highest honor. For **Gregory** <note>Pope Gregory the Great, a foundational figure for medieval morality.</note> says: "The desire for high office is born from the boasting of the heart. And whoever desires the highest place on earth shall find confusion in the heavens." Indeed, the danger of presiding over others must be avoided with all one's strength, because **Gregory** says: "The higher the place a pastor holds, the greater the danger in which he moves." 

For this reason, the princes of old did not allow their sons to be appointed to office unless they were truly capable of leading. This is related by the historian **Helinand** <note>Helinand of Froidmont, a medieval chronicler and poet.</note> regarding Vellius <note>original: "belyo"; likely referring to a Roman figure mentioned in historical anecdotes.</note>. When he was made Emperor from the rank of Senator, and the Senate pleaded with him to name his son as 'Augustus Caesar' alongside him, he said: "Should it not suffice that I myself have reigned against my will, though I did not deserve it? For leadership is not owed to blood, but to merit; and he reigns uselessly who is merely born a king."

Undoubtedly, he does not know the proper affection of a parent who crushes his little ones by placing an unbearable weight upon them. For this is to suffocate one's children, not to promote them according to merit. They must first be nourished and exercised in virtues. And when they have made progress in these, let them be proven to surpass those whom they ought to exceed in honor. For they have fulfilled the precept by their work. Whence **Ecclesiasticus** 7 says: "Do not seek to become a judge unless you have the strength of virtue to break through iniquity." 

Whence it is said in the sixth book of the **Policraticus** <note>original: "ypolicrates"; a reference to the 'Policraticus' by John of Salisbury, a 12th-century treatise on government.</note> that although **Octavian** <note>The Emperor Augustus.</note> could have been satisfied with the great glory of his sons, he did not wish it if they could not sufficiently protect their own and others' concerns through virtue. Therefore, he began to train them in the military grade: in running, jumping, the practice of swimming, and in casting stones by hand or by sling. Truly, he instructed his daughters in wool-working <note>term: "lanificium"; the traditional Roman domestic art of spinning and weaving wool, held up as a model of female virtue.</note> so that if fortune should, against all hope, cast them into extreme poverty, they could sustain their lives through their skill. For they possessed not only the art but the habit of sewing and weaving, and of fashioning and assembling garments. Thus the Church commanded.

<summary>This page uses a fable of an eagle judging a peacock and pheasant to argue that leadership should be based on merit and virtue rather than pride or lineage. It cites historical examples, such as Augustus training his children in practical skills, to emphasize the necessity of preparation over entitlement.</summary>

# If you have sons, educate them, and so forth. If you have daughters: safeguard their bodies. <note>original: "Si tibi filij sunt erudi illos... Si tibi filie sunt: serua corpus illarum." This is a reference to the biblical book of Sirach (Ecclesiasticus) 7:23-24.</note>

## Of the Crow and the Fig-pecker: Chapter 62.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration framed by a black border. On the left side, three birds are depicted standing on a grassy or plain ground; two face to the right, while one in the middle faces left. On the right side, a single bird is perched upon a leafy bush or nest. Below this illustration, on the left side of the text block, is a large square decorative initial 'C' filled with intricate floral and foliate scrollwork.</image-desc>

**Papias** <note>An 11th-century lexicographer whose dictionary was a standard reference in the Middle Ages.</note> says the crow <note>original: "Cornix"</note> is a bird that is said to live up to a thousand years. Now, on a certain occasion, she was making her nest, because of which she became plucked of her feathers and thin while nourishing her eggs. Then the fig-pecker <gloss>that is, the "pope-fig" or beccafico</gloss> approached her and greatly rebuked her, nor would he leave her in peace. The crow, however, unable to leave her eggs, was greatly troubled, yet she kept all these things in her heart. But when the young had been led out, the crow began to recover and grow strong. But the fig-pecker returned and began to repeat his insulting words and to disparage her beauty. Indignant at this and desiring revenge, the crow plucked the fig-pecker entirely and cruelly disemboweled him, saying: "He who wishes to quarrel with others deserves to be torn apart."

For thus many, when they see someone depressed and placed in calamity, do not cease to scold and injure them. But when you see a man in calamity, [your mocking] is heavier than the infirmity itself to the one in distress. Even laughter at such a person is an injury. The ancient princes also felt compassion for the wretched and the distressed in their misfortunes. 

Whence **Valerius** <note>Valerius Maximus, a Roman author whose collection of "Memorable Deeds and Sayings" was a favorite source of moral examples for medieval writers.</note> relates in his fifth book that when **Caesar** beheld the head of **Pompey** <note>Pompey the Great, Caesar’s rival, who was murdered in Egypt.</note>, he shed pious tears. Likewise, he relates in the same place that when **Marcus Marcellus** <note>The text reads "Marcellinus," but refers to the Roman general who captured Syracuse in 212 BCE.</note> had captured the **Syracusans**, and was established in the citadel of that most opulent city, he could not restrain himself from weeping for the afflicted women deploring their fortune. In the same place he also tells of the clemency of Pompey toward the **King of Armenia** <note>Tigranes the Great.</note>, who had waged great wars against the Roman people. He did not suffer the defeated king to lie longer as a suppliant in his sight, but having restored him with kind words, he ordered the diadem which the king had cast aside to be replaced on his head, and restored him to his former rank; judging it to be equally beautiful to conquer kings and to make them. He tells a similar story of

<summary>A fable of a crow's revenge on a mocking fig-pecker serves as a warning against insulting those in distress, followed by historical examples of Roman clemency.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a series of moral exempla regarding virtuous leadership and social conduct. The previous page concluded a fable about a crow's revenge, leading into this historical anecdote about Roman clemency and subsequent fables regarding honesty and the limitations of nature.</meta>

...a certain consul named Paullus <note>Lucius Aemilius Paullus Macedonicus (c. 229–160 BCE), a Roman statesman and general known for his conquest of Macedonia.</note> who, when he heard that a certain captured king was being brought to him, went out to meet him. When the king wished to prostrate himself to beg for mercy, Paullus lifted him up with his right hand and looked upon him with hope. He then gave him the first seat in his council and did not judge him unworthy of the honor of his table. For if it is an excellent thing to conquer an enemy, it is no less praiseworthy to know how to show mercy to the unfortunate.

### ¶ Of the Night-Raven and the Lark. Chapter 64.

The <term>Nicticorax</term> <note>From the Greek *nyktikorax*, literally "night-raven." While often identified as a night heron or an owl, in medieval bestiaries it was a symbol of those who shun the light of spiritual truth.</note> is named from *nyctos*, which is night, and *corax*, which is raven; thus it is, as it were, a raven of the night. It is so called because it flies by night or because it keeps watch by night, as says Brito. <note>William Brito (c. 1204–1256), a Franciscan scholar and grammarian.</note> Isidore says in the twelfth book of the *Etymologies*: <note>Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636), whose encyclopedia was the standard reference for the Middle Ages.</note> The night-raven is itself nocturnal because it loves the night.

To this bird the lark <note>original: "lauda," a medieval variant of *alauda*.</note> went, saying: "Dearest sister, I earnestly pray that tomorrow at noon you might join me, because my lover desires to see me in the sun. If I am joined by you, I shall appear more beautiful." The night-raven promised to fulfill all these things, for she was ashamed <note>original: "verecundabatur," implying a bashfulness or social pressure to comply.</note> to refuse the request. When the day arrived and the sun shone brightly, the lark waited for what was promised. But the night-

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting two birds in profile. The larger bird on the left (the nicticorax) has detailed plumage patterns, while the smaller bird on the right (the lauda) faces it. They stand on a ground indicated by vertical and horizontal hatching.</image-desc>

raven, not daring to go out into the sun because she can see nothing in the day, did not come. Because of this, the troubled lark has always abhorred and persecuted her. For this reason, the night-owl does not dare to fly by day for fear of the lark. She flies by night and seeks food for herself.

No one ought to affirm that which they cannot perform. Therefore, we must be careful what we promise, lest it be impossible and we be found to be liars. If it happens that it does not please you to promise what a friend asks, do not promise it merely out of the shame of saying no. For the philosopher <note>Often a reference to Seneca or Aristotle in this context.</note> says: "Beware lest the shame of denying <note>original: "verecundia negandi"</note> bring upon you the necessity of lying." Indeed, a person is less deceived if they are refused quickly. Yet many, because of their generous spirit, are ashamed to deny what is asked. 

Just so, it is written of the liberality of the Emperor Titus <note>Roman Emperor (79–81 CE), famously described by Suetonius as "the delight of the human race."</note> in the *Gests of the Romans* <note>original: "gestis romanorum," a popular medieval collection of anecdotes and legends used for moral instruction.</note> that he decreed no one should be dismissed without hope who approached him for the sake of a request. And when his friends asked why he promised more than he could provide, he replied that it was not fitting for anyone to depart sad from the speech of a prince. Likewise, it is read in the *Gests of the Romans* that when his friends argued that he was more common <gloss>that is, accessible or humble</gloss> toward everyone than was fitting for an emperor—namely, by condescending to all—Trajan <note>Roman Emperor (98–117 CE), often paired with Titus as a model of the "Good Prince."</note> replied that he wished to be toward all people such as he would have wished the emperor to be toward him.

### ¶ Of the Wagtail and the Pheasant. Chapter 65.

<summary>This page discusses the virtues of mercy and honesty through the historical examples of Roman emperors and a fable about a night-raven who makes an impossible promise to a lark.</summary>
<keywords>mercy, clemency, Lucius Aemilius Paullus, night-raven, lark, honesty, Titus, Trajan, Gesta Romanorum, Isidore of Seville</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page continues the collection of moralizing fables and religious anecdotes. It follows the entry on the night-raven and the lark, shifting focus toward the theme of hypocrisy and the importance of self-reflection before criticizing others, drawing from both natural history fables and the "Lives of the Desert Fathers."</meta>

The <term>Wagtail</term> <note>original: "Cauda tremula," literally "shaking tail." In medieval bestiaries, the bird's constant motion was often used as a metaphor for restlessness or specific character traits.</note> is so named from its behavior, because it always has its tail in a tremor. This bird went to the pheasant, saying: "Why, you wretch, do you not wipe your eyes, but instead show them to be so tearful? The foulness of your eyes makes you an abomination." The pheasant, however, replied indignantly: "Are you not ashamed, you mad creature? For you are a tiny and dejected thing, having a trembling tail, and yet you wish to point out my faults. Go first and correct your own vices, and then you shall be my healer." The Wagtail, hearing this, blushed and retreated in confusion, saying: "One ought first to purge oneself if they wish to condemn another."

![Woodcut illustration of two birds. A larger bird stands on the left on a small patch of hatched ground, facing right. To its right, a smaller bird is perched on what appears to be a low fence or branch, looking back toward the larger bird.]

In this way, some people, not attending to their own vices, desire to rebuke the faults of others. And as <term>Brito</term> <note>William Brito (c. 1204–1256), a Franciscan scholar whose works were frequently cited for their etymological and moral definitions.</note> says: "Many know many things, yet they inspect others while abandoning themselves." To such people the Savior speaks in the Gospel of Luke, saying: "And why do you see the speck in your brother's eye, but do not consider the beam that is in your own eye? Or how can you say to your brother: 'Brother, let me cast out the speck from your eye,' when you yourself do not see the beam in your own eye? You hypocrite, first cast the beam out of your own eye, and then you will see clearly to remove the speck from your brother's eye." <note>Luke 6:41–42. The "beam" or "log" (trabem) refers to a large structural timber, emphasizing the magnitude of one's own faults compared to others'.</note> It is read in the *Lives of the Fathers* <note>original: "vitis patrum," the *Vitae Patrum*, a famous collection of the lives and sayings of the early Christian hermits who lived in the Egyptian desert.</note> that when a gathering was held in <term>Scetis</term>, <note>original: "syntbi," likely a scribal corruption of Scetis (Wadi El Natrun), the famous center of early Egyptian monasticism.</note> a brother spoke about another brother who was at fault and disparaged him. <term>Abbot Pastor</term> <note>Abba Poemen (c. 340–450 AD), one of the most famous Desert Fathers, known for his wisdom and refusal to judge others.</note> remained silent. And when he carried a sack full of sand behind him, and placed a small amount of the same sand in front of him, he was asked what this meant. He said that the great amount of sand was his own sins, which, being placed behind him, he did not care about; but the small amount of sand placed before his face was the sins of the brother whom he was judging. And he added: "It ought not to be so, brothers, but my own sins should be before me and I should think upon them." Hearing this, the fathers said: "Truly, this is the way of salvation."
