### ¶ Of the Nightingale, the Crow, and the Eagle ordering the Crow to be killed. Chapter 66.

![A circular woodcut ornament featuring a symmetrical, stylized floral or foliate design. It includes curling leaves and a central stalk topped with a cone-shaped bud or flower.]

There was once a great feast in which the eagle dined with the birds. On account of the meal, the eagle called to the <term>Nightingale</term> <note>original: "philomena," the Latin name for the nightingale, often associated with sweet and learned song.</note> saying: "Go, daughter, and break your voice and sing as you have been taught, so that our hearts may be consoled by it." The Nightingale, being obedient, began to sing so peacefully that all the birds listened with joy. Meanwhile, a crow passing by and observing this said to himself: "I too wish to sing with the nightingale; I have a great voice and I shall be heard from afar." He began to croak foully, and the nightingale then fell silent, not being able to listen to him singing so hideously. The birds indeed...

<summary>This page uses a fable of a wagtail and a pheasant, alongside a famous anecdote from the Desert Fathers, to argue against hypocrisy. It begins a new chapter about a singing contest between a nightingale and a crow at an eagle's feast.</summary>
<keywords>hypocrisy, self-reflection, Wagtail, Pheasant, Luke 6:41, Lives of the Fathers, Abba Poemen, Scetis, Nightingale, Crow, pride</keywords>

--- Page 81 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralized natural history from the previous page, moving from the wagtail’s hypocrisy to the crow’s ill-timed speech, eventually transitioning to Chapter 67 regarding the stork and the swallow.</meta>

![A woodcut illustration depicting four birds in a rural landscape. On the left, a large eagle-like bird stands over and attacks another bird on the ground. To the right, a crow-like bird stands watching, and on the far right, a much smaller bird (a swallow) stands near a patch of grass. The background shows a simple horizon with clouds.]

All were disturbed and abhorred him, because he brought shame upon their feast. Therefore the <term>eagle</term> <note>original: "aquila"</note> commanded him to withdraw or be silent. But he replied that he did not wish to hasten away with the birds, nor did he intend to remove himself for her sake. Again the eagle sent word to him that he should depart, because he was not heard with pleasure. The <term>crow</term> <note>original: "corvus"</note>, however, refusing to listen to the eagle, began to croak. For this reason, the eagle ordered him to be killed, saying: "It is foolish to be singers unless there are willing listeners." For thus it is foolish to speak where the listeners are weary of hearing. Therefore, <term>Ecclesiasticus</term> <note>Referring to the Book of Sirach (Ecclesiasticus) 32:4-6.</note> admonishes us: *Where there is no hearing, pour not out words.* And where there are elders, do not speak much, and in the midst of great men, do not presume to speak.

Indeed, just as the crow sang poorly, so did a certain ambassador of Athens in the presence of King <term>Philip</term> <note>Philip II of Macedon (382–336 BC), father of Alexander the Great.</note>, of whom <term>Seneca</term> tells in the third book of <term>On Anger</term> <note>original: "De ira," a famous Stoic treatise by Seneca the Younger on controlling the passions.</note>. When the ambassadors of the Athenians had come to him, and their embassy had been heard, Philip said kindly: "Tell me what I can do that would be pleasing to the Athenians." To which <term>Democrates</term>, one of the ambassadors, replied: "That you hang yourself, he said, would be pleasing to the Athenians." And when those standing around were indignant and wished to rush upon him, the king ordered him to be dismissed unharmed, saying to the other ambassadors: "Tell the Athenians that those who say such things are much <insert>more arrogant</insert> than those who listen to them patiently."

### ¶ On the stork and the swallow. Chapter 67.

![A large decorative woodcut initial "I" framed in a square border, featuring ornate foliate designs and stylized flowers.]

In a certain tower, a <term>stork</term> <note>original: "cyconia"</note> was making a nest on the outside at the top; a <term>swallow</term> <note>original: "yrundo"</note> was making one on the inside. But the swallow sang very often and rejoiced over her newly hatched chicks. The stork, however, was saddened by this. For when she wished to rest with her young, she was unable to do so because of the chattering of the swallow. Therefore, while the swallow was absent, the stork destroyed her nest and killed her chicks. When the swallow had returned to the nest, she was distressed beyond belief by the death of her children; but because she did not know who had done this, she did not take revenge.

<summary>This page concludes a lesson on the folly of unwelcome speech using the crow and King Philip of Macedon as examples, before beginning a new fable about a stork that kills a swallow's chicks due to their noise.</summary>
<keywords>Eagle, Crow, Seneca, Philip of Macedon, Democrates, Silence, Stork, Swallow, Chattering, Revenge</keywords>

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the previous discussion of King Philip and Seneca’s views on anger and speech to a series of fables concerning the stork and the swallow, followed by Aesop's tale of the eagle and the fox. It then begins Chapter 66 regarding the "Picard" bird.</meta>

A little while later, the **swallow** <term>swallow</term> <note>original: "yrundo"</note> recovered herself once more, built a nest, and produced offspring, in whom she rejoiced and chirped most loudly. The **stork** <term>stork</term> <note>original: "ciconia"</note>, however, being disturbed as before, began to cry out, saying: "Truly, I shall do to you as I have done before; I will cast down your chicks along with the nest. Because of your chirping, you do not allow me to rest with my own children." Hearing this and knowing that the stork had previously destroyed her young, the swallow desired with all her might to take revenge. Therefore, on a certain occasion while the stork was sleeping with her young, the swallow placed fire in her nest and burned her along with her children, saying: "He who sees himself avenged looks forward to being comforted." In this it appears that it is not permitted to harass or injure those who are lower than us, lest they secretly bring evil upon us. For a man often receives from another what he himself has done to others. Thus, **Seneca** <term>Seneca</term> <note>The Roman Stoic philosopher (c. 4 BC – 65 AD).</note> says: *Expect from another what you have done to others.* <note>original: "Ab alio expecta quod alijs feceris."</note> And **Colossians** <note>Referring to the biblical Epistle to the Colossians 3:25.</note> says: *He who does injury shall receive for that which he has done wickedly.* <note>original: "Qui iniuriam facit accipiet quod iniquē gessit."</note>

As **Aesop** <term>Aesop</term> <note>The legendary Greek fabulist.</note> relates, the **eagle** <term>eagle</term> <note>original: "aquila"</note> flew to a high mountain and found the children of the **fox** <term>fox</term> <note>original: "vulpis"</note>; she snatched them from their den and carried them to her nest so that she might eat them with her own young. When the fox arrived, she did not find her children and began to go down to the woods and cry out; she heard them wailing in the eagle's nest. The fox spoke sweetly and humbly to the eagle, asking that her children be returned. The eagle utterly refused to listen to her. Then the fox, becoming angry, found straw and dry vines; she surrounded the tree and brought a torch in her mouth. The abundance of smoke began to kill the eagle's chicks. Seeing this, the eagle humbled herself and descended; choosing the lesser of two evils, she was forced to return the fox's children. In this, man is taught that even if he is greater, he should fear to offend one smaller than himself, and should always choose the lesser between two evils. Thus the author says: *Let there be no one who strives to harm a smaller person, for a smaller person can indeed harm a greater one.*

### ¶ Regarding the Picard, the Lictus, and the Predator. Chapter 66.

**Hugutio** <term>Hugutio</term> <note>Hugutio of Pisa (d. 1210), a famous medieval grammarian and canon lawyer.</note> says the **Picard** <term>Picard</term> <note>original: "picardus"; likely a variant of "pica" (magpie) or a specific regional bird name.</note> is a certain bird. It is said to be named after *pige*, which means a depression, perhaps because it is small; it is numbered among the animals fit for eating. While this bird was being pursued in the air by a **predator** <term>bird of prey</term> <note>original: "rapax"</note>, it turned toward a **lictus** <term>lictus</term> <note>original: "alictū/lictus"; likely referring to a kite (milvus) or a similar large bird, as defined in medieval lexicons.</note> to escape, saying: "You are great, powerful, and merciful. Therefore, I flee to you for refuge, since I am small and powerless, having no other refuge nor any defense within myself. Protect me under the shadow of your wings and defend me from the fury of the predator." The *lictus*, as **Papias** <term>Papias</term> <note>An 11th-century lexicographer who wrote the "Elementarium Doctrinae Rudimentum."</note> says, is...

<summary>This page features fables about the dangers of harassing those who are smaller or weaker, illustrating how the small (a swallow and a fox) can take vengeance on the large (a stork and an eagle). It concludes by introducing Chapter 66 with a bird seeking sanctuary from a predator.</summary>
<keywords>fables, Aesop, Seneca, revenge, humility, stork, swallow, eagle, fox, justice, Hugutio, Papias</keywords>

--- Page 83 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing bird fables from the previous page, moving from the story of the eagle to historical anecdotes about the Roman Emperor Trajan’s justice, and then beginning Chapter 69 about the onocrotalus.</meta>

...a bird similar to an **eagle** <term>eagle</term> <note>original: "aquila"</note> but larger. Moved by pity, it responded: "Since you are a most humble and powerless bird, sit and rest with me; fear nothing as long as you are with me," saying: "The humble are to be protected and never driven away." In this, it appears that the powerless, the poor, and the humble are to be protected by those more powerful and helped in their needs, because this is mercy and humility. And as **Brito** <term>Brito</term> <note>Likely William the Breton (c. 1165–1225), a chronicler and poet.</note> says: *The virtue of humility is proven greater in the great, and in the more illustrious, it is proven clearer.* <note>original: "Virtus humilitatis maior in maioribus et in clarioribus clarior comprobatur."</note> **Seneca** <term>Seneca</term> indeed says: *He who can help one about to perish and does not, kills him.* <note>original: "Qui succurrere potest perituro: et non succurrit occidit."</note> For the ancient princes had affection toward their inferiors, as is clearly expressed in their deeds. Whence **Helinand** <term>Helinand</term> <note>Helinand of Froidmont (c. 1160–1229), a medieval poet and chronicler.</note> in the *Deeds of the Romans* <note>original: "gestis romanorū"</note> tells of **Trajan** <term>Trajan</term> <note>Roman Emperor (98–117 AD), famously regarded in the Middle Ages as a "virtuous pagan."</note> who, when he had hurriedly mounted up for war, a certain widow met him tearfully, saying: "I beseech you that you deign to avenge the blood of my innocent son who was killed." To whom Trajan said: "If I return safe, I will testify to the vengeance." The widow said: "And who will provide this for me if you perish in battle?" He responded: "The one who will rule after me." To whom the widow said: "And what will it profit you if another does justice for me?" At this, Trajan replied: "Certainly nothing." To whom the widow said: "Is it not better for you that you yourself do justice for me and thereby receive a reward, rather than passing this on to another?" Then Trajan, moved by pity, descended [from his horse] and there avenged the innocent blood. Another [story]: while a certain son of Trajan was riding through the city too lawlessly, he killed the son of a certain widow. When the widow tearfully explained this to Trajan, he gave his own son who had done this to the widow in place of her deceased son, and he endowed her magnificently.

->¶ On the **onocrotalus** and the **donkey**. Chapter 69.<-

The **onocrotalus** <term>onocrotalus</term> <note>original: "onocratulus". This refers to the pelican, though medieval bestiaries often derived its name from the Greek "onos" (donkey) due to its braying cry.</note>, Brito says, is a certain bird. And it is named from *onos* <note>Greek for "donkey".</note> which is a **donkey** <term>donkey</term> <note>original: "asinus"</note> because it bears the face of a donkey. This bird gives birth to eggs in the desert, builds a nest, and produces offspring. But when it could not find food for itself and its children in the desert, it came to the city and called out amically to a donkey, saying: "Brother, as you see, I am like you, because I bear a donkey-like face as you do. Therefore you ought to take care of me. Come then with me, do not be afraid." Then the innocent donkey, seeing the donkey-like face, went with her simply. The onocrotalus, however, led the little donkey to the marketplace [to find] food and provisions for herself and her children...

<summary>This page discusses the moral obligation of the powerful to protect the humble, illustrated by the famous medieval legend of Emperor Trajan's justice for a widow. It then introduces the onocrotalus (pelican), a bird which medieval lore claimed had the face of a donkey.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the moralizing fables and exempla, specifically focusing on the dangers of the world and the spiritual risks of leaving one's vocation. ...purchased [provisions] abundantly and loaded the **donkey** <term>donkey</term> <note>original: "asinum"</note> as much as it could carry, saying: "Go with me, brother, and let us carry food and nourishment for our children. I will satisfy you according to your desire." But the donkey traveled for a long time through the desert with the **onocrotalus** <term>onocrotalus</term> <note>original: "onocratulo"; a large water-bird, often identified as a pelican or a bittern. In medieval bestiaries, it is sometimes associated with the desert.</note>, yet at the end, with great labor, he reached the nest of the chicks. He deposited the burden and, receiving his payment, began his return journey. But because he was in the desert amidst snow and water, not knowing how to return, he perished in the desert, saying: "No one should pass through there if he does not know how to return." From which it is clear that no one ought to travel to remote places if he is ignorant of the return and does not know the way. And this is because of the various dangers found throughout the world. Because as it is said in the fifth chapter of **Proverbs** <term>Proverbs</term> <note>While the text cites Proverbs 5, the following quote "The whole world is set in wickedness" is actually from 1 John 5:19.</note>: *The whole world is set in wickedness.* <note>original: "Totus mundus positus est in maligno."</note> This means that wherever a man is, he is always in danger. Therefore, in **2 Corinthians 12** <term>2 Corinthians</term> <note>The text cites chapter 12, but the list of dangers is found in 2 Corinthians 11:26.</note>: *Dangers from rivers, dangers among false brothers.* <note>original: "Periculum fluminum periculuz ī falsis fratribus."</note> Therefore, as **Gregory** <term>Gregory</term> <note>St. Gregory the Great (c. 540–604), a Pope and influential theologian.</note> says: *We must always fear, since we are always in danger.* <note>original: "Semper timere debemus cū semper in periculo simus."</note>

As it is read in the **Conferences of the Fathers** <term>Conferences of the Fathers</term> <note>original: "collationibus patrum"; a famous collection of stories about the Desert Fathers by John Cassian (c. 360–435).</note> that a certain hermit served God most holily, and the **devil** <term>devil</term> <note>original: "dyabolus"</note> subtly sought his perdition. Therefore, he took the form of a **packhorse** <term>packhorse</term> <note>original: "roncini"</note> having on its haunch a **valise** <term>valise</term> <note>original: "valisiam"; a traveling bag or trunk.</note> full of gold, silver, and precious stones. And entering the cell of the hermit, it stood there. But he, seeing the horse, wondered what this might be. Finally, seeing that no one came to seek it, he began to touch and open the valise. In it, he found all worldly things—namely clothes, shoes, and an infinite amount of money. Overcome by temptation, he dressed himself splendidly and, opening [the bag], he mounted the horse and arrived with all the money at a city and entered an inn, drinking and eating very well. Indeed, the host, seeing his money, gave him his daughter as a wife, and he stayed with him in the house.

But the devil appeared to the host in the form of a man, saying: "What have you done? To whom have you given your daughter? He is a cleric and an **apostate** <term>apostate</term> <note>original: "apostata"; a person who has abandoned their religious vows or faith.</note>; by law, she cannot be his wife." Seeing this, [the host] subtly examined his head <note>Likely looking for a tonsure, the shaved patch on a monk's head that would mark him as a man of the Church.</note> and secretly killed him. When, however, he wished to open the valise, he found nothing but water and smoke. For this reason, **Ecclesiastes** <term>Ecclesiastes</term> <note>Eccl. 9:12</note> says: *Man knows not his end; but as a fish is caught by a hook and a bird is trapped in a snare, so the sinful man is caught while living in his sins.* <note>original: "Nescit homo finem suum sed sicut piscis capitur hamo 7 auis cōprehēditur laqueo. sic capitur homo peccator viuens in peccatis."</note> He lives in many dangers. In the great sea of **Marseille** <term>Marseille</term> <note>original: "marsilie"</note>, out of four ships passing through, barely one escapes. Out of many passing through dangers of this kind, however, barely a few escape. Whence in the *Lives of the Fathers*, **Abbot Theodore** <term>Abbot Theodore</term> <note>A 4th-century desert father and disciple of Pachomius.</note> said to a certain brother saying to him: "A certain brother..."

## ¶ Concerning the swan and the raven. Chapter 70.

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration depicting two swans and a crow. On the left, one swan is in a body of water with stylized waves; in the center, a second swan stands on the bank. On the right, a large crow stands on the ground, facing the swans. The background shows simple hills and grass.</image-desc>

The **swan** <term>swan</term> <note>original: "Cignus"</note> is an entirely white bird. The **raven** <term>raven</term> <note>original: "Coruus"; though the image description calls it a crow, the Latin "corvus" typically refers to the raven, often used in medieval lore as a symbol of the devil.</note>, by contrast, is an entirely black bird. And so the raven envied the swan because of its whiteness and its purity. Therefore, with all its effort, it tried to pollute and blacken the swan. And because it was not at all able to do this while the swan was awake, it shrewdly desired to act while it was sleeping. Hence, one night while the swan was sleeping, the wicked raven secretly entered its nest and with the darkest color polluted and blackened the whole swan. But when day arrived and the swan was awakened from sleep, seeing itself stained and polluted, it went as quickly as it could to a fountain and washed itself entirely there until it emerged whitened and purified, saying:

> He who wishes to be blessed, let him always be spotless. <note>original: "Qui vult fieri beatus semper sit immaculatus."</note>

The raven is the **devil** <term>devil</term> <note>original: "dyabolus"</note> who cannot bear to look upon the cleanliness and purity of God’s servants. Hence, with every effort, he strives to pollute them; but because he cannot do this while they are awake, he busies himself to perpetrate it while they sleep. Therefore, we must be watchful, as **Augustine** <term>Augustine</term> <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), one of the most influential Latin Church Fathers.</note> says: "The enemy watches and you sleep." And for this reason **Peter** <term>Peter</term> <note>The text refers to the First Epistle of Peter, chapter 5, verse 8.</note> says: *Beloved, be sober and watch in prayers, because your adversary the devil circles like a roaring lion seeking someone to devour; resist him, strong in faith.* <note>original: "Karissimi sobrij estote & vigilate in orationibus quia aduersarius vester dyabolus tanq̃ leo rugiens circuit querens quem deuoret. cui resistite fortes in fide"</note> Because of this, **Isidore** <term>Isidore</term> <note>Saint Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636 AD), a scholar famous for his encyclopedia, the Etymologies.</note> says: "Those whom the unclean spirits see and cannot overcome while they are awake, they attack fiercely while they are sleeping." Hence, in the **Lives of the Fathers** <term>Lives of the Fathers</term> <note>original: "Vitas Patrum"; a famous collection of hagiographic literature focusing on the desert hermits of Egypt.</note>, they mocked a certain brother in such a way that when the brothers were supposed to receive communion, while he slept, images of women appeared to him and polluted him <note>A reference to nocturnal emission, often treated in monastic literature as a spiritual trial or a demonic "illusion."</note>. When he consulted the fathers what should be done about this...

<summary>This page presents a moralizing fable where a raven (representing the devil) attempts to blacken a white swan (representing a pure soul) while it sleeps, emphasizing the need for spiritual vigilance. The text cites Saint Augustine, Saint Peter, and Isidore of Seville to warn that the devil attacks most fiercely when the faithful are caught off guard.</summary>
<keywords>swan, raven, devil, purity, spiritual vigilance, Saint Augustine, Saint Peter, Isidore of Seville, Lives of the Fathers, temptation</keywords>

Afterward, the devil did not deceive him anymore, manifesting that this was done so that he might draw him back from such a great good.

### ¶ Concerning the Wood-Hen and the Hens. Chapter 71.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a group of chickens. A large rooster with a prominent comb and tail feathers stands on the left. Beside and behind him, several hens and smaller chicks are clustered. One of the birds is pecking at a small, shallow bowl on the ground.</image-desc>

<image-desc>A large, ornate historiated initial 'O' set within a circular border. The interior of the initial is filled with intricate floral motifs and scrolling foliage. In the center of the design, two stylized bird-like creatures or griffins are depicted back-to-back within the vine work.</image-desc>

The **wood-hen** <term>wood-hen</term> <note>original: "Ornix id est gallina siluestris"; likely referring to a forest bird or partridge used here in a fable.</note>, seeing the eggs of a **peacock** <note>original: "pauonis"</note>, made a nest at the house of a certain rich man. When the chicks were hatched, they were very much delighted or beloved by the master, for which reason he fed the wood-hen excellently so that she might better nourish the chicks.

But the wood-hen was then bitter toward the other **hens** <note>original: "gallinis"</note> because of the favor of the master and the chicks, so that she did not permit them to eat or peck with her. She was unobservant and unconcerned about the future, but believed she would always remain in the same status, being supported by the master against everyone else and the others being driven away by him.

The hens, however, being embittered, kept silent, waiting for the time of vengeance. And when the chicks had grown, they left the wood-hen and returned to their peacock nature. Then the master did not support the wood-hen in the same way, but permitted her to peck with the others.

The hens, indeed not unmindful of the wood-hen's persecution, took their revenge as best they could, not permitting her to peck in peace with them. Then the wood-hen, coming to her senses, wept saying: "Alas, I have nourished and raised sons, but they have despised me." <note>This is a quote from the Book of Isaiah 1:2: "Filios enutrivi et exaltavi, ipsi autem spreverunt me."</note> But the hens did not cease from their blows, saying: "Let no one live in cruelty while in prosperity."

Let anyone keep this in mind when they are in high office or in prosperity. They should not trample on their inferiors or subjects, because times change through time. For all things have a season, and everything under heaven passes in its own space. As **Ecclesiastes** <note>original: "eccle.i."; citing the Book of Ecclesiastes 3:1</note> says: There is a time of prosperity and a time of adversity. Indeed, time turns like a **little wheel** <term>little wheel</term> <note>original: "rotule"; a reference to the medieval concept of the "Wheel of Fortune" (Rota Fortunae) which raises some up while casting others down.</note>. Others

<meta>catchword: Others</meta>

<summary>This page concludes a story about spiritual perseverance before detailing a fable about a wood-hen who becomes arrogant due to temporary favor, only to be humbled when her status changes. It serves as a moral warning against mistreating others during times of success.</summary>
<keywords>wood-hen, peacock, hens, pride, arrogance, Wheel of Fortune, Ecclesiastes, Isaiah, moral fable, prosperity</keywords>

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page continues the previous moral lesson on pride, specifically how those in high positions often look down on others, before transitioning into a new fable regarding the dangers of seeking protection from powerful predators.</meta>

...some rise, while others indeed fall. But those rising should not despise those falling. Because, as it says in **Ecclesiasticus 7** <note>original: "eccle. vij"</note>: "Do not mock a man in the bitterness of his soul." But as the same author says in chapter 11: "In the day of good things, do not be unmindful of evils." And this is because of the change of time and duties. For as **Isidore** <note>Isidore of Seville, a 7th-century scholar and encyclopedist</note> says: "Temporal power is fleeting and fragile." Tell me, where are the kings? Where are the princes? Where are the emperors? Where are the wealthy? Where are the powerful of the world? 

Certainly, the most arrogant and powerful king of the **Medes and Persians** <note>referring to Xerxes I</note>, who overturned mountains and leveled seas, when he looked down from a high place upon an infinite and innumerable multitude, he began to weep, for the reason that after a hundred years, not one of those he then beheld would still be alive. Another leader, when he went against the Greeks with a thousand armed knights, twelve hundred ships, and three thousand transport ships, seeing before him such an immense army and thinking of the change and brevity of times and life—to what end that whole army would come—is said to have been humbled and remarked: "They call me a king, a man so strong and great; but I confess myself to be dust and ashes." The **Duke of Lorraine** <note>original: "Dux lothoringie"</note>, when he was at death’s door, looking at his houses and castles, said: "O God, how these temporal things are to be despised! For I, who had so many castles and palaces and so many lodgings, now do not know where I must go or where I ought to be lodged."

### ¶ Concerning the Quail, the Lark, and the Hawk. Chapter 72.

<image-desc>A horizontal woodcut illustration showing a scene with several birds. On the left, a large bird of prey (accipiter) is depicted in flight, swooping down with talons extended toward another bird on the ground. To the right, four smaller birds (larks or quails) stand on the ground, observing the scene.</image-desc>

<column-break/>

<image-desc>A square woodcut containing a circular ornamental motif. The design consists of symmetrical, interlacing foliate vines and stylized leaves that grow from a central base and meet at a pinecone-like fruit at the top, all enclosed within a decorative circular border.</image-desc>

<column-break/>

The **quail** <term>quail</term> <note>original: "Qualea"</note> began to think to herself: "The **hawk** <term>hawk</term> <note>original: "ancipiter," a common medieval term for a bird of prey</note> is the most wicked devourer of my kind. But if I could have his peace and friendship, I would remain very much secure." Therefore, she called the **lark** <term>lark</term> <note>original: "lauda," likely a pun on the Latin "laus/laudare" meaning praise</note> to her, asking and saying: "You are worthy of being praised, for because of your excellence you are named a Lark. For that reason, I request of you that you go on my behalf to the hawk, greet him, and plead for me, saying: 'The quail, subject and obedient to you, desires to bind herself to you in friendship and to establish peace with you.' For this reason she sent me to you, so that I might bring back a pleasing response to her."

<summary>This page concludes a series of moral reflections on the fragility of earthly power using historical figures like Xerxes and the Duke of Lorraine, before introducing a fable about a quail attempting to negotiate a peace treaty with a hawk.</summary>
<keywords>humility, transience of life, Xerxes, Duke of Lorraine, fables, quail, lark, hawk, animal allegory</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing fables involving birds and animals. It concludes the story of the Hawk's betrayal of the Lark and Quail before moving into a series of anecdotes about social hierarchy and a new chapter on a predatory bird called the Ison.</meta>

by your courtly behavior. For praise proceeded simply, and he repeated these words to the **hawk** <note>original: "ancipitiri"</note>. The hawk, however, pretending to be disturbed by the praise, said: "This is a hard saying and heavy to hear <note>original: "Durus est hic sermo et grauis ad audiēdum," a common biblical phrasing found in the Gospel of John</note>. Nevertheless, I wish for her to come with you before me and speak for herself." The lark then returned to the **quail** <note>original: "qualeam"</note> and with joy announced the hawk’s words. Hearing this, the quail also rejoiced, and she set out with the lark to the hawk so that they might confirm peace and friendship. But as soon as the hawk saw them, he was seized by greed; he snatched them and ate them, saying: "No one ought to make himself equal with the great, but should keep his distance." For in this way, those who have great and powerful enemies stronger than themselves should not provoke them to friendship, lest they fall into their snares. Hence it says in **Ecclesiasticus** <note>original: "eccle." referring to the Book of Sirach 9:13</note>: "Keep far from the man who has the power to kill, for you will soon enter into the midst of snares." For it is said that a **little donkey** <note>original: "asellus"</note>, seeking some amusement in the woods, happened upon a **wild boar** <note>original: "aprum"</note> and dared to greet him, saying: "Greetings, my brother." Hearing this, the boar was moved by anger and thought to tear him apart with his tusks; however, he restrained himself, saying: "I would tear you apart, were it not that my noble tusk scorns such vile food as your flesh—but you are safe because of your own worthlessness." In this, man is taught that if he is foolish, he should not approach the wise, nor should the humble and poor approach the powerful and the wealthy with jokes and mockery. For the humble and the poor ought to be afraid and cautious everywhere because they are punished. The wealthy and the powerful, however, do not fear but are bold because they are not punished. For this reason, the **roosters** <note>original: "galli"</note> respond to the hawk: "Why is it that you fear men so much, with whom you were raised and nourished, while we are tame and return to their hands of our own accord? Tell us if you have ever seen twenty hawks together in one sight as you see of us? We flee from them because they punish us cruelly."

->¶ Concerning the predatory Ison. Chapter 74.<-

This bird in its youth committed many evils, snatching the chicks of others and disturbing both men and birds. At last, in its old age, it was struck by remorse and repented, wishing to restore what was stolen,

to do penance, and to labor in works of mercy and justice. But because it had not become accustomed to such things in its youth, it was unable to perform them in old age. Wherefore it reproached itself; but because it did not have a good beginning in youth, it could not have a good end in

<meta>This page continues the moralizing themes established in the previous section, moving from the dangers of social hierarchy and predatory behavior (the hawk and the quail) to the importance of developing virtuous habits in youth. It transitions from general ethical warnings to a specific fable about a water-bird named the Mergulus.</meta>

...about old age, saying: "He who does not first take up good things will not be wise in old age." Therefore, it is clear that it is good to gather good things in youth so they may be found in old age. For this is what the book of **Ecclesiasticus** <note>original: "eccle." referring to the Book of Sirach 25:5</note> says: "If you have gathered nothing in your youth, how will you find it in your old age?" Son, in your youth, restrain <note>original: "contēta," likely a form of "continere," meaning to hold back or guide</note> your soul, and if it should be wicked <note>original: "neq," short for "nequam"</note>, do not give it power. **The Philosopher** <note>original: "phs." referring to Aristotle, the primary authority on natural philosophy in the Middle Ages</note> says: "He who does not become accustomed to virtues while he is young does not know how to turn away from vices when he grows old." And regarding habit—because habit is a second nature—the philosopher says: "So great is the force of habit that what a person is accustomed to doing while awake, they sometimes do while sleeping; and what they are accustomed to doing while seeing, they do while blind."

Hence it is told of a certain **physician** <note>original: "medico"</note> to whom fourteen pounds were owed, to be paid over three years. When he was laboring at the end of his life and was urged to make his confession so that he might receive the **Eucharist** <note>The sacrament of Holy Communion, taken as "viaticum" by those near death</note>, nothing could be pulled out of him except: "Fourteen pounds over three years." And so, speaking thus, he died because of his bad habit.

Evil judges and lawyers usually act foolishly in their own business; even near death, their wisdom turns into folly. While one was being urged at his end to receive the Eucharist, he asked for a delay. When his friends would not grant it because of the danger of death, he **appealed from a manifest grievance** <note>original: "appellavit a manifesto gravamine," a formal legal maneuver used to stall a court ruling</note> because of the habit of making appeals which he had practiced.

### ¶ On the negligent diver-bird. Chapter 74

<image-desc>A large ornamental woodcut initial letter 'M' decorated with intricate foliate and floral patterns, set within a square frame.</image-desc>

**Isidore** <note>Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636 AD), whose "Etymologies" served as the primary medieval encyclopedia for the origins of words and nature</note> says that the **diver-bird** <note>original: "mergulus," a term generally used for cormorants or other diving waterfowl</note> is named from "diving" <note>original: "a mergendo"</note>. It is a bird that seeks food for itself by diving. This diver-bird, having become negligent, said within himself: "Wretch that I am, what am I doing? None among the birds seeks food for itself with as much labor as I do, by diving into the water. For daily I plunge myself into the waves and whirlpools so that I might find food, and perhaps one day I will perish in the waves. It is better that I go to the land and acquire food with the others; I shall not eat the **bread of labor and sorrow** <note>A biblical allusion to Psalm 127:2</note>, but I shall feast better without labor."

<summary>This page discusses the power of habit, illustrating through anecdotes how professional obsessions (like a doctor's fees or a lawyer's appeals) can distract from spiritual salvation at death. It then introduces a fable about a diver-bird (Mergulus) who decides to abandon its difficult natural labor for an easier life on land.</summary>
<keywords>habit (consuetudo), youth and old age, Aristotle, Isidore of Seville, Mergulus (diver-bird), deathbed confession, legal appeals, spiritual negligence</keywords>

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing themes from the previous section. While the previous page focused on the importance of forming good habits in youth to avoid vice in old age, this page uses a natural history fable about a pelican to transition into a discussion on the spiritual necessity of solitude and contemplation.</meta>

**P**ellicanus <note>original: "Pellicanus"; the Pelican was a common symbol in medieval bestiaries for both Christ and the contemplative life</note>, as **Papias** <note>original: "papias"; an 11th-century grammarian whose dictionary was a standard reference for medieval scholars</note> says, is a small bird that delights in solitude. On a certain occasion, a **duck** <note>original: "anneta"</note> and a **goose** <note>original: "anser"</note> held a great feast to which they invited all the domestic birds. However, so that the feast might be more noble, they wandered into the wilderness and brought the pelican along with them. 

When the dinner was over, all the birds implored the pelican to stay overnight with them among the people, so that he would not have to travel in such a vast wilderness and would not have to endure such great hardship. Indeed, the pelican, overcome by the pleas of the birds, stayed with them for a short while, and feasted splendidly with them. However, when he desired to pray and contemplate God, he was unable to do so because of the chattering <note>original: "garritum"</note> and the noise of the birds; nor could he live soberly, as he was accustomed to, because of the abundance of food and drink. Therefore, returning to his senses as quickly as he could, he returned to the wilderness, where he "fought the good fight, finished the race, and kept the faith" <note>A paraphrase of 2 Timothy 4:7</note>, and he preserved his devotion and peace in quietude, saying:

-> He who wishes to contemplate God ought to dwell alone. <-

So you too, servant of God, should always remain solitary when it is appropriate, so that your spirit may be exercised in divine readings, meditations, prayers, and contemplations with a quiet mind, and may continually delight in conversations with the Most High. 

Hear **Bernard** <note>original: "Bernardum"; referring to St. Bernard of Clairvaux, a 12th-century mystic and monk</note> saying: 

-> O holy soul, be alone so that you may preserve yourself for the Lord of all alone, whom you have chosen from among all others. <-

Flee from created things if you desire to have the Creator. Flee the world if you wish to be clean. For as **Augustine** <note>original: "Augustinus"; the influential 4th-century Church Father</note> says: 

-> If you wish to be clean <note>original: "mundus"</note>, let the world <note>original: "mundus"</note> no longer delight you. <- <note>This is a play on the Latin word "mundus," which can mean both "the world" and "clean" or "pure."</note>

In the *Lives of the Fathers* <note>original: "vitis patrum"; a famous collection of stories and sayings from the early Christian hermits of the Egyptian desert</note>, it is said that Abbot **Arsenius** <note>original: "arsenius"; a prominent 4th-century Desert Father</note> was once praying, "Lord, direct me to salvation." And a voice came to him saying: 

-> Flee men, be silent, and be still, and you shall be saved. <-

For he who sits in solitude and is still is snatched away from three dangers: namely, the dangers of hearing, speaking, and seeing. 

A certain solitary man went out into the desert, dressed only in a linen sack. And as he walked for three days...

While the previous page featured the story of the Pelican seeking the wilderness, this page concludes an anecdote about a desert hermit and transitions into Chapter 78, which uses the natural behavior of the turtledove as an allegory for faithfulness and mourning.</meta>

...he climbed up the rock and found beneath it green grass and a man grazing like a beast. Descending secretly, he startled him. But because he was naked and could not bear the smell of men, being distressed, he barely escaped from his hands by fleeing. The other indeed ran after him, shouting: "Wait for me! I follow you for God's sake." To whom he replied, "And I flee from you for God's sake." Then he <note>the pursuer</note> threw off the garment in which he was clothed and followed him. When the other saw this, he waited for him, saying: "Since you have cast away the material of the world <note>original: "materiam mundi"; referring to his physical clothing as a symbol of worldly attachment</note> from you, I have waited for you." To him he said: "Father, tell me the word by which I may be saved." He replied: "Flee from men and keep silence, and you shall be saved; for these are the primary things of salvation. For where there is a crowd, there is confusion." 

Hence **Seneca** <note>original: "seneca"; referring to a popular medieval paraphrase of Seneca’s 7th Epistle: "As often as I have been among men, I have returned less a man."</note> says: "As often as I was in a crowd, I returned troubled." **Jerome** <note>original: "Hieronimus in ten."; Jerome’s commentary on "Threnos" or Lamentations 3:28</note> says: "Blessed is the man who has given himself over to solitude and will be silent, for he shall raise himself above himself." /

### ¶ Concerning the Chaste Turtledove and Other Lustful Birds. Chapter 78.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting six birds in a landscape with tufts of grass. On the left, two birds stand upright, facing right. On the right, a cluster of four birds is gathered together; some appear to be sitting on the ground or a nest, while others stand over them.</image-desc>

<image-desc>A large ornamental initial letter 'T' decorated with elaborate foliate scrollwork and floral motifs.</image-desc>

**T**he **Turtledove** is the most chaste <term>chaste: in medieval bestiaries, the turtledove was a symbol of "monogamy" and spiritual fidelity, believed to never take a second mate</term> among other birds, for as long as she lives with her mate, she never loves another. And if her mate dies, she never receives another husband or companion thereafter. She always drinks muddy water <note>In medieval lore, the turtledove avoids clear water so she does not see her own reflection and forget her grief</note>, and never rests on a green branch. 

Now there was a certain widowed turtledove, because of which, being distressed, she never wished to be comforted or seen, but led her life most chastely in sorrow. However, the other birds pitied her and saw her failing in her grief. Therefore, many gathered to her, saying: "Sister, why do you wish to waste away from grief? Come with us, and you will be somewhat comforted by us, because we most willingly desire to support you and dwell together..."

<summary>This page concludes a lesson on the necessity of silence and solitude for salvation before beginning a natural history allegory about the turtledove's legendary faithfulness and mourning for its lost mate.</summary>
<keywords>solitude, silence, Seneca, Jerome, desert fathers, turtledove, chastity, mourning, bestiary, animal allegory</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing allegories from the previous section. While the previous page focused on the virtue of solitude through the examples of desert hermits and the warnings of Seneca and Jerome, this page concludes the story of the turtledove as a model of chastity. It transitions into Chapter 79, which uses the partridge and the eagle to discuss justice and theft.</meta>

The turtledove, however, hearing this and having been moved by a certain spirit <note>original: "aliq̃tulum aspirata"; literally "somewhat inspired"</note>, proceeded toward them charitably. Indeed, all the birds received her in a friendly manner and, carrying her back to their nest, they shared their own things with her. But the turtledove, armed with the zeal for chastity, sensed that they were committing fornication together. Unable to bear the stench of the harlot <note>The author uses "stench" metaphorically to describe the moral repulsion the turtledove feels toward the other birds' behavior</note>, she fled as quickly as possible and did not wish to enter that place any longer. Because she did this often, she eventually left the company of those who were lewd and returned to the purity of chastity, saying: "I wish to be modest and clean, never slippery <note>original: "lubricunda"; meaning morally unstable or prone to lust</note>." 

For those who are bound to observe chastity ought to act in this way; they must not only flee the harlot but also the places where harlots dwell, on account of the abomination and the stenches of lust <note>original: "luxurie"; in this medieval context, "luxury" refers specifically to the sin of lust or excess</note>. For nothing is more foul-smelling and abominable than the stench of pollution and desire. Wherefore a certain strong scholar created a stench in his house, and a certain archdeacon sensed it; it clung to him so much that he himself also fell into fornication, as **Brito** <note>likely a reference to the chronicler William the Breton</note> records. The appetite for lust is full of anxiety, the act is abominable and filthy, and the outcome is poverty and shame. 

**Ambrose** <note>St. Ambrose (c. 340–397), an influential Bishop of Milan</note> tells in the third book of *On Virginity* about a mother and her daughters who, embracing each other as if leading a dance, threw themselves into the riverbed so that they would not suffer the disgrace of losing their chastity among the citizens. **Augustine** <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430), author of *The City of God*</note> reports a similar story in *The City of God* regarding **Lucretia**, who, when she had been violated by the son of King Tarquin, moved her family to seek vengeance. At last, sick and impatient because of the crime committed against her, she killed herself. And although this should not have been done, **Augustine** says: "No one ought to kill themselves to avoid the lust of another." For it does not pollute a person if it is another's lust, provided the pure intention remains, through which the body deserved to be sanctified; the violence of another's lust does not take away the holiness of the body itself, which the perseverance of its own continence preserves. Nevertheless, the hatred of vanity, the love of honesty, and the perseverance of fortitude in such matters are to be commended. 

Regarding the continence of a chaste woman, the third book of *The Trifles of Philosophers* <note>original: "de nugis philoꝝ"; referring to the *Policraticus* by John of Salisbury</note> also tells a story: when her husband, named **Duellius** <note>original: "dantele"; likely a scribal variation of the name Duellius</note>, was reproached because he had a foul defect of the mouth—that is, he had bad breath—he complained to his wife that she had not warned him to seek a medical remedy for it. She replied, "I would have done so," she said, "had I not believed that all men's mouths smelled that way." It was very likely, then, that she had never placed her mouth to the mouth of any other man.

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of the turtledove’s chastity on the previous page to Chapter 79, which uses the partridge as an allegory for theft and hypocrisy. It then moves into Chapter 80, introducing the story of the magpie.</meta>

l iii <note>This is a signature mark, used by early printers to keep the pages in the correct order for binding.</note>

<image-desc>A large decorative historiated initial 'P' at the top left of the page, featuring complex woodcut patterns of foliage and intertwining vines within the frame of the letter.</image-desc>

**The partridge** <note>original: "Perdix"</note> is so named from the words "to lose" or "to destroy" <note>original: "a perdo perdis"</note>, because it willingly loses and steals the eggs of others. Now, a certain partridge stole the eggs of another while she was away. But when the victim returned to her nest and did not find her eggs, she grieved; anxious for her many eggs, she searched for them. At last, with great effort, she found both the eggs and the thief. She quickly reported this to the judge. The judge summoned the thief and examined the business with subtlety. But the thief would not confess, denying the charges with all his might. And because the judge had no witnesses, he could not give a legal judgment.

The accuser then said to the judge, "You ought to torture him and wring the truth out." The judge replied, "You too must prove what you say, so that my judgment may appear just. Otherwise, I will torment <note>original: "martyrisabo"; literally "to make a martyr," here meaning to subject to trial by pain</note> you along with him." Since she could not prove what she accused, the judge began to grow suspicious; therefore, he raised them both upon the rack <note>original: "eculeo"; a Roman-style wooden horse used for torture</note> and began to question them about the theft. Then the thief made everything public. Even the one who had brought the accusation revealed many thefts that she herself had secretly committed. Wherefore the judge had them both hanged, saying: "He who steals and accuses, falsely excuses himself."

Therefore, ask of yourself in your own soul who you are that wishes to accuse another—whether you might be blamed for a similar word or deed. Hence **Paul** says to the Romans: *You are inexcusable, O man, whoever you are who judges, for in whatever you judge another you condemn yourself; for you who judge practice the same things. Why do you teach another and not teach yourself? You preach against stealing, yet you steal. You preach against adultery, yet you commit adultery.* <note>original: Romans 2:1, 21-22. The author is paraphrasing the Vulgate Bible to warn against hypocrisy.</note> Hence **Cato** says: *Do not do yourself the fault you are accustomed to blame. It is a disgrace for a teacher when his own fault refutes him.* <note>original: "Que culpa soles ea tu ne feceris ipse. Turpe est doctori cum culpa redarguit ipsum." These are famous maxims from the Distichs of Cato, a standard medieval schoolbook.</note>

When a certain tyrant judged that a particular thief should be hanged, a certain philosopher who was present saw this and laughed. When he was asked by the tyrant why he laughed, he replied, "Because big thieves are judging little ones." **Augustine**, in the fourth book of *The City of God*, introduces the example of **Alexander** and **Dionides** the pirate. When the pirate had been captured and was asked by Alexander why he infested the sea, he answered insultingly: "I do not do it to subject the world to myself; but because I do it with one small ship, I am called a thief. You, however, because you do it with a great fleet, are called an Emperor." If Alexander had been captured alone, he would be a thief; if the people served at Dionides' command, Dionides would be an emperor. "By the unfairness of my fortune, I am a thief; but intolerable and insatiable greed makes you a thief. If fortune were changed, I might perhaps become better, but you, being more fortunate, will be more wicked." Alexander, marvelling at the pirate’s constancy, said: "I shall see if you will be better. I will change your fortune so that what you do wrong from now on may be blamed on your character rather than your circumstances." And he had him enrolled in military service so that he could henceforth serve as a soldier within the safety of the law.

In this they rejoiced greatly, desiring also to chatter and to speak with a human voice; wherefore

to have the lamb that peasant is carrying?" And they said: "We do." And he positioned his companions
in different places where the peasant was going to come, saying that each of them should ask
the peasant if he wanted to sell that dog. And when the first one asked, the peasant replied
that it was not a dog but a lamb. But when others had asked similarly, at last he believed
that it was a dog. So also some people believe everything that is told to them, which
is a very great danger. Because of this, Ecclesiastes says: "Do not believe every word,
but in every deed, one must look at the possibility and the outcome." Just as Aesop
relates: The mice held a council to make a bell and place it on the cat's neck,
so that whenever the cat went about, the mice would hear the bell and hide themselves.
There was then among them one wiser who said: "Supposing that the bell is made,
which of you will put it on his neck?" And when no one was found who would want to put [it on]—

### ¶ Concerning the kite who deceived the chicks of a certain bird: Chapter 76.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration showing a rooster on the left, a larger bird (a kite or hawk) in the center, and several chicks on the right. The kite appears to be interacting with or protecting the chicks.</image-desc>

<image-desc>A large ornate circular woodcut initial "M" featuring symmetrical floral patterns, scrollwork, and a central vertical divider, serving as the first letter of the following paragraph.</image-desc>

A certain rapacious kite <note>A kite is a medium-sized bird of prey, often used in medieval fables to represent a deceptive or greedy person.</note> seized the chicks of a certain bird <note>original: "ornicis," a medieval Latin variation of the Greek *ornis*, meaning bird. Given the woodcut, this likely refers to a hen.</note> and ate them. Because of this, the bird, greatly inflamed with anger, never spared him, but pursued him with all her might.

A long time afterward, the kite, feeling remorse, sought forgiveness, asking many times—both by himself and through messengers—to be pardoned. But the bird, hardened in her heart, would not have mercy on him nor grant him pardon, saying: "I will never spare you while you are alive, nor will you be safe in my coffin <note>original: "archam," meaning a chest or sarcophagus</note> when I am dead." For in this way, many hardened people never grant forgiveness to one who asks it. Against such people the Savior says: "If you do not forgive everyone their sins, neither will your Father forgive you your sins" [Matthew 6:15]. Augustine says: "Each person is destined to receive such grace from God as they themselves have given to their neighbor."

Wherefore Ecclesiasticus says: "Forgive your neighbor who has harmed you, and then when you pray, your sins will be loosed" [Sirach 28:2]. For Augustine says that the wise man not only forgets injuries; indeed, he denies that he has received injuries at all. Just as Bernard says in his book on the constancy of the wise man, inquiring what the wise man would do when struck by blows <term>collaphis: a Latin term for a blow with the fist or a slap to the face</term>. He replied that he did as Cato <note>Cato the Younger, a Roman statesman famous for his stoic endurance and moral integrity.</note> did. When his face was struck, he neither listened to the offense nor sought vengeance; however, he did not merely forgive it, but denied that it had even been done to him. For this seemed more honorable to him than other responses.

Likewise, Seneca, in the third book of *On Anger and Society*, says that when he was walking through the city and was struck by a blow, he is reported to have said nothing more than that it was a nuisance that men did not know when they ought to go out with or without a helmet. Likewise, in the same book, it is told of the philosopher Diogenes that when he was arguing a case with Lentulus, and Lentulus—becoming agitated—gathered a thick spit and spat it into the middle of his forehead with all his might, Diogenes wiped his face and said: "I will affirm, O Lentulus, that those who deny you have a mouth <note>original: "os habere." This is a pun: Lentulus was literally using his mouth to spit, but Diogenes mocks his lack of oratorical "mouth" or skill.</note> are lying." Seneca reports that...

<summary>This page tells a fable of a kite seeking forgiveness from a bird whose chicks he ate, only to be refused. The text then provides a moral discourse on the importance of forgiveness, citing authorities like Augustine, Seneca, and Diogenes to advocate for Stoic patience and the denial of personal injury.</summary>
<keywords>kite, chicks, forgiveness, anger, Saint Augustine, Seneca, Diogenes, Cato, Stoicism, fables, Dialogus Creaturarum</keywords>

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with a quote from Saint Augustine regarding the necessity of forgiveness. This page continues the theme of patience and mercy through historical anecdotes before starting a new fable in Chapter 82.</meta>

Socrates had two wives who were exceedingly jealous and argumentative. One day, they attacked him and threw him to the ground. On another occasion, when one of them had thundered many threats at him, he ignored her and went to sit by the wall of the house. She then poured foul water <note>original: "imunda," referring to dirty wash-water or perhaps a chamber pot</note> over his head. But Socrates, not at all disturbed by this, began to wipe his head, saying: "I knew that after the thunder, rain would follow." <note>This anecdote is a classic piece of medieval folklore regarding the philosopher Socrates and his infamously difficult wife, Xanthippe. It suggests that a wise man should treat domestic strife with humor and patience.</note> 

When a certain person asked the Emperor Theodosius <note>Theodosius I (the Great), a Roman Emperor known for his Christian piety</note> why he endured his own injuries in such a way, listening to those who harmed him without putting them to death, he said: "We do this so that we might call the dead back to life—that is, bringing the impious back to virtue. For it is no great thing to kill the living; even foul and tiny animals like snakes, spiders, and the like can do that. But it is a great thing, and possible for God alone, to raise the dead."

### ¶ Concerning the owl who wished to be the lord of the birds: Chapter 82.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration contained within a rectangular frame. On the left stands a large, majestic eagle in profile, facing right. In the center, a group of approximately five smaller birds of varying species are clustered together. On the right, a horned owl (bubo) stands facing the group, characterized by its large, round eyes and prominent ear-tufts.</image-desc>

The owl <term>Bubo <gloss>specifically the Great Horned Owl or Eurasian Eagle-Owl</gloss></term>, also known as a *dugo* or *alocus* <note>These are medieval Latin terms for different species of owls</note>, is a nocturnal bird, most foul and despised among the winged creatures. From this comes the verse: "The owl is a foul bird; the toad is the foulest beast." <note>original: "Turpis auis bubo. turpiſſima beſtia buffo." In medieval symbolism, both owls and toads were often associated with darkness or sin.</note>

When all the birds were celebrating a gathering and, after dinner, were resting together peacefully and without hesitation, behold, the owl deceived himself <note>original: "se falsificauit," implying he harbored a delusion or misrepresented his own status</note>, saying: "I am trampled upon among the birds, and not honored as my nobility requires. But now I wish to exalt myself; I wish to rise up with my friends and kinsmen among the birds. I will slaughter all the great ones so that I may then stand as the leader and prince of birds."

Therefore, he called to himself the purple swamphen <note>original: "porphirionē"</note>, the night-raven <note>original: "nicticoracem"</note>, the bat <note>original: "veſpertilionem"</note>, and the screech owl <note>original: "ſuetam"</note>, as well as all the other nocturnal birds. And with them, he attacked the other birds with armed force, wishing to strike down the great ones and usurp the lordship of the city. But all the birds, awakened from sleep and seeing the traitors, rushed to arms. They captured them and led the defeated ones to the eagle, so that judgment might be pronounced upon them. The eagle, hearing this, [gave] her sentence...

<summary>This page presents anecdotes about the patience of Socrates and the mercy of Emperor Theodosius, followed by the beginning of a fable about a hubristic owl who attempts to overthrow the other birds.</summary>
<keywords>Socrates, Xanthippe, Emperor Theodosius, Owl (Bubo), Eagle, mercy, patience, hubris, medieval fables, nocturnal birds</keywords>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing tale of the owl from the previous page, shifting from the owl's failed ambition to broader lessons on the vanity of power, featuring historical figures like Alexander the Great and Saladin, before beginning a new fable about land and water birds.</meta>

...against traitors he brought forth a judgment that they should immediately be dragged through the city and afterwards be hanged on a gallows. Likewise, the entire species of owl <note>original: "bubonis"</note> was given over to perpetual persecution and harassment, and was avoided by other birds. This is the reason why birds pursue the owl and hiss at it. Therefore, in the daytime, it does not dare to appear among the winged creatures, but flying at night it seeks food for itself, saying: "He who falls perishes, and he who betrays falsely rises poorly." <note>original: "Male levat se qui cadit perit et qui false tradit." This is presented as the owl's mournful nocturnal cry.</note> For so some people in their cities manage their affairs, for they greatly desire to extinguish the powerful so that they might possess the dominion of the city. Let such people beware lest they undergo a similar penalty.

For it is said in Ecclesiasticus chapter 7: "Do not do evil things and they will not take hold of you; do not sin in the multitude of the city, nor thrust yourself into the crowd." <note>Ecclesiasticus 7:1-7. A deuterocanonical book of the Bible containing ethical teachings.</note> And in Proverbs: "He who thinks to do evil will be called a fool, but he who walks simply walks confidently." <note>Proverbs 10:9 and 24:8. The text emphasizes that integrity is safer than cunning ambition.</note> For the great ought to be content with their greatness and not violently usurp lordship. For "all power is brief," says Ecclesiasticus chapter 10. And just as one understands this brevity, one later understands the punishment. In the Book of Wisdom, chapter 5: "What did pride profit us, or what did the boasting of riches bring us? All things have passed away like a shadow." <note>Wisdom 5:8-9. This passage is a classic "memento mori," reminding readers that life and status are fleeting.</note>

Valerius <note>Valerius Maximus, a 1st-century Roman author who compiled "Memorable Deeds and Sayings."</note> narrates in his eighth book that when Alexander the Great had heard from Anaxarchus, his companion—on the authority of Demetrius, his primary teacher—that there were innumerable worlds, he said: "Alas, miserable me, who has not yet gained mastery even of one!" And yet after a little while, his grave was only five feet long. For as that poet excellently says: "Good man, why do you care for vile things, things that will perish? You will be of no profit, but food for future worms. No one stays at the summit for long, but quickly passes away. Any glory in the world is brief and light." <note>These rhyming Latin verses were common medieval mnemonic devices used to encourage humility.</note>

Therefore, Quintus Curtius <note>A Roman historian who wrote a famous biography of Alexander the Great.</note> reports that a certain man said to Alexander: "See to it that when you reach the summit you strive for, you do not fall from the very branches you have grasped." Saladin, the most noble king of the <term>Agarenes</term> <note>Agarenes: A medieval term for Muslims or Saracens, referring to the biblical Hagar.</note>, after he knew he would soon die, ordered the cloth of his shroud <note>original: "pannum sui sudarij"</note> to be taken and placed upon a lance like a banner, and to be proclaimed through the city that of his whole kingdom and of all his riches and treasures, he would carry away nothing except this cloth of his shroud.

<meta>This page continues a series of moral lessons and fables. The opening text concludes a fable where land birds and water birds confront each other over resources and gluttony, followed by a list of historical figures praised for their temperance.</meta>

...they have gathered, and they ascend to the land and devour our portions and take them away; and they have summoned them before them, saying: "For we are very much stripped of resources because of you. You eat at your pleasure in the waters, yet it is not enough for you; instead, after you are full, you ascend to the land and desire to swallow our food. Beware from now on lest such things be done, otherwise we will pierce you all through." To whom the water birds replied: "Sisters, you speak poorly. Never have we forbidden you to eat in the waters; rather, we implored you to come. We beseech you that you deign to feast with us on our food, for our hearts will be gladdened." 

The birds of the land, however, because of their gluttony—not knowing the food of the waters—flew with them into the waters upon hearing this. But because they did not know how to swim, they were endangered by the tides and the waves. Therefore, they cried out for mercy. The water birds, moved by piety and not returning evil for evil, led them back to the land by swimming. Therefore, having taken their leave of them, they feed at pleasure both in the waters and on the lands, saying: "The sober endure longer than the gluttonous, and even more than the envious." For in this way, many gluttonous people are saddened seeing others eat, thinking that what they need for themselves is lacking. But God made this world and those things which are in it. 

It is said in the Acts of the Apostles that he is the one who gives life and breath and all things. <note>Acts 17:25.</note> And for this reason Chrysostom <note>John Chrysostom, a prominent 4th-century Church Father known for his preaching.</note> says: "God provides carnal benefits to both the worthy and the unworthy," therefore we ought to be content with what God bestows. But as Bernard <note>Saint Bernard of Clairvaux, a 12th-century mystic and abbot.</note> says: "The most predatory bird is content with the air, the wolf with the land, the pike <note>original: "luctus", likely intended as "lucius", a predatory fish</note> with the water; but the human predator would despoil the land, the water, the air, and even heaven if he could for his own nourishment." 

For the ancient princes were not like this; rather, temperance of the throat <term>gule|gluttony</term> flourished in them. For it is read of Alexander <note>Alexander the Great.</note> that while walking on a journey, he was accustomed to eat bread with the ancients. The same is read of Scipio. And Cato is read to have been content with the wine which the rowers or serving sailors used. And of Hannibal it is read that he did not rest before night, and was accustomed to rise in the night, and devoted time to his dinner at twilight. In the same place it also tells of the temperance of a wonderful army under Marcus Scaurus <note>A Roman politician and general.</note>: that when there was a fruit-bearing tree, it was left with its fruits untouched on the day after the camp was moved. 

It is read in the *Gesta Romanorum* <note>original: "gestis romanorum", a medieval collection of anecdotes and moral tales used by preachers.</note> that Augustus Caesar was a man of very little food. He desired bread, tiny fish, buffalo cheese, and green figs. And he ate in whatever place and time his stomach desired. And not only did temperance of the throat flourish in men, but it was also found in women, so that they should not fall into any disgrace; for food, the belly, and the genitals are neighbors. Augustine and Seneca say: "A belly boiling with pure wine quickly foams over into lust <term>libidinem|sensual desire</term>."

---

### ¶ Concerning the rustic and the bees. Chapter LXXXVIII.

<image-desc>A large historiated initial 'R' composed of interlacing acanthus leaves and scrolls. Inside the upper bowl of the 'R', a small human face with a beard is nestled within the foliage.</image-desc>

A certain rustic <note>A peasant or simple country man.</note> kept many hives <term>foramina|literally "holes" or "openings"</term> of bees in his courtyard, from which he made much profit; however, he sometimes received stings from the bees when he carried out the honeycombs. On one occasion, however, having been stung, he was in pain, on account of which, being indignant, he began to threaten the bees, saying: "I promise God and you that if you sting me more, I will cast you down and drive you out." To whom the bees replied: "You wish well, rustic, to gather from the sweetness, but not to feel the pain. Bear the bitterer things in peace if you wish for the sweeter things; otherwise we will leave you, nor will you gain profit from us..."

<summary>This page uses a fable about birds and historical anecdotes of Roman leaders to argue against gluttony and for temperance, ending with the start of a new fable about a peasant and his bees.</summary>
<keywords>gluttony, temperance, Alexander the Great, Augustus Caesar, bees, fables, sobriety, greed, Saint Bernard, John Chrysostom</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing fables from the previous section, concluding a lesson on gluttony and temperance before transitioning into a new chapter regarding the nature of terrestrial animals, beginning with the lion and the eagle.</meta>

...you will be. But a mad peasant, while he was collecting a honeycomb, was stung by a certain bee. Because of this, he became furious and destroyed and devastated all the openings of the beehives. The bees, truly offended, quickly left him and did not return home. 

The peasant, however, collapsed into poverty—he who used to flow with delights—and recognizing the utility of the bees, he mourned his former state, saying: "He who wishes to have sweetness must endure hardships." <note>original: "Debet dura sustinere qui de dulci vult habere." A common medieval proverb emphasizing that rewards require sacrifice.</note> But many today are such that they wish to live perversely, seeking to rejoice with the world and yet reign with Christ. 

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a man in a tunic and boots standing beside three beehives (skeps). He is pointing with one hand towards a hive while several bees fly around him. The background is minimalist with simple ground textures.</image-desc>

For many seek Christ amidst delights. But as Job says in chapter 28: "It is not found in the land of the living." <note>Job 28:13. In a medieval context, this was interpreted to mean that spiritual wisdom is not found among those living for worldly pleasures.</note> Therefore Jerome <note>St. Jerome, the 4th-century scholar who translated the Bible into Latin.</note> says: "For those who wish to live according to the Gospel, their whole life will be a martyrdom." The Lord did not cast man out of paradise so that he might establish another paradise for himself here on earth. So says Bernard. <note>St. Bernard of Clairvaux, a 12th-century mystic and Cistercian abbot.</note>

Hence, in the *Lives of the Fathers*: <note>The *Vitae Patrum*, a famous collection of the lives and sayings of the early Christian hermits who lived in the Egyptian desert.</note> A certain brother asked Abbot Achilles, saying: "When I sit in my cell, I suffer from apathy." <term>accidia: a state of spiritual sloth or listlessness, considered one of the seven deadly sins.</term> To whom the Abbot replied: "It is because you have never seen the rest which we hope for, nor the torments which we fear. For if you were to look closely with your mind's eye—even if your cell were full of worms up to your neck—you would remain among them voluntarily and stay there without apathy." 

Also in the *Lives of the Fathers*: A brother asked a holy elder that he might rest from his heavy labor. The elder replied: "Believe me, O son: even Abraham will repent when he sees the great and illustrious gifts of God, because he did not struggle even more greatly." 

Likewise, some said to a certain hermit: "How are you content here, and why do you endure such great labor?" He replied: "The whole time of my labor which I endure here is not fit to be compared to a single day of the torments which are prepared for sinners in the future."
