## ¶ Here begins the order of the beasts of the earth. And first, concerning the lion who fought with the eagle. Chapter 85.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration showing a group of various animals and mythical beasts gathered together. On the far left is a griffin with large wings. In the center, a lion and an eagle are positioned as if in confrontation. To the right, several other animals are visible, including a spotted leopard or panther, a goat or ram with long curved horns, and other quadrupeds.</image-desc>

<summary>This page uses the fable of a foolish peasant and the sayings of the Desert Fathers to argue that spiritual rewards require earthly suffering. It then introduces a new section of the text focused on terrestrial animals, starting with a legendary battle between a lion and an eagle.</summary>
<keywords>fable, bees, asceticism, Desert Fathers, Job, St. Jerome, St. Bernard, accidia, spiritual sloth, lion, eagle, bestiary</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the sequence of moralized fables, moving from the previous lessons on gluttony and the wisdom of bees to a lesson on diplomacy and the virtue of peacemakers, illustrated through the figures of the Lion, the Eagle, and the Griffin.</meta>

The Lion, the king of beasts, was fighting fiercely with the Eagle, the king of the birds. He had all the animals with him and stood encamped upon the ground. The Eagle, however, fought against the beasts with the birds among the trees, using spears and arrows. A Griffin <note>A mythical creature with the body of a lion and the head and wings of an eagle, often used in medieval heraldry to represent dual natures.</note> passing by and seeing this was wonderfully displeased. Therefore, he positioned himself upon a hill and did not fly toward either side. 

The Lion, however, being afraid, said: "That creature has a dangerous enemy, and he is like a bird in his flight. If he joins the winged ones against me, I shall be defeated." The Eagle, thinking similarly, said: "This one has a tail and feet like the animals; I believe that if he stands with the beasts against me, I will not endure." Because of this, each sent messengers to him to clarify who he was and why he sat there. To them, the Griffin said: "I am both bird and animal, but I am neither Guelph nor Ghibelline <note>original: "nec guelfus nec gebellinus." These were the two primary opposing political factions in medieval Italy and Germany; their mention here is an anachronism typical of medieval storytelling to make the moral relevant to the contemporary audience.</note>. I sit here for the love of peace, and I love neither faction nor the sound of rumor." When he had said this, he was graciously received by all.

The Griffin then walked at his leisure with both the beasts and the birds, and because of this, no one suspected him. Meanwhile, the Griffin began to treat for peace, and since he was not under suspicion, the parties freely submitted to his arbitration. He then delivered his sentence: that they should all move from the field as quickly as possible and remain no longer in battle but in peace, saying: "He who wishes to possess peace must not take a side." <note>original: "Qui vult pacem possidere partē non debet tenere." A medieval proverb suggesting that neutrality is the key to successful mediation.</note> Thus, everyone ought to act among those who disagree so that they may become a child of Him who said in Matthew 5: "Blessed are the peacemakers" <note>Matthew 5:9; original: "bti pacifici."</note>—that is, those carrying peace between those in discord—"for they shall be called the children of God." And likewise: "Blessed are the feet that bring peace." <note>A variation of the biblical verse found in Isaiah 52:7 and Romans 10:15.</note> And on the contrary, just as the feet that bring peace are blessed, so the feet that bring discord are cursed. As Ecclesiasticus 28 says: "The whisperer and the double-tongued are cursed, for they have troubled many who were at peace." <note>original: "Susurrus vero et bilinguis maledictus est." Refers to Sirach 28:13.</note>

We read in the *Lives of the Fathers* <note>The *Vitae Patrum*, a famous medieval collection of stories about the early Christian hermits of Egypt.</note> that when there was a dispute between a certain Gentile and a Christian, and they came together armed with their relatives and friends, ready for battle, the Blessed Apollonius <note>Saint Apollonius of Hermopolis, a 4th-century desert monk.</note> asked them for peace. One man, however, who was the head of the discord and dissension—a turbulent and malignant man—hindered the peace, saying that peace would not be made until death. Then the holy man said: "Let it be as you say. For no one besides you shall perish today, but a fitting tomb shall be made for your bones in the bellies of beasts and birds." And so it happened; for he was killed immediately in the fight, remaining there as one trampled in the dust. In the morning, when his relatives came to take him and bury him, they found him devoured by vultures and dug up by beasts.

### ¶ Of the lion who married off two cubs and gave them good teaching. Chapter 88.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicts a man in a tunic and pointed hat stooping to grasp a lion cub by its hind leg and tail. To his right, two adult lions are present: one is crouching and looking back towards the man, while the other stands facing right, also looking back. The scene is framed by a simple black line border.</image-desc>

<vocab>Lion, eagle, griffin, peace, blessed are the peacemakers, Apollonius, Guelph, Ghibelline, Gesta Romanorum</vocab>

<summary>This page uses the fable of a war between the Lion and the Eagle to demonstrate how a neutral Griffin (representing a peacemaker) can resolve conflict. It concludes with a stern moral warning from the Lives of the Fathers regarding the fate of those who refuse to make peace.</summary>
<keywords>Griffin, Lion and Eagle, peacemaking, neutrality, Guelphs and Ghibellines, Saint Apollonius, medieval fables, Gesta Romanorum</keywords>

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page continues the sequence of moralized fables. The previous page concluded with a Griffin acting as a neutral mediator between the Lion (beasts) and the Eagle (birds). This page transitions into a series of parables about the Lion’s cubs to illustrate the virtues of obedience to authority and the dangers of pride.</meta>

m iij

<image-desc>A large ornate woodcut initial letter 'L' featuring intricate floral scrollwork, acanthus-like leaves, and a central lily-like flower.</image-desc>

The Lion married off his two sons, the cubs, and for each of them, he gave a great forest as a dowry. But before he sent them away from him, he warned them of three things, saying: "My little sons, keep these three rules and it shall be well with you. First, keep peace with your neighbors. Second, guard the forest that I give you so that the animals may multiply. Third, never fight against a man." The elder cub, keeping these commands, lived in prosperity. The younger, however, began to cause trouble within his own house; he had no peace at home, and because of this, he entered the forest in a rage, killed all the animals, and quickly stripped the woods bare.

One day, he visited his brother and, seeing him living in wealth, said: "I live in wretchedness, in poverty and turmoil; yet you exult in luxury and peace." To which the elder replied: "You did not keep our father's commands; therefore these evils have come upon you. But come with me into the forest and see how the ancestral goods are preserved." While they were walking through the forest together, behold, a certain hunter appeared who was stretching out snares to catch the animals. The younger cub said: "Do you not see that little man <note>original: "homunculum," a diminutive for man, suggesting the lion's disdain for human size compared to his own strength.</note> who desires to destroy you?" But the elder said: "Do you not know the command we have from our father, that we must not fight with a man?" The younger replied: "Wretch! Do you wish to lose your leonine nobility for the sake of fabled words? I shall tear him apart." When he had run toward the man, not paying attention to the snares, he fell into the nets and was entangled. The elder returned home, saying: "He who keeps the father's commands keeps himself safe." Therefore, it is clearly shown that it is safe to guard the commands of parents and to obey what is just. To the Ephesians: <note>original: "Ad ephe." refers to the Epistle to the Ephesians 6:1.</note> *Children, obey your parents in the Lord, for this is right.*

It is also told in a fable that a certain lion was lying in his den, and his strongest cub came to him. The father said: "Rejoice, son, because all the animals fear you. I tell you only one thing: do not fight with a man, because he is the strongest of all." Having been tempted, the cub went looking for a man. He found two oxen joined under a yoke and questioned them, saying: "Are you men?" And they answered: "No, but we are brought under the yoke by man." The cub, tempted even more, found a great iron-shod horse with a bridle and saddle, and said to him: "Are you the man I seek?" He replied: "No, but I am bridled by him." 

Wondering at this, the lion proceeded and found a man splitting wood, to whom he said: "Prepare yourself quickly, for I wish to battle with you." The man replied: "Let us divide this log first; afterward we shall fight." And saying this, he made a great split in the wood with an ax and taught the lion how to put his claws <term>claws: the text uses "griffes," which refers to the lion's talons, but also punningly connects to the Griffin in the next chapter</term> into the crack so that it might be split more quickly. When the lion had placed them there, the man pulled the wedge <note>original: "cuneum," the tool used to hold the split open.</note> out of the wood and trapped the lion's claws, crying out for the help of his neighbors. At his cry, everyone from the village came out with swords and clubs to kill the lion. The lion, seeing himself placed in the greatest danger, escaped only by leaving his claws behind in the crack of the wood; he returned to his father with bloody feet and great shame, confirming his father’s advice through experience.

¶ Concerning the tyrant Griffin. -> Chapter 77. <-

<meta>This page contains the conclusion of a fable regarding a Griffin acting as a neutral mediator between the Lion and the Eagle. It concludes with the heading for Chapter 88, "About the lion who married off two cubs." While the user provided a "previous page" translation concerning these cubs, the Latin text provided here actually contains the preceding story and the introductory heading for the cub narrative.</meta>

The Lion, king of the beasts, was fighting fiercely with the Eagle, the king of the birds. The Lion, having all the land animals with him, stood encamped upon the ground. The Eagle, however, fought against the beasts from the trees with the birds, using javelins and arrows. A Griffin, passing by and seeing this, was wonderfully displeased by it; therefore, he settled himself upon a hill and did not fly to join them.

The Lion, being afraid, said: "That creature has a dangerous nature; he is like a bird in flight. If he joins the winged ones against me, I shall be defeated." The Eagle, thinking similarly, said: "He has a tail and feet like the land animals. I believe that if he stands with the beasts against me, I will not endure."

Because of this, each side sent messengers to him to ask who he was and why he sat there. To them the Griffin replied: "I am both bird and beast, but I am neither a Guelph nor a Ghibelline <note>The Guelphs and Ghibellines were rival political factions in the Middle Ages; the author uses them here as a metaphor for any two opposing sides in a conflict.</note>. I sit here for the love of peace; I love neither side nor the noise of battle." When he had said this, he was graciously received by everyone. The Griffin then wandered at his pleasure among both the beasts and the birds, so that no one was suspicious of him.

During this time, the Griffin began to treat for peace. Since he was not suspected, the parties freely submitted their case to him for judgment. He delivered a sentence that everyone should move from the field as quickly as possible and remain no longer in fight, but in peace, saying: "He who wishes to possess peace must not take a side."

Thus, everyone ought to act among those who are quarreling so that he may be a child of Him who said in Matthew 5: "Blessed are the peacemakers" <note>original: "beati pacifici"</note>, that is, those who bring peace among those in discord, for they shall be called the children of God. And likewise: "Blessed are the feet that bring peace." On the contrary, just as the feet that bring peace are blessed, so the feet that bring discord are cursed. As it says in Ecclesiasticus 29: "The whisperer and the double-tongued is cursed, for he has troubled many who were at peace." <note>A "double-tongued" person is one who tells different stories to different people to incite conflict.</note>

It is read in the *Lives of the Fathers* <term>Lives of the Fathers: Also known as the "Vitas Patrum," this was a famous medieval collection of the lives and sayings of early Christian desert hermits.</term> that when there was a dispute between a certain Gentile and a Christian, they came together armed with their relatives and friends, ready for battle. Saint Apollonius begged them for peace. One man, however, who was the head of the discord and dissension—a turbulent and malignant man—hindered the peace, saying that peace would not be made until death.

Then the holy man said: "Let it be as you say. For no one but you shall perish today, but a fitting tomb for your goods shall be made for you in the bellies of beasts and birds." And so it was done; for he was immediately killed in the fight and remained there, trampled. In the morning, when his parents came to take him and bury him, they found him devoured by vultures and dug up by beasts.

**L**eo raised two cubs, his sons, and for each he gave a large forest as an inheritance <note>original: dote; literally "dowry," but here referring to a paternal portion or gift</note>. But before he sent them away from him, he warned them of three things, saying: "Little sons, keep these three things and it will be well with you. First, have peace with your neighbors. Second, guard the forest I give you so that the animals may multiply. Third, never fight with Man." The elder cub, keeping these rules, continued in prosperity. The younger one, however, began to disturb his own household; nor did he have peace at home. Because of this, he entered the forest with fury and slew all the animals, quickly despoiling the forest. One day he visited his brother and, seeing him overflowing with goods, said: "Unhappy me, I live in poverty and turmoil, but you exult in delights and peace." The elder said to him: "You did not keep our father's commands; therefore these evils have come upon you. But come with me into the forest and see how the paternal goods are preserved." As they traveled through the forest together, behold, a certain hunter appeared who was setting snares to catch animals. The younger said: "Do you not see that little man who desires to destroy you?" But the elder said: "Do you not know we have a command from our father not to fight with Man?" The younger said: "Wretched one, do you wish to lose your leonine nobility for the sake of fabled words? I shall tear him apart." When he had run toward the man, not noticing the snares, he fell into the nets and was ensnared. The elder returned, saying: "He who keeps his father's commands keeps himself safe." Therefore it is clearly evident that it is safe to keep the commands of parents and to obey what is just. To the Ephesians <note>A reference to the biblical verse Ephesians 6:1</note>: "Children, obey your parents in the Lord, for this is right."

It is told in another fable that a certain lion was lying in his den, and his very strong cub came to him. The father said: "Rejoice, son, because all animals fear you. I say only one thing to you: do not fight with Man, because he is the strongest of all." The tempted cub sought out the man. He found two oxen yoked together and asked them: "Are you men?" And they answered: "No, but we are subjugated by Man." The cub, even more tempted, found a large horse shod with iron, with a bridle and saddle, and said to it: "Are you the man I seek?" The horse answered: "No, but I am controlled by him." Wondering, the lion proceeded and found a man splitting wood, to whom he said: "Prepare yourself quickly, for I wish to battle with you." The man said: "Let us divide this log first; afterward we shall battle." Saying this, with an axe he made a great cleft in the wood and taught the lion to put his <term>claws (griffes)</term> into the cleft so it might be split more quickly. When the lion had placed them, the man pulled the wedge out of the wood and trapped the lion's claws, shouting for the help of his neighbors. At his shout, all from the village came out with swords and clubs to kill the lion. The lion, seeing himself in the greatest danger, left his claws in the cleft of the wood and barely escaped; with bleeding feet and great shame <note>original: confusione; implying both embarrassment and a state of being confounded</note> he returned to his father, confirming his advice through experience.

### ¶ About the tyrant griffin. Chapter 77.

<summary>This page recounts two fables: one where a lion cub is captured in a hunter's net after ignoring his father's advice, and another where a cub is outwitted and maimed by a woodcutter.</summary>
<keywords>lion, cubs, obedience, man vs beast, woodcutter trap, hunter, animal fables, moral education</keywords>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the story of the Lion and his cubs on the previous page to a new moral fable concerning a Griffin who becomes a king. It follows the structure of the "Dialogus creaturarum," a popular medieval collection of moralizing dialogues between animals and inanimate objects. The text continues the theme of leadership and the consequences of ignoring paternal or moral advice.</meta>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicts a griffin on the right facing a group of five birds on the left. The griffin has the head, wings, and talons of an eagle and the hindquarters of a lion. The birds on the left stand in a dense group, with one looking back towards the griffin.</image-desc>

**The Griffin**, as Isidore says in the twelfth book of the *Etymologies* <note>Isidore of Seville was a 7th-century scholar whose "Etymologies" served as an encyclopedia for the Middle Ages</note>, is a feathered bird and a quadruped. For this kind of wild beast is born in woody mountains. In every part of its body it has the likeness of a lion, but in its wings and face it is similar to an eagle. They are indeed vehemently hostile to horses and they tear living men apart.

Now, in a certain province, the griffins accepted one as king; but because of his tyranny and greed in the land, he issued these commands:
1. First, that no one should be received for the purpose of selling or bartering.
2. Second, that no one from other parts should come to his lands.
3. Third, that no one of his own people should cross over to other lands.

Keeping these three rules, he lived in luxuries <note>original: in delicijs; referring to a life of indulgence and pleasure</note>, by which he collected much and gave none of it away; but by the judgment of God, at a certain time, lightning devastated that whole province. The citizens, however, ran to the griffin shouting: "We are hungry, and we are dying of famine!"

He then sent ambassadors to neighboring nations so that they might sell some of their goods and bring them, promising they would receive whatever price they desired. They replied: "You never sold any of your goods to us, and so now we will sell nothing to you." 

Again, he directed other messengers so that people might pass through safely to sell their wares. To which they replied: "You never wished to receive us, nor will you receive us now." 

Finally, he sent to them asking that they would be willing to receive him and his people, and not leave them in calamity. But they said: "You never came to us; therefore, we will not receive you, and if you come, we will cast you out." 

Thus, abandoned by all, he perished miserably with his people, saying: "He who does not serve is not served, nor is he helped in times of trouble."

Therefore, it is good to be courtly <note>original: curiales; meaning refined, polite, or hospitable in the manner of a courtly society</note> and to receive foreigners, to offer hospitality, and to trade with others; to give of one's own to others and even to share. For the Savior says: **"All things, therefore, whatsoever you would that men should do to you, do you also to them. This is the law and the prophets."** <note>Matthew 7:12</note> And Basil says: "Be such to others as you greatly desire others to be toward you."

It is read that while Alexander <note>Alexander the Great</note> was traveling along a certain way and he with his army were almost failing from thirst, someone gave him a cup of water as a great gift, which he immediately ordered to be poured out. For he did not wish to drink alone since he could not share it with his soldiers. 

In Ecclesiasticus <note>Sirach 11:19</note>, the greedy man says the opposite: "I have found rest for myself, and I shall eat of my goods alone." For such greedy people are not to be promoted <note>original: perficiendi; literally "to be completed" or "perfected," but in a civic context, to be advanced to office</note>. Whence in ancient times, those pursuing greed were not promoted in the republic. As Valerius relates in the sixth book <note>Valerius Maximus, a Roman historian</note>: when two consuls were to be sent to Spain and a council was held concerning them, Scipio said: "Neither pleases me, because one..."

<summary>This page uses the mythical griffin and historical figures like Alexander the Great to illustrate a moral lesson against greed and isolationism. It argues that a leader who refuses to help or interact with others will find no help in their own time of need.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the moralizing discourse following the story of the Griffin king. The previous page ended with the Griffin, having hoarded wealth during a famine, finally sending ambassadors to neighbors for help. The text now transitions into a philosophical reflection on the nature of greed before beginning a new fable about a Leopard and a Unicorn.</meta>

...he has nothing. For the greedy, nothing is enough; he judges poverty and avarice to be equally evil in powerful men. Whence Valerius <note>Valerius Maximus, a 1st-century Roman author of "Memorable Deeds and Sayings"</note> relates that Tiberius Caesar rarely changed the governors of the provinces because new ones were always intent on robbery. He gives the **example of a certain wounded man**: when a multitude of flies had covered his wounds and someone tried to drive them away, he said, "You have done a bad thing. These flies were already full; if they leave, other hungry ones will come and afflict me even more." For judges, whenever they are poor or greedy, are always eager to snatch things away.

It is read in the fables of the poets that **King Midas** <note>original: "Migdas"</note> asked Apollo that whatever he touched might become gold, and it was granted to him. When he touched food or drink with his lips, it was immediately converted into gold. Thus, an abundance of riches makes the greedy man famished and leads him to his own destruction.

It is also read in the chronicles that **Queen Tomyris** <note>original: "Thamaris"; the Queen of the Massagetae who defeated Cyrus the Great</note>, when she had captured the King of the Persians, had his head cut off and plunged into a small jug full of blood, saying: "You thirsted for blood; now drink blood!" So it shall be said to the greedy and to tyrants in the future, who thirsted for the blood of the poor—that is, for their profit and their labors. The greedy shall drink liquefied gold in Hell. Regarding this, a certain philosopher relates that the **Emperor Nero** was seen in Hell bathing himself in molten gold; and when he saw a crowd of advocates <note>Lawyers or legal representatives, often criticized in medieval literature for their greed</note>, he said to them: "Come, you venal race of men, for I have reserved the best part for you!"

### ¶ On the little leopard and the unicorn who fought with the dragon. Chapter 78.

A **little leopard** was fighting with a dragon, but he did not prevail. For this reason, he ran to a **unicorn** and humbly beseeched him, saying: "You are eminent and virtuous and skilled in war; I beg you earnestly to defend me from the fury of the dragon." The unicorn began to exalt himself, and hearing such things said about him, he replied: "You speak the truth, for I am indeed skilled in battles; therefore, I shall defend you. Do not be afraid. When the dragon opens his mouth, I will pierce him through the throat with my horn." When they had come together to the dragon, the little leopard announced the battle, trusting in...

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page concludes the previous fable involving a unicorn and a dragon, transitioning from a warning against unnecessary intervention in the affairs of others to a new moral tale concerning an elephant and a lion. It maintains the theme of physical nature versus moral character.</meta>

...with the help of the **unicorn**. But the **Dragon** made himself terrifying toward them and sent forth fire and stenches from his mouth. But when he opened his mouth, the **unicorn** ran as quickly as possible, wishing to pierce him through the throat. The **Dragon**, however, shook his head, and the **unicorn** fixed his horn into the ground, saying as he died: "He who wishes to fight for another desires to slaughter himself." Thus it is indeed foolish to trust in oneself and to struggle over that which does not pertain to oneself. Whence Ecclesiastes <note>original: "eccle."; specifically Ecclesiasticus (Sirach) 11:9</note> says: "Do not strive in a matter that does not concern you."

Therefore, ask within your own mind what it is you wish to do. Whether that deed pertains to you or rather to another; you ought not to interfere. Do not fight for another, nor increase the discord among those who are quarreling. But do as Seneca <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca, the Roman Stoic philosopher</note> says: "Let disagreement always begin from the other, but reconciliation from you." 

Enemies attacked a certain man, but someone else came along wishing to defend him and armed himself against those enemies. They, however, said: "Friend, we do you no injury. Take what is yours and go, for we seek vengeance for our own affair." He, not yielding to their words, prepared himself for war against them. But they, being indignant, mutilated him along with his enemy.

->¶ Concerning the elephant who knelt before the lion. Chapter 89.<-

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a lion in the center standing on grass, facing an elephant on the right. Behind the lion on the left side of the frame are four spotted canine-like animals (possibly leopards or hyenas) with sharp teeth and pointed ears. The elephant has a long trunk curled toward the lion and visible tusks.</image-desc>

<image-desc>A large ornamental woodcut initial letter 'E' decorated with elaborate swirling foliage, flowers, and vine patterns within a circular border, which in turn is set within a square frame.</image-desc>

Although the **Elephant** is the most renowned and famous among the other beasts, he nonetheless cannot kneel, because he has no knees. <note>This reflects a common medieval belief derived from ancient writers like Aristotle and Pliny, who claimed elephants had no joints in their legs and had to sleep leaning against trees.</note> On a certain occasion, as the **lion** was going through the forest, when all the beasts were passing by, they all bent their knees to him as the king of beasts. The **elephant** did not bend his knee because he was unable to do so. Whence certain beasts gathered in judgment before the **lion** and defamed the **elephant**. The **lion**, however, approached the **elephant**, saying: "Why are you so hard and bitter that you do not bend your knees before me like the others?" To whom the **elephant** replied: "Lord, with all my power I love and honor you, but I cannot bend myself, because I have no knees." But the **lion** [replied], "If you..."

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page concludes the story of the elephant and the lion from Chapter 89 (though the text later jumps to Chapter 85) and provides a series of moral "exempla" or historical anecdotes regarding the necessity of impartial and evidence-based justice. It continues the previous page’s discussion of moral character and societal conduct.</meta>

“...you do not reject in your heart, and you greet me excellently. It is enough to be able to honor the lord who labors for his own.” Wherefore he condemned the accusers and exalted the **elephant**, saying: “No one ought to be judged before they are proven guilty.” <note>original: "Nullus iudicari debet anteq̃ probari"</note> For judges ought to pay attention in this way; they should not judge according to rumors <note>or "diffamationes," referring to public scandal or hearsay</note> but according to the truth of the matter. Because it is not the one who is accused, but the one who is found guilty, who is the criminal. 

Whence **Isidore** <note>Isidore of Seville, a 7th-century scholar and bishop</note> says: “First prove, then judge.” This is as **Valerius** <note>Valerius Maximus, a 1st-century Roman historian who collected famous anecdotes</note> relates concerning the husband **Torquatus** <note>Titus Manlius Torquatus, a Roman consul famous for his severity</note>. When his own son had been accused and convicted, he spoke this sentence over him: “Since it has been proven to me that my son, while serving as a counselor, accepted money from our allies, I judge him unworthy of my house.” And so, having been thus judged, the son consumed himself by hanging out of sadness. 

For a judge ought to act in this way. He must not pervert justice on account of love or hatred. Because **Bernard** <note>St. Bernard of Clairvaux</note> says: “Love and hatred do not know the judgment of truth.” Therefore, Valerius narrates in his sixth book that when **Zaleucus** <note>original: "zalericus"; a celebrated Greek lawgiver</note> was in the city founded by himself—which was fortified by most wholesome laws, among which was a law established that anyone caught in the crime of adultery should be deprived of both eyes—it happened that his own son was caught in such a crime. Though the entire city pleaded for him, he resisted for some time. At last, overcome by the prayers of the people, he had one of his son's eyes plucked out and one of his own, leaving the use of sight to both. Thus he rendered the debt of punishment to equity with a marvelous temperament, and divided himself between being a merciful father and a lawgiver. 

Likewise, he tells of **Carneides of Tyre** <note>likely a medieval misattribution of Charondas, a Sicilian lawgiver</note> who had given a law that if anyone entered the assembly while armed with iron, he should be killed immediately. After some time had passed, when he himself was returning home from the distant countryside and proceeded with a sword girded to his side, he was warned about the breaking of the law by someone who was near him. Straightway, drawing the iron sword which he had, he fell upon it. He did not wish to conceal his fault or defend his error; he preferred to suffer the penalty rather than break the law.

Likewise, **Valerius** narrates in his sixth book that when a certain man had judged wickedly, **King Cambyses** <note>Cambyses II, King of Persia</note> had the skin stripped from his body and placed it upon the judge's seat, and he made the man's son sit in it as he was to judge thereafter. For he provided this new punishment so that no judge might be able to be corrupted afterward. So also the Divine Law commands in **Deuteronomy 16**: “Appoint judges in all your gates so that they may judge the people with a just judgment, and do not let them decline toward either side.” <note>Deuteronomy 16:18</note>

<meta>This page follows a discussion on the impartiality of judges (referencing Zaleucus). It shifts here to a series of moral anecdotes, or "exempla," focusing on domestic life, specifically the medieval virtues and vices associated with marriage and the behavior of wives. The transition from legal justice to domestic "order" was a common theme in moralizing literature of this period.</meta>

<sig>ij</sig>
<meta>Catchword: per/</meta>

**The Satyr** is a monstrous animal distinguished by the likeness of a man above, but having the form of a goat below. According to the error of the pagans, he is declared to be the god of the forests. This creature took to wife the daughter of a **centaur** <note>original: "ypocentauri"; a mythical creature with the upper body of a human and the lower body of a horse</note>, who is a being mixed of man and horse, powerful and virtuous. 

And when he was to lie with his wife, he first began to warn her of three things, saying: "Never lie, never curse me, and do not presume to violate my bed. If you observe these three things, you may spend the night with me in peace." A little later, the satyr wished to test her to see if she would remain obedient, and he said: "O wife, tell me the names of your parents." To which she replied: "I never had parents." At this, the satyr, quickly grieving, said: "You have broken my rules." But she, out of audacity, began to blaspheme the satyr's parents, because of which he repudiated her and separated her from his bed. Not long after, this young woman allowed herself to be debauched by a certain donkey. The satyr, knowing this, judged with all his people that she should be put to death. As she was dying, she said: 

"It is best to obey and serve one's husbands." 

So it is that some women are contrary to their husbands and disobedient, wherefore leadership does not suit them. For **Ecclesiasticus** <note>The Book of Sirach (Ecclesiasticus) 25:30 in the Vulgate</note> says: "A woman, if she has leadership, is contrary to her husband." Likewise: "It is better to dwell in a desert land than with a quarrelsome and angry woman." <note>Proverbs 21:19</note>

A certain man once punished a bad and disobedient wife in this way: he pretended to go to the market and said to his wife: "By no means put your finger in that hole." He then hid himself in a neighboring house. The wife, however, began to think: "Why did he forbid me this? Certainly, I will not obey him in this," and with great force she thrust her hand into the hole. But in the hole there were very sharp nails in which her fingers were transfixed. Whence, out of great distress, she began to cry out, so that the husband ran to her, saying: "Why did you not obey my commands?" and thus he corrected her until she became obedient. 

When, in a certain ship, a storm was imminent, the sailors shouted that the heavier things should be thrown into the sea. A certain man, having a wife with an intolerable tongue, presented her, saying that in the whole ship there was nothing heavier than her tongue. 

Whence **Seneca** <note>Roman Stoic philosopher often quoted for moral maxims</note> says: "Just as nothing is simpler than a kind spouse, so nothing is more cruel than a tainted woman." And the **Philosopher** <note>term: "Philosophus"; usually referring to Aristotle, though here it refers to a generic wise authority on marriage</note> says: "A wife is either a perpetual refuge or a perennial torment." If she is bad, she is a perennial torment; if she is good, she is a perpetual refuge. Because good wives are obedient to their husbands and love them above all things. 

**Jerome**, in his work *Against Jovinian* <note>original: "Hieronimus contra Iouinianū"; a 4th-century treatise defending virginity and detailing the virtues of Roman matrons</note>, provides an example regarding three Roman matrons who, after losing their husbands, refused to take another. 

The first of these was called **Marcia**, the daughter of Cato. When she was asked why she would not take a second husband, she replied that she could not find a man who wanted her for herself, but rather for her possessions—for she was perhaps plain, but strong and wealthy. 

The second was called **Valeria**. When she was asked whether she would take a second husband, she replied that she could not do so because her husband was not dead, but living. For as long as she herself lived, she would carry her husband with her in her heart, and he would live together with her. 

The third was called **Anna**, who was urged by her parents to take a second husband because he was young and rich. She replied that she could not do this, because she had a good husband; therefore, if she took a second, either he would be good or bad. If he were good, she would always be in fear lest she [lose] him through...

<summary>This page presents various moral anecdotes (exempla) regarding the necessity of a wife's obedience to her husband, using both mythical figures like the Satyr and historical Roman matrons to illustrate the qualities of a "good" versus "bad" wife.</summary>

### Concerning the dromedary and its race, and the lion. Chapter 91.

<image-desc>A horizontal woodcut illustration depicting a lion standing between two dromedaries (camels). The dromedary on the left is bowing its head to the ground; the lion in the center has a thick mane and raised tail; the dromedary on the right is sitting in a couchant position.</image-desc>

<image-desc>A large square woodcut decorative initial 'D' containing intricate symmetrical foliate and floral patterns.</image-desc>

The **dromedary**, as some say, is an animal that travels as far in only one day as a horse does in three. Now the **lion** cried out, saying: "Among all others, you are praised by me because you know how to run and leap; therefore, I want you to go quickly into the East and inquire concerning the person and character of the **griffin** who wants to wage war with me." But so that I may prepare myself for this contest, report to me more quickly, so that afterward you may receive a reward and honor from me. 

The dromedary, however, began to exalt itself when it heard itself praised; for this reason, it began the journey and started to run beyond its strength so that it might be praised even more. On that account, it ran and leaped so much that it fell down and died <note>original: "expiravit"; literally 'breathed out' or expired</note>, saying: "That which we know how to do, let us do in such a way that we never destroy ourselves." 

So also we ought to do those things which we do and know with such discretion that we do not waste away our bodies and limbs. For **Augustine** says in his Rule: "He who afflicts his flesh beyond measure kills his own fellow citizen" <note>original: "Qui carnem suam supra modum affligit civem suum occidit"; a quote attributed to the Rule of St. Augustine, emphasizing that the body is a "citizen" or companion to the soul and should not be destroyed by extreme asceticism</note>. For then a man kills his fellow citizen—that is, his own body—when he burdens and fatigues it beyond what it is able to do. 

Wherefore **Isidore** <note>Isidore of Seville, a 7th-century scholar whose writings were foundational to medieval knowledge</note> says: "In every work, it is necessary to have moderation and balance; for whatever is done with moderation and balance is healthful; but whatever is too much or beyond measure is ruinous." 

There is a story <note>original: "fabula"; the author is introducing the Greek myth of Daedalus and Icarus</note> that a certain **philosopher** was placed with his son in a certain tower standing alone in the sea. The son, suffering from weariness, asked his father to make him escape from that captivity. But he found many bird feathers and, with pitch and glue, he stuck them together and fashioned wings for himself and his son so that they might fly and depart from there. Then the father taught his son the way, saying: "Take care that you do not fly too high, nor descend too low."

<summary>This page uses the fable of a dromedary that runs itself to death and the myth of Daedalus and Icarus to warn against the dangers of pride and lack of moderation. It features citations from St. Augustine and Isidore of Seville to reinforce the medieval virtue of "discretio" or balanced living.</summary>

<keywords>dromedary, lion, moderation, St. Augustine, Isidore of Seville, Daedalus, Icarus, pride, discretion, animal fables</keywords>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing narrative from Chapter 91, concluding the story of the dromedary and a parallel allegory of Daedalus and Icarus. It then transitions into Chapter 92, which uses a monastic setting for animal fables.</meta>

...but hold the middle path if you wish to be happy, for the blessed have held the middle path <note>original: "quia medium tenuere beati"; a classical proverb often used to describe the "Golden Mean" or moderation</note>. The father did thus and escaped from prison by the straight path. The son, however, feeling himself flying, became joyful and kept ascending; and it happened that the heat of the sun scorched his feathers and consumed them; and so, falling, he died. Hence **Bernard** <note>likely Saint Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a prominent Cistercian abbot and mystic</note> says: "Always hold the middle if you do not wish to lose your moderation."

# Concerning the lion who built a monastery. Chapter 92.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicts a lion on the right, standing on its hind legs and gesturing with its front paws toward a small, multi-storied stone building on the left. Several animals, possibly donkeys or goats, are clustered inside the building, peering out of the doorway at the lion.</image-desc>

<image-desc>A large ornamental drop-cap letter 'L' featuring intricate foliage and floral designs within a square frame.</image-desc>

For the redemption of his soul and those of his parents, the **lion** built a most excellent **monastery** <term>monastery (original: cenobium); a communal dwelling for those living under a religious rule</term> in which he organized many animals, giving them a manner and form of living. He chose as **prior** <note>the officer in charge of the discipline and daily running of a monastery</note> a **unilus** <term>unilus; identified by the medieval scholar Papias as a "son of stags" with a coat of many colors, likely a species of mountain goat or chamois</term>, believing him to be a devout monk. The unilus, however, just as he was varied in color, so too was he varied in his faith. For he began to divide the brothers and set them into factions by appointing officials; and after a little while, he would dismiss them and appoint others. Those who were dismissed spoke out against him, while those he appointed supported him. He did this often and maliciously, so that they would not conspire against him but would instead remain awake at night in disagreement. 

At last, the brothers armed themselves, wishing to tear each other apart for their respective factions. But a certain **palfrey** <term>palfrey (original: palafredus); a gentle, high-quality riding horse</term>, wise and old, said: "Stop, brothers! It is not good to do this. It is better to dismiss this wicked prior and choose and raise up another who is peaceful." This speech pleased the brothers, and they unanimously corrected him, saying: "Harmony must be held and maintained among brothers." In this, it appears that **harmony** is a virtue acceptable among brothers. For **Ecclesiastes** <note>The author is actually citing the Book of Sirach (also known as Ecclesiasticus) 25:1, which was often grouped with the books of Solomon in the Middle Ages</note> says: "In three things my spirit is pleased, which are approved before God and men: the love of brothers, the love of neighbors, and a man and woman who are constant or in agreement with each other in what is good." And as **Augustine** says: "He cannot have harmony with Christ or within himself who wishes to have discord with a Christian." But there are some who, while they are in peace, do not know...

<summary>This page concludes a lesson on the importance of the "middle path" using the myth of Icarus, before beginning a fable about a lion who founds a monastery. The fable serves as a political and moral warning against leaders who maintain power by creating division and discord among their followers.</summary>
<keywords>moderation, Daedalus and Icarus, Bernard of Clairvaux, lion, monastery, unilus, discord, harmony, leadership, Saint Augustine, Sirach</keywords>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page concludes the themes of Chapter 92 regarding social order and harmony (concord), and begins Chapter 93, which warns against arrogance and the refusal to seek expert advice.</meta>

...to live. Whence a fisherman, who was entering the water of a spring belonging to the local inhabitants, responded to those who were rebuking him for the disturbance of the water: "If this water were not disturbed, I would not be able to live." <note>This refers to the Aesopic fable of the Fisherman Stirring the Water, a metaphor for those who profit from social unrest.</note> Concord is nothing if not most useful to a city, as the **Apostle** says. Therefore, it is good to strive for the attainment of friendship and concord. Regarding concord, **Vegetius** <note>Publius Flavius Vegetius Renatus, a 4th-century Roman military writer</note> relates in his third book that when **Hannibal** besieged **Casilinum** <note>original: "asalinam"; a city in Campania famous for its resistance against Hannibal in 216 BCE</note>, the people were entangled in such great scarcity that a single loaf of bread was sold for a thousand denarii. In this case, he who sold the bread died of hunger, while the other who bought it was fed and survived. This was done so that they might not disagree with one another <note>This anecdote usually illustrates that in times of extreme crisis, even a seemingly unfair trade is better than a total breakdown of social agreement.</note>.

### ¶ Concerning the onocentaur who had a palace built. Chapter 93.

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration. On the left, a man in a tunic gestures toward a small, gabled stone building with a single arched door and a small window. On the right, an onocentaur (a creature with a human torso and head but the lower body of an ass or satyr-like beast) faces the man, also gesturing. To the far left, there are ruins of a larger stone structure.</image-desc>

<image-desc>A circular woodcut medallion featuring an intricate, symmetrical floral and foliate design with scrolling leaves and a central budding stalk.</image-desc>

The **Onocentaur**—that is, a donkey mixed with a human—is so called because it is said to be of half human appearance and half donkey, as **Huguccio** <note>Huguccio of Pisa (d. 1210), a scholar whose "Derivationes" was a standard medieval dictionary</note> says. This creature was having a most beautiful palace built for himself. However, trusting in his own judgment, he wished to determine and arrange all things himself, not paying attention to the advice of the **architect**. For while the master of the builders <term>architect (original: architectos); from the Greek roots for "chief" and "builder"</term> consulted on what was useful as he discerned through his skills, the [onocentaur], puffed up with pride, said: "I am wise and ingenious; it is not permitted for you to lecture me <note>original: "dogmatisare"; to instruct or lay down rules</note> about the limits of such labor."

When the palace was completed, because it was not well-founded nor well-constructed, it sought its own ruin and fell to the ground. And then the onocentaur, being confounded, said: "He quickly dissolves who did not wish to be guided by the learned." For in this way, many do not wish to believe the counsel of the wise, but wish to determine all things by their own sense. You, however, should not always trust your own conscience, nor rely on your own sense, nor always acquiesce to your own will; but do all things with counsel and with those more prudent and wise than yourself, and acquiesce to their warnings, lest you be able to deviate in any way from the way of truth. For indeed, it is said in the **Book of Wisdom** <note>referring to the biblical book of Ecclesiasticus/Sirach 32:24</note>: "Do nothing without counsel, and after the deed you shall not repent." Because, as it is said in the same place: "The shrewd man does all things with counsel." <note>Proverbs 13:16</note>. But those who do all things...

<sig>n iij</sig>

<summary>This page discusses the necessity of social concord and the danger of pride, using the fable of an onocentaur whose palace collapses because he ignores his architect's expertise.</summary>
<keywords>concord, Hannibal, Casilinum, onocentaur, architecture, wisdom, counsel, Huguccio, Vegetius</keywords>

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on seeking advice, specifically concluding Chapter 93 with a story about the foresight required for wise counsel, before moving to Chapter 94 regarding respect for the elderly.</meta>

...with counsel they are ruled by wisdom. This is the difference between a wise counsel and a foolish one: for the fool regards nothing but the beginning of a deed, whereas the wise man regards the end and the steps that lead to that end. We read of a certain philosopher who, sitting in a prominent place in the marketplace, announced that he wished to sell wisdom <note>original: "sensum"; here meaning common sense or practical wisdom</note>. The prince of that region sent messengers and gold to him so that he might buy this wisdom. The philosopher wrote on a small slip of paper <note>original: "cedula"</note>, saying: "In all things you are about to do, always think of what might happen to you at the end." When the messengers mocked him and wished to throw the paper away, he said: "Carry it safely to your lord, for it is well worth the price." When the prince received it, he ordered it to be written in golden letters upon the door of his palace. After much time had passed, certain of his enemies conspired with a barber to have the prince's throat cut. However, when the barber entered through the door and read that writing—for he knew how to read—he began to tremble and grow pale. Seeing this, the prince had him seized, and by extracting the truth through torture, the prince spared himself but put the authors of the crime to death. By this it is clear how useful it is to weigh the final outcome. Whence the philosopher says: "Whatever you do, do it wisely, and look to the end" <note>original: "Quicquid agas sapienter agas et respice finem"; a popular medieval maxim often attributed to various classical authors</note>.

### Concerning the rhinoceros who despised the elderly. Chapter 94.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicts a landscape with two central male figures in tunics. To the left, a rhinoceros-like creature with a long, straight horn on its snout and a scaly, plate-like hide sits on the ground. To the right stands an elderly, bearded man in long robes, and behind him is another beast with a shorter horn and a head resembling a wolf or a donkey. The figures appear to be in mid-gesture, suggesting a dialogue or scene from a fable.</image-desc>

The rhinoceros, says Papias <note>Papias the Lombard, an 11th-century lexicographer whose "Elementarium Doctrinae Rudimentum" was a standard reference</note>, is an animal having a horn on its nose. The rhinoceros is a wild beast of such an untamable nature that if it were captured, it could in no way be held. This rhinoceros, on account of his own strength and youth, could in no way bear to look at the elderly; whenever he caught sight of old men, he would hiss behind them, and seeing them bent over, he would point them out to others with his finger in mockery. However, as time passed and he himself became old, the youth in turn despised him. He then endured it patiently, saying: "He who desires to grow old must hold the elderly dear." From this it appears that the elderly are not to be despised by the young; rather, they are to be honored all the more. Regarding this, we have a commandment in the book of Leviticus: "Rise up before the hoary head and honor the person of the aged" <note>Leviticus 19:32</note>.

<summary>This page concludes a lesson on the life-saving power of foresight (the "look to the end" proverb) and begins a fable about a rhinoceros who learns the necessity of respecting the elderly after his own youth fades.</summary>
<keywords>foresight, wisdom, barbers, conspiracies, rhinoceros, aging, respect for elders, Leviticus, Papias</keywords>

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter 94 regarding respect for one's elders, finishing the moral lessons drawn from history and nature before beginning a new chapter on the dangers of mocking the afflicted.</meta>

...of the elder. Whence in the canonical epistle of Peter it is said: "Young men, be subject to your elders" <note>original: "Adolescentes subditi estote senioribus"; see 1 Peter 5:5</note>. Cato also says: "Give way to your superior" <note>original: "Cede locum maiori"; a maxim from the Distichs of Cato, a standard medieval schoolbook on ethics</note>. Just as Valerius <note>Valerius Maximus, a 1st-century Roman author of "Memorable Deeds and Sayings"</note> tells of Alexander the Great, who earned the highest honor and love from his soldiers through his mercy. Regarding this, he says that once, while he was leading his army through a storm, he saw a soldier overwhelmed by smoke and numbed by the cold. Though Alexander himself was sitting in a high, exalted seat, he immediately descended from his throne as the soldier approached and, with his own hands, placed that soldier in his own seat. <margin>On the nature of the stork</margin> Likewise, Valerius reports that when storks grow old, their children place their parents in the nest; they gather them against their own breasts like infants, and they cherish, nourish, and keep them warm. Rational human beings ought to do the same for their elders and parents. Concerning this, the same Valerius tells of a certain noble woman who had been handed over to prison for a certain crime so that she might die of hunger there. Her married daughter was permitted by the judge to visit her every day. However, she was first carefully searched to ensure she brought no food with her. But the daughter, drawing out her own breast, nourished her mother every day with her own milk. When the judge marveled that the mother survived for such a long time, the truth of what was happening was discovered. Then the judge, moved by pity, granted a pardon. Solinus <note>original: "solarius"; likely Gaius Julius Solinus, a Roman geographer known for his "Collection of Remarkable Facts"</note> relates a story identical in all respects concerning an elderly father supported by his daughter. Only vultures permit their parents to die <note>Bestiaries often contrasted the "pious" stork with the "cruel" vulture to teach moral lessons about family duty</note>.

### ¶ Concerning the oryx who mocked the sick. Chapter 95.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration shows a bearded man in a short tunic walking with two wooden crutches or walking sticks. He is flanked by two animals with long ears: to his left, a dromedary or camel-like creature is seated; to his right, a dog or donkey-like creature stands. The ground is sparsely decorated with tufts of grass.</image-desc>

---

<image-desc>A large circular woodcut ornament featuring a symmetrical, ornate foliate design with scrolls, stylized leaves, and floral patterns.</image-desc>

Brito <note>William Brito, a 13th-century friar and grammarian</note> says here that this is an animal that is clean for eating but unclean for sacrifice. This oryx <note>original: "oxir"; a species of antelope often mentioned in medieval encyclopedias</note> lived in such perfect health that he was never sick; for that reason, he used to mock the infirm when they were ill, saying: "These people only pretend to be in pain so that they can be free from work and rest." Speaking in this way, he would never serve the sick. Eventually, however, he began to suffer from fever and fall ill, at which point he began to groan and weep, saying: "Alas, miserable me! I never served those who were weak, but instead I slandered them as much as I could. Now, however, I promise the God of heaven and earth that if He delivers me, I will always serve the sick."

<summary>This page concludes a series of moral exempla regarding filial piety and respect for elders (Chapter 94) and introduces a fable about an oryx (Chapter 95) who learns empathy for the sick through his own suffering.</summary>
<keywords>respect for elders, Alexander the Great, stork, Roman Charity, oryx, illness, mercy, Valerius Maximus, empathy</keywords>

--- Page 114 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the previous discussion on filial piety and the respect due to elders into a new chapter (likely Dialogue 95 of the "Dialogus Creaturarum") regarding the spiritual and social dangers of vengeance and the mockery of God's creation.</meta>

The **Little Chameleon-Leopard**, as Papias says <note>Papias was a 11th-century Italian lexicographer who compiled one of the first great medieval dictionaries</note>, is so named because it is a combination of a chameleon and a leopard. Isidore says in the twelfth book of his *Etymologies* <note>Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636) was the author of a massive encyclopedia that served as a standard reference for the Middle Ages</note>: "The Chameleon-Leopard is named because it is spotted like a white leopard, but has a neck like a horse, making it a monstrous creature." And it is considered a single species.

This creature became a magnificent painter, but he always depicted **Christ** as a monster to avenge himself <note>The creature blames God for his own "monstrous" appearance and uses his art as a weapon of spite</note>. Because of this, many people began to disparage Christ, saying: "How is this man supposed to save us, when he possesses neither beauty nor a proper form?"

On a certain occasion, this chameleon-leopard was painting Christ with deep colors, doing everything in his power to show him as monstrous and despicable. Because of this, Christ appeared to him in a state of distress, saying: "Why do you defile me so, showing me to be a monster, when I am fairer than the sons of men? <note>original: "pulcher pre filijs hominum"; a reference to Psalm 45:2</note>. Even the angels long to look upon me, yet you wish to strip me of my honor and my beauty."

The Little Chameleon-Leopard replied to him: "Do you not think I remember that you fashioned me as a monster and did not make me beautiful? Now I shall have my revenge upon you, and I will never spare you."

But Christ, being troubled while the creature was painting high up on his perch, cast him down, saying: "He who loses his life through his own revenge carries out a poor vengeance indeed." For indeed, many people are so bitter and harsh that they never wish to let go of an injury, but always want to seek revenge. Regarding this, the book of Ecclesiastes <note>Likely referring to the Book of Sirach 28:1, which was often grouped with Ecclesiastes in medieval citations</note> says: "He who seeks to be avenged by God shall find vengeance." And **Seneca** <note>The Roman Stoic philosopher</note> says: "We ought to forget injuries, for the remedy for an injury is forgetfulness."

The ancient leaders were merciful and kind in giving to the unworthy and in forgiving their enemies. Regarding this, **Valerius** <note>Valerius Maximus, a 1st-century Roman collector of historical anecdotes</note> relates in his sixth book concerning the consul **Camillus** that while he was besieging the city of the Falerians, a local schoolmaster led their most noble children into the Roman camp. There was no doubt that the townspeople would have surrendered their city to get the children back. Camillus, however, not only despised this treachery, but ordered that the children should lead their master, bound, back to their parents. Because of this act of justice, the Falerians opened their gates to the Romans.

**Ambrose** relates in his work *On Duties* <note>St. Ambrose’s *De Officiis*, a manual on Christian ethics</note> that it is found in the Roman chronicles how the physician of King **Pyrrhus** <note>original: "porri regis"; a medieval spelling variation for Pyrrhus of Epirus, a famous enemy of Rome</note> came to **Fabricius**, the Roman leader, claiming that he would give Pyrrhus poisoned medicine, and thus...

<summary>This page tells a moral fable about a monstrous "chameleon-leopard" painter who tries to mock Christ through art and is punished, followed by classical examples of Roman mercy and integrity.</summary>
<keywords>Chameleon-leopard, Christ, vengeance, mercy, Camillus, Fabricius, Pyrrhus, Isidore of Seville, Roman history, moral fable</keywords>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing examples of virtue and vice, moving from the previous account of Christ and the Chameleon-Leopard into classic Roman historical anecdotes before beginning Dialogue 72 of the "Dialogus Creaturarum" regarding the Gull (the Greedy Sailor).</meta>

...after the king was dead, he might obtain victory <note>This refers to a famous story where a physician offered to poison King Pyrrhus; the Roman general Fabricius refused to win by such dishonorable means</note>. Fabricius ordered him to be bound and sent back to his master. And **Ambrose** <note>Saint Ambrose (c. 340–397), an influential bishop and theologian</note> adds: "Indeed, it is magnificent that he who had undertaken a contest of virtue should refuse to win through fraud." Then **Pyrrhus** is said to have remarked: "This is that Fabricius, who could be turned from the path of lawfulness more difficultly than the sun from its course." And so he made peace with him as he wished. 

**Valerius** <note>Valerius Maximus, a 1st-century Roman author known for his collection of historical anecdotes</note> narrates in his fifth book that when the ambassadors of the Carthaginians came to the city to ransom captives, twenty-seven hundred and forty young men <note>original: "XXVII. v. L. xl."; referring to the 2,740 captives returned by the Roman Senate as a gesture of goodwill</note> were returned immediately without any money being accepted. That so great an army of enemies was dismissed, that such vast sums of money were refused, and that so many wrongs were forgiven is a wonder beyond measure.

Likewise, Valerius tells that when the city of **Privernum** had been taken by the Romans, with some people killed and others captured or wounded, and no help remained for them but in prayers, their leader was asked what punishment he and his people deserved. He replied: "That which those deserve who judge themselves worthy of liberty." And when he was asked again what kind of peace the Romans would have with them if they were left unpunished, he answered steadfastly: "If you give us a good peace, you shall have it forever; if a bad one, it will not last long." By these words, it came to pass that the conquered were given not only a pardon, but even the right and benefit of Roman citizenship.

## ¶ Concerning the Gull, the Greedy Sailor. Chapter 72.

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration depicting a large, woven nest or basket floating on stylized, swirling waves. Inside the nest are three large eggs marked with cross-hatching. To the left, a bird with its wings partially raised stands on the water's edge. To the right, a group of three similar birds stands together, observing the nest. The birds appear to be seagulls or similar large aquatic birds.</image-desc>

The **Gull** <note>original: "Laurus," a likely misspelling of "Larus," the Latin term for a seagull or sea-mew</note> is a creature living both on land and in the water; for it flies and swims, as is said in **Deuteronomy 14** <note>The Bible categorizes the gull among the birds that are ritually "unclean"</note>: it flies like an eagle and swims like a fish. 

This creature was a very great sailor, but he always overloaded his fleet more than was proper, being overconfident in himself. His friends rebuked him for this very thing, but he did not correct himself because of his wicked desire for profit <note>original: "impietatem lucri"; refers to the sin of greed or avarice</note>. On a certain occasion, he loaded a ship which began to sink into the waves, for it was not able to steer itself because of the heavy burden and the crashing waters. Therefore, being driven toward the land and suffering shipwreck, the sailor said:

<summary>This page recounts historical examples of Roman integrity and mercy before introducing a fable about a seagull (portrayed as a greedy sailor) whose overladen ship is wrecked by his own avarice.</summary>
<keywords>Fabricius, Pyrrhus, Valerius Maximus, Privernum, Larus (Gull), greed, Roman virtue, Dialogus Creaturarum, shipwreck</keywords>

--- Page 116 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing examples from the "Dialogus Creaturarum" (Dialogue of Creatures). The previous page concluded a series of Roman historical anecdotes about integrity (Fabricius and the Carthaginians) and now transitions into fables regarding the dangers of greed and the wisdom of gradual progress.</meta>

Profit with security is truly good. Let merchants heed this, lest for the sake of the impiety of gain they hasten to be enriched with danger; rather, let them make progress little by little with security. For **Bernard** <note>Saint Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a highly influential Cistercian abbot and mystic</note> says: "I do not wish to become the greatest suddenly, but I wish to progress little by little." A certain peasant had a single hen that laid one egg for him every day. He gained much by gathering and selling the eggs. Thinking that many eggs could be found in its belly and wishing to have all the profit at once, he cut her open; finding no eggs, he lost everything—as is commonly said: *he lost both the eggs and the hen.* Merchants should also beware of acquiring anything for themselves unjustly, because the riches of the unjust quickly slip away. Whence the **Philosopher** <note>original: "phls."; an abbreviation for 'Philosophus,' almost always referring to Aristotle in medieval scholastic texts</note> says: "Riches from illicit gain cannot last long." 

It is told that when a certain merchant had sold wine diluted with half water for as many coins as if it were pure, and had opened his small chest on a ship where he was managing the gold coins received from the price of the wine, there happened to be a certain **monkey** <note>original: "symia"</note> on the ship. It came secretly and took the pouch with the gold pieces. It fled up to the anchor and began to open the pouch, throwing one gold coin into the sea and the other into the ship until the pouch was empty, so that the merchant reported nothing gained from his fraud. <note>The moral being that the sea took back the "water" portion of the profits, leaving the merchant only with the portion earned from the wine itself.</note>

### ¶ Concerning the hunting lion. &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; Chapter 100.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a lion on the right, with a thick mane and its tongue out, facing a smaller animal (possibly a deer or dog) on the left that is leaping away. The scene includes stylized tufts of grass and a single flower in the center foreground.</image-desc>

A certain lion was a very great hunter. He always acted in such a way that when he hunted and looked at the animals, he would spot one of the better ones and pursue it. But when that animal was in full strength, it fled from him quite quickly. The lion, saddened because he did not have what he desired and wishing to catch those he had left behind, was not at all able to do so because they were now hiding. The lion remained in sadness and never hunted, saying: "Let us never let go of the certain, nor relax our hold for the sake of the uncertain." So also some people cannot do those things which are suitable for them; they do not know how to begin, desiring better things. Therefore they are often deceived, and they do not find those things which they were able to have before; they even wish to seize those things which they cannot, and

<summary>This page uses the fables of the "Hen that laid the Golden Egg," a thieving monkey, and a prideful hunting lion to warn against the vices of greed and the folly of abandoning certain gains for uncertain ambitions.</summary>
<keywords>greed, merchants, Saint Bernard, Aristotle, fables, lion, monkey, dishonesty, Dialogus Creaturarum</keywords>

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing fables from the "Dialogus Creaturarum" (Dialogue of Creatures). The previous page used the example of a merchant and a monkey to illustrate how ill-gotten gains are easily lost. This page shifts focus to the folly of grieving over the past and the danger of abandoning certain reality for uncertain dreams.</meta>

...to recover things lost and unrecoverable, but because they are unable to do so, they grieve most bitterly.

Against these people, it is said in the **Third Book of Kings** <note>original: "tertio regum"; in the medieval Vulgate Bible, the books of Samuel and Kings were numbered 1–4 Kings. This specific quote is from 2 Samuel 12:23</note> regarding a dead son: "Since he is dead, why should I fast? Will I be able to call him back? I shall go to him instead, but he truly will not return to me." Whence it is told in a fable about a **nightingale** <note>original: "philomena"</note> who said to the youth who captured her: "Never grieve over a thing lost and unrecoverable," as is read in the story of **Barlaam** <note>referring to *Barlaam and Josaphat*, a popular medieval Christianized version of the life of the Buddha</note>. For it is madness and a danger to abandon a secure and certain thing for another that is uncertain and vain. **Aesop** <note>the famous Greek fabulist</note> relates that a certain dog was carrying a piece of meat across a bridge; seeing its shadow in the water, it dropped the meat it had in its mouth to grab the meat appearing in the water, and so it lost everything. Many people do the same, for out of a desire for acquiring more, they abandon the secure things they have to acquire those they do not. Whence Aesop says: "Certain things ought not to be abandoned for vain ones." And just as it is madness to abandon secure things, so it is folly to hope for vain things. For the thoughts of men are vain, as the **Psalmist** <note>referring to the author of the Psalms, specifically Psalm 94:11</note> says. Hence, when a certain lady had given her maidservant milk to sell and the maid was carrying it to the city along a ditch, she began to think that with the price of the milk she would buy a hen, which would produce chicks; when these grew into hens, she would sell them and buy piglets, then change them for sheep, and the sheep for oxen. Thus enriched, she would marry some nobleman and so she rejoiced. While she was thus glorying and thinking with how much glory she would be led to that man upon a horse, saying "**Giddy-up, giddy-up!**" <note>original: "gio gio"; an onomatopoeic cry to urge a horse forward</note> while striking the ground with her foot as if she were pricking the horse with her heels, her foot slipped and she fell into the ditch. Thus she did not have what she hoped she would obtain.

## ¶ Of the goat-stag architect and the tyrant. Chapter 101.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicts a stone building with several arched windows and a doorway. The roof and part of the left wall are shown in a state of collapse. A composite creature, known as a tragelassus (a "goat-stag"), is shown falling or leaping head-first from the top of the crumbling structure. To the right of the building, two men dressed in long, draped robes and caps observe the event. The man in the foreground is holding a long, thin rod or branch.</image-desc>

**Tragelassus** <note>original: "Tragellassus"; a mythical creature combining a goat and a deer</note>, also known as a **goat-stag** <note>original: "ycoceruus" or hircocervus</note>. **Brito** <note>William Brito, a 13th-century monk and grammarian who wrote a popular dictionary</note> says the name is composed from *tragros* <note>Greek: tragos</note>, which is a goat, and *lachos* <note>Greek: elaphos</note>, which is a stag. This creature was made an excellent **architect** <note>term: "architector" — a master builder or designer</note> but was the greatest deceiver. For when he was consulted to build a structure, he would construct such a foundation that the building would quickly suffer a collapse, saying to himself: "I shall profit again when I rebuild it." In this way, he would plunder men when he was consulted. Meanwhile, a certain **tyrant** <note>term: "tyrannus" — in the medieval sense, a powerful ruler who often governs without legal restraint</note> wishing a marvelous palace to be built, sent for this architect and gave him countless money for the purpose of constructing the building. The architect, however, laid a weak foundation, as was his custom.

<summary>This page recounts several moral fables, including the dog and its shadow and the milkmaid's lost dreams, before introducing the deceptive Tragelassus, a goat-stag architect who builds faulty structures for profit.</summary>
<keywords>Aesop, Barlaam and Josaphat, greed, milkmaid fable, Tragelassus, goat-stag, architectural fraud, medieval ethics, fables</keywords>

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing fables from the "Dialogus Creaturarum" (Dialogue of Creatures). The previous page discussed the folly of abandoning a certain thing for an uncertain one, using the examples of a dog and its shadow and a nightingale's advice.</meta>

...and so, when the work was finished, the palace began to crack and shake. The Tyrant, seeing this and being saddened, summoned the architect and said to him: "Why have you wickedly deceived me?" To which the architect replied: "I deceived myself when I laid the foundation of the palace. But it is necessary to level it so that it may be better founded." The Tyrant, however, seized him and had him placed next to the wall and thrown headlong from the palace, saying: "Through wicked counsel many often suffer loss. Therefore, beware of giving false and most wicked counsel." For it often happens that those who give bad advice have it fall back upon them. As it says in **Ecclesiasticus** <note>original: "Eccle.xvij"; the quote is actually from Ecclesiasticus (Sirach) 27:27</note>: "Upon him who gives most wicked counsel, it shall fall back, and he shall not know from whence it comes to him." 

As **Orosius** <note>Paulus Orosius, a 5th-century historian and theologian</note> relates, while the tyrant **Phalaris** <note>a cruel ruler of Acragas in Sicily, known for his cruelty</note> was condemning many innocents, **Perillus** the silversmith <note>original: "argētarius"</note>, wishing to please him, made a golden bull in the side of which he built a door so that the condemned could be shut inside. He gave it to the tyrant so that those whom the tyrant hated could be enclosed within while fires were placed beneath; and when they let out groans, they would not seem like the groans of a man but the lowing of an ox or a beast. But the tyrant abhorred the deed and said that Perillus should enter first, so that he might know how the lowing sounded through the mouth of the bull; and thus he shut him in and punished him.
