The animal on the left is seated, looking towards the right, while the one on the right is lying down with its head turned back toward the left. Between them on the ground are various objects: an axe, several vessels or pots, and what appear to be shoe soles or patterns.</image-desc>

### ¶ Of the Buffalo, a Cobbler who turned Apothecary. Chapter 102.

<image-desc>A large, ornate decorative initial 'B' featuring intricate foliate patterns and two grotesque masks or faces—one at the top center and one at the bottom center within the curves of the letter.</image-desc>

The Buffalo, says **Brito** <note>refers to William Brito, a 13th-century monk and lexicographer whose dictionary was a standard reference</note>, is a wild ox. This character became a most renowned shoemaker <note>original: "caligarius"</note> who, through his craft, lived splendidly with a large household. But when he had become rich, he began to loathe his craft, saying: "This craft is shameful. I always have black hands. I live in filth, licking leather and feet. I wish to raise myself up and become an **apothecary** <note>original: "apothecarius"; a pharmacist or dealer in medicinal spices</note>, because afterward I will smell sweet." And when he had become a spice-seller <note>original: "speciarius"</note>, everyone mocked him because he did not know the trade, nor how to sell, nor how to calculate. Therefore, he quickly squandered his wealth and, falling into poverty, said: "It is better to stand well [in one's place] than to [move] poorly..."

<summary>This page concludes a lesson on the dangers of giving bad advice using the legend of the Brazen Bull, then begins a new fable about a successful cobbler who ruins himself by trying to become an apothecary out of vanity.</summary>
<keywords>Phalaris, Perillus, Brazen Bull, Buffalo, Cobbler, Apothecary, Ambition, Counsel, Orosius, Brito</keywords>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing arguments about the importance of stability and perseverance, contrasting worldly ambition with philosophical constancy. It marks the transition from classical philosophical anecdotes to a specific fable about a bullock.</meta>

...to change. Therefore, let everyone beware and consider their own state, lest they suddenly change their position.

For the Apostle <note>original: "apostolus"; referring to Saint Paul in 1 Corinthians 7:20</note> says: *In whatever vocation you were called, remain in the same.* For Seneca <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca, the Roman Stoic philosopher</note> says: *A plant that is frequently moved does not grow strong.* Indeed, perseverance is a stable and perpetual abiding in a well-ordered reason. Tullius <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, the famous Roman orator</note> says in his *Rhetoric*: *In this perseverance, a man should remain immovable; he should not be undone by adversities nor puffed up by prosperity.* Likewise, he should not be terrified by threats nor swayed by promises.

Seneca says in the third book of *Natural Questions*, and as the commentator on Boethius’s *The Consolation of Philosophy* <note>a highly influential 6th-century work on fate and fortune</note> notes: The philosophers placed two jars at the threshold of the house of Jove <note>Jupiter, the king of the gods</note>. According to them, this house is this world. The two jars are Prosperity and Adversity, of which those entering the world must taste.

Indeed, Alexander <note>Alexander the Great</note> could not overcome the constancy and perseverance of Diogenes <note>Diogenes the Cynic, a philosopher famous for his extreme poverty and rejection of social norms</note>. When Alexander came to him while he was sitting in the sun and asked if there was anything he wished to have granted to him, that man of robust constancy replied: "I would wish above all else that you do not block the sun from me"—that is, that you do not stand between me and the sun. From this arose the proverb: *Alexander tried longer to push Diogenes from his position than it took him to push Darius <note>Darius III, the King of Persia whom Alexander defeated</note> from his royal throne.*

Therefore, Seneca says in his fifth book *On Benefits*: Diogenes was much more powerful and wealthy than Alexander, who possessed all things. For what Diogenes refused to receive was more than what Alexander could give. On that day, Alexander was defeated because he saw a man to whom he could neither give anything nor take anything away.

The philosophers regarded these temporal things as vile. Thus, a certain philosopher <note>likely Heraclitus, known as the "weeping philosopher"</note> wept because he saw men laboring diligently in vineyards, fields, and buildings as if perpetual happiness were found in them. Another <note>Democritus, the "laughing philosopher"</note>, as if mocking men, always laughed at the vanity of the flesh and the cares of the world.

Hence, Jerome <note>original: "hiero."; Saint Jerome, here quoting the prophet Jeremiah 51:17</note> says: *Every man is made foolish by his own knowledge; every man is confounded by his carved image.* A "carved image" <note>original: "sculptile"</note> is said to be whatever a man establishes as a source of delight in this world.

### ¶ Concerning the Bullock Cook and His Master. Chapter 52.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicts a scene from a fable. On the left, a bullock (young bull) stands on its hind legs in an anthropomorphic pose, gesturing with its forepaws as if speaking. On the right, a bearded man, presumably the master, sits in a high-backed chair or throne. He wears a flat cap and long robes, gesturing back toward the bullock. A stylized three-leaved plant is positioned on the ground between the two figures.</image-desc>

A bullock—that is, a fine young calf—was a cook. He knew how to prepare various and delicate dishes, but he would spoil them because he salted them too much. Therefore, his master called him to himself, saying: "Why do you ruin the dishes with too much salt?" He always replied: "Because it tastes better to me." Seeing that he did not correct himself...

<summary>This page uses classical anecdotes about Alexander the Great and Diogenes to emphasize the virtue of constancy and the vanity of worldly goods, followed by the start of a fable about a bullock who ruins food by over-salting it according to his own taste.</summary>
<keywords>perseverance, Seneca, Cicero, Alexander the Great, Diogenes the Cynic, Boethius, prosperity and adversity, Bullock Cook, Dialogus Creaturarum</keywords>

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page concludes a series of moral warnings against gluttony—contrasting it with the health and virtue found in moderation—and begins a new chapter featuring a jester named Capreolus and a king.</meta>

...because of his gluttony, [it] beat him, despoiled him, and cast him out. For thus it happens to him as it does with many. But because of his greed for flavor and appetite, he was slaughtered and fell miserably, saying: "More die by the throat <note>original: "gullam"; referring to the sin of gluttony</note> than are separated in war." So also many gluttons desire nothing except what tastes good to them and what they crave; and those who follow the appetite of their throat are like unclean animals. Hence Seneca <note>the Roman Stoic philosopher</note> says: "Those obedient to the belly live the life of unclean animals, not of men." 

But as the Gloss <note>a standard medieval biblical commentary</note> says: "It is not the food but the appetite that is at fault." It is very shameful for a Christian to worship the belly as a god <note>original: "ventrem pro deo colere"; a reference to Philippians 3:19</note> and to expel the virtues of the soul through the lusts and appetites of the throat. For Gregory <note>Pope Gregory the Great</note> says: "Lusts for food are detriments to the soul. The more the belly is filled with them, the more the virtues are diminished." And Hippocrates <note>the ancient Greek "Father of Medicine"</note> says: "The more you nourish unclean bodies, the more you harm them." 

In France, there was a certain abbot who feasted splendidly every day and abounded in riches. He was sickly and could not be restored by medicines. Despairing of his life, he entered the Cistercian order <note>a Catholic religious order known for its austerity and manual labor</note>, where, through the parsimony of fasting, he recovered and became robust. Hence Galen <note>an influential Greek physician in the Roman Empire</note> says: "Abstinence is the highest medicine." Ecclesiasticus 36 <note>referring to the Book of Sirach</note> says: "He who is abstinent shall prolong his life." It is read that the Brahmins <note>original: "braginei"; an Indian priestly caste often idealized in medieval literature for their simple, healthy lifestyle</note> wrote to Alexander [the Great]: "The distension of the belly is illicit among us. Therefore we are without illness; while we live, we are healthy, nor do we use any medicine for ourselves." These men sought from Alexander only the immortality which they lacked. 

Now in our own times, a certain old man lived for many years. When he was asked why he lived so long and so healthy, he replied: "I never rose from the table so saturated that my belly was stuffed. Nor did I ever use bloodletting <note>original: "flebotomia"; a common medieval medical practice of withdrawing blood to cure illness</note>. Finally, I never knew a woman. For these three reasons, I have lived long and remained healthy."

***

## -> ¶ Concerning Capreolus the jester and the king. Chapter 104. <-

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicts a king seated on an ornate, high-backed throne on the right side of the frame. He wears a crown and holds a long scepter or rod. In the air between the king and the stag, five small, rounded objects (possibly loaves of bread or stones) appear to be suspended or tossed. <margin><image-desc>Two hand-drawn manicules (pointing hands) appear in the left margin. the upper one points towards the chapter title and the illustration; the lower one is larger and points towards the start of the text block below the image.</image-desc></margin>

There was a certain Capreolus <note>Latin for "Little Roe Deer," likely the name of the jester depicted in the woodcut</note> who was a most excellent jester, who sang, danced, and spoke eloquently. However, he was held in low esteem because he joked too much. Also, he lived very poorly. One day he hurried to the king, saying: "Lord, as you know, I know how to jest better than others. But I am not heard gladly in your kingdom. For many do not..."

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between a king and his jester, Capreolus, concluding a discussion on the dangers of excessive speech and transition to a new fable about a hare who studies law in Paris.</meta>

...[others] know how to joke and are rewarded magnificently; give me an accounting." To him the king replied: "As you say, you know how to joke politely, but in you this turns into excess, so that those listening to you are filled with loathing. But if you wish to be heard with pleasure, strive to speak and joke with moderation." <margin><note>manicule: a pointing hand symbol used to draw attention to a specific passage</note></margin> Capreolus, doing this, very quickly grew quiet, saying: "Sweet things are shunned if they are too often repeated <note>original: "renonata," likely a misspelling of "renovata"</note>." So we too must act regarding the things we know and do; let us act so moderately that we do not appear annoying. Hence Isidore <note>Isidore of Seville, a 7th-century scholar and encyclopedist</note> says: <margin><note>manicule</note></margin> "Everything that is excessive turns into disgust." Hence a certain poet says: "Those who speak too many words are not blessed. Birds are often deceived by sweet songs." <margin><note>manicule</note></margin> A certain small boy asked a certain old man, saying: "Is it good to speak or to be silent, father?" To whom the old man replied: "If the words are useless, leave them be. If however they are good, do not draw them out for long, but speak what you have to say quickly." Because Ecclesiastes <note>referring here to the Book of Sirach or Ecclesiasticus 32:7-9, which was often grouped with Ecclesiastes in the Middle Ages</note> says: "Young man, speak in your own cause only if necessary, and barely if you have been asked twice; let your speech be brief <note>original: "habeat caput mensum tuum"</note>. Where elders are present, do not speak much, and in the company of great men, do not babble." It is read in the *Lives of the Fathers* <note>a collection of biographies and sayings of the early Christian desert monks</note>: Someone asked an old man, saying: "How long must silence be kept?" He replied: "Until you are questioned." <margin><note>manicule</note></margin> <insert>In every place, if you remain silent, you will have rest; keep one thing—silence—until the right time.</insert> For it is written: "A wise man remains silent until the proper time." Theotistus the philosopher said to a peasant who was silent at a feast: "You are learned in this alone, that you keep quiet." And as Isidore says: "Speak at the appropriate time and be silent at the appropriate time. Do not speak unless you have been questioned; do not speak before you have heard. Let the question open your mouth."

## ¶ Concerning the Hare who was a Lawyer, the Lion, the Fox, and the
## Hunter. Chapter 105.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicts a hunter on the far left, wearing a short tunic and boots, holding a drawn bow and aiming an arrow. To his right are four animals: a large beast (possibly a bear) lies on its stomach at the bottom; a fox-like animal stands behind it; a hare sits upright in the foreground; and a lion sits on the far right, facing the others with its mouth open and tail curled.</image-desc> <margin><note>large manicule pointing left towards the start of the next paragraph</note></margin>

A hare went to Paris to study, and he became a supreme lawyer <note>term: "iurista"; a scholar or practitioner of law</note>. Because of this, he came to the lion, saying: "Lord, I have spent my resources on the study of letters; I ask that I be given a salary so that I may live honorably under the shadow of your wings <note>original: "sub umbra alarum tuarum"; a common biblical metaphor for protection</note>." But the lion said: "I want to test you first before granting a salary. Come with me to see various things." When they were traveling together through the forest, behold, a certain hunter appeared having a bow and a quiver, and he shot at a bear and a fox. The fox, however, being clever...

--- Page 122 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative from the previous page, where the Hare—representing a student—is undergoing a practical examination of his wisdom and education before the Lion. The catchword "nes ci" on the previous page likely began the word "nescio" (I do not know) or a similar phrase, though this page begins mid-story with the Bear's encounter.</meta>

When [the hunter] looked, he avoided the blow and saved himself; but the Bear, arrogant and trusting in his own strength, began to rush impetuously toward the man, wishing to tear him apart. The hunter, however, releasing an arrow, took nothing from him but his life. Seeing this, the Lion said to the Hare: "From this, make a proverb so that your knowledge may be recognized, for which you wish to be rewarded." <margin><image-desc>A manicule (pointing hand) is drawn in the left margin pointing to the next sentence.</image-desc></margin> The Hare then wrote in the book, saying: "It is worth more against the blows of death to be wise than to be strong." The Lion praised the proverb and led him into the cities, where they found a certain lord parading his servants. One of them listened patiently; the other, however, began to speak wordily against the lord and did not stop, for which the angry lord beat him most severely and threw him out, stripped of his clothes. But he kept and exalted the patient one. Regarding this, the Hare wrote to the Lion:

¶ "It is much better to be silent than to answer poorly." And the Lion magnified this proverb and led the Hare into a village. There they found a certain oxherd who was yoking oxen and sending them to plow the farm, giving them a bundle of hay for each. One carried the bundle without complaint; the other, however, out of boldness, began to speak wordily, saying: "Why should we carry such a small bit of hay? It will not satisfy us." And saying this, he refused to carry the hay with him. When they came to the labor and worked until evening, the one who had the bundle of hay fed himself; the other, exhausted and having nothing with which he could refresh himself, died of hunger. <margin><image-desc>A manicule (pointing hand) is drawn in the left margin pointing to the next sentence.</image-desc></margin> For this, the Hare dictated to the Lion, saying: "It is better to possess something than to have nothing." Seeing all this, the Lion said: "Rightly, my son, you have studied and have not lost your time, since you know how to answer reasonably to questions." Wherefore he granted him a salary, saying: "He who wishes to be raised high must become prudent and sensible." For students should attend to this and learn frequently so that they may be raised high. For the mind of a man is nourished and rejoiced by learning. Therefore, it ought to have no end. For Seneca <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca, Roman Stoic philosopher</note> says: "Do not fail in learning" <note>original: "Discendo ne defeceris"</note>. For the end of learning and living ought to be the same. ¶ Another says: "If I had one foot in the grave, I would still want to learn." Prudence is desirable for its own sake and also because it is useful for many things. The commentator on the *Ethics* <note>referring to Aristotle's *Nicomachean Ethics*</note> says: "Truly blessed is the one who finds wisdom and who abounds in prudence" <note>original: "Vere est beatus qui invenit sapientiam..."—this is actually a quote from the Bible, Proverbs 3:13</note>, in the third chapter of Proverbs. ¶ Therefore, ancient princes had their teachers, just as Trajan had Plutarch, Nero had Seneca, and Alexander had Aristotle. For when Alexander was born, Philip his father wrote a letter to Aristotle: "Philip to Aristotle, greetings. Know that a son has been born to me, for which I give thanks to the gods, not so much because he was born, but because it happened during your lifetime. For I hope that, having been thus raised and educated by you, he will be worthy of us and of the affairs of our kingdom." ¶ Note that a prince ought to be wise not only in managing human affairs and their laws, but also in divine laws. As the Lord says in Deuteronomy <note>Deuteronomy 17:18-19</note>: "After the king has sat upon the throne of his kingdom, he shall describe for himself this second law <note>The word "Deuteronomy" literally means "second law" in Greek</note> in a volume, taking a copy from the priests of the Levite tribe, and he shall have it with him. And he shall read it all the days of his life, so that he may learn to fear the Lord his God and keep his ceremonies and the things commanded in the law." If by chance the prince is illiterate, it is necessary for the king to use the advice of the learned; wherefore he is commanded to receive a copy of the law from the priests and from ecclesiastical and Catholic writings, as Helinand <note>Hélinand of Froidmont, a 13th-century monk and author</note> explains. For Cicero says in the second book of *Tusculan Disputations*: "Philosophy is the cultivation of the soul which draws out vices and cleanses their roots, and prepares the soul to bear fruit." For as [the author] says: The Academy was a village shaken by frequent earthquakes, a mile distant from Athens...

# ¶ Concerning the Dog and the Wolves.
### Chapter 56.

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration depicting a scene from a fable. On the right, a simple timber-framed building with a gabled roof and tiled top has a dog jumping out of its open doorway. To the left, a group of four wolves or dogs are gathered; some are standing with bared teeth and aggressive postures, while one lies on the ground. The background is minimal, showing only a few blades of grass.</image-desc>

<image-desc>An ornate woodcut initial "C" featuring decorative foliate scrollwork and floral patterns within a square frame. A small animal figure is visible nestled within the foliage at the base of the letter's curve.</image-desc>

There was once a certain large dog, an excellent barker and a fierce fighter against wolves, so much so that he would never allow them to enter the sheepfold <term>ovile: a pen or enclosure for sheep</term>. Many wolves, however, hated the dog and desired to destroy him. Therefore, they gathered together in the fields and sent two of their elders to the dog, saying: "You are great and handsome, powerful and full of virtue. Because of this, we have all gathered in the field so that we may make you our king. Come then, most noble one, in safety, so that we may crown you with honor." 

The foolish dog began to think highly of himself when he heard himself praised; leaving the enclosure <note>original: "curtino"; a courtyard or protected yard</note> where he lived in greater safety, he began to go with them. The wolves, however, cautiously led him to the rest of the pack. When the others saw him, they fell upon him terrifyingly and tore him completely to pieces, saying: "He who desires to be clever should stay where he stands safe" <note>The Latin text here is a rhyming maxim: "Esse qui cupit astutus maneat vbi stat tutus"</note>. 

Even so, when a man is in a safe place, he should not depart from there on account of the promises and praise of flatterers, for many deceivers are found who seduce the unwary with honeyed words. One who hides his intentions well harms his enemy more quickly. Just as Aesop <note>The legendary Greek storyteller to whom these fables are attributed</note> wished to show that a person ought to consider why something is being promised or given to them, he introduces this comparison: A certain thief came to a person's house, and the house dog began to bark. But the thief pulled bread from his cloak and began to coax the dog.

<summary>A fable illustrating the dangers of vanity and flattery, where a guard dog is lured from his safety by wolves promising him a crown, only to be ambushed and killed.</summary>
<keywords>Aesop, fable, flattery, wolves, dog, Plato, Academics, wisdom, deception</keywords>

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page follows a discussion of the Dog and the Wolves (Chapter 56). The text begins mid-sentence, concluding the moral lesson of that fable by warning against deceptive flattery and highlighting the importance of testing those we trust.</meta>

...into your house, that is, into your heart, many are the snares of the deceitful. Whence the same [is said]: if you possess a friend, possess him through testing <note>original: "tentatione"; here meaning to put a friend to the proof before granting full trust</note> and do not easily believe him. Before a trial, a man should not easily trust a friend; but after he has tested him, proved him, and found him faithful, he ought to securely entrust himself to him. 

Hence it is read of Alexander <note>Alexander the Great</note> that when the physician <note>original: "philosuphus medicus"; historical accounts identify him as Philip the Physician</note> and proven friend was about to give him medicine, letters arrived sent by rivals containing the claim that the physician had put poison in the medicine. When Alexander had read these, he first drank the medicine and afterward gave the letters to the physician to be read, because of the great confidence he had in him; he could not suspect evil of him. 

And although there is great utility in friends, yet we ought not to be swift in acquiring them. For we ought not to bind ourselves to any friendship or association before testing. For Martial the Cook <note>likely a medieval reference to the Roman poet Martial, though "the cook" is a curious medieval epithet</note> says to a certain friend of his: "Before you love, test." Because the philosopher <note>original: "ph’s"; usually referring to Aristotle in this period</note> says: because of unproven friends, you may perish. Whence Seneca: "Deliberate all things with a friend, but deliberate about the friend first." For not every companion is good or faithful; indeed, one must distinguish one companion from another. 

For it is said that two companions were walking along a road when a bear met them and charged at them. Out of fear, one of them climbed a tree. But the other feigned that he was dead upon the ground. The bear came to the one who lay as if dead, and thinking him dead, it went away. Then the companion descended from the tree and asked his friend, saying: "What did the bear counsel you in your ear?" To which he replied: "It told me not to care for such a companion who abandoned me in such danger." 

Note that enemies who have seriously offended one another can never have a true peace between them. For we have an analogy: a certain peasant, having an only son, permitted him to go to a place and play, where a serpent bit and killed him, and he was buried. Afterward, the peasant, seeking out and considering if he could kill the serpent, found it; and while he wanted to kill the serpent, it fled, and as it entered its hole, he cut off its tail. Thus enmity was created between the peasant and the serpent. The peasant, however, later spoke of peace. To which the serpent said: "It cannot be; for as long as you see your son's tomb <term>tumulum: a burial mound or grave</term> and I see my severed tail, there cannot be firm peace between you and me."

<meta>This page continues the moralizing fables from the previous section. While the last page focused on the importance of testing friends, this page shifts to a fable about a doe and a wolf to illustrate the dangers of greed and the virtues of Roman frugality.</meta>

# **The Doe and the Wolf**

**D**amnula <note>original: "Damnula"; a medieval Latin term for a small doe or wild goat</note>, as Papias <note>a mid-11th-century lexicographer who wrote a famous dictionary</note> says, is a wild goat. Once, when she was staying overnight with a certain countryman and was returning from the pastures, a wolf attacked her, saying: "Now I shall fill my belly with you, for you cannot escape."

The doe, however, knelt down and said: "I beg you by God that you permit me first to return to the fold. My kid <note>original: "edus"; a young goat</note> has not suckled today, because of which he could die of hunger. For I promise you that I will return to you afterward." 

The greedy wolf thought to have both and said: "Go to the fold, and you shall bring the kid back with you. For I desire to see him, and I will have mercy on him and on you. But without him, you shall not escape." 

The doe swore an oath to all these things, but when she had returned home, she nourished her offspring and did not return to the wolf. And the wolf lamented, saying: "He who is secure with gold should not lose it for a treasure." <note>This proverb warns against risking a certain gain for the hope of a larger, uncertain one—similar to "a bird in the hand is worth two in the bush."</note> 

For in this way, many people, though they have enough, want to swallow everything because of avarice and greed <term>cupiditas</term> <note>a specific type of longing or desire, often focused on wealth or power</note>, like wolves who are never satisfied; because of this, they remain empty. A covetous and greedy person is like a vessel without a bottom that is never filled. According to that passage from Ecclesiastes <note>referring to Ecclesiastes 5:10</note>: "The greedy man shall not be satisfied with money." 

And therefore, the ancient princes rejected greed and avarice. For they did not love to rule for the sake of acquiring money, but for the sake of glory and the protection of the commonwealth <term>reipublice</term> <note>the "public thing" or the state</note>. 

Whence Valerius <note>Valerius Maximus, a 1st-century Roman author known for his collection of historical anecdotes</note> relates in his third book: When Scipio <note>Scipio Africanus, the Roman general who defeated Hannibal</note> was accused before the Senate concerning money, he responded: "When I subjected all of Africa to your power, I brought back nothing from it that could be said to be mine, except for my surname." For he was called Scipio Africanus because he conquered Africa. 

And Valerius relates concerning Manius Curius <note>Manius Curius Dentatus, a Roman hero famed for his frugality and incorruptibility</note>, who was a standard of Roman temperance and a spectacle of fortitude. For when the Samnite ambassadors had come to him while he was sitting on a rustic bench and dining from a wooden dish by the hearth, and they had brought a great weight of gold and invited him with kind words to use the gold, he broke into a laugh, saying: "You have brought this gold in vain. But tell the Senators: Manius Curius would rather rule over the wealthy than become wealthy himself. And report back that I can be corrupted neither by the enemy's battle line nor by money." 

In the same place, he relates that while ambassadors sent by the Samnites had come to Rome, they found Fabricius <note>Gaius Fabricius Luscinus, another Roman leader celebrated for his poverty and integrity</note>, a leader of the Romans, sitting and warming himself. When they offered him many weights of gold for his freedom, he spoke not for his own benefit, but for the common utility, saying: "Go away and take your gold with you. For Romans wish rather to rule over those who have gold than to have the gold themselves." 

Augustine refers in *The City of God* <note>a foundational work of Christian philosophy by St. Augustine of Hippo</note> that Lucius Valerius, who died during his consulship, was so poor that his burial had to be paid for with coins collected from the people. For these princes themselves, while they held a most wealthy commonwealth, were poor in their own homes. Whence Augustine says in the same place—where he relates that a certain one of them was twice proconsul—that he was expelled from the Senate because he was found to have ten pounds of silver in his vessels. The same Valerius first took care to increase the property of every individual, not his own. And being poor in...

<summary>This page uses the fable of the Doe and the Wolf to condemn greed, followed by historical anecdotes of Roman leaders like Scipio and Fabricius who prioritized public service over personal wealth.</summary>
<keywords>Avarice, Fable, Wolf and Doe, Scipio Africanus, Manius Curius Dentatus, Fabricius, Roman Virtue, Poverty, St. Augustine, Valerius Maximus</keywords>

--- Page 126 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing discourse on greed and poverty. The previous page concluded with a reference to the Book of Ecclesiastes regarding the insatiability of the covetous. This page transitions from historical examples of Roman and Macedonian frugality into a new fable about the arrogance of those defined by their outward wealth.</meta>

...rich in authority <note>original: "imperio"</note> rather than wanting to be a rich man living among the poor. He provides the example of the [Roman] consuls, who were so poor in God's sight when they died that they did not have the means to give their daughters in marriage. However, the poverty of these illustrious men was aided by the Senate, and the Senate was accustomed to giving their daughters honorably in marriage. 

Whence Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), an influential Christian philosopher</note> says in his fifth letter: "Those who act more prudently say that it is more to be grieved that poverty has perished than that opulence has." For Rome was preserved in its integrity of morals during its poverty. But opulence, worse than any enemy, has corrupted the harvests of men. Whence it is read that the army of Alexander [the Great], having defeated Darius <note>Darius III, the last King of Kings of the Achaemenid Empire</note>, was enriched beyond measure by spoils. A little later, when they engaged again with the enemy, they were defeated. Seeing this, Alexander ordered all the spoils that the servants had acquired to be burned, saying: "As long as they were without money, there was no one who could resist them; but burdened with gold and silver, they have become idle and lazy." Once these things were lost, they fought bravely as before. 

Likewise, it is read of the Romans: that formerly, when they fought to acquire glory, to preserve the republic, and to achieve liberty, they always conquered; but after they turned to avarice, they were defeated.

->¶ Concerning the Varus and the Sylarus mocking other animals. Chapter 51.<-

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration. On the left side, a monkey-like creature sits on the ground and reaches out to touch the muzzle of a dog or wolf-like animal. In the center, there is a nest containing several small birds. Behind the nest, another dog-like creature and an owl are visible. On the right, a dog or wolf stands on its hind legs with its paws placed on the chest of a large owl.</image-desc>

Although the **Varus** <note>likely an ermine or a stoat, prized for its "vair" fur</note> is small, it is a most excellent animal because of the nobility of its skin; likewise also the **Sylarus** <note>possibly a type of marten or squirrel</note>. These two formed a partnership, saying: "We are exalted and similarly valued above all others because of the worth of our skins. Since we are noble, let us persecute the vile and the despised." 

Therefore, they together found a toad <note>original: "buffonem"</note>, whom they began to harass. To them the toad said: "God made us, and not we ourselves. If you are more beautiful, praise your Creator." But they, being disturbed, plucked out its eyes, saying: "Do you dare to speak against us, you hideous thing?" Finally, they found another toad making a nest in a tree...

--- Page 127 ---
<meta>This page continues the moral fables exploring social hierarchy and justice. The previous page ended with a transition into a story about a toad (buffo) and those who mocked him, illustrating the dangers of arrogance and the true nature of nobility.</meta>

...striving, those who hissed after him fled, and they destroyed his eggs and his nest. The Toad <note>original: "Buffo"; while usually a toad, the text describes him flying, suggesting a legendary creature or a slip in the scribe's terminology</note>, however, flew up sadly, saying: "You have done evil, brothers, for they are God’s creatures just as you are. But another, different from you, shall avenge me." Eventually, they found a Monkey playing in a tree. They said to him: "Why do you shame us, you who are worse than everyone because you have no tail and show us your backside?" The Monkey, however, climbed down from the tree and disemboweled them, saying: "There are noble courtiers, and there are ignoble mockers." Therefore, the noble and the powerful, on account of their own magnificence, ought never to harass the humble and the poor; for nobility is not found in the high rank of one's parents, but in the composition of one's character. Whence Prosper <note>likely Prosper of Aquitaine, a 5th-century Christian writer</note> says: "Nobility is found in manners." And Augustine says: "Nobility is that alone which adorns the soul with good character." It is commonly said: "He is a peasant <note>original: "Villanus", referring to a low-born rustic or serf</note> who performs a villainy, not he who is born in a village." It is read that a certain lion in his youth had done many evil things. Indeed, he had wounded with his claws a boar, a bull, and a young donkey. It happened, however, that he reached old age, and being chilled in the winter time, he fell into the snow, unable to get out by himself. Seeing this, the boar remembered his wound and struck him heavily with his tusks. Likewise, the bull pierced the lion’s belly with his horns; the donkey truly struck him heavily in the forehead with his hooves. The lion, then, yielding to his pain, said: "The things I once conquered now conquer me; my strength sleeps. My honor and the work of my honor have receded. Behold: those whom I harmed now harm me; what I did to others now descends upon me."

### ¶ Concerning the Horse, the Boar, and the Mule. Chapter 61.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting four animals in a landscape. On the left are a donkey and a horse facing right. In the center, a wild boar runs toward the left. On the right, a horse is depicted in a galloping or rearing pose above a large stylized leaf.</image-desc>

A horse was in a long dispute <note>original: "questionabatur", referring to a legal or verbal dispute</note> with a boar, but because of the length of the dispute, the horse exhausted his resources. Therefore, he went to the mule, asking him to lend him ten marks <note>a "mark" was a traditional unit of weight for precious metals, used here as currency</note>. The horse recovered himself with these ten marks and, winning the dispute, gained much wealth. Later, the mule sought his marks back, just as they had been lent. But the horse, because of his own rudeness, insulted the mule, saying: "You hybrid <note>original: "Adultrine", literally 'adulterous' or 'bastard', referring to the mule being the crossbred offspring of a horse and a donkey</note>, are you not ashamed to ask such things of me?" Because of this, the mule was disturbed and summoned him before a judge. The horse, however, inflamed with anger, called out to the donkey and the camel, and with these he fought violently. The judge, knowing this, pronounced such a sentence: that the money should be restored quickly, and these animals, because of their violence and striking, should pay one hundred marks. The camel, however, having nothing with which to pay, left the city and was banished. The donkey was also placed in the stocks and in prison...

<meta>This page continues the discourse on the virtues of poverty versus the corruption of wealth, transitioning from historical examples of Roman and Macedonian discipline into a new fable about the arrogance of those who possess outward beauty or status, represented by the fur-bearing Varus and Sylarus.</meta>

...rich in power because the rich man wished to live among the poor. And he provides an example regarding the consuls who were so poor by God’s will while they were in office that they did not have the means to provide dowries for their daughters' marriages. However, the poverty of these illustrious men was aided by the Senate, and the Senate was accustomed to honorably provide for the marriage of their daughters. Whence Augustine says in an epistle: <note>original: "epl'a. v"; referring to Augustine’s Epistola 5 or similar correspondence</note> Those who act more wisely say that one should grieve more that poverty has perished than that wealth has.

For Rome, in its poverty, preserved its integrity of character. But wealth, like a harvest of men, corrupted them worse than any enemy. Whence it is read that the army of Alexander, having defeated Darius, was enriched beyond measure with spoils. Shortly after, when they engaged with the enemy again, they were defeated. Seeing this, Alexander ordered all the spoils that the soldiers <note>original: "serui"; literally "servants" or "slaves," but here referring to the common soldiers</note> had acquired to be burned, saying: "As long as they were without money, there was no one who could resist them. But burdened with gold and silver, they have become lazy and sluggish." Once these were lost, they fought bravely as before. Thus it is read of the Romans: that formerly when they fought for the acquisition of glory, for the preservation of the republic, and for the attainment of liberty, they always conquered; but after they turned to greed, they were defeated.

### ¶ Chapter 60: Concerning the Varus and the Sylarus Mocking Other Animals. <note>original: "Capitūlū. L. x."; L.x. is the Roman numeral for 60</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting various animals in a landscape. On the left, a monkey sits and interacts with a slender, spotted animal (a varus or stoat). In the center, a nest containing four small owlets is watched over by two larger owls. On the right, a third large owl is being attacked at the face by a spotted animal standing on its hind legs. Another spotted animal stands in the middle foreground, looking toward the right. The ground is indicated by simple tufts of grass.</image-desc>

The **Varus** <note>original: "Varus"; likely referring to a variety of stoat or ermine prized for its fur</note>, although it is small, is a most excellent animal on account of the nobility of its skin; likewise the **Sylarus** <note>original: "sylarus"; possibly a pine marten or similar fur-bearing creature</note>. These two established a partnership, saying: "We are elevated and likewise valued above all others because of the worth of our skins. Since we are noble, let us persecute the vile and despised." 

Wherefore they together found a **Toad** <note>original: "buffonē"</note> and began to harass him. To whom the Toad said: "God made us, and not we ourselves. <note>A paraphrase of Psalm 100:3</note> If you are more beautiful, praise your Creator." They, however, being provoked, plucked out his eyes, saying: "Do you dare to speak against us, you deformed creature?" Finally, they found another Toad making a nest in a tree... <note>The text breaks off here as the Toad's "nest" likely refers to a hole or shelter, or continues the theme of the "flying Toad" from the previous page's fable.</note>

The Toad, however, arrived flying sadly, saying: "You have done an evil thing, brothers, for I am God's creature just as you are. But another shall avenge me against you." Finally, they found a Monkey <note>original: "symia"</note> playing in a tree. They said to him: "Why do you shame us, you who are worse than everyone else because you have no tail and show us your hindquarters?" Therefore, the noble and the powerful, because of their own magnificence, should by no means harass the humble and the poor. For nobility does not consist in the status of one's parents, but in the composure of one's character. Whence the philosopher says: "Nobility is found in manners." <note>original: "Nobilitas ē i moꝛibus"</note> And Augustine says: "Nobility alone is that which adorns the soul with good character." <note>original: "Nobilitas sola ē q̄ aiuz moꝛibus oꝛnat"</note> Commonly it is said: "A villain <term>villain: originally meaning a farm laborer or someone from a 'villa' (village), here used to distinguish social class from moral behavior</term> is he who performs villainy, not he who is born in a village." 

It is read that a certain lion in his youth had done many evil things. He had wounded a boar, a bull, and a little donkey with his claws. However, it happened that he reached old age, and in the winter time, frozen by the cold, he fell into the snow and was unable to get out by himself. Seeing this, the boar remembered his own wound and struck the lion heavily with his tusks. Similarly, the bull pierced the lion's belly with his horns, and the little donkey struck him heavily in the forehead with his hooves. The lion, acknowledging his suffering, said: "The things I once conquered now conquer me; my strength sleeps; the reward and work of good things have receded. Behold, he whom I harmed now harms me; the things I did to others descend upon me."

# Concerning the horse, the boar, and the mule. Chapter 61.

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration showing four animals in profile within a simple landscape. On the far left is a donkey or mule with long ears. To its right is a horse. In the center, a wild boar with tusks is shown mid-stride. On the far right, another horse stands facing left. A large, multi-lobed leaf lies on the ground in the foreground.</image-desc>

A Horse disputed with a Boar for a long time, but because of the length of the dispute, the Horse wasted his resources. Therefore, he went to the Mule, asking him to lend him ten marks. <note>original: "marchas"; a medieval unit of weight or currency</note> With these ten marks, the Horse recovered his position and, winning the dispute, gained a great deal. 

Afterward, the Mule asked for his marks back, as they had been lent at interest. But the Horse, because of his own rudeness, disparaged the Mule, saying: "You bastard! <note>original: "Auultrine"; likely a corruption of "adulterine," a slur against the mule as a hybrid animal</note> Are you not ashamed to ask such things of me?" Enraged by this, the Mule summoned him before a judge. The Horse, however, became inflamed with anger and called upon the Donkey and the Camel, and with them, he violently maligned the Mule. The judge, knowing this, pronounced a sentence: that the money must be restored quickly, and that these others, because of their violence and the blow they dealt, should pay a hundred marks. The Camel, having nothing with which to pay, left the city and was banished. The Donkey, also, was placed in the stocks and in prison, where his life...

--- Page 130 ---
<meta>This page continues from the conclusion of a dispute between a horse and a mule (Chapter 61). The previous page ended with an old lion being attacked by animals he once tormented, but the final meta-note suggested a jump to a legal conflict where a horse is ultimately stripped of its burden.</meta>

...ended miserably. But the horse, now set loose and stripped of his burden <note>original: "bānuz," likely referring to a pack or harness</note>, remained, saying: "If someone owes and does not repay, it is just if he meets an evil end." In this way also many are found to be ungrateful, who do not recognize the benefits they have received nor return thanks to their benefactors. Against such people, Cato <note>Dionysius Cato, the traditional author of a popular medieval collection of moral sayings</note> says: "Be mindful of a benefit received." <note>original: "Bñficij accepti memoᷣ esto"</note> He also says: "When a poor friend gives you a small gift, accept it calmly and always remember to praise it." <note>original: "Exiguū mun⁹ cũ dat tibi pauper amicus. Accipito placide sēp̄ laudare memento."</note> But many today do not only fail to praise, but indeed act falsely against their benefactors. 

Whence it is read that when a certain king elevated a particular friend of his above all others, that friend began to think within himself, saying: "This king has burdened me with so much favor that I shall never be free of the debt." For this reason, he began to wish for the king's death. But the king, knowing this, summoned him; and once the truth was discovered, he judged that the man should be dragged through the city. While he was being dragged, everyone threw filth upon him. He truly sustained all things patiently, because in the time of his prosperity he had never been kind to anyone, but rather proud and hostile to all. 

However, when a certain friend of his, whom he had loved as himself, threw a stone at him and it slightly struck his toe, the man cried out and was greatly saddened. When he was later presented before the king, the king said to the ministers: "How did this wretched man behave during his suffering?" They responded: "Wisely enough; but he lamented excessively over one particular blow." The king then questioned the man: "Why did you grieve so much for that?" To whom he replied: "Because I had elevated that man above all others, and from him I hoped for help, not torment." To which the king said: "By your own mouth I judge you, servant, for you acted in exactly this way against me." <note>A reference to the Parable of the Talents in the Gospel of Luke 19:22: "Out of thine own mouth will I judge thee, thou wicked servant."</note> Wherefore he said to the ministers: "Go and hang him on the gallows as he deserves." 

For even men who possess reason are often ungrateful, while even irrational animals recognize benefits bestowed upon them. It is read that while a certain lion was caught in a hunter's ropes, a certain shepherd, feeling compassion for it, released it from its bonds. Later, this same lion was caught by the emperor's hunters and enclosed in a cage with other beasts. Into that same cage, the shepherd was thrown to be devoured by the beasts because of some crime. But the lion, recognizing him, not only did not harm him but protected him unharmed from the other animals. 

Likewise, it is read that a certain knight found a lion fighting with a huge serpent which had already wound itself around the lion's neck and was striving to kill it with poison. The knight, recognizing the nobility of the lion and the malice of the serpent, cut off the serpent's head. The lion, following him like a domestic animal, never left him. And for the sake of his deliverer, the lion fought many battles against the knight's enemies.

--- Page 131 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the legal and moral consequences of ingratitude (illustrated by the story of the king and his ungrateful friend) into a series of fables regarding contentment, the dangers of seeking "greener pastures," and the risks of choosing a powerful but predatory protector.</meta>

An ox and an ass lived together with a certain peasant, who fed them splendidly; but at times they grew weary because they were goaded <note>original: "aculeaba[n]t[ur]" — pricked with a spur or pointed stick to keep them moving</note>. On a certain occasion, however, the peasant struck the ass, who, being troubled, went to the ox and said: "What shall we do? This ox-driver beats us and tires us out. Let us flee from him and remain without the yoke, while we are still young and can enjoy ourselves the most."

Now, while they were leaving the house and leaping through the pastures, they rejoiced exceedingly. Meanwhile, the dark night arrived, and they complained together, saying: "How shall we sleep? We have no stable as we used to. It was certainly better to endure than to leave." While they were lying upon the ground, wishing to fall asleep, behold, Isengrin <note>original: "ysengrin[us]" — the traditional name for the wolf in medieval beast fables, such as the Reynard the Fox cycle</note> began to roar and the ox began to grow uneasy, so that they did not sleep at all through the whole night, saying: "It is not good to remain like this; let us join ourselves with the flocks, for we shall be protected by the shepherds and the dogs."

And when they approached the flock, wishing to eat and spend the night with them, the shepherds drove them away and beat them with clubs. They, truly weeping, made an agreement with another peasant who burdened them even more and fed them poorly. Wherefore, being struck with remorse, they returned to their former state, saying: "It is better to stay at home than to wander through the world."

In this, we can recognize that it is a danger to travel abroad, to leave one's own place, and to be a guest in another. For Ecclesiasticus <note>original: "eccle." — referring to the Book of Sirach/Ecclesiasticus 29:24</note> says: "A wretched life is that of a guest, moving from house to house." It is also better and more useful to suffer some hardship than to endure a greater one. And when someone does something good, he ought always to consider what might happen to him. Whence the wise man says: "Whatever you do, do it prudently and consider the end." <note>original: "Quicq[ui]d agas prudenter agas & respice fine[m]"</note> I wish to bear the lesser things, lest I bear the heavier <note>original: "Ferre minora volo ne grauiora fera[m]"</note>.

Whence it is said that a certain kite <note>original: "miluus" — a type of bird of prey</note> was severely harassing some pigeons, snatching away their young. The pigeons sometimes defended themselves and, by striking him, he would retreat and not snatch them. At last, they held a council and accepted for themselves as king a hawk <note>original: "ancipitre[m]" — or accipiter</note> to drive back the wars of the kite. But the King began to do more harm than the enemy. Every day he took one of the most precious ones and ate it. The pigeons then began to complain about the king and say that it would be healthier to suffer the wars of the kite than to die as soldiers <note>The Latin "miltitate" is likely a scribal error for "militantes" or related to "mactari" (to be slaughtered)</note> and without defense. It can also be seen in this that it is better to have bitter things with peace and security than delightful things with danger and anxiety.

As Aesop relates, a country mouse came to the town and was well received by a town mouse. The town mouse truly praised the expenses of the city and led him to the bishop's cellar, sharing his delicacies with him. Meanwhile, the keys rattled and the steward entered. The town mouse fled to his usual hole. The country mouse, however, crashed into the wall and barely escaped alive. At last, they came together and the country mouse said: "I prefer to gnaw on beans and return to gnawing them. Keep these good things for yourself, which are joys for you alone. A modest wealth enriches the poor; a simple life's diet provides a companion for my feasts."

Each story emphasizes that security and contentment in one's current state are preferable to the dangers of seeking a more "luxurious" or "free" life.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the theme of the dangers of isolation and the difficulty of changing one's inherent nature, following the previous page's story about the ox and the ass who regretted leaving their master’s protection.</meta>

A large flock was gathered together and was eating in the pastures. A wether <note>original: "veruēz" — a castrated ram</note>, that is to say, a ram, called out to a goat in the pastures, saying: "Let us go out from the flock; why do we remain here so basely?" And when they went out from the flock and spurned their society, a rapacious wolf who was lying in wait leaped out at them, bit them, and tore them apart, saying: "It is better to be united than to remain set apart." In this it appears that he who spurns society spurns security. Because Ecclesiastes says: "Woe to him who is alone, for if he falls, he will have no one to lift him up" <note>original: "Ve soli. qz si ceciderit nō habebit sublevantem" — a reference to Ecclesiastes 4:10</note>. A certain bird had many chicks whom she eagerly supported. When a kite <note>a medium-sized bird of prey</note> arrived, however, the bird called the chicks to her so that she might protect them from the predator. Indeed, all who fled to her were protected and defended. One of them, however, wished to peck for food by himself alone and did not care for the group. Wherefore the predator snatched him and carried him away.

## ¶ Concerning the Panther and the Pig. Chapter 114.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration shows a striped pig on the left and a spotted leopard-like animal (panther) on the right. Both animals are depicted in profile, standing on a ground line with horizontal hatching and stylized flowers featuring five pointed petals. The pig appears to be moving toward the panther.</image-desc>

The panther <note>In medieval bestiaries, the panther was often described as a beautiful, multicolored animal with a sweet-smelling breath that attracted other creatures</note> is an animal of various colors, beautiful and sweet-smelling, but once it was tempted to eat of a pig. Because it desired a delicacy and abhorred the unclean, it raised one pig for itself, making a very clean bed for it; it very often cleaned the pig and did not permit it to wallow in the mud. The sow, however, was troubled by this, for she desired more to roll in the mud and be foul. On a certain occasion, the sow, rushing out of the house with others, made herself completely foul, because of which the panther abhorred and rejected the unclean pig, saying: "To change someone from their nature is a hard task" <note>original: "Remutare de natura aliquē est pena dura"</note>. For so do sinners and the unclean act. Wherefore they are not able to cast off a bad habit and come out of the mud of concupiscence <note>strong desire, particularly lust</note> and luxury. Because as the philosopher <note>referring to Aristotle</note> said: "Habit is a second nature," and therefore one should "resist the beginnings" <note>original: "principijs obstat" — a quote from the Roman poet Ovid, suggesting it is easier to stop a vice at the very start</note>. For temptation in its birth is like a small twig which can be quickly pulled out before it grows into a tree through age. But as the gloss <note>The *Glossa Ordinaria*, a standard medieval biblical commentary</note> says, the sin of habit is like a long-standing sickness which is cured with difficulty. Wherefore Jerome <note>Saint Jerome, quoting the prophet Jeremiah</note> says: "Can the Ethiopian change his skin or the leopard his spots? So also you can do good when you have learned to do evil." It is read concerning the Emperor Vespasian <note>Roman Emperor (69–79 AD), often characterized in medieval literature by his legendary greed</note> that since he was greedy in this habit now as an old man, someone said to him in a parable that a fox can change its coat but not its spirit. To such men, we should offer a response of laughter, while ensuring for ourselves a full correction.

## ¶ Concerning the wild ass and the boar arguing before the lion. Chapter 115.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicts a wild boar on the left and a wild ass on the right, standing before a lion seated on an elaborate, multi-tiered throne. The lion has a human-like face and mane, and the throne is flanked by two stylized towers or turrets. A handwritten label "Lion" in a later ink script is visible on the base of the throne.</image-desc>

<insert>Lion</insert>

Papias <note>original: "Papias" — a renowned 11th-century lexicographer</note> says the wild ass <note>original: "onager"</note> is a donkey of the wilderness. And the boar <note>original: "aper"</note> is a forest pig. These two found the Lord <note>referring to the Lion, acting as the sovereign judge of the animals</note> while wandering in the forest. They said: "Lord, help us and give a righteous judgment concerning us." The Lord asked them: "What do you want me to do for you?" 

The ass said to him: "My master has deceived me. For he keeps a tiny dog who feeds on the master’s own food and sleeps in his bed. The master himself pets him and plays with him, but he has never touched me nor shown me affection—I who carry a heavy pack every day and wear myself out in his service." And the Lord said: "Wild ass, if you wish to be touched and looked upon with pleasure, be stainless <note>original: "imaculatus"</note> and not covered in filth. For it is because the dog remains clean <note>original: "mundus"</note> that he stays joyfully with his master." 

The boar also said: "My master keeps a nightingale <note>original: "philomena"</note> near him in a gilded cage, and he sings with her and rejoices greatly. But he does not listen to me with pleasure when I let out my grunt; likewise, my companion's voice is not heard. Therefore we ask: set some order in these matters." To this the Lord responded: "You do not know what you are asking. If you indeed desire to be heard and understood, you ought to have refined words, not foul ones, and not a rough voice. Because the nightingale speaks gently and sings, she is heard with gratitude; but you are not so." They truly fell silent, saying: "Let us not seek what is not permitted; let us not speak of what we do not know." 

Therefore, we too, if we desire to be looked upon and touched with pleasure, let us strive to be stainless, not polluted or defiled. Furthermore, if we wish to be heard and understood, let us speak gentle words, not shameful or vile ones. For there are some whose throat is like a grave that reeks when it is opened. Of these the Psalmist says: "Their throat is an open sepulcher" <note>original: "Sepulchruʒ patens est guttur eorū" — Psalm 5:9</note>, referring to wicked men because they always speak foul things. Regarding them, the same Psalmist says: "May the Lord destroy all deceitful lips and the boastful tongue" <note>original: "Disperdat dominus vniuersa labia dolosa & linguā magniloquā" — Psalm 12:3</note>, namely those who speak against God and their neighbor. For those who speak such injurious and insulting words are not to be listened to. According to that saying of Seneca <note>the Roman Stoic philosopher</note>: "Ears must be closed to evil voices." 

Diogenes the philosopher <note>the famous Cynic philosopher known for his honesty and poverty</note>, when someone reported something bad about him supposedly proven by a friend, said: "It is doubtful whether my friend said this, but it is certain to me that you are speaking ill." Antisthenes <note>a pupil of Socrates and founder of Cynicism</note>, when someone said to him, "That man said this evil thing about you," replied: "He did not say this of me, but of the one who recognizes such traits in himself." Aristippus <note>a philosopher of pleasure and student of Socrates</note>—who was wise as a Solomon—responded to someone saying to him: "As you are the master of your tongue, so I am master of my ears." It was as if he were saying: "Just as you can speak evil with your tongue, so I can place a barrier upon my ears." To another person saying to him, "Evil is being spoken of you," he responded: "I do not care."

<summary>This page uses a fable about a wild ass and a boar complaining to a lion to teach moral lessons on the importance of cleanliness, refined speech, and the stoic dismissal of slander.</summary>
<keywords>wild ass, boar, lion, Papias, nightingale, cleanliness, speech, slander, Diogenes, Seneca, Aristippus, Solomon</keywords>

--- Page 134 ---
<meta>This page concludes Chapter 115, which uses animal fables and historical anecdotes to teach the virtue of patience in the face of slander. It then transitions to Chapter 116, discussing the salamander and the hydra.</meta>

... because hearing ought to be stronger than the tongue. Since every man has but a single tongue, yet two ears, it follows that a man should endure with his two ears more than anyone can speak with a single tongue. It is told in the book *The Trifles of the Philosophers* <note>original: "de nugis philoꝝ" — likely referring to the *Policraticus* by John of Salisbury, a popular medieval source for stories about the ancients</note> that Titus <note>Titus Vespasianus, Roman Emperor known for his virtue</note> responded to one who was slandering him: "It is easy to speak ill of me, but I am not going to respond." In the same book, it is told that the philosopher Zeno responded to one slandering him: "You have learned how to speak evil, and I have learned how to keep silent, for I know how to despise an insult." Note that the endurance of insults and reproachful words was common among the ancients. 

We read of Alexander <note>Alexander the Great</note>, as it says in the third book of *The Trifles of the Philosophers*, that when Antigonus said to him: "It is time for your age to rule," he meant to say: "Are you not ashamed that the king’s will in your body is dominated by luxury?" or "You are unworthy of the kingdom by reason of your youth and your pursuit of pleasure." And yet, Alexander bore this most patiently. In the same place, it is told of the patience of Julius Caesar: for when he took his baldness <note>original: "caluiciū"</note> poorly and would pull his thinning hair from the back of his head toward his forehead, a soldier said to him: "It is easier for you, O Caesar, not to be bald than for me to have done or to do anything fearsome in the Roman service." Caesar sustained this with compassion. Furthermore, even when famous pamphlets and mocking songs were publicly circulated against him, he bore these reproaches with great patience. When a certain matron, despising his origins, called him a "cloth-maker" <note>original: "pannificum"</note>, he bore it as a joke. 

In the same place, it is told of Augustus Caesar that when someone said to him, "O tyrant," he replied, "If I were one, you would not say so." It is also told there of Scipio Africanus <note>The Roman general who defeated Hannibal</note> that when someone called him a "fighter by force" <note>implying he was a common brawler rather than a skilled leader</note> and argued with him based on this, the General responded: "My mother bore me to be a commander <note>original: "imperatoꝝ"</note>, not a common brawler." We also read of Augustus Caesar in the fourth book of *The Trifles of the Philosophers* that when Tiberius complained to him that many people were speaking ill of him, Augustus replied: "It is enough if we have this much power: that no one is able to actually do us harm. Therefore, do not be indignant if someone has spoken ill of you." 

Regarding the patience of Antigonus, Seneca <note>A Roman Stoic philosopher</note> tells in the third book of *On Anger* <note>original: "de ira"</note> that when the king heard some people thinking and speaking ill of him—for he was unknown to those speaking—he merely raised his hand slightly toward them and said: "Depart from here, lest the king hear you." Seneca also relates in the fourth book of *On Anger* regarding the same King Antigonus that when he heard some of his soldiers one day cursing him with every kind of evil, he approached those who were working the hardest. When he had heard them, and they were unaware of who was listening, he admonished them, saying: "Now you have spoken ill of Antigonus, but now you should wish him well, for he loves you." Indeed, the humility of the king was marvelous, as he did not disdain to stoop to those laboring, and his patience was marvelous, as he was not indignant toward those slandering him.

### ¶ Concerning the salamander and the hydra serpent. Chapter 116.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting four creatures arranged in pairs. On the left side stand two large birds with hooked beaks and detailed feathers, resembling eagles or vultures. On the right side stand two dragons or wyverns with scales, wings, and coiled, serpentine tails. In the center foreground, on the ground between the two groups of animals, are two small, indistinct shapes, possibly representing the salamander and hydra mentioned in the following chapter title.</image-desc>

<meta>This page continues the sequence of animal allegories used for moral instruction. The previous page discussed the patience of ancient rulers like Alexander and Caesar. This page concludes the story of the salamander and water-snake before introducing a tale about demonic influence and a new chapter on the legal folly of lending money.</meta>

The salamander is a venomous animal living in fire; the water-snake <term>hydrus (water-snake)</term> however is a poisonous serpent living in the waters. These two used to fight one another, but whenever the salamander was being overcome by the water-snake, it would throw itself into the fire to escape. When, on the other hand, the water-snake was being overcome by the salamander, it would cast itself into the water. Thus they preserved themselves and did not perish, saying: "He who returns to his own home is neither put to flight nor destroyed." So we, too, ought to protect ourselves. For when we are attacked by infernal enemies with the fire of concupiscence <note>original: "concupiscẽtie" — excessive or worldly desire</note> and luxury, we ought to cast ourselves into the waters of chastity and purity. When, however, we are attacked by the water of greed and avarice, we ought to throw ourselves into the fire of charity and generosity. 

As we read in the example of the dove regarding the properties of birds <note>original: "de proprietatib⁹ auium" — likely a reference to a medieval encyclopedia like those by Bartholomaeus Anglicus</note>, there are certain hawks <term>ancipitres (birds of prey)</term> that do not seize a bird except in the air, and there are others that do not take them except on the ground. This dove naturally recognizes this, and therefore, if she is chased by a hawk that catches in the air, she immediately drops herself to the earth. If she is pursued by a hawk that catches on the ground, she keeps herself in the air, and thus she escapes. So we too ought to have the wit to act against the infernal hawk <note>a metaphor for the Devil</note> who has a thousand arts of harming. Whence Paulinus <note>Paulinus of Nola, a 4th-century Christian poet</note> says: "Our enemy, to whom there belong a thousand arts of harming, must be fought by as many various means as he attacks with snares." And therefore it is said my enemy who pursues me has a thousand arts of harming. 

It is said that a certain demoniac <note>a person possessed by a demon</note> was led to a certain holy man, who commanded the demon to come out and state its name. And the demon replied: "We are three who inhabit this man. I am called 'Closing the Heart'; my other brother is called 'Closing the Mouth'; and the third is called 'Closing the Purse'." For my duty is to harden the sinner's heart so that he cannot have contrition, and if by chance he feels contrition, my brother works to prevent him from confession. And if by chance he confesses, the third prevents him from making satisfaction <note>the act of penance or making amends for a sin</note>. And this third is called "Closing the Purse." And through this method, we gain nearly everyone.

## ¶ Concerning the Ape and the Badger. Chapter 117.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicts a monkey on the left and a badger on the right. They are standing on a flat ground facing each other. Between them is a pile of small, round objects resembling coins or stones. The badger's paw is extended toward the pile.</image-desc>

The badger <term>taxus (badger)</term> is an animal that sleeps a great deal. An ape approached him, saying: "Brother, lend me a hundred marks <note>a medieval unit of weight for silver or gold, used as currency</note>, for I desire to trade in the regions beyond the mountains and faithfully divide the profit with you." To which the badger replied: "I will do this gladly, since I greatly desire to rest, if you give me guarantors <term>fideiussores (legal sureties)</term> and create a legal instrument <term>istrumẽtũ (formal document)</term> by the hand of a notary." 

The ape then appointed a buffalo and a bull as guarantors, leaving the signed deed <term>cyrographũ (chirograph)</term> in the hands of Sanipes <note>original: "sanipedis" — possibly a reference to a specific official or a transcription error for a legal clerk</note>; the ape then traveled to her own regions and never returned. The badger, seeing that he had been mocked by the ape, sought the lent funds from the guarantors. They, however, hissed at him and despised him greatly. The badger then sought a judgment, showing the deed. The judge therefore caused the guarantors to be summoned and proclaimed by sentence that they must restore what they had been bound to by the document. They, being indignant, always persecuted the badger and at the last struck him severely. The wounded badger said: "He who does not desire to lend never desires to litigate." So also it happens many times that a good man loses friends when he seeks to recover his debts. Whence the Psalmist says: "A joyful man who has mercy..."

<meta>This page continues the series of animal fables used for moral and spiritual instruction. While the previous page focused on the physical safety found in returning to one's nature (the salamander and the dove), this page shifts focus to the necessity of heeding wise counsel from elders and the triumph of intelligence over brute strength.</meta>

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'A' featuring floral and foliate motifs within a square border.</image-desc>

Five lambs <note>original: "Agni quinq;"</note> were left by their parents like wards and orphans, and they were ruled by their relatives and tutors. On a certain occasion, however, a she-wolf called one of them secretly to herself. "I desire to have compassion for you as an orphan," she said. "Therefore, I wish to teach you your letters <note>original: "litterā insinuare" — likely meaning basic education or 'the way of the world'</note> so that you may succeed. Truly, go first to your brothers and tell them to come with you to me, to be strengthened with me." The innocent lamb rejoiced with very great joy and went to his brothers and shared all these things. They said, "This pleases us very much. But let us go first to our tutors so that they may advise us." The tutors said to them: "Beware of such a teacher, for if you return to him, he will slit your throats." They, however, not paying attention to the counsel of their elders, went to the she-wolf. When the she-wolf saw them, she was made joyful; leaping upon them, she slit their throats and fed upon them with her cubs, saying: "He who does not believe his elders goes badly and stumbles." Therefore, we ought to believe in the counsel of our seniors. As Job says: "In the ancient is wisdom, and in length of time is prudence." <note>A reference to Job 12:12</note> We ought to keep the counsel given to us, especially by friends. For the Preacher <note>original: "eccle." — referring to the Book of Ecclesiastes or Ecclesiasticus (Sirach)</note> says: "Keep counsel, and it shall be your life and your grace upon your lips."

It is also said that a certain farmer worked the land, sowing flax seed in it. Seeing this, a learned and old swallow <term>yrundo (swallow)</term> gathered all the birds together, saying: "This field and this seed threaten us with evil chains <note>original: "mala vincula" — referring to the nets made from flax</note>. Therefore, let us destroy them before they grow." They did not wish to listen, but instead rebuked her, saying and reproaching her with various insults. Meanwhile, the herb grew out of the ground, and the swallow again warned that dangers were pressing. But the birds mocked her again, saying that the swallow was only trying to please men and flatter them with her song. Meanwhile, the flax was harvested and nets <note>original: "rbetia," a likely OCR error for "retia"</note> were made, with which the birds were captured. Then the birds recognized their error and repented, but it was too late. Hence, he who scorns useful counsel receives what is useless, and he who feels too safe rightly falls into the nets. And note that sometimes the counsel of the lowliest person is most useful, and that wit is worth more than strength; hence the verse: "Intelligence overcomes strength, and the greatest wisdom belongs to the wise." <note>original: "Ingenium superat vires et summa prudentia est prudenti"</note>

As it is written, an eagle flying through the air and seeing a moving tortoise <term>testitudo (tortoise)</term> lowered itself upon it and seized it with its feet. But the tortoise pulled its feet and neck into its shell, so that the eagle could in no way eat what it wanted from it. But a certain crow, seeing this, said: "Although you are powerful as the king of birds, you do not know everything by yourself. Listen safely to my counsel regarding what it carries in its shell: it is very good food for you. Break the shell by carrying it up high <note>and dropping it</note>." The eagle did so, and ate with sweetness.

### ¶ Concerning many reptiles and the basilisk. Chapter 70.

Many reptiles were comforting themselves upon the ground in the sun, but the basilisk <term>basiliscus (a mythical king of serpents)</term> leapt out into their midst, crying: "Whoever desires to leap with me in war, let him come and I will fight with him." A tortoise, however, came forward into the midst to fight with him. While they were fighting together, the basilisk desired to bite and poison it. But the tortoise pulled its head...
