[He] put his feet into a horn, and afterwards drew them out and stung the basilisk <note>original: "basiliscum" — a mythical serpent king said to kill with its gaze or breath</note> with teeth and claws. Thus, the defeated serpent was put to shame. A little while later, it made itself terrifying once again and, wishing to extend its victory, it said: "I wish to avenge myself; if there is any warrior among you, let him come to me, and I shall take care of him." 

The prickly hedgehog <term>ericius</term> <note>From the Latin "ericius," the root of the modern word "erinaceous," meaning relating to hedgehogs.</note>, hearing this, went forth to war fully armed. When the serpent saw him, it attacked him with the greatest fury; but the hedgehog, strengthening himself, sharpened his quills <note>original: "pennas suas" — literally "his feathers," a common medieval term for a hedgehog's spines</note> and fixed them into the serpent, wounding him until he bled. Indeed, the basilisk, as if confounded by grief, began to fail. 

Meanwhile, a frog <term>rana</term>, seeing this and desiring to avenge itself upon the serpent, climbed upon him, wishing to finish him off. Therefore the serpent, observing the frog while he was weary and famished, gathered all his strength, seized her, and swallowed her, saying: "He fights poorly who is not prepared nor well armed."

In this it is noted that when we see our stronger enemies somewhat suppressed, we ought not to attack them immediately, but rather wait and see the end of the matter. **Seneca** <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca the Younger, a Roman Stoic philosopher often quoted in medieval moral texts.</note> says: "It is a learned thing to fear an enemy, however humble." Therefore, we ought not to rejoice over the death of an enemy, lest by chance similar things happen to us. Whence the **Preacher** <note>original: "eccle." — referring here to the Book of Ecclesiastes or similar wisdom literature.</note> says: "If your enemy falls, do not congratulate yourself; nor let your heart rejoice over the death of an enemy, lest similar things come upon you." For he who rejoices in the fall of an enemy quickly falls into it himself. 

It is read that a certain Emperor had two craftsmen: one a tailor <term>sartorem</term> and the other a barber <term>barbitonsorem</term>. The cutter of cloths <note>original: "incisor pannorum" — another term for a tailor.</note> hated the barber because the Emperor honored the barber more. Therefore, he accused him before the Emperor, claiming that the barber was slandering him by saying he could not bear the stench of the Emperor's breath while shaving his beard. 

Hearing this, the Emperor was indignant and ordered him to be thrown into the sea with a stone around his neck. However, while this was being signaled to the sailors, the Emperor lost his ring, which fell into the sea. The barber, meanwhile, bribed the sailors with money and traveled with them to other distant nations where he prospered. Buying a fish, he found the Emperor's ring in its entrails; with this, he came to him and gave it to the Emperor, clearing himself of the crime charged against him. At last, as a reward, he asked that the cutter of cloths—who had rejoiced in his death—be sent in the same manner where he himself had been sent. And so he escaped, and the enemy who thought himself victorious perished. Whence **Seneca** says: "Expect from another what you have done to another." 

And this concludes the section concerning beasts.

# On the Nature of Man and Woman

**H**umanity <note>original: "Homo" — refers here to mankind or the male gender in a philosophical context</note> is, according to the philosopher <note>likely Aristotle, who was known simply as "The Philosopher" in medieval scholarship</note>, an incarnate mind, a phantom of time, a spectator of life, a slave to death, a passing traveler, a guest of a place, a hardworking animal, and a dwelling place for a short time.

**Woman** is, according to the philosopher, the confusion of man, an insatiable beast, a continuous anxiety, an unceasing battle, a human slave <note>original: "humanum mancipium"</note>, and the shipwreck of a continent man. Just as a certain chaste and immaculate man, because he wished to have conversation and familiarity with a woman, was ensnared and quickly lost the seal of chastity. For by attending to the sweetness of her words and looking upon the beauty of her face, his resolve was undone <note>original: "facies dissipatus est"</note>, and he said: "Because of women many are dead and wounded." 

Wherefore the sting <note>original: "aculeus" — likely referring to the "sting of sin"</note> says: woman is the form of sin and the condition of death. **Jerome** <note>St. Jerome, a 4th-century Church Father known for his asceticism</note> says: She is the gate of the devil, the way of iniquity, the sting of a scorpion, and a harmful race. The same author says that the appearance of a woman is a flaming sword. Remember that conversation with a woman cast man out of paradise. **Augustine** says: Woman is the most effective glue for catching souls. What was stronger than Samson? What was wiser than Solomon? What was holier than David? All these were subverted by lust. **Ecclesiasticus** <note>original: "eccle." — referring to the Book of Sirach 25:24</note> says: From woman came the beginning of sin, and through her we all die. 

Wherefore the ancients kept themselves away from them. As **Vegetius** <note>Publius Flavius Vegetius Renatus, though this anecdote is more commonly found in the works of Valerius Maximus or Quintus Curtius Rufus</note> relates in his second book concerning the self-restraint <term>continentia</term> <note>refers to the virtue of sexual self-control and moderation</note> of **Alexander**. When an exceptionally beautiful maiden was handed over to him, who was betrothed to a certain prince, he spared her with the highest abstinence, so that he would not even look upon her, but sent her back to her betrothed; by which release he reconciled the minds of the woman and the princes to himself. 

**Valerius** <note>Valerius Maximus, a Roman historian</note> relates a similar thing about **Scipio** in his tenth book, saying that when he understood that a maiden of exceptional beauty was betrothed to a certain nobleman among the hostages who were at Carthage—after Carthage had been captured by him—he called her parents and her betrothed and handed the inviolate maiden over to him. And the gold which had been offered for her ransom, he gave to the maiden as a dowry or to her husband as a wedding gift; through which munificence and self-restraint he won their hearts to his cause. 

Valerius also relates in the same book the wondrous self-restraint of **Xenocrates** the philosopher <note>a student of Plato known for his austere character</note>, saying...

In Athens, certain young men promised an unchaste woman money if she could bend the mind of a philosopher toward lust. Coming by night, she lay down next to him, but his restraint did not falter in any way. And when the young men mocked her because she had not been able to bend his mind, she replied that she had gone not to a man, but to a statue. For she called the philosopher a statue on account of his immovable self-restraint <note>original: "continentiam" — referring to his chastity and lack of physical response</note>.

## ¶ On Life and Death. Chapter 122.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration positioned to the right of the chapter's opening text. It depicts two figures standing on a hatched ground. On the left is a skeletal figure representing Death, with its ribcage and internal organs (intestines) clearly visible. On the right is a living man, gesturing with both hands and wearing a simple loincloth. The figures appear to be in dialogue.</image-desc>

Death is, according to the philosopher <note>likely Aristotle, or a general reference to Stoic thought</note>, an eternal sleep, the dread of the wealthy, the longing of the poor, an inescapable event, a thief of man, the flight of life, and the dissolution of man. Life, however, is the joy of the good, the sadness of the wretched, and the expectation of death. And because life is the expectation of death, one proceeds with prayers toward death and says: "O inevitable death, hear me and have mercy on me. Do not send upon me the punishment that I expect from you; take away gold and silver, precious stones, municipalities, horses, farms, estates, palaces, possessions, and whatever you wish; only do not touch me." 

To which Death replied: "You ask for impossibilities, O sister <note>Death addresses the personified Life as a sister</note>. Nothing should be sought from God except what is honorable and possible; therefore you have not spoken wisely." Because Death said to the man: "Death awaits you everywhere; and if you are wise, you will await it everywhere." For the Psalmist <note>referring to Psalm 89:48</note> says: *What man is he who lives and shall not see death?* <note>original: "Quis homo qui viuit ꝛ non videbit mortem"</note> as if to say no one is living who is not destined to die. From which comes the verse: *By no lot shall you be able to evade death. Death cuts, death kills what is dearly created.* <note>original: "Mors secat. mors necat quod care creatur"</note> Therefore, receive me patiently, because I have come to do nothing [new] to you. 

For Seneca <note>The Roman Stoic philosopher who often wrote on the "art of dying"</note> says: "No one is so ignorant that he does not know he will eventually die; yet when it approaches near, he trembles and weeps." Why do you weep? What do you hope for? You waste the efforts of the world which buys all things. What is new to you? You were born to this law. This happened to your father, this to your mother and your ancestors, this to all before you, and this to all after you. For life was given with the expectation of death. It is a universal law that commands being born and dying; you should understand this, moreover, by how you conduct your life. 

The same author says: "We must bear what we cannot avoid." Take the example of David concerning his dead son <note>King David in 2 Samuel 12</note>: "Since he is dead, why should I fast? Will I be able to call him back? I shall go to him rather, but he shall not return to me." Similarly, when the death of his son was announced to a philosopher, he said: "When I fathered him, I knew he was destined to die." Valerius <note>Valerius Maximus, a collector of historical anecdotes</note> narrates in his sixth book, saying that when Anaxagoras heard of his son's death, he replied: "You announce nothing unexpected or new; I knew he was born of me to be subject to the law of nature, to be accepted in hope and returned by law; and that no one dies who has not lived." Thus, neither does anyone live who is not destined to die. 

It is narrated in the same place about another philosopher, who, upon hearing of the death of his eldest son who had fallen in battle, was content to merely set aside his crown, for he was performing a solemn sacrifice. Then, having inquired how he had fallen, when he heard that he had died fighting most bravely, he replaced the crown upon his head.

<summary>This page explores the Stoic and medieval concept of "memento mori," using anecdotes of philosophers and King David to argue that death is a natural law that should be faced with calm acceptance rather than fear.</summary>
<keywords>Death, Life, Seneca, Anaxagoras, King David, Stoicism, mortality, self-restraint, Athens, Valerius Maximus</keywords>

Man is, according to the philosopher <note>original: "scdm phl̄m" (secundum philosophum) — likely referring to Aristotle or a general scholastic authority</note>, an incarnate mind, a phantom of time <note>original: "fasina [phasma] temporis"</note>, a watcher of life, a slave of death, a passing traveler, a guest of a place, a hardworking animal, and a dwelling of a short time. Woman is, according to the philosopher, the confusion of man, an insatiable beast, a continuous worry, an unending battle, a human slave <note>original: "mancipium" — a term for a bondservant or property</note>, and the shipwreck of a man practicing self-restraint. Just as a certain chaste and untainted man once wished to have conversation and familiarity with a woman, in which he became ensnared and quickly lost the seal of chastity; for attending to the sweetness of her words and gazing upon the beauty of her face, he was undone, saying: "Because of women, many are dead and wounded." Whence the "sting" says: woman is the form of sin and the condition of death. 

**Jerome.** <note>original: "Hiero." — Saint Jerome, a Church Father known for his ascetic writings</note> The door of the devil, the way of iniquity, the sting of a scorpion, and woman is a harmful race. Likewise, the appearance of a woman is a fiery sword. Remember that the conversation of a woman cast man out of paradise. 

**Augustine.** <note>original: "Augu." — Saint Augustine of Hippo</note> Woman is the most effective glue for catching souls. What was stronger than Samson? What wiser than Solomon? What holier than David? All these were subverted by lust <term>lust (original: "luxuria") — in this medieval context, the term refers specifically to sexual desire or excess that leads away from spiritual duty</term>. 

**Ecclesiasticus.** <note>original: "eccle." — The Book of Sirach from the Bible</note> From woman sin had its beginning, and through her we all die. Whence the ancients kept themselves from them. Just as **Vegetius** relates in the second book, concerning the restraint of Alexander <note>Alexander the Great</note>: who, when a maiden of extraordinary beauty betrothed to a certain prince was handed over to him, spared her with the greatest abstinence, so that he did not even look at her, but sent her back to her betrothed; by which release he reconciled the minds of the women and the princes to himself. 

**Valerius** <note>Valerius Maximus, a Roman historian</note> relates something similar about Scipio <note>Scipio Africanus</note> in the first book, saying that when he understood that a maiden of extraordinary beauty betrothed to a certain nobleman was among the hostages who were at Carthage—after Carthage had been captured by him—he summoned the parents and the betrothed and handed the untouched maiden over to him. And the gold that had been offered for her ransom he gave to the maiden as a dowry or to the husband as a wedding gift. Through this generosity and restraint <note>original: "continentiam"</note>, he won their hearts for himself. **Valerius** also tells of the wondrous restraint of the philosopher Xenocrates in the same book, saying

<summary>This page presents a stark medieval philosophical and theological comparison between the nature of man and woman, utilizing harsh ascetic tropes to warn against lust, followed by classical examples of male restraint in war.</summary>
<keywords>asceticism, misogyny, self-restraint (continentia), Scipio Africanus, Alexander the Great, Saint Jerome, Saint Augustine, medieval philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 141 ---
<meta>This page continues the moralizing discourse common in medieval "Mirrors" or fable collections, likely the "Dialogus Creaturarum." The previous page likely dealt with themes of virtue or specific parables of human conduct.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<sig>r iiij</sig>
# On Life and Death. Chapter 122.

¶ In Athens, certain young men promised an unchaste woman a sum of money if she could bend the mind of a philosopher toward lust. Coming by night, she lay down beside him, yet his self-control did not falter in the slightest. When the youths mocked her because she had been unable to bend his will, she replied that she had not gone to a man, but to a statue. Indeed, she called the philosopher a "statue" because of his immovable <term>continence</term> <note>self-restraint, specifically regarding physical desires</note>.

---

<image-desc>A vertical woodcut illustration positioned to the right of the first fifteen lines of the main text block. It depicts two figures standing on a hatched ground. On the left is a skeletal personification of Death, with a visible intestinal coil in its torso. On the right, a living man, mostly nude, walks toward Death with his hands raised as if in speech or appeal.</image-desc>

Death is, according to the philosopher, "eternal sleep, the dread of the rich, the desire of the poor, an incurable event, a thief of man, the flight of life, and the dissolution of man." Life, however, is "the joy of the good, the sadness of the wretched, and the expectation of death." And because life is the expectation of death, it <note>Life personified</note> approaches Death with prayers and says: "O inevitable death, hear me and have mercy on me. Do not release upon me the punishment I expect from you; take from me gold and silver, precious stones, towns, horses, lands, estates, palaces, possessions, and whatever you wish—only do not touch me." To which Death replies: "You ask for impossibilities, O sister. Only things that are honorable and possible should be asked of God; therefore, you have not spoken wisely." 

Because it is said to man: "Death awaits you everywhere; and if you are wise, you will await it everywhere." For the Psalmist says: *What man is there who lives and shall not see death?* <note>original: "Quis homo qui viuit & non videbit mortem" — from Psalm 89:48</note> as if to say, there is no one living who is not destined to die. Hence the verse: *By no fate can you evade death; death cuts and death kills what is created with care.* <note>original: "Per nullas sortem poteris euadere mortem. Mors secat. mors necat quod care creatur."</note> Therefore, receive me patiently, for I have come to do nothing new to you. 

For Seneca <note>a Roman Stoic philosopher</note> says: "No one is so ignorant as not to know that they will die someday; yet when it approaches, they tremble and weep. Why do you weep? What do you wish for? You waste your labor; the world claims all things. This is nothing new to you; you were born under this law. This happened to your father, to your mother, and to your ancestors; this happened to all before you and will happen to all after you." For life was given with the expectation of death; it is a universal law that commands one to be born and to die. 

You should understand this while conducting your life. He <note>Seneca</note> also says: "We must bear what we cannot avoid." Consider the example of David concerning his dead son <note>King David in 2 Samuel 12</note>; since he was dead, he said: "Why should I fast? Can I bring him back? I shall go to him, but he will not return to me." Similarly, when the death of his son was announced to a philosopher, he said: "When I fathered him, I knew he was destined to die." Valerius <note>Valerius Maximus, a 1st-century historian</note> relates in his sixth book that when Anaxagoras <note>a Greek philosopher</note> heard of his son's death, he replied: "You report nothing unexpected or new. I knew that he was born of me by the law of nature—that a law is given with the hope of receiving and the duty of returning—and that no one dies who has not lived, just as no one lives who is not destined to die." 

It is told in the same place of another philosopher <note>historically Xenophon</note> that upon hearing of the death of his eldest son, who had fallen in battle, he was content only to set aside his crown, for he was performing a solemn sacrifice. Then, having asked how he had fallen, and hearing that he died fighting most bravely, he replaced the crown upon his head.

<vocab>Mors (Death), Vita (Life), Seneca, David, Valerius, Anaxagoras, Philosophus (Philosopher), Continentia (Self-restraint)</vocab>

<summary>This page discusses the inevitability of death through philosophical definitions, a personified dialogue between Life and Death, and historical anecdotes of Stoic endurance. It emphasizes that death is a universal law that must be accepted with dignity rather than fear.</summary>
<keywords>stoicism, death, Seneca, Anaxagoras, King David, Athenian philosopher, memento mori, inevitability</keywords>

--- Page 142 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on Death, moving from the philosophical definition of death to historical and hagiographic examples of stoic acceptance, concluding with a vivid "memento mori" description of physical decomposition.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>

And by the gods to whom he sacrificed, he testified that he felt greater pleasure from the virtue of his son than bitterness from his son's death. Jerome <note>St. Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), Church Father and translator of the Vulgate Bible</note> relates that when Saint Melania <note>Melania the Elder (c. 325–410 AD), a Roman noblewoman and desert mother</note>, a most noble woman, had not yet buried the small body of her husband, she lost two sons at the same time. She was about to give a response that sounds incredible, but with Christ as my witness, I shall not be deceived. Who would not think she would lie there with disheveled hair and torn clothes? Yet not a drop of a single tear flowed. She stood immobile and, throwing herself at the feet of Christ as if holding Him in an embrace, she said: "I am ready to serve You, Lord, for You have freed me from such a great burden." 

It is read in the chronicles of the emperors that a certain wife buried Octavia <note>Likely referring to Livia, the wife of Emperor Augustus, who buried her sister-in-law Octavia</note>, and another buried her son named Drusus. And although she was a pagan, yet through the great natural wisdom existing in her, she laid aside all signs of grief, saying: "What profit is there in weeping for what cannot be avoided, and once it comes, cannot be recalled?" Hence Seneca <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca (c. 4 BC – 65 AD), Stoic philosopher</note> says: "The wise man is not afflicted by the loss of children nor friends." For their death should be borne in the same way he expects his own—certainly not as something better. 

Death is like a certain <term>bridle</term> <note>a metaphor for restraint</note> restraining men so that they might overflow and run about less through the broad paths of desires and lusts. The meditation on death is philosophy, as Plato says. Hence it is read in the life of John the Almsgiver <note>St. John the Merciful (d. 619 AD), Patriarch of Alexandria</note>: in ancient times, after an emperor was crowned, the builders of monuments would immediately enter to him, saying: "From what or from what kind of metal does your majesty command your monument to be made?" They were suggesting to him that he was a corruptible and transitory man, so that he might take care of himself and dispose and govern his kingdom piously. According to that saying in Ecclesiastes 7: "Remember your end, and you shall never sin." 

For Alphonsus <note>Petrus Alfonsi (c. 1062–1140), author of the 'Disciplina Clericalis'</note> recounts in his treatise on prudence concerning the death of Alexander the Great: when a golden tomb was being made, several philosophers gathered at it, of whom one said: "Alexander made a treasure out of gold; now, on the contrary, gold makes a treasure out of him." Another also said: "Alexander used to command the peoples; today the peoples command him." And another said: "Yesterday Alexander could have freed many from death; today he could not avoid the darts of death itself." And another said: "Yesterday Alexander led an army; today he is led by them to burial." And another said: "Yesterday Alexander trod upon the earth, and today he himself is pressed down by it." And another said: "Yesterday the nations feared Alexander; today they consider him worthless." And another said: "Yesterday Alexander had friends; today he has all as equals." And another said: "Yesterday the whole world was not enough for him; today he is content with a five-foot grave." 

If anyone would consider these things well, he would restrain himself in words and ways. It is said of the wicked <note>original: "begetio", likely a corruption of "peccatore" (sinner) or a reference to the "hypocrite" in Job 20</note> that he shall perish like dung in the end. Job 20. Therefore Ecclesiastes commands: "Remember the end. It is better to go to the house of mourning than to the house of feasting; for there the end of all men warns a person, and the youth considers what shall be for him, who must be closed by a similar end." Ecclesiastes 7. 

Therefore pay attention and consider that in the death of anyone, the nose grows cold, the teeth turn black, the face pales, and the nerves of the body are broken; the heart, as it is said, splits from excessive pain. All the limbs wither like wood and stones. Nothing in the world is so abominable and tedious as the corpse of a dead man. It is not thrown into the waters lest the waters be infected. It is not hung in the air lest the air be corrupted. But like the worst poison, it is thrown into a pit, and lest it be seen any longer, earth is heaped upon it more swiftly. Behold how the glory of the world is ended! For it is shut in a most stinking pit, where his heart quickly decays. The eyes waste away in their strength; the ears fall from the head; the nose is torn from the face; the tongue rots in the mouth; his heart bursts in the body. But alas, alas for me, Lord! How will the eyes delight to see beautiful things? [How will] the ears [delight to hear]...

<summary>This page uses historical and biblical examples to argue for the stoic acceptance of death and concludes with a graphic description of the body's decomposition to emphasize the vanity of worldly glory.</summary>
<keywords>Memento Mori, Saint Melania, Alexander the Great, Stoicism, physical decay, vanity, Seneca, John the Almsgiver, Ecclesiastes, Job</keywords>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page concludes the philosophical and theological treatise on mortality. It follows a discussion on the inevitability of death, using the physical decline of the body and the disappearance of social status as final arguments for spiritual humility.</meta>

...dreadful vanities. The nose to smell sweet things. The tongue to speak shameful and useless things. The mouth to taste sweets. The heart to think of vain and vile things. Thus Bernard <note>Saint Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a Cistercian abbot and influential mystic</note> says: "Why are you proud, dust and ashes? Whose conception is in sin, who is born in misery, whose life is a punishment, and whose death is an agony?" Especially when a wretched man declines toward death or old age, his heart is shaken, his head is afflicted, hope languishes, his breath stinks, his face is wrinkled, his stature is bent, his eyes grow dim, his joints wobble, his nostrils run, his hair fails, his teeth rot, and he loses his strength; one moment he is joyful, the next sad, then he becomes infirm. O wretched condition! Why do you not notice how miserable this life is? Consider, therefore, your parents, children, and ancestors, and you will find they are no more. And as Bernard says: "Tell me, where are the lovers of the world who a short time ago were with us in the world? Absolutely nothing remains of them but ashes." And so I ask you, please: where now are the barons, where are the princes, where are the leaders? Surely they have passed like a shadow and have been reduced to nothing. Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational theologian of Western Christianity</note> says the same: "Go to the tomb, take up the bones and discern, if you can, who was the master and who the servant; who was beautiful and who deformed; who was noble and who common; who was wise and who a fool. You will not be able to tell them apart." Therefore, think on where you came from, and blush for where you are; groan for where you are going, and tremble so that you may manage to return to the place from which you were expelled <note>Referring to the fall of man and the desire to return to Paradise</note>. May He who lives and reigns as God without end deign to grant this to us. Throughout all ages of ages. Amen.
